Exam FAM Study Manual

A syllabus-aligned review of short-term insurance and reinsurance, severity/frequency/aggregate loss models, parametric estimation, credibility, ratemaking and reserving, option pricing fundamentals, and the theory of long-term (life contingent) insurance coverages — with worked examples and practice problems for the Society of Actuaries Fundamentals of Actuarial Mathematics Exam.

How to Use This Manual

This manual is organized into ten parts that mirror the ten official SOA learning-outcome topics for Exam FAM, weighted the same way the real exam is weighted. The first six parts cover short-term (general insurance) topics — coverage modifications and reinsurance, loss models, estimation, credibility, ratemaking/reserving, and option pricing. The final four parts cover long-term (life contingent) topics — insurance coverage fundamentals, mortality models, present value random variables, and premium/policy value calculations. Each part is broken into chapters with worked examples, and closes with a practice problem set with full solutions.

Click any entry in the left-hand outline to jump straight to that section — the outline stays visible as you scroll so you can move between topics without losing your place. On a phone or narrow window, tap the ☰ Contents button in the top-left corner to open the outline.

Suggested order: the short-term topics (Parts I–VI) and long-term topics (Parts VII–X) are largely independent of each other and can be studied in either order — but within each half, work sequentially, since later parts build on earlier ones (e.g. Part IX depends on the mortality notation built in Part VIII; Part X depends on the present-value machinery built in Part IX). Attempt every practice problem before revealing its solution, and revisit the Formula Reference (Appendix A) throughout.
Source note: This manual is an original work written for exam preparation, aligned to the SOA Exam FAM syllabus's topic structure and weighting. All theory explanations, worked examples, and practice problems — including the full-length practice exam in Appendix B — are original to this manual, not reproduced or adapted from any textbook or third-party question bank.

This manual offers no guarantee of exam results, and candidates should always confirm current exam rules, syllabus weights, and permitted materials directly on the SOA’s official website before sitting for the exam.

Exam Overview & Study Plan

The Fundamentals of Actuarial Mathematics Exam (Exam FAM) is a 3.5-hour, 34-question multiple-choice exam administered as a computer-based test. Each question has five answer choices (A–E) and unanswered questions are scored as incorrect, so plan to answer every question. The exam assumes knowledge of probability, mathematical statistics, and financial mathematics as covered in Exam P and Exam FM — if either of those feels shaky, a quick refresher before starting this manual will pay off, since FAM builds directly on that foundation. Candidates receive a table packet during the exam (distribution formulas, life tables) but should not rely on it for anything beyond a lookup — knowing when and how to use each formula is the actual skill being tested.

Part I — Short-Term Insurance & Reinsurance Coverages (5–10%)

This part covers the smallest but highly testable corner of Exam FAM: how a property/casualty (short-duration) contract reshapes the raw loss random variable X once deductibles, limits, and coinsurance are layered on top of it, how inflation quietly erodes the value of a fixed deductible over time, and how an insurer can pass along a share of that risk to a reinsurer through proportional or excess-of-loss treaties. Every idea here reduces to careful bookkeeping with the "limited expected value" function E[X∧k] — master that one building block and the rest of the part falls into place quickly. Because this topic carries a light exam weight, expect one or two multi-part questions rather than an entire block of the exam, but the calculations are quick to set up and are a reliable source of partial-credit-friendly points.

1. Coverage Modifications: Deductibles, Limits & Coinsurance

Learning Outcomes Covered: Recognize the standard ways an insurance contract modifies the ground-up loss amount before the insurer pays a claim (ordinary deductible, franchise deductible, policy limit, coinsurance), and compute the resulting insurer payment for a given loss, including the distinction between the per-loss and per-payment versions of the payment random variable.

Let X denote the ground-up loss — the full dollar amount of damage suffered by the policyholder, before any policy provisions are applied. A real contract almost never pays X dollar-for-dollar. Instead it applies some combination of the four modifications below, and the exam wants you comfortable translating any combination into a single payment formula.

1.1 Ordinary Deductible

Under an ordinary deductible of size d, the policyholder absorbs the first d of every loss, and the insurer pays only the amount by which the loss exceeds d. If the loss never reaches d, the insurer pays nothing at all — there is no partial reimbursement below the deductible.

Ordinary Deductible PaymentPayment = (X − d)₊ = max(X − d, 0)

1.2 Franchise Deductible

A franchise deductible uses the same threshold d to decide whether a payment is made, but not how much is paid. Once the loss exceeds d, the insurer pays the loss in full — including the portion below d — rather than just the excess. Below the threshold, nothing is paid, exactly as with the ordinary version.

Franchise Deductible PaymentPayment = 0 if X ≤ d, Payment = X if X > d
Common trap: students frequently assume a franchise deductible is simply "more generous" and pays extra on top of the ordinary deductible amount. It is more precise to say a franchise deductible removes the deductible's bite entirely once a claim qualifies — the insurer's payment jumps discontinuously from 0 to the full loss X the instant X crosses d. An ordinary deductible payment, by contrast, rises continuously from 0 starting exactly at X = d. Sketch both payment functions against X if you ever lose track of which is which — the franchise graph has a jump of height d at X = d.

1.3 Policy Limit

A policy limit u caps the loss amount that is eligible for reimbursement — losses above u are simply truncated to u before any further adjustment. Combined with an ordinary deductible, this produces a payment that is capped at u − d, called the maximum covered loss.

Deductible + Limit PaymentPayment = min(X,u) − min(X,d) = X∧u − X∧d

1.4 Coinsurance

Coinsurance is a fixed proportion α (with 0 < α ≤ 1) that the insurer pays of whatever amount survives the deductible and limit provisions; the policyholder is responsible for the remaining 1 − α as a form of continuing cost-sharing on every dollar of a covered loss, not just below the deductible.

1.5 The Combined Payment Formula

Putting all four pieces together gives the single formula that governs the vast majority of coverage-modification problems on this exam. Let YL denote the per-loss payment variable — "per-loss" because it is defined for every loss event X, including those that produce a zero payment.

Per-Loss Payment (ordinary deductible)YL = α · [min(X,u) − min(X,d)] = α · (X∧u − X∧d)

1.6 Per-Loss vs. Per-Payment Variables

The random variable YL above is defined on the same sample space as the original loss X: whenever X ≤ d, YL simply equals 0. That zero is a real, meaningful outcome that pulls down the mean of YL. In many applications, though, we only care about the size of a payment given that a payment happens at all — for instance, when pricing based on claims data, only reported claims (which by definition triggered a payment) appear in the dataset. This conditional version is called the per-payment variable, written YP.

Per-Payment PaymentYP = YL | X > d

Because YP is the distribution of X − d conditional on X > d (before applying the limit and coinsurance), statisticians describe it as left-censored and shifted at d: "shifted" because the origin has been moved from 0 to d — the variable measures the excess over the deductible, not the loss itself — and "left-censored" is the term inherited from the per-loss variable YL, which does pile probability mass at exactly 0 for every loss that fails to reach d. The per-payment variable removes that atom at zero by conditioning it away, leaving a proper continuous (or discrete) distribution on (0, u-d].

Relationship between the two meansE[YL] = E[YP] · P(X > d)
A fast way to remember the direction of the inequality: since YP throws away all the "free" zero outcomes that drag the per-loss average down, we always have E[YP] ≥ E[YL], with equality only in the degenerate case d = 0.
Example 1.1

A commercial property policy carries an ordinary deductible of 500, a policy limit of 10,000 (maximum covered loss, applied to X before the deductible is subtracted), and 80% coinsurance. Find the insurer's payment for ground-up losses of 300, 1,200, and 15,000.

Show Solution

Use YL = 0.8·(min(X,10000) − min(X,500)).

For X = 300: since 300 < 500, the loss never reaches the deductible, so payment = 0.

For X = 1,200: payment = 0.8·(1,200 − 500) = 0.8(700) = 560.

For X = 15,000: the loss exceeds the limit, so it is first capped at 10,000: payment = 0.8·(10,000 − 500) = 0.8(9,500) = 7,600.

Example 1.2

Compare an ordinary deductible of 1,000 against a franchise deductible of 1,000 (no limit, no coinsurance) for ground-up losses of 800 and 1,500.

Show Solution

At X = 800: this is below the threshold under either version, so both provisions pay 0.

At X = 1,500: the ordinary deductible pays only the excess, 1,500 − 1,000 = 500. The franchise deductible pays the loss in full because it exceeded the threshold: 1,500. The franchise payment exceeds the ordinary payment by exactly d = 1,000 whenever a payment is triggered under both.

Example 1.3

Ground-up losses follow an exponential distribution with mean θ = 1,000. The policy has an ordinary deductible of 500, no limit, and full coinsurance (α = 1). Find E[YL] and E[YP].

Show Solution

For an exponential distribution, E[X∧d] = θ(1 − e−d/θ). Here E[X∧500] = 1,000(1 − e−0.5) = 1,000(1 − 0.6065) = 393.47.

The per-loss mean is E[YL] = E[X] − E[X∧500] = 1,000 − 393.47 = 606.53.

The exponential distribution is memoryless, so the amount by which a loss exceeds 500, given that it does exceed 500, has the same exponential distribution as the original loss. Hence E[YP] = θ = 1,000 exactly — notably larger than E[YL]. As a check, P(X>500) = e−0.5 = 0.6065, and E[YP]·P(X>500) = 1,000(0.6065) = 606.5 ≈ E[YL], confirming the relationship between the two means.

Example 1.4

Ground-up losses are uniformly distributed on (0, 10,000). A policy applies an ordinary deductible of 1,000, a policy limit of 8,000, and 75% coinsurance. Find E[YL].

Show Solution

For X ~ Uniform(0,b), E[X∧k] = k − k²/(2b) for k ≤ b.

E[X∧1,000] = 1,000 − 1,000,000/20,000 = 1,000 − 50 = 950.

E[X∧8,000] = 8,000 − 64,000,000/20,000 = 8,000 − 3,200 = 4,800.

E[YL] = 0.75·(4,800 − 950) = 0.75(3,850) = 2,887.50.

On multi-part exam questions, it almost always pays to compute E[X∧d] and E[X∧u] as two separate numbers first, and only then combine them with coinsurance. Trying to build one giant formula in your head is where sign errors creep in.

2. Loss Elimination Ratio & Inflation

Learning Outcomes Covered: Define and compute the loss elimination ratio produced by a deductible, and quantify how uniform inflation of ground-up losses changes both the expected limited loss and the loss elimination ratio when the deductible and limit are held fixed in nominal dollars.

2.1 Loss Elimination Ratio

The loss elimination ratio (LER) measures the fraction of expected ground-up losses that a deductible removes from the insurer's obligation — in other words, how much of the risk the deductible "eliminates" before insurance even engages.

Loss Elimination RatioLER(d) = E[X∧d] / E[X]

A larger deductible always produces a larger LER, since E[X∧d] is non-decreasing in d and eventually approaches E[X] as d → ∞. The LER is bounded between 0 (no deductible) and 1 (a deductible so large that essentially no loss ever exceeds it).

2.2 The Effect of Inflation on a Fixed Deductible

Suppose every ground-up loss inflates uniformly by a factor of (1+r) — a reasonable model for claim severity trend from one policy year to the next — but the contract's deductible d stays fixed in nominal dollars (this is standard: a policy renewed with the same stated deductible does not automatically index that number for inflation). The inflated loss is X′ = (1+r)X, and because (1+r) is a positive constant, the min operator factors out cleanly:

Inflated Limited Expected ValueE[(1+r)X ∧ d] = (1+r) · E[X ∧ d/(1+r)]

The intuition: a nominal deductible of d applied to inflated losses behaves exactly like a smaller, "real" deductible of d/(1+r) applied to the original, pre-inflation losses, then re-inflated. Because inflation shrinks the deductible's real purchasing power, a larger share of each loss distribution now clears the threshold, which pushes E[X∧d] upward toward d and — since the denominator E[X] is scaling up by the full factor (1+r) while the numerator scales up by less than that — pushes the loss elimination ratio itself downward.

Common trap: it is tempting to think inflation raises the LER because "the deductible now covers more of the loss in dollar terms." The opposite happens to the ratio: the deductible fails to keep pace with rising losses, so proportionally fewer losses are fully absorbed below it, and the LER falls. This deductible-erosion effect is a recurring exam theme — always compare the ratio, not just the raw dollar amount of E[X∧d].

2.3 The Leveraging Effect on Losses Above the Deductible

A related and testable consequence: because the deductible effectively shrinks in real terms, the expected amount the insurer pays in excess of the deductible, E[(X−d)₊] = E[X] − E[X∧d], grows faster than the underlying inflation rate r. This is often called the leveraging effect of inflation on excess losses, and it is one of the main reasons layered reinsurance pricing (Chapter 4) must be re-evaluated frequently even when overall claim severity trend looks moderate.

Example 2.1

Ground-up losses follow an exponential distribution with θ = 2,000. A policy has an ordinary deductible of 1,000. Find the loss elimination ratio.

Show Solution

E[X∧1,000] = 2,000(1 − e−1,000/2,000) = 2,000(1 − e−0.5) = 2,000(1 − 0.6065) = 786.94.

LER = 786.94 / 2,000 = 0.3935, or about 39.35%.

Example 2.2

Using the same exponential loss distribution as Example 2.1 (θ = 2,000, deductible fixed at 1,000 in nominal terms), suppose losses inflate uniformly by 10% before the next policy period. Find the new expected limited loss and the new loss elimination ratio.

Show Solution

The effective real deductible becomes d/(1+r) = 1,000/1.1 = 909.09.

E[X∧909.09] = 2,000(1 − e−909.09/2,000) = 2,000(1 − e−0.4545) = 2,000(1 − 0.6347) = 730.52.

E[(1.1)X∧1,000] = 1.1(730.52) = 803.57. This is the new expected amount the deductible absorbs, in inflated dollars.

New mean loss: E[(1.1)X] = 1.1(2,000) = 2,200.

New LER = 803.57 / 2,200 = 0.3653, or 36.53% — down from 39.35% before inflation, exactly the erosion effect described above.

Example 2.3

For the same policy as Example 2.2, verify the leveraging effect: show that the percentage increase in expected losses excess of the deductible exceeds the 10% inflation rate.

Show Solution

Before inflation: E[(X−1,000)₊] = E[X] − E[X∧1,000] = 2,000 − 786.94 = 1,213.06.

After inflation: E[((1.1)X−1,000)₊] = E[(1.1)X] − E[(1.1)X∧1,000] = 2,200 − 803.57 = 1,396.43.

Percentage increase = (1,396.43 − 1,213.06)/1,213.06 = 0.1512, i.e. 15.12% — noticeably larger than the 10% loss trend, confirming that a fixed deductible leverages inflation for the layer of loss it does not absorb.

Whenever a problem gives you an inflation rate alongside a fixed deductible, your first move should be to replace d with d/(1+r) inside the original (pre-inflation) limited expected value formula, then multiply the whole result by (1+r) at the end. Skipping the final re-inflation step is the single most common arithmetic slip on this topic.

3. Proportional Reinsurance

Learning Outcomes Covered: Describe how quota share and surplus share reinsurance treaties operate, including the "line" concept underlying surplus share, and calculate how each form splits both losses and premium between the ceding insurer and the reinsurer, along with the resulting impact on the insurer's retained loss ratio.

Reinsurance is insurance purchased by an insurance company (the cedant or ceding company) from another carrier (the reinsurer) to share in the risk it has written. Proportional (also called pro rata) reinsurance is the simplest structural form: the reinsurer agrees up front to take a specified share of every loss on a covered risk — and, critically, that exact same share of the associated premium. Nothing about the size of any individual claim changes which share applies; only the percentage split matters.

3.1 Quota Share

Under a quota share treaty, the insurer retains a fixed percentage α of every loss (and every premium dollar) written within the treaty's scope, ceding the remaining 1 − α to the reinsurer on every single risk, regardless of that risk's size.

Quota Share SplitRetained loss = α·X, Ceded loss = (1 − α)·X

Because premium is ceded in the exact same proportion as losses, a quota share treaty leaves the insurer's retained loss ratio unchanged from its gross loss ratio — the treaty does not, by itself, make the retained book of business any more or less profitable per premium dollar. Reinsurers typically pay the ceding company a ceding commission on the ceded premium (compensation for underwriting and acquisition expenses the insurer already incurred), and this commission — not the loss ratio — is where quota share primarily affects the insurer's net financial result.

3.2 Surplus Share

A surplus share treaty also splits both losses and premium proportionally, but the retained percentage is no longer a single fixed number — it varies risk by risk, based on the size of each individual risk relative to a company-selected retention amount called the line. If a risk's total insured value is at or below the line, the insurer retains it in full (a quota share of 100% for that risk). If the insured value exceeds the line, the insurer retains only the line amount and cedes the remaining "surplus" above it.

Surplus Share Retention Fraction (risk with insured value S > line L)Retained fraction = L / S, Ceded fraction = (S − L) / S

That retention fraction, once set for a given risk, applies proportionally to every loss that occurs on that risk during the treaty period — it behaves exactly like a quota share, just with a retention percentage individually calibrated per risk instead of one blanket percentage for the whole book. This lets an insurer keep small, well-understood risks entirely on its own books while automatically ceding a larger share of unusually large risks, which is the main practical advantage of surplus share over a flat quota share.

Proportional Reinsurance: Quota Share vs. Surplus Share
FeatureQuota ShareSurplus Share
Retention percentageSingle fixed α for the whole treatyVaries by risk: min(1, L/S)
Small risksStill cede (1−α) even if unnecessaryRetained in full if insured value ≤ line
Large risksSame cession percentage as small risksCede a larger percentage automatically
Administrative burdenLow — one ratio applies to everythingHigher — a line and cession fraction must be tracked per risk

3.3 Impact on Reserving and Pricing

Because proportional reinsurance splits every loss — case reserves as well as paid claims — by the same fixed or risk-specific fraction, the insurer's net (retained) reserve is simply its gross case reserve multiplied by the applicable retained fraction; no separate reserving methodology is needed beyond tracking which cession fraction applies to each risk or treaty year. Pricing scales the same way: a net rate equals the gross rate times the retained fraction, since both the underlying risk and the associated premium shrink by the identical multiplicative factor regardless of loss size. This clean proportionality is exactly what makes quota share and surplus share administratively simple compared with excess-of-loss structures, where — as the next chapter shows — the retained and ceded pieces do not move together in any single fixed ratio.

Example 3.1

An insurer cedes 40% of a book of business under a quota share treaty (retaining α = 0.6). A covered loss of 50,000 occurs, and the associated policy carried a premium of 200,000. Find the retained and ceded amounts of both the loss and the premium.

Show Solution

Retained loss = 0.6(50,000) = 30,000; ceded loss = 0.4(50,000) = 20,000.

Retained premium = 0.6(200,000) = 120,000; ceded premium = 0.4(200,000) = 80,000.

Example 3.2

An insurer's gross premium for a treaty year is 1,000,000 and gross losses are 650,000. The insurer cedes 30% quota share. Show that the retained loss ratio equals the gross loss ratio.

Show Solution

Gross loss ratio = 650,000/1,000,000 = 65%.

Retained premium = 0.7(1,000,000) = 700,000; retained losses = 0.7(650,000) = 455,000.

Retained loss ratio = 455,000/700,000 = 65% — identical to the gross ratio, illustrating that a pure quota share cession (before considering ceding commission) leaves the insurer's loss ratio unaffected.

Example 3.3

Continuing Example 3.2, the reinsurer pays the ceding company a ceding commission of 25% of ceded premium to reimburse acquisition expenses. Find the commission received and comment on its effect.

Show Solution

Ceded premium = 0.3(1,000,000) = 300,000.

Ceding commission = 0.25(300,000) = 75,000, paid by the reinsurer to the insurer.

This commission does not appear in the loss ratio calculation at all — it is an expense offset. It improves the insurer's overall net financial result from the treaty without changing the 65% retained loss ratio computed above, which is exactly why ceding commission (not loss ratio movement) is the primary lever insurers negotiate on a quota share treaty.

Example 3.4

An insurer writes a surplus share treaty with a line of 200,000. Risk A has an insured value of 800,000 and Risk B has an insured value of 150,000. A loss of 100,000 occurs on Risk A, and separately a loss of 60,000 occurs on Risk B. Find the insurer's and reinsurer's shares of each loss.

Show Solution

Risk A: insured value 800,000 > line, so retained fraction = 200,000/800,000 = 25%, ceded fraction = 75%. On the 100,000 loss: insurer pays 0.25(100,000) = 25,000; reinsurer pays 0.75(100,000) = 75,000.

Risk B: insured value 150,000 < line of 200,000, so the risk is retained in full. On the 60,000 loss, the insurer pays the entire 60,000 and the reinsurer pays 0.

On surplus share problems, always compute the retention fraction from the insured value (the sum insured / policy limit), not from the size of the actual loss. The fraction is set once per risk at issue and then applied to every loss on that risk, however large or small that particular loss turns out to be.

4. Excess-of-Loss Reinsurance

Learning Outcomes Covered: Describe how per-risk excess-of-loss and aggregate excess-of-loss (stop-loss) reinsurance treaties operate, and allocate specific claim amounts — or aggregate loss totals — between the ceding insurer and the reinsurer under a stated treaty structure, including the expected-value layering formula built from limited expected values.

Unlike proportional reinsurance, excess-of-loss (XOL) reinsurance does not split every loss by a fixed percentage. Instead, the reinsurer pays only the portion of a loss that falls within a specified dollar layer, defined by a retention (or attachment point) r and a layer width m. Losses below the retention stay entirely with the insurer; losses above the top of the layer are capped from the reinsurer's perspective, with any remaining excess reverting back to the insurer.

4.1 Per-Risk Excess of Loss

Per-risk XOL applies the layer separately to each individual claim. A treaty is typically quoted as "m excess of r," meaning the reinsurer's layer runs from r up to r+m.

Reinsurer's Per-Risk XOL PaymentReinsurer pays = min(max(X − r, 0), m)
Insurer's Retained AmountInsurer pays = X − Reinsurer's payment = min(X,r) + max(X − (r+m), 0)

Notice the insurer's retained payment has two pieces: the retention itself on any loss that reaches it, plus — if the loss blows through the top of the layer — whatever spills back over above r+m. A large loss therefore leaves the insurer holding both the bottom slice and the top slice, with the reinsurer responsible only for the middle band.

4.2 Aggregate Excess of Loss (Stop-Loss)

Rather than applying a layer to each individual claim, an aggregate excess-of-loss (also called stop-loss) treaty applies the retention and width to the total of all losses S over a defined period (typically one year), protecting the insurer against an unusually bad aggregate year rather than any single large claim.

Reinsurer's Aggregate XOL PaymentReinsurer pays = min(max(S − r, 0), m)

The mechanics are identical to per-risk XOL — only the underlying random variable changes, from a single claim X to the aggregate loss S.

Proportional vs. Excess-of-Loss Reinsurance
FeatureProportional (Quota/Surplus Share)Excess of Loss
Basis of splitFixed percentage of every lossLayer defined by retention and width
Premium sharingSplit proportionally with lossesReinsurer charges a separate negotiated premium, not a fixed share of the insurer's premium
Effect on small lossesReinsurer participates in every loss, however smallReinsurer pays nothing until the retention is reached
Effect on large lossesReinsurer's share grows linearly with loss sizeReinsurer's payment is capped once the layer is exhausted
Primary purposeCapacity relief / surplus reliefProtection against catastrophic single claims or bad aggregate years

4.3 Impact on Reserving and Pricing

Reserving and pricing under excess-of-loss reinsurance are considerably less mechanical than under a proportional treaty, precisely because the split is not a fixed percentage. The insurer cannot obtain its net case reserve by scaling the gross reserve down by a constant factor: a claim currently reserved entirely below the retention stays fully on the insurer's own books, a claim reserved within the layer is split between insurer and reinsurer at whatever point it currently sits, and a claim reserved above the top of the layer requires the insurer to hold reserves for both the bottom retention and the top spill-back simultaneously, with only the middle slice ceded. Pricing an XOL layer likewise requires modeling the full severity distribution — as in Example 4.4 below — rather than applying one retained percentage, since the expected cost of a layer depends on exactly how much probability mass a given severity curve places inside that specific band of dollar amounts. This is also why a layer's price must be revisited whenever severity trend or inflation assumptions change materially: the leveraging effect from Chapter 2 hits excess layers especially hard, since a fixed retention erodes in real terms every year that claim severity keeps rising.

4.4 Expected Reinsurer Payment via Limited Expected Values

Just as in Chapter 1, the expected value of a layer payment reduces neatly to a difference of two limited expected values, since min(max(X−r,0),m) = (X∧(r+m)) − (X∧r).

Expected Layer PaymentE[Reinsurer's payment] = E[X∧(r+m)] − E[X∧r]
Example 4.1

A per-risk XOL treaty is written as 400,000 excess of 100,000. Find the insurer's and reinsurer's shares of individual losses of 50,000, 350,000, and 700,000.

Show Solution

The layer runs from 100,000 to 500,000.

X = 50,000: below the retention. Insurer pays 50,000; reinsurer pays 0.

X = 350,000: excess over retention is 250,000, within the 400,000 width. Reinsurer pays 250,000; insurer pays the retention, 100,000.

X = 700,000: excess over retention is 600,000, which exceeds the 400,000 width, so the reinsurer's payment caps at 400,000. The insurer pays the retention of 100,000 plus the amount above the top of the layer, 700,000 − 500,000 = 200,000, for a total of 300,000. Check: 400,000 + 300,000 = 700,000. ✓

Example 4.2

An insurer buys a per-risk XOL treaty of 300,000 excess of 200,000 (layer from 200,000 to 500,000). Five claims are reported during the treaty year: 150,000, 320,000, 480,000, 900,000, and 50,000. Allocate each claim between insurer and reinsurer, and find the totals.

Show Solution
Claim-by-claim allocation
ClaimInsurer paysReinsurer pays
150,000150,000 (below retention)0
320,000200,000120,000
480,000200,000280,000
900,000200,000 + (900,000 − 500,000) = 600,000300,000 (layer exhausted)
50,00050,000 (below retention)0

Totals: insurer pays 150,000 + 200,000 + 200,000 + 600,000 + 50,000 = 1,200,000; reinsurer pays 0 + 120,000 + 280,000 + 300,000 + 0 = 700,000. Combined, 1,200,000 + 700,000 = 1,900,000, matching the sum of the five ground-up claims exactly, which is a useful self-check on any allocation problem.

Example 4.3

An insurer buys an aggregate stop-loss treaty of 1,000,000 excess of 2,000,000 on total annual losses S. Find the reinsurer's and insurer's shares if S = 2,600,000, and again if S = 3,500,000.

Show Solution

The aggregate layer runs from 2,000,000 to 3,000,000.

If S = 2,600,000: excess over the attachment is 600,000, within the 1,000,000 width. Reinsurer pays 600,000; insurer retains 2,000,000.

If S = 3,500,000: excess is 1,500,000, above the layer width, so the reinsurer's payment caps at 1,000,000. Insurer retains 2,000,000 + (3,500,000 − 3,000,000) = 2,500,000. Check: 1,000,000 + 2,500,000 = 3,500,000. ✓

Example 4.4

Ground-up claim sizes follow an exponential distribution with θ = 250,000. A per-risk XOL treaty is written as 300,000 excess of 200,000 (layer from 200,000 to 500,000). Find the expected payment per loss for the reinsurer and for the insurer.

Show Solution

E[X∧200,000] = 250,000(1 − e−200,000/250,000) = 250,000(1 − e−0.8) = 250,000(1 − 0.4493) = 137,668.

E[X∧500,000] = 250,000(1 − e−2) = 250,000(1 − 0.1353) = 216,166.

E[Reinsurer's payment] = 216,166 − 137,668 = 78,498.

E[Insurer's payment] = E[X] − 78,498 = 250,000 − 78,498 = 171,502. As a check, this also equals E[X∧200,000] + (E[X] − E[X∧500,000]) = 137,668 + 33,834 = 171,502. ✓

Common trap: when a loss exceeds the top of a per-risk layer, students often forget that the insurer's retained amount includes both the bottom retention and the spill-back above the exhausted layer — not just one or the other. Always compute the reinsurer's capped payment first, then let the insurer absorb the ground-up loss minus that capped amount; this avoids missing the spill-back piece entirely.

Part I Practice Problems

1. A policy has an ordinary deductible of 500 and 90% coinsurance, with no policy limit. Find the insurer's payment for a ground-up loss of 3,200.
Show Answer
Payment = 0.9(3,200 − 500) = 0.9(2,700) = 2,430.
2. Repeat problem 1, but with a franchise deductible of 500 instead of an ordinary deductible (coinsurance of 90% still applies to the full loss once the threshold is crossed). Find the payment for losses of 400 and 3,200.
Show Answer
At X = 400: below the threshold, payment = 0. At X = 3,200: the threshold is exceeded, so the full loss qualifies: payment = 0.9(3,200) = 2,880, compared to 2,430 under the ordinary deductible in problem 1.
3. A policy has an ordinary deductible of 2,000, a policy limit of 50,000, and 80% coinsurance. Find the insurer's payment for a ground-up loss of 75,000.
Show Answer
The loss first gets capped at the limit: min(75,000, 50,000) = 50,000. Payment = 0.8(50,000 − 2,000) = 0.8(48,000) = 38,400.
4. Ground-up losses follow an exponential distribution with θ = 5,000. A policy has an ordinary deductible of 1,000, no limit, and no coinsurance. Find E[YP], the expected payment given that a payment occurs.
Show Answer
By the memoryless property of the exponential distribution, the excess over the deductible, given that the deductible is exceeded, is exponential with the same mean as the original distribution: E[YP] = θ = 5,000.
5. Ground-up losses follow an exponential distribution with θ = 3,000. A policy has an ordinary deductible of 2,100. Find the loss elimination ratio.
Show Answer
E[X∧2,100] = 3,000(1 − e−0.7) = 3,000(1 − 0.4966) = 1,510.24. LER = 1,510.24/3,000 = 0.5034, or 50.34%.
6. For the policy in problem 5, suppose losses inflate uniformly by 8% while the deductible stays fixed in nominal terms at 2,100. Find the new loss elimination ratio.
Show Answer
Effective real deductible: 2,100/1.08 = 1,944.44. E[X∧1,944.44] = 3,000(1 − e−0.6481) = 3,000(1 − 0.5230) = 1,430.85. Re-inflated: E[(1.08)X∧2,100] = 1.08(1,430.85) = 1,545.32. New mean loss = 1.08(3,000) = 3,240. New LER = 1,545.32/3,240 = 0.4770, or 47.70% — down from 50.34%, illustrating deductible erosion from inflation.
7. Using the results from problems 5 and 6, find the percentage increase in the expected loss excess of the deductible, E[(X−2,100)₊], and compare it to the 8% inflation rate.
Show Answer
Before inflation: E[X] − E[X∧2,100] = 3,000 − 1,510.24 = 1,489.76. After inflation: 3,240 − 1,545.32 = 1,694.68. Percentage increase = (1,694.68 − 1,489.76)/1,489.76 = 0.1376, or 13.76%, which exceeds the 8% inflation rate — the leveraging effect described in Chapter 2.
8. An insurer cedes 35% of a risk under a quota share treaty. A covered loss of 220,000 occurs. Find the retained and ceded amounts.
Show Answer
Retained = 0.65(220,000) = 143,000. Ceded = 0.35(220,000) = 77,000.
9. A surplus share treaty has a line of 150,000. A risk has an insured value of 450,000, and a loss of 60,000 occurs on it. Find the insurer's and reinsurer's shares.
Show Answer
Retained fraction = 150,000/450,000 = 1/3. Insurer pays (1/3)(60,000) = 20,000; reinsurer pays (2/3)(60,000) = 40,000.
10. An insurer has gross premium of 500,000 and gross losses of 310,000 for the year. It cedes 40% quota share, and the reinsurer pays a ceding commission of 20% of ceded premium. Find (a) the insurer's retained loss ratio and (b) the ceding commission received.
Show Answer
Gross loss ratio = 310,000/500,000 = 62%. Retained premium = 0.6(500,000) = 300,000; retained losses = 0.6(310,000) = 186,000; retained loss ratio = 186,000/300,000 = 62%, unchanged from the gross ratio. Ceded premium = 0.4(500,000) = 200,000; ceding commission = 0.2(200,000) = 40,000.
11. A per-risk XOL treaty is written as 200,000 excess of 100,000. Find the insurer's and reinsurer's shares of a claim of 250,000.
Show Answer
Layer runs from 100,000 to 300,000. Excess over retention = 150,000, within the 200,000 width. Reinsurer pays 150,000; insurer pays the retention, 100,000.
12. Using the same treaty as problem 11 (200,000 excess of 100,000), find the insurer's and reinsurer's shares of a claim of 380,000.
Show Answer
Excess over retention = 280,000, above the 200,000 width, so the reinsurer's payment caps at 200,000. Insurer pays the retention of 100,000 plus the spill-back above the layer's top of 300,000: 380,000 − 300,000 = 80,000, for a total of 180,000. Check: 200,000 + 180,000 = 380,000. ✓
13. An insurer buys an aggregate stop-loss treaty of 800,000 excess of 1,500,000 on annual aggregate losses. If aggregate losses for the year total 2,100,000, find the reinsurer's and insurer's shares.
Show Answer
Layer runs from 1,500,000 to 2,300,000. Excess = 600,000, within the 800,000 width. Reinsurer pays 600,000; insurer retains 1,500,000.
14. Ground-up claim sizes follow an exponential distribution with θ = 100,000. A per-risk XOL treaty is written as 150,000 excess of 50,000. Find the expected payment per loss for the reinsurer.
Show Answer
Layer runs from 50,000 to 200,000. E[X∧50,000] = 100,000(1 − e−0.5) = 100,000(0.3935) = 39,347. E[X∧200,000] = 100,000(1 − e−2) = 100,000(0.8647) = 86,466. E[Reinsurer's payment] = 86,466 − 39,347 = 47,119.

Part II — Severity, Frequency & Aggregate Models (12.5–17.5%)

This part builds the probabilistic toolkit that sits at the center of short-term actuarial modeling. We start with a single claim's size (severity) — how to summarize it with moments and percentiles, and how to tell whether a fitted model behaves reasonably in the tail. We then turn to the count of claims (frequency) and study the (a,b,0) and (a,b,1) families, a small set of counting distributions whose entire probability mass function can be generated from one recursive rule. Finally we combine the two pieces into an aggregate loss — the total dollar amount paid on a block of business — and learn how to compute or approximate its distribution, and how to price protections such as stop-loss reinsurance using percentile-based risk measures like VaR and TVaR. Expect this part to be tested heavily and computationally: nearly every learning outcome here reduces to a formula you must be able to apply quickly and correctly under exam conditions.

The seven chapters that follow are deliberately sequenced to build on one another. Chapters 1 and 2 establish the severity toolkit (moments, percentiles, the limited expected value, scale families) that reappears, largely unchanged in form, once we move to aggregate losses in Chapter 7. Chapters 3 and 4 develop the frequency toolkit, culminating in a single recursive machine — the (a,b,0)/(a,b,1) recursion — capable of generating the Poisson, binomial, negative binomial, and their zero-truncated and zero-modified variants from one unifying idea. Chapters 5 through 7 then show how frequency and severity combine into an aggregate loss random variable, first through exact moment formulas, then through practical approximations and exact convolution, and finally through the percentile-based risk measures used to price reinsurance and set capital. Read in order, the part tells a single continuous story: how do we describe one claim, how do we describe how many claims there are, and how do we describe — and manage the risk of — the total.

1. Severity Moments, Percentiles & Tail Weight

Learning Outcomes Covered: Computing raw and central moments of a severity distribution directly from its density; computing percentiles (including the median) from the distribution function; defining and computing the limited expected value E[X∧d]; determining whether a given moment of a distribution exists, with the Pareto family as the primary diagnostic example; and building intuition for what makes a severity model "heavy-tailed" versus "light-tailed."

A severity distribution describes the dollar size of an individual claim, conditional on a claim having occurred. Before we can compare two candidate severity models, price a policy, or set reserves, we need a compact numeric summary of the distribution — and that summary almost always starts with moments and percentiles.

It helps to keep a concrete picture in mind throughout this chapter: an actuary has fit two or three candidate severity curves to the same claims data, and all of them may show a similar mean. The decisions that follow — how much reinsurance to buy, how much capital to hold, whether a proposed pricing model is defensible — hinge on details the mean alone cannot reveal: how spread out the distribution is (variance), where its center of mass sits relative to its middle observation (comparing mean to median), and, critically, how much probability the model places on rare but catastrophic outcomes far out in the right tail. Everything in this chapter is aimed at extracting that information reliably and quickly.

Raw and Central Moments by Direct Integration

For a continuous, non-negative random variable X with density f(x), the k-th raw moment is obtained by weighting each possible value by its likelihood and integrating over the support:

k-th Raw MomentE[Xk] = ∫0∞ xk f(x) dx

Setting k=1 gives the mean; the variance then follows from Var(X) = E[X2] − (E[X])2, which is almost always faster on the exam than integrating (x−μ)2f(x) directly, since it reuses the first two raw moments you would compute anyway. Higher central moments (skewness, kurtosis) are built the same way from E[X3], E[X4], etc., but for FAM the mean and variance — plus occasionally E[X3] for a skewness check — are what you will be asked to produce under time pressure.

Percentiles

The p-th percentile πp of X solves F(πp) = p, i.e. it is the value below which a proportion p of the probability mass lies. For a strictly increasing, continuous F this is just F−1(p); for the median, solve F(x) = 0.5. Percentiles are more robust to a badly-fit right tail than the mean, which is one reason percentile-based measures reappear later in this part when we discuss VaR and TVaR for aggregate losses — the same solve-the-inverse-CDF logic applies verbatim to a sum of claims, not just to a single claim.

The Limited Expected Value E[X∧d]

A recurring building block — for reinsurance pricing, for stop-loss premiums, and later for the mean of a censored aggregate loss — is the expected value of X capped at a limit d, written X∧d = min(X,d). Two equivalent computation routes exist:

Limited Expected ValueE[X∧d] = ∫0d x f(x) dx + d·S(d) = ∫0d S(x) dx

where S(x) = 1 − F(x) is the survival function. The second form (integrating the survival function) is usually faster because it avoids integration by parts, and it also generalizes cleanly: it is exactly the tool we will reuse in Chapter 7 to compute expected stop-loss payments on an aggregate loss S, since E[S∧d] has the identical structural definition with S in place of X.

Shortcut for the exponential. If X ~ Exponential(θ), the memoryless property gives a clean closed form: E[X∧d] = θ(1 − e−d/θ). Memorize this one — it appears constantly, both as a severity limited-value calculation and embedded inside compound-distribution problems.

Existence of Moments and Tail Weight

Not every distribution has finite moments of every order. Whether E[Xk] is finite depends on how fast the density's right tail decays relative to how fast xk grows — if the tail is "heavy" (decays like a power of x), then for k large enough the integrand xkf(x) stops shrinking fast enough for the integral to converge, and the moment simply does not exist.

The cleanest illustration is the Pareto distribution, with density and survival function

Pareto(α, θ)f(x) = αθα / (x+θ)α+1, S(x) = (θ/(x+θ))α, x > 0

As x → ∞, the integrand of the k-th moment behaves like xk·αθαx−α−1 = αθαxk−α−1. A power function xp has a convergent integral near infinity exactly when p < −1, so we need k − α − 1 < −1, i.e. k < α. This single inequality is the entire content of the "existence of moments" learning outcome for Pareto: the shape parameter α is literally a moment-existence ceiling. A Pareto with α = 2.3 has a finite mean and can appear to have a finite variance under casual inspection, but its third moment (needed for a skewness computation) is infinite.

By contrast, distributions like the gamma, exponential, and Weibull have every moment finite (their tails decay at least exponentially, so xk is always eventually dominated), and their moment generating functions exist in a neighborhood of 0. A distribution possessing an MGF is a strong light-tail signal; the Pareto and (equivalently, in log space) the lognormal have no MGF at all, which is the formal marker of a heavy-tailed model.

It is worth being precise about what "heavy-tailed" actually buys you as a modeler. Two severity distributions can share an identical mean and even an identical variance, yet behave completely differently at the 99.5th percentile — which is exactly the region that determines reinsurance attachment points and solvency capital. A light-tailed model (exponential-type decay) will almost always understate the frequency of an extreme loss relative to a heavy-tailed model (polynomial-type decay) calibrated to the same body of the data. This is why the existence-of-moments question is not just an abstract integration exercise: a practitioner who fits a gamma when the data is truly Pareto-like will systematically underprice protection against large losses, often by an order of magnitude or more once you get far enough into the tail.

Do not confuse "the mean exists" with "all moments exist." A Pareto with α just above 1 has a finite mean but an infinite variance — always check the specific inequality k < α for the specific moment being asked about, not just whether the distribution "looks reasonable."
Example 1.1

A severity model has density f(x) = 3x2/θ3 for 0 < x < θ, with θ = 1000. Find the mean, the variance, and the median of X.

Show Solution

For this density, E[Xk] = ∫0θ xk·3x2/θ3 dx = (3/θ3)·θk+3/(k+3) = 3θk/(k+3).

Mean: k=1 gives E[X] = 3θ/4 = 750. Second moment: k=2 gives E[X2] = 3θ2/5 = 600,000. Variance = 600,000 − 7502 = 600,000 − 562,500 = 37,500.

For the median, first find F(x) = ∫0x 3t2/θ3 dt = x3/θ3. Setting x3/θ3 = 0.5 gives x = θ(0.5)1/3 = 1000(0.7937) ≈ 793.7. Note the median (793.7) sits below the mean (750 is actually below 793.7 here) — wait, compare carefully: mean = 750, median = 793.7, so this particular distribution is left-skewed (median > mean), consistent with its density rising toward the upper endpoint θ.

Example 1.2

Claim severity is modeled as Exponential with θ = 800. A reinsurance treaty caps the ceding company's exposure per claim at 500. Find the expected retained loss per claim, E[X∧500].

Show Solution

Using the exponential shortcut, E[X∧d] = θ(1 − e−d/θ):

E[X∧500] = 800(1 − e−500/800) = 800(1 − e−0.625) = 800(1 − 0.5353) = 800(0.4647) ≈ 371.8.

As a sanity check, this must be less than both 500 (the cap) and 800 (the uncapped mean), which it is.

Example 1.3

Losses follow a Pareto distribution with α = 2.5 and θ = 1000. (a) Find E[X]. (b) Find E[X2]. (c) Does E[X3] exist?

Show Solution

For Pareto(α,θ), the standard closed forms (valid when the relevant inequality on α holds) are E[X] = θ/(α−1) for α>1, and E[X2] = 2θ2/[(α−1)(α−2)] for α>2.

(a) α = 2.5 > 1, so E[X] = 1000/1.5 ≈ 666.7.

(b) α = 2.5 > 2, so E[X2] = 2(1,000,000)/[(1.5)(0.5)] = 2,000,000/0.75 ≈ 2,666,667.

(c) E[X3] requires k=3 < α = 2.5, which is false. E[X3] does not exist — the integral ∫x3f(x)dx diverges because the tail term behaves like x3−2.5−1 = x−0.5, and x−0.5 does not integrate to a finite value on [0,∞). Practically, this means you could never trust a sample skewness statistic computed from this model.

Example 1.4

Two severity models are both fit to the same claims data and both have mean 1,000: Model A is Gamma(α=2, θ=500); Model B is Pareto(α=3, θ=2000). Without computing any percentiles, explain which model assigns more probability to a catastrophic claim (say, X > 20,000), and confirm using the survival functions.

Show Solution

The gamma's survival function decays like an exponential (multiplied by a polynomial for integer shape), while the Pareto's survival function S(x) = (θ/(x+θ))α decays only polynomially in x. Polynomial decay is always eventually slower than exponential decay, so Model B (Pareto) is heavier-tailed and assigns more probability to extreme claims despite sharing the same mean.

Numerically: Pareto S(20000) = (2000/22000)3 = (0.0909)3 ≈ 0.000751. Gamma(2,500) has S(x) = e−x/θ(1+x/θ); S(20000) = e−40(41), which is on the order of 10−16 — astronomically smaller. This confirms the heavy-tail intuition: at 20,000 the Pareto is over ten orders of magnitude more likely to produce a claim that large, even though both models have identical means.

2. Scale Parameters & Distribution Families

Learning Outcomes Covered: Defining a scale parameter and distinguishing it from a shape parameter; identifying which standard severity distributions are scale families; using the scale property to re-parameterize a fitted distribution after a proportional change such as inflation or currency conversion; and recognizing relationships between named distributions (special cases, sums, and mixtures).

What a Scale Parameter Is

A parameter θ is called a scale parameter of X if, for every constant c > 0, the random variable cX has the same distributional form as X but with θ replaced by cθ, while every other (shape) parameter is left untouched. Formally, if X has survival function S(x; α, θ), X is a scale family in θ when S(x; α, θ) = S(x/θ; α, 1) — the parameter θ only ever appears dividing x. This is exactly what happens when you convert a claim-size model from dollars to another currency, or inflate historical losses to today's price level: multiplying every possible outcome by a constant c should feel like "the same shape of distribution, just relabeled."

Scale Family PropertyIf X has scale parameter θ, then Y = cX has scale parameter cθ (c > 0), with all shape parameters unchanged.

By contrast, a shape parameter (like α in a gamma or Pareto, or τ in a Weibull) controls the fundamental form of the distribution — skewness, number of modes, tail heaviness — and does not change under a pure rescaling of dollars.

This distinction has an immediate practical payoff. Suppose an actuary has already fit a severity curve to several years of claims data at last year's price level, a process that may have taken considerable effort (choosing a family, estimating parameters, checking goodness of fit). When trend or inflation pushes claim costs up by a known percentage next year, there is no need to refit the entire model from scratch — if the family is a scale family, only the scale parameter needs to move, by exactly the inflation factor, and every shape-driven property of the distribution (skewness, coefficient of variation, the relative likelihood of a claim being, say, triple the mean versus half the mean) carries over unchanged. This is precisely the mechanism used throughout ratemaking to "trend" a severity distribution forward in time without re-estimating shape parameters every period.

Which Standard Distributions Are Scale Families

Exponential(θ), Gamma(α,θ), Pareto(α,θ), and Weibull(τ,θ) are all scale families in θ, with their respective α or τ acting as pure shape parameters. The Lognormal is a slightly different animal: if X is Lognormal(μ,σ2), then cX is Lognormal(μ + ln c, σ2) — so rescaling shifts the location parameter μ by ln c rather than multiplying a "θ," but the effect is analogous: σ (the true shape driver of a lognormal) is completely unaffected by proportional rescaling, so the lognormal behaves like a scale family once you view exp(μ) as playing the role of θ.

Fast exam check: to see whether a parameter is a scale parameter, look at where it sits in the formula. If it appears only as x/θ or (x+θ) inside the survival/density function — never combined with x through addition inside an exponent, and never appearing "bare" without x nearby — it is almost certainly the scale parameter.

Relationships Between Named Distributions

Several of the FAM-syllabus severity distributions are special cases or simple transforms of each other, and recognizing these relationships saves derivation time on the exam:

Selected severity distribution relationships
RelationshipDetail
Exponential is Gamma with shape 1Gamma(α=1, θ) has density f(x) = e−x/θ/θ, identical to Exponential(θ).
Gamma with integer shape is a sum of exponentialsIf α = n is a positive integer, Gamma(n,θ) is distributed as the sum of n independent Exponential(θ) variables (the "Erlang" special case).
Weibull with τ=1 is ExponentialWeibull(τ,θ) reduces to Exponential(θ) exactly when the shape τ = 1; τ ≠ 1 introduces increasing (τ>1) or decreasing (τ<1) hazard over time.
Pareto as a gamma mixture of exponentialsIf, conditional on a parameter Λ, claim severity X is Exponential with mean 1/Λ, and Λ itself varies across risks according to a Gamma distribution, then the unconditional (marginal) distribution of X is a Pareto. This is a conceptual result — mixing (blending) a light-tailed family over a randomly varying parameter is a standard mechanism that manufactures a heavy tail.
Lognormal as exponentiated normalIf Z ~ Normal(μ,σ2), then X = eZ is Lognormal(μ,σ2); percentiles of X are obtained by exponentiating percentiles of Z.
A common exam trap: assuming every two-parameter distribution is a scale family. It is not automatic — you must check that the second parameter behaves purely as a shape parameter. On FAM, treat this as verified for the named distributions above; do not assume it holds for an unfamiliar parameterization without checking.
Example 2.1

Claim severity this year follows Gamma(α=3, θ=500). Actuaries expect uniform claims inflation of 8% next year. Give the distribution of next year's severity and its new mean.

Show Solution

Since Gamma is a scale family in θ, inflating every claim by a factor of 1.08 simply rescales θ: new severity ~ Gamma(α=3, θ=540) (500 × 1.08 = 540), with α unchanged at 3.

New mean = αθ = 3(540) = 1620, matching what you'd get by directly inflating the old mean: 3(500)(1.08) = 1500(1.08) = 1620. ✓

Example 2.2

Using the two gamma distributions from Example 2.1 (before and after inflation), show numerically that the coefficient of variation is unchanged by the rescaling, confirming that θ carries no shape information.

Show Solution

For Gamma(α,θ), Var(X) = αθ2, so CV = SD/Mean = (√α · θ)/(αθ) = 1/√α — a function of α alone.

Before inflation: Var = 3(5002) = 750,000, SD ≈ 866.0, mean = 1500, CV = 866.0/1500 ≈ 0.5774.

After inflation: Var = 3(5402) = 874,800, SD ≈ 935.3, mean = 1620, CV = 935.3/1620 ≈ 0.5774.

Both equal 1/√3 ≈ 0.5774, confirming the shape (as measured by CV) is invariant to the rescaling — only the dollar scale moved.

Example 2.3

Ground-up losses satisfy ln X ~ Normal(μ=6, σ=0.8). Find P(X > 2000).

Show Solution

ln(2000) ≈ 7.601. Standardize: z = (7.601 − 6)/0.8 = 2.001 ≈ 2.00.

P(X > 2000) = P(Z > 2.00) = 1 − Φ(2.00) = 1 − 0.9772 = 0.0228.

Example 2.4

Prove directly from the definition that if X ~ Pareto(α,θ), then Y = cX (c>0) is Pareto(α, cθ) — i.e., confirm θ is a genuine scale parameter for the Pareto family.

Show Solution

Start from the Pareto CDF, F(x) = 1 − (θ/(x+θ))α. For Y = cX:

FY(y) = P(cX ≤ y) = P(X ≤ y/c) = 1 − (θ/(y/c + θ))α.

Multiply numerator and denominator inside the parentheses by c: θ/(y/c+θ) = cθ/(y+cθ). So FY(y) = 1 − (cθ/(y+cθ))α, which is exactly the Pareto(α, cθ) CDF. Confirmed: α is untouched, θ scales by c.

3. Frequency: The (a,b,0) Class

Learning Outcomes Covered: Stating and applying the (a,b,0) recursive relationship for claim-count probabilities; identifying the Poisson, binomial, and negative binomial distributions as the three members of this class, along with each member's (a,b) values in terms of its usual parameters; generating a full probability mass function from p0 using the recursion; and reverse-engineering which family (and which parameter values) generated a short list of given probabilities.

The Recursive Definition

A discrete counting distribution N (taking values 0,1,2,...) with probabilities pk = P(N=k) belongs to the (a,b,0) class if there exist constants a and b such that

(a,b,0) Recursionpk / pk−1 = a + b/k, k = 1, 2, 3, ...

This single recursive rule, together with a starting value p0, completely determines the entire probability mass function: p1 = p0(a+b), p2 = p1(a+b/2), and so on. Remarkably, only three named distributions satisfy this recursion for all k — and the sign of a is what separates them.

The three members of the (a,b,0) class
DistributionUsual parametersabp0
Poissonλ > 00λe−λ
Binomialm ∈ {1,2,...}, 0<q<1−q/(1−q)(m+1)q/(1−q)(1−q)m
Negative Binomialr > 0, β > 0β/(1+β)(r−1)β/(1+β)(1+β)−r
Geometric (NB with r=1)β > 0β/(1+β)01/(1+β)

Notice the clean pattern in the sign of a: a = 0 for the Poisson (the "boundary" case), a < 0 for the binomial (whose support is bounded above by m, forcing the ratio pk/pk−1 to eventually go negative — impossible unless the recursion terminates, which happens automatically once b/k = −a), and a > 0 for the negative binomial (whose support is unbounded, consistent with a strictly positive geometric-like decay rate). This a > 0 / a = 0 / a < 0 trichotomy is one of the most heavily tested single facts in this topic.

It is also worth connecting this trichotomy to a fact you may already know about these three distributions from a first probability course: the relationship between the mean and the variance. Poisson has mean equal to variance; binomial has variance strictly less than its mean (since Var(N)=mq(1−q)<mq=E[N]); negative binomial has variance strictly greater than its mean (Var(N)=rβ(1+β)>rβ=E[N]). This is not a coincidence — it is the same underlying trichotomy showing up in a different guise. A quick rule of thumb: if sample data shows variance noticeably above the sample mean ("overdispersion"), a negative binomial is usually a better starting candidate than a Poisson; if variance is below the mean ("underdispersion"), a binomial-type model is worth considering.

The single most common (a,b,0) error is swapping which parameter the binomial's a and b use. Remember: binomial's a is negative and involves q/(1−q); if you compute a > 0 from given data, the answer cannot be binomial — it must be negative binomial (or geometric if b happens to equal 0).

Using the Recursion in Both Directions

The recursion is used two ways on FAM: (1) forward — given a named distribution's parameters, generate p0, p1, p2, ... one at a time without re-deriving the pmf formula from scratch each time; and (2) backward — given three or four consecutive probabilities (often disguised as "the observed relative frequencies"), compute the ratios p1/p0 and p2/p1, solve the two linear equations a+b = ratio1 and a+b/2 = ratio2 for a and b, and then use the sign and value of a to identify the family and back out the original parameters.

It is worth internalizing why a recursive definition is more useful here than simply memorizing three separate pmf formulas. First, it unifies calculation: once you have p0 and the pair (a,b), generating the next ten probabilities is a single repeated multiplication, which is far less error-prone under time pressure than re-plugging into a factorial- or combinatorial-heavy formula at every step. Second, and more importantly for the exam, it gives you a diagnostic tool: any time a problem hands you a handful of raw claim-count probabilities and asks "what distribution generated this," the (a,b,0) recursion turns an otherwise open-ended model-identification question into two linear equations in two unknowns — entirely mechanical once you recognize the setup.

Example 3.1

N ~ Poisson(λ=3). Use the (a,b,0) recursion to compute p0 through p3.

Show Solution

For Poisson, a=0, b=λ=3, and p0 = e−3 ≈ 0.0498.

p1 = p0(0 + 3/1) = 0.0498(3) ≈ 0.1494.

p2 = p1(0 + 3/2) = 0.1494(1.5) ≈ 0.2240.

p3 = p2(0 + 3/3) = 0.2240(1.0) ≈ 0.2240.

Check against the closed form p3=e−333/3! = 0.0498(27)/6 = 0.2240. ✓

Example 3.2

N ~ Negative Binomial(r=2, β=1). Find a, b, p0, and use the recursion to find p1, p2, p3.

Show Solution

a = β/(1+β) = 1/2 = 0.5. b = (r−1)β/(1+β) = (1)(1)/2 = 0.5. p0 = (1+β)−r = 2−2 = 0.25.

p1 = p0(a+b) = 0.25(1.0) = 0.25.

p2 = p1(a+b/2) = 0.25(0.75) = 0.1875.

p3 = p2(a+b/3) = 0.1875(0.6667) ≈ 0.1250.

Cross-check with the direct NB pmf pk = C(k+r−1,k)(1/(1+β))r(β/(1+β))k = (k+1)(0.25)(0.5)k: p3 = 4(0.25)(0.125) = 0.125. ✓

Example 3.3

A block of policies has observed relative claim-count frequencies p0=0.2963, p1=0.2963, p2=0.1975. Assuming these come from an (a,b,0) member, identify the distribution and its parameters.

Show Solution

Ratio 1: p1/p0 = 0.2963/0.2963 = 1.0000 = a + b.

Ratio 2: p2/p1 = 0.1975/0.2963 = 0.6667 = a + b/2.

Subtracting the second equation from the first: b/2 = 1.0000 − 0.6667 = 0.3333, so b = 0.6667, and a = 1.0000 − 0.6667 = 0.3333.

Since a > 0, this must be negative binomial. From a = β/(1+β) = 1/3: solving gives β = 0.5. From b = (r−1)β/(1+β) = (r−1)(1/3) = 0.6667: r−1 = 2, so r = 3.

Check: p0 = (1.5)−3 = 1/3.375 = 0.2963 ✓ — matches the given value exactly, confirming r=3, β=0.5.

Example 3.4

N ~ Binomial(m=5, q=0.3). Find a, b, and p0 through p2 via the recursion.

Show Solution

a = −q/(1−q) = −0.3/0.7 ≈ −0.4286. b = (m+1)q/(1−q) = 6(0.3)/0.7 ≈ 2.5714. p0 = (0.7)5 = 0.16807.

p1 = p0(a+b) = 0.16807(2.1429) ≈ 0.36015.

p2 = p1(a+b/2) = 0.36015(0.8571) ≈ 0.30870.

Direct check: p2 = C(5,2)(0.3)2(0.7)3 = 10(0.09)(0.343) = 0.30870. ✓

4. Frequency: The (a,b,1) Class & Zero-Modification

Learning Outcomes Covered: Describing how the (a,b,1) class generalizes (a,b,0) by allowing p0 to be set independently; constructing zero-truncated distributions by rescaling; constructing zero-modified distributions with an arbitrary chosen p0; and matching the choice of truncated versus modified model to the data-generating scenario.

Freeing Up p0

The (a,b,0) class forces every probability, including p0, to be tied together by the single recursion pk/pk−1 = a+b/k starting at k=1. In practice, real claim-count data often has too many or too few zeros relative to what a pure Poisson or negative binomial would predict — for example, a large fraction of auto policies file no claims because of a deductible, more than a Poisson model would suggest. The (a,b,1) class handles this by keeping the same recursion pk/pk−1 = a+b/k for k = 2, 3, 4, ... (i.e., the shape of the tail beyond zero is unchanged), but letting p0 — and consequently p1, since it's no longer tied to p0 by the k=1 step of the recursion — be chosen separately.

(a,b,1) Recursionpk / pk−1 = a + b/k, k = 2, 3, 4, ... (p0 set independently)

Two Special Cases: Truncated and Modified

Let pk denote the probabilities of the underlying (a,b,0) member (the "parent" distribution). Two named constructions dominate practice:

  • Zero-truncated: set p0T = 0 (the count 0 is structurally impossible — e.g., data was only recorded when at least one claim occurred), and rescale every remaining probability to sum to 1: pkT = pk/(1−p0) for k ≥ 1.
  • Zero-modified: set p0M to any chosen value in [0,1) (not necessarily 0), and rescale the remaining mass proportionally so everything still sums to 1: pkM = [(1−p0M)/(1−p0)]·pk for k ≥ 1.
Zero-Modified RescalingpkM = [(1 − p0M) / (1 − p0)] · pk, k ≥ 1

Zero-truncation is the special case p0M=0 of zero-modification, so you only need to memorize the one rescaling formula above. Beyond k=1, both constructions continue to obey the same a and b as the parent (a,b,0) member — only the "entry point" p1 changes, and everything from p2 onward is generated from p1 by the ordinary recursion.

Zero-truncated data typically arises from reporting mechanisms (you only observe a policy in the dataset because a claim occurred). Zero-modified (with p0M larger than the natural p0) typically arises from deductibles or coverage triggers that suppress small/no-claim years relative to a Poisson-type baseline. Match the mechanism in the problem's narrative to the correct construction — this is exactly the "identify appropriate distribution for an application" learning outcome.

It is worth stressing why the (a,b,1) class only needs to touch p0 (and, as a consequence, p1) rather than allowing arbitrary tinkering at every k. If you tried to freely reset two or three probabilities in the middle of the distribution, you would lose the closed-form, one-parameter-per-adjustment tractability that makes this whole family so useful for both hand calculation and computer implementation — likelihood functions, moment formulas, and compound-distribution recursions (a topic that extends beyond FAM into more advanced loss-model coursework) all rely on the fact that only the entry point changes and the recursive shape of the tail is preserved intact. This is the sense in which (a,b,1) is a minimal, surgical generalization of (a,b,0) rather than an unrestricted family of counting distributions.

Example 4.1

The underlying frequency is Poisson(λ=2). Data is only recorded for policies that filed at least one claim. Find the zero-truncated probabilities p1T, p2T, p3T.

Show Solution

Base Poisson: p0=e−2=0.1353, p1=0.2707, p2=0.2707, p3=0.1804. Rescale factor: 1/(1−0.1353) = 1/0.8647 = 1.1565.

p1T = 0.2707(1.1565) ≈ 0.3130. p2T = 0.2707(1.1565) ≈ 0.3130. p3T = 0.1804(1.1565) ≈ 0.2086.

Example 4.2

The underlying frequency is Poisson(λ=1.5), but empirical experience shows 50% of policies have zero claims (higher than the Poisson's natural p0). Build the zero-modified model with p0M=0.5 and find p1M and p2M.

Show Solution

Base Poisson: p0 = e−1.5 ≈ 0.2231, p1 = 1.5(0.2231) = 0.3347, p2 = (1.52/2)(0.2231) = 0.2510.

Rescale factor: (1−0.5)/(1−0.2231) = 0.5/0.7769 ≈ 0.6436.

p1M = 0.3347(0.6436) ≈ 0.2154. p2M = 0.2510(0.6436) ≈ 0.1616.

Notice all of p1M, p2M, ... are shrunk by the same constant factor relative to the Poisson baseline — only p0 was set independently; every other probability just got "squeezed" proportionally to make room for the extra mass at zero.

Example 4.3

An (a,b,1) model has parent parameters a=0.5, b=0.5 (negative binomial shape), and the modeler has directly set p1M=0.25 (its value is chosen from data, not derived from p0M). Find p2M and p3M.

Show Solution

The recursion for k ≥ 2 uses the same a, b as the parent family, applied starting from the given p1M:

p2M = p1M(a+b/2) = 0.25(0.5+0.25) = 0.25(0.75) = 0.1875.

p3M = p2M(a+b/3) = 0.1875(0.5+0.1667) = 0.1875(0.6667) ≈ 0.1250.

This is the key mechanical point of the (a,b,1) class: once p1 is known (however it was obtained), you never need to touch a or b again — the tail beyond k=1 propagates exactly as it would in the ordinary (a,b,0) member.

Example 4.4

An insurer studies bicycle-theft claims two ways: (i) a household survey asking "how many bicycles were stolen from you this year" (including zero), and (ii) police theft reports (which by definition only exist when a theft occurred). Which data source should be modeled with a zero-truncated distribution, and which might call for a zero-modified (not truncated) distribution?

Show Solution

Source (ii), the police reports, structurally cannot contain a zero — a report only exists if a theft happened — so it should be modeled as zero-truncated (p0T=0 exactly).

Source (i), the household survey, can contain zeros, but survey non-response or recall bias might make the observed proportion of zero-theft households differ from what a clean Poisson/NB would predict (e.g., people who were victimized may be more motivated to respond, deflating the apparent zero count) — this calls for a general zero-modified model, where p0M is fit freely rather than fixed at 0.

5. Aggregate Models: Collective Risk Model

Learning Outcomes Covered: Defining the collective risk model for an aggregate loss S; deriving the mean and variance of S using the law of total expectation and total variance; specializing the variance formula to compound Poisson, compound binomial, and compound negative binomial models; and applying these formulas to compute numeric answers.

Setting Up the Collective Risk Model

The collective risk model represents the total loss from a block of business (or a single policy over a period) as a random sum:

Collective Risk ModelS = X1 + X2 + ... + XN, N random, X1,X2,... iid severities, independent of N

Here N is the frequency (claim count) random variable — often an (a,b,0) or (a,b,1) member from the previous two chapters — and each Xi is drawn independently from the same severity distribution studied in Chapters 1–2, independent of N and of each other. If N=0, S=0 by convention.

Mean and Variance of S

The mean of S follows immediately from the law of total expectation: conditioning on N, E[S|N] = N·E[X], so E[S] = E[E[S|N]] = E[N]·E[X]. The variance requires the law of total variance, and it is worth deriving in full since exam questions frequently probe whether you actually understand where the two terms come from (rather than just recalling the formula):

Law of Total VarianceVar(S) = E[Var(S|N)] + Var(E[S|N])

Conditional on N=n, S is a sum of n iid draws of X, so Var(S|N=n) = n·Var(X), i.e. Var(S|N) = N·Var(X) as a random variable in N. Taking expectation over N gives the first term: E[Var(S|N)] = E[N]·Var(X). For the second term, E[S|N] = N·E[X], and since E[X] is just a constant multiplying the random variable N, Var(E[S|N]) = Var(N·E[X]) = (E[X])2·Var(N). Adding the two pieces:

Aggregate Mean & VarianceE[S] = E[N]E[X] Var(S) = E[N]Var(X) + Var(N)(E[X])2

Both terms matter: the first captures the variability contributed by claim sizes bouncing around (weighted by how many claims typically occur), and the second captures the variability contributed by the claim count itself bouncing around (weighted by the square of the typical claim size). Dropping either term is a serious, very common error.

The most frequent mistake on this formula is forgetting the second term entirely and writing Var(S) = E[N]Var(X) — this is only correct in the very special case where N is deterministic (Var(N)=0), which is essentially never true for an insurance frequency model. Always check whether the problem gives you Var(N); if it does, you almost certainly need it.

Compound Special Cases

Plugging each (a,b,0) member's own mean and variance for N into the general formula gives three standard specializations. For compound Poisson (E[N]=Var(N)=λ), the variance formula collapses beautifully:

Compound PoissonE[S] = λE[X] Var(S) = λE[X2]

(since λVar(X) + λ(E[X])2 = λ[Var(X)+(E[X])2] = λE[X2] — the two-term formula reduces to "λ times the raw second moment of severity," a shortcut worth memorizing on its own). For compound binomial, N~Binomial(m,q) has E[N]=mq, Var(N)=mq(1−q); for compound negative binomial, N~NegBinomial(r,β) has E[N]=rβ, Var(N)=rβ(1+β). These plug directly into the general two-term formula — there is no need to memorize separate variance formulas for every compound family as long as you remember the general E[N], Var(X), Var(N), E[X] formula and each frequency distribution's own mean/variance.

The collective risk model is the workhorse structure of short-term actuarial pricing precisely because it cleanly separates two very different sources of uncertainty. Frequency risk (how many claims will occur) is driven by exposure, underwriting selection, and — for liability lines especially — the legal and social environment; severity risk (how large each claim will be) is driven by the nature of the loss itself, medical cost trends, repair costs, and so on. Modeling them separately, then combining them through the aggregate variance formula, lets an actuary diagnose where the volatility in a book of business is actually coming from: a portfolio with highly volatile claim counts but modest, well-behaved claim sizes calls for very different risk management (e.g. aggregate stop-loss protection) than one with stable claim counts but a few potentially catastrophic individual claims (which calls for per-occurrence excess reinsurance instead).

Example 5.1

Annual claim counts are Poisson with λ=50. Claim severity is Gamma(α=2, θ=300), independent of count. Find E[S] and Var(S).

Show Solution

E[X] = αθ = 600. Var(X) = αθ2 = 2(90,000) = 180,000. E[X2] = Var(X)+(E[X])2 = 180,000+360,000 = 540,000.

E[S] = λE[X] = 50(600) = 30,000.

Var(S) = λE[X2] = 50(540,000) = 27,000,000 (SD ≈ 5196).

Example 5.2

Annual claim counts are Negative Binomial with r=10, β=0.4. Claim severity is Exponential with θ=1000, independent of count. Find E[S] and Var(S).

Show Solution

E[N] = rβ = 4. Var(N) = rβ(1+β) = 10(0.4)(1.4) = 5.6.

E[X] = 1000. Var(X) = θ2 = 1,000,000.

E[S] = E[N]E[X] = 4(1000) = 4,000.

Var(S) = E[N]Var(X) + Var(N)(E[X])2 = 4(1,000,000) + 5.6(1,000,000) = 4,000,000 + 5,600,000 = 9,600,000.

Example 5.3

A cohort of 20 independent, identical policies each has a 25% chance of producing exactly one claim this year (so N~Binomial(m=20, q=0.25)); no policy can produce more than one claim. Severity per claim has mean 800 and variance 640,000. Find E[S] and Var(S).

Show Solution

E[N] = mq = 20(0.25) = 5. Var(N) = mq(1−q) = 20(0.25)(0.75) = 3.75.

E[S] = E[N]E[X] = 5(800) = 4,000.

Var(S) = E[N]Var(X) + Var(N)(E[X])2 = 5(640,000) + 3.75(640,000) = 8.75(640,000) = 5,600,000.

Example 5.4

Confirm algebraically that for compound Poisson specifically, Var(S) simplifies to λE[X2], starting from the general two-term aggregate variance formula.

Show Solution

General formula: Var(S) = E[N]Var(X) + Var(N)(E[X])2. For Poisson, E[N] = Var(N) = λ, so both coefficients become λ:

Var(S) = λVar(X) + λ(E[X])2 = λ[Var(X) + (E[X])2] = λE[X2], using the identity Var(X)+(E[X])2=E[X2]. Confirmed. This shortcut is unique to the Poisson case — it relies on E[N] and Var(N) being equal, which is a defining feature of the Poisson and does not hold for the binomial or negative binomial.

6. Individual Risk Model, Approximations & Convolution

Learning Outcomes Covered: Defining the individual risk model and contrasting it with the collective risk model; computing the mean and variance of an aggregate loss built from independent-but-not-identical policy-level losses; applying the normal approximation to an aggregate distribution; applying the lognormal approximation by matching the first two moments; and computing an exact aggregate distribution for discrete severity via convolution.

The Individual Risk Model

Where the collective risk model sums a random number of iid claims, the individual risk model instead fixes the number of policies at a known n and sums one loss per policy:

Individual Risk ModelS = Y1 + Y2 + ... + Yn, Y1,...,Yn independent but NOT identically distributed

Each Yi represents the loss from policy i, and it is standard to write Yi = IiBi, where Ii is a Bernoulli(qi) indicator of whether policy i has a claim at all, and Bi is the claim severity given that a claim occurs, independent of Ii. Because policies differ in their claim probability qi and their severity distribution, the Yi are independent but generally have different distributions from one another — this is the single most important conceptual distinction from the collective risk model's iid Xi.

This is the classic exam trap for this topic: the individual risk model sums independent, not identically distributed losses. Do not apply an iid shortcut (like "Var(S) = n·Var(Y)") — you must sum each policy's own variance individually, since qi and the severity distribution can differ policy by policy.

Deriving the moments of a single Yi=IiBi with μi=E[Bi], σi2=Var(Bi): since Ii2=Ii (an indicator squared is itself),

Per-Policy MomentsE[Yi] = qiμi Var(Yi) = qiσi2 + qi(1−qi)μi2

and then, using independence across policies, E[S] = Σ E[Yi] and Var(S) = Σ Var(Yi) — sums, not multiples, because the terms are not identical.

The individual risk model shows up naturally whenever an insurer is analyzing a specific, enumerable book of business — a group life or group health contract with n named employees, a commercial property schedule with n named locations, or a reinsurer evaluating a treaty covering n specifically identified underlying policies. In each of these settings the number of "units" at risk is known and fixed in advance (it is n, not a random N), which is the defining feature that separates this model from the collective risk model of Chapter 5. It is entirely possible — and common in practice — to see both models applied to the same portfolio at different levels: the individual risk model at the policy level (summing n known policies), nested inside a broader collective risk model at the portfolio level if the number of policies itself is allowed to vary.

Normal and Lognormal Approximation

Once E[S] and Var(S) are known (from either the collective or individual risk model), exact percentiles of S are often hard to obtain, so two standard moment-matching approximations are used. The normal approximation simply treats S as approximately Normal(E[S], Var(S)) — reasonable when S is a sum of many roughly comparable, only mildly skewed pieces (a CLT-style argument).

Normal ApproximationP(S ≤ s) ≈ Φ((s − E[S]) / √Var(S))

Aggregate losses are frequently right-skewed and bounded below by 0, which the normal approximation ignores (it can assign positive probability to S<0). The lognormal approximation fixes this by matching E[S] and E[S2] to a Lognormal(μ,σ2), whose own moments are E[S]=eμ+σ²/2 and E[S2]=e2μ+2σ². Solving these two equations for μ and σ2:

Both approximations share the same starting ingredients — only E[S] and Var(S) — which is exactly why Chapters 5 and 6 spent so much effort deriving those two quantities precisely. Neither approximation requires knowing the full shape of the severity or frequency distributions beyond their first two moments, which is a major practical advantage: an actuary can produce a defensible percentile estimate for a large, complex aggregate loss using only summary statistics, without ever needing to convolve anything. The tradeoff, of course, is accuracy in the tails — both approximations can be noticeably off at extreme percentiles (the 99.5th percentile and beyond) if the true distribution's higher moments (skewness, kurtosis) diverge substantially from what a normal or lognormal shape would predict, which is exactly the scenario where the exact convolution approach below becomes valuable despite its computational cost.

Lognormal Approximation (Moment Matching)σ2 = ln(1 + Var(S)/(E[S])2) μ = ln(E[S]) − σ2/2

The lognormal approximation is generally preferred when S's coefficient of variation is not small, since it automatically respects S ≥ 0 and produces a right-skewed shape instead of the symmetric normal.

Exact Computation via Convolution

When severity X is discrete (or has been discretized), the exact distribution of S = X1+...+XN can be built up exactly using convolution, without any approximation. Define fX*n as the pmf of the sum of n iid copies of X (fX*0 is degenerate at 0, fX*1=fX), computed recursively:

Convolution RecursionfX*n(s) = Σx fX*(n−1)(s−x) fX(x)

Then the aggregate pmf is obtained by mixing over the distribution of N: fS(s) = Σn pn fX*n(s). This is computationally heavy for realistic distributions (which is exactly why the normal/lognormal approximations and the Panjer-type recursive shortcuts exist), but a small worked table makes the mechanics completely transparent, which is what the exam actually tests.

Two practical remarks are worth keeping in mind when convolution appears in a problem. First, N technically has infinite support for most frequency distributions (Poisson, negative binomial), so an exact convolution table is, strictly speaking, an infinite sum; in practice — and on the exam — N is either given a small, explicit finite support (as in the examples below) or the tail contribution beyond some cutoff is small enough to be declared negligible. Second, the convolution method is exact given the assumed discretized severity distribution, unlike the normal or lognormal approximations, which are always approximations no matter how good the moment match is — this exactness is the entire reason convolution is worth the extra computational effort when the aggregate distribution's shape (not just its mean and variance) actually matters, for instance when pricing a stop-loss layer with a low attachment point where the shape of the left part of the distribution matters a great deal.

Example 6.1
