Three independent one-year policies have (claim probability, fixed claim amount if a claim occurs) of (0.05, 2000), (0.10, 1500), and (0.20, 1000) respectively. Find E[S] and Var(S) for the total loss S using the individual risk model.

Show Solution

Since each severity is a fixed (degenerate) amount, σi2=0 for all i, so Var(Yi) = qi(1−qi)μi2.

E[Y1]=0.05(2000)=100, E[Y2]=0.10(1500)=150, E[Y3]=0.20(1000)=200. E[S] = 100+150+200 = 450.

Var(Y1)=0.05(0.95)(20002)=190,000. Var(Y2)=0.10(0.90)(15002)=202,500. Var(Y3)=0.20(0.80)(10002)=160,000.

Var(S) = 190,000+202,500+160,000 = 552,500 (SD ≈ 743.3). Note we summed three different variances — an iid shortcut would have been invalid here.

Example 6.2

Using S from Example 6.1 (E[S]=450, Var(S)=552,500), approximate P(S > 1000) using the normal approximation.

Show Solution

√552,500 ≈ 743.3. z = (1000−450)/743.3 ≈ 0.740.

P(S>1000) ≈ 1 − Φ(0.74) = 1 − 0.7704 = 0.2296.

Example 6.3

An aggregate loss has E[S]=4,000 and Var(S)=9,600,000 (as in the compound negative binomial of Example 5.2). Find the lognormal approximation's parameters, and compare P(S>10,000) under the lognormal versus normal approximation.

Show Solution

E[S2] = Var(S)+(E[S])2 = 9,600,000+16,000,000 = 25,600,000.

σ2 = ln(25,600,000/16,000,000) = ln(1.6) ≈ 0.4700. μ = ln(4000) − 0.4700/2 = 8.294 − 0.235 = 8.059.

Lognormal: ln(10000)=9.210. z=(9.210−8.059)/√0.4700 = 1.151/0.6856 ≈ 1.679. P(S>10000) ≈ 1−Φ(1.68) = 1−0.9535 = 0.0465.

Normal: z=(10000−4000)/√9,600,000 = 6000/3098.4 ≈ 1.937. P(S>10000) ≈ 1−Φ(1.94) = 1−0.9738 = 0.0262.

The lognormal approximation assigns roughly 78% more probability to this right-tail event than the normal approximation does, reflecting the lognormal's built-in right skew — generally the more defensible choice for a positively-skewed, non-negative aggregate loss.

Example 6.4

Severity X takes values 0, 1, 2 with probabilities 0.5, 0.3, 0.2. Claim count N takes values 0, 1, 2 with probabilities 0.4, 0.4, 0.2. Build the full convolution table for S = X1+...+XN and find fS(0) through fS(4).

Show Solution

First build fX*2(s) = Σ fX(x)fX(s−x):

fX*2(0)=0.5(0.5)=0.25. fX*2(1)=2(0.5)(0.3)=0.30. fX*2(2)=2(0.5)(0.2)+0.3(0.3)=0.20+0.09=0.29. fX*2(3)=2(0.3)(0.2)=0.12. fX*2(4)=0.2(0.2)=0.04. (Sum = 0.25+0.30+0.29+0.12+0.04=1.00 ✓)

Now mix over N using fS(s) = p0fX*0(s) + p1fX*1(s) + p2fX*2(s), where fX*0 puts probability 1 on s=0:

fS(0) = 0.4(1) + 0.4(0.5) + 0.2(0.25) = 0.4+0.20+0.05 = 0.650.

fS(1) = 0.4(0.3) + 0.2(0.30) = 0.12+0.06 = 0.180.

fS(2) = 0.4(0.2) + 0.2(0.29) = 0.08+0.058 = 0.138.

fS(3) = 0.2(0.12) = 0.024.

fS(4) = 0.2(0.04) = 0.008.

Total: 0.650+0.180+0.138+0.024+0.008 = 1.000 ✓. (In practice N would have unbounded support and the table would extend further, but truncating at the point where remaining probability is negligible is standard practice.)

7. Stop-Loss Insurance, VaR & TVaR

Learning Outcomes Covered: Computing the expected payment under a stop-loss reinsurance treaty using the limited-expected-value machinery; defining and computing Value at Risk as a percentile of the aggregate loss; defining and computing Tail Value at Risk both as a conditional expectation and via the direct VaR-plus-stop-loss formula; stating the four coherence properties of a risk measure; and explaining why VaR can fail subadditivity while TVaR cannot.

Expected Stop-Loss Payment

A stop-loss reinsurance treaty with deductible d pays the reinsurer's share (S−d) whenever the aggregate loss S exceeds d, and nothing otherwise — i.e. it pays (S−d)+ = max(S−d, 0). Because S = (S∧d) + (S−d)+ always holds (the loss is either capped at d, or the excess above d is paid separately), taking expectations gives:

Expected Stop-Loss PaymentE[(S−d)+] = E[S] − E[S∧d]

This is exactly the limited-expected-value idea from Chapter 1, applied to the aggregate loss S instead of a single severity X — once you have E[S] and a way to compute E[S∧d] (from a formula, an approximation, or a convolution table), the stop-loss premium follows immediately.

Value at Risk

Value at Risk at security level p, VaRp(S), is simply the p-th percentile of the aggregate loss distribution — the smallest s such that FS(s) ≥ p:

Value at RiskVaRp(S) = min{ s : FS(s) ≥ p }

It answers "what loss level will not be exceeded with probability p," and is the same percentile computation from Chapter 1's discussion of severity percentiles — only now applied to the aggregate S.

Tail Value at Risk

Tail Value at Risk goes a step further and averages over the bad outcomes beyond the VaR threshold, rather than just reporting the threshold itself: TVaRp(S) = E[S | S > VaRp(S)]. For a continuous S this conditional expectation reduces to a clean closed form built directly from the stop-loss machinery above:

Tail Value at RiskTVaRp(S) = VaRp(S) + E[(S − VaRp(S))+] / (1 − p)

Intuitively: start at the VaR threshold, then add back the "average excess beyond the threshold," where the excess is spread over the (1−p) probability mass living beyond that threshold. TVaR is always at least as large as VaRp(S) (the added term is non-negative), which matches the intuition that "average of the worst (1−p) outcomes" should be at least as bad as "the (1−p) cutoff itself."

Coherence of a Risk Measure

A risk measure ρ (a rule turning a random loss into a single dollar number, used e.g. to set capital requirements) is called coherent if it satisfies four properties:

The four coherence properties
PropertyInformal meaning
Translation invarianceρ(S+c) = ρ(S)+c — adding a guaranteed constant c to the loss just adds c to the risk measure.
Positive homogeneityρ(cS) = c·ρ(S) for c>0 — doubling every possible loss outcome doubles the risk measure.
MonotonicityIf S1 ≤ S2 in every outcome, then ρ(S1) ≤ ρ(S2) — a uniformly worse risk can't get a smaller risk number.
Subadditivityρ(S1+S2) ≤ ρ(S1)+ρ(S2) — pooling/diversifying two risks together should never look riskier than holding them separately.

VaR satisfies the first three properties in general but can fail subadditivity — there exist pairs of risks where the VaR of the combined risk exceeds the sum of the individual VaRs, which is a serious practical problem since it means VaR can penalize diversification. TVaR, by contrast, satisfies all four properties (it is coherent) — one intuitive reason is that TVaR always averages over an entire tail region rather than reporting a single threshold, which smooths out exactly the kind of "threshold-crossing" discontinuity that breaks VaR's subadditivity.

Why does coherence matter beyond being a mathematical curiosity? A capital or risk-margin standard built on a non-subadditive measure can produce the perverse conclusion that a merged, diversified company needs more total capital than the sum of its standalone divisional requirements — exactly backwards from the actuarial intuition that pooling independent (or imperfectly correlated) risks should reduce relative volatility. This is the central reason regulatory and rating-agency capital frameworks have increasingly favored TVaR-style (also called Conditional Tail Expectation, or CTE) measures over pure VaR for setting economic capital, even though VaR remains extremely common in practice because it is simpler to communicate ("the loss we are 99% confident will not be exceeded") and does not require modeling the full shape of the tail beyond the threshold.

"VaR is not coherent; TVaR is coherent" is a frequently tested one-line fact. The failure point is always subadditivity — VaR satisfies translation invariance, positive homogeneity, and monotonicity without issue.
Example 7.1

Using the aggregate distribution from Example 6.4 (fS(0)=0.650, fS(1)=0.180, fS(2)=0.138, fS(3)=0.024, fS(4)=0.008), find E[S], E[S∧2], and the expected stop-loss payment with deductible 2.

Show Solution

E[S] = 0(0.650)+1(0.180)+2(0.138)+3(0.024)+4(0.008) = 0.180+0.276+0.072+0.032 = 0.560.

E[S∧2] = 0(0.650)+1(0.180)+2(0.138)+2(0.024)+2(0.008) = 0.180+0.276+0.048+0.016 = 0.520.

E[(S−2)+] = E[S]−E[S∧2] = 0.560−0.520 = 0.040. (Direct check: the excess is 1 at s=3 and 2 at s=4, so E=(1)(0.024)+(2)(0.008)=0.024+0.016=0.040 ✓.)

Example 7.2

Using the same fS from Example 7.1, find VaR0.80(S) and VaR0.90(S).

Show Solution

Cumulative: F(0)=0.650, F(1)=0.830, F(2)=0.968, F(3)=0.992, F(4)=1.000.

VaR0.80: smallest s with F(s)≥0.80. F(0)=0.650<0.80, F(1)=0.830≥0.80, so VaR0.80(S) = 1.

VaR0.90: F(1)=0.830<0.90, F(2)=0.968≥0.90, so VaR0.90(S) = 2.

Example 7.3

Using Example 7.2's VaR0.90(S)=2 and Example 7.1's E[(S−2)+]=0.040, compute TVaR0.90(S).

Show Solution

TVaR0.90(S) = VaR0.90(S) + E[(S−VaR0.90)+]/(1−0.90) = 2 + 0.040/0.10 = 2 + 0.40 = 2.40.

As expected, TVaR0.90=2.40 exceeds VaR0.90=2 — the extra 0.40 reflects the average severity of outcomes beyond the 90th percentile, not just the percentile threshold itself.

Example 7.4

Two independent risks A and B are each: 0 with probability 0.99, and 100 with probability 0.01. Show that VaR0.99 fails subadditivity for A and B, i.e. VaR0.99(A+B) > VaR0.99(A)+VaR0.99(B).

Show Solution

For a single risk, F(0)=0.99 ≥ 0.99, so VaR0.99(A) = 0 and likewise VaR0.99(B) = 0. Sum: VaR0.99(A)+VaR0.99(B) = 0.

For the combined risk A+B (A, B independent), the possible outcomes are 0 (both zero: 0.99×0.99=0.9801), 100 (exactly one nonzero: 2×0.01×0.99=0.0198), and 200 (both nonzero: 0.01×0.01=0.0001).

FA+B(0)=0.9801 < 0.99, so 0 does not qualify. FA+B(100)=0.9801+0.0198=0.9999 ≥ 0.99, so VaR0.99(A+B) = 100.

Since 100 > 0+0, subadditivity fails: pooling the two risks makes the 99th-percentile risk measure look worse, even though intuitively pooling independent small risks should diversify away some risk. This is the standard counterexample showing VaR is not, in general, a coherent risk measure.

Part II Practice Problems

1. A severity model has density f(x) = 2x/θ2 for 0 < x < θ, with θ = 100. Find E[X].
Show Answer
E[X] = ∫₀^θ x(2x/θ²)dx = 2θ/3 = 66.67.
2. For the same distribution as Problem 1, find Var(X).
Show Answer
E[X²] = ∫₀^θ x²(2x/θ²)dx = θ²/2 = 5000. Var(X) = 5000 − 66.67² ≈ 5000 − 4444.9 = 555.1.
3. Find the median of the distribution in Problem 1.
Show Answer
F(x) = x²/θ². Setting equal to 0.5: x = θ√0.5 = 100(0.7071) ≈ 70.71.
4. X ~ Pareto(α=1.8, θ=500). Does E[X²] exist? Justify briefly using the existence condition from Chapter 1.
Show Answer
No. E[Xk] exists only for k < α. Here k=2 and α=1.8, so 2 < 1.8 is false — the tail integrand behaves like x2−1.8−1=x−0.8, which does not integrate to a finite value over [0,∞).
5. X ~ Exponential(θ=200). Find E[X∧150].
Show Answer
E[X∧d] = θ(1−e−d/θ) = 200(1−e−0.75) = 200(1−0.4724) = 200(0.5276) ≈ 105.5.
6. Severity is Gamma(α=4, θ=250). Claims inflate 5%. Find the new θ and the new mean.
Show Answer
New θ = 250(1.05) = 262.5 (α unchanged at 4). New mean = αθ = 4(262.5) = 1050 (consistent with 1000×1.05).
7. X is Lognormal with μ=5, σ=1. A currency conversion multiplies all losses by 3. Give the distribution of Y=3X.
Show Answer
Y is Lognormal with μ' = μ+ln3 = 5+1.0986 = 6.0986, and σ unchanged at 1.
8. True or False: for a Weibull(τ,θ) distribution, τ is the scale parameter. Explain.
Show Answer
False. θ is the scale parameter (it appears as x/θ inside the survival function); τ is a shape parameter that controls whether the hazard rate increases or decreases over time and is unaffected by proportional rescaling of losses.
9. N ~ Poisson(λ=4). Use the (a,b,0) recursion to find p₀, p₁, p₂.
Show Answer
a=0, b=4. p₀=e−4≈0.0183. p₁=p₀(4)≈0.0733. p₂=p₁(4/2)=p₁(2)≈0.1465.
10. N ~ Binomial(m=8, q=0.25). Find a, b, and p₀.
Show Answer
a = −0.25/0.75 ≈ −0.3333. b = 9(0.25)/0.75 = 3. p₀ = 0.758 ≈ 0.1001.
11. N ~ Negative Binomial(r=4, β=0.6). Find p₀ and p₁ using the recursion.
Show Answer
a=0.6/1.6=0.375, b=(3)(0.6)/1.6=1.125. p₀=(1.6)−4≈0.1526. p₁=p₀(a+b)=0.1526(1.5)≈0.2289.
12. An (a,b,0) member has p₀=0.046656, p₁=0.186624, p₂=0.311040. Identify the family and its parameters.
Show Answer
Ratios: p₁/p₀=4.0=a+b; p₂/p₁≈1.6667=a+b/2. Subtracting: b/2=2.3333, b≈4.6667, a≈−0.6667. Since a<0, this is binomial. Solving −q/(1−q)=−0.6667 gives q=0.4; then (m+1)(0.4)/0.6=4.6667 gives m+1=7, so m=6, q=0.4. (Check: p₀=0.66=0.046656 ✓.)
13. N is Geometric with β=2 (i.e., NB with r=1). Find the zero-truncated probabilities p₁T and p₂T.
Show Answer
Base: p₀=1/3≈0.3333, a=β/(1+β)=2/3, b=0. p₁=p₀(a)=0.3333(0.6667)≈0.2222. p₂=p₁(a)≈0.1481. Rescale by 1/(1−p₀)=1.5: p₁T=0.2222(1.5)≈0.3333; p₂T=0.1481(1.5)≈0.2222.
14. Base frequency is Poisson(λ=2.5). A zero-modified version sets p₀M=0.1. Find p₁M.
Show Answer
Base p₀=e−2.5≈0.0821, p₁=2.5(0.0821)≈0.2052. Rescale factor=(1−0.1)/(1−0.0821)=0.9/0.9179≈0.9805. p₁M=0.2052(0.9805)≈0.2012.
15. In one sentence, explain the practical difference between choosing a zero-truncated model versus a zero-modified model for a given dataset.
Show Answer
Zero-truncated is used when a zero count is structurally impossible to observe (the data source guarantees at least one event); zero-modified is used when zeros are possible but occur at a different rate than a standard (a,b,0) member would predict, so p₀ is fit freely rather than fixed at 0.
16. N ~ Poisson(λ=20). X ~ Gamma(α=3, θ=100). Find E[S] and Var(S).
Show Answer
E[X]=300, Var(X)=3(100²)=30,000, E[X²]=30,000+90,000=120,000. E[S]=20(300)=6,000. Var(S)=λE[X²]=20(120,000)=2,400,000.
17. N ~ Negative Binomial(r=5, β=0.8). X ~ Exponential(θ=400). Find E[S] and Var(S).
Show Answer
E[N]=4, Var(N)=5(0.8)(1.8)=7.2. E[X]=400, Var(X)=160,000. E[S]=4(400)=1,600. Var(S)=4(160,000)+7.2(160,000)=11.2(160,000)=1,792,000.
18. Starting from Var(S)=E[Var(S|N)]+Var(E[S|N]), briefly derive Var(S)=E[N]Var(X)+Var(N)(E[X])² for the collective risk model.
Show Answer
Given N=n, S is a sum of n iid X's, so Var(S|N)=N·Var(X) and E[S|N]=N·E[X]. Then E[Var(S|N)]=E[N]Var(X), and Var(E[S|N])=Var(N·E[X])=(E[X])²Var(N). Summing the two pieces gives the stated formula.
19. Two independent policies have (q,fixed severity) of (0.15, 5000) and (0.25, 3000). Find E[S] and Var(S) under the individual risk model.
Show Answer
E[Y₁]=750, E[Y₂]=750, E[S]=1,500. Var(Y₁)=0.15(0.85)(5000²)=3,187,500. Var(Y₂)=0.25(0.75)(3000²)=1,687,500. Var(S)=4,875,000 (SD≈2207.9).
20. Using Problem 19's S, approximate P(S>3000) with the normal approximation.
Show Answer
z=(3000−1500)/2207.9≈0.679. P(S>3000)≈1−Φ(0.68)=1−0.7517=0.2483.
21. An aggregate loss has E[S]=5000, Var(S)=3,000,000. Find the lognormal approximation's μ and σ².
Show Answer
E[S²]=3,000,000+25,000,000=28,000,000. σ²=ln(28,000,000/25,000,000)=ln(1.12)≈0.1133. μ=ln(5000)−0.1133/2≈8.5172−0.0567=8.4605.
22. X takes values 0,1 with probabilities 0.6, 0.4. N takes values 0,1,2 with probabilities 0.5, 0.3, 0.2. Build fS(0), fS(1), fS(2) by convolution.
Show Answer
fX*2: (0)=0.36, (1)=2(0.6)(0.4)=0.48, (2)=0.16. fS(0)=0.5(1)+0.3(0.6)+0.2(0.36)=0.5+0.18+0.072=0.752. fS(1)=0.3(0.4)+0.2(0.48)=0.12+0.096=0.216. fS(2)=0.2(0.16)=0.032. (Sum=1.000 ✓)
23. Using Problem 22's fS, find E[S∧1] and the expected stop-loss payment with deductible 1.
Show Answer
E[S]=0(0.752)+1(0.216)+2(0.032)=0.216+0.064=0.280. E[S∧1]=1(0.216)+1(0.032)=0.248. E[(S−1)+]=0.280−0.248=0.032 (matches direct calc: excess=1 only at s=2, weight 0.032).
24. Using Problem 22's fS (cumulative F(0)=0.752, F(1)=0.968, F(2)=1.000), find VaR0.80(S) and TVaR0.80(S).
Show Answer
F(0)=0.752<0.80, F(1)=0.968≥0.80, so VaR0.80(S)=1. E[(S−1)+]=0.032 (from Problem 23). TVaR0.80(S)=1+0.032/(1−0.80)=1+0.032/0.2=1+0.16=1.16.
25. Explain informally, in a sentence or two, why VaR can fail the subadditivity coherence property while TVaR does not.
Show Answer
VaR only reports a single threshold (a percentile), so combining two risks can push a small amount of extra probability mass just past that threshold and cause a disproportionate jump in the combined VaR (as in the two-independent-small-risks counterexample); TVaR instead averages over the entire tail region beyond the threshold, which smooths out that kind of discontinuity and preserves subadditivity in general.
26. A Weibull severity model has shape τ=2 and scale θ=300. Claims inflate 10%. Using the Weibull median formula, median = θ(ln2)1/τ, find the old and new medians and confirm the scale-family property.
Show Answer
(ln2)1/2=(0.6931)0.5≈0.8326. Old median=300(0.8326)≈249.8. New θ=330, new median=330(0.8326)≈274.8, and 274.8/249.8≈1.10 — confirming the median scaled by exactly the same 10% inflation factor, as expected for a scale family.

Part III — Parametric Estimation (2.5–7.5%)

Once you can name a distribution and describe its shape, the next question is always: which member of that family best matches a particular batch of claims data? Parametric estimation answers this with a single unifying idea — Maximum Likelihood Estimation (MLE). Instead of separate ad-hoc rules for every situation, MLE gives one recipe: write down the probability (or probability density) of observing exactly the data you have, as a function of the unknown parameters, and find the parameter values that make that probability as large as possible. This part builds the recipe from the ground up: first for the simplest case of individually recorded losses, then for data that has been grouped into intervals, and finally for the realistic insurance setting where deductibles truncate the data from below and policy limits censor it from above. The mechanics differ slightly in each case, but the underlying maximization principle never changes. Because this topic carries only a small slice of the exam's weight, the goal here is not encyclopedic coverage of every possible distribution — it is fluency with the recipe itself, so that whichever named distribution a question hands you, you can write the likelihood, take the log, differentiate, and solve (or recognize when solving requires numerical iteration instead of algebra).

1. MLE: Complete Individual Data

Learning Outcomes Covered: Construct the likelihood and log-likelihood function for a sample of fully observed, individual severity or frequency values; derive the maximum likelihood estimator by solving the score equation; apply the method to named severity and frequency distributions and evaluate the resulting estimate numerically.

1.1 Building the Likelihood

Suppose x1, x2, …, xn are independent observations from a distribution with density f(x;θ) (or probability function p(x;θ) in the discrete/frequency case), where θ may represent one or several unknown parameters. Because the observations are independent, the joint density of the whole sample — viewed as a function of the unknown parameter rather than of the data — is called the likelihood function.

Likelihood (individual data)L(θ) = f(x1;θ)·f(x2;θ)·…·f(xn;θ) = ∏i=1n f(xi;θ)

Products of many small numbers are numerically awkward and hard to differentiate, so we work instead with the natural log, which converts the product into a sum and preserves the location of the maximum (since ln(·) is strictly increasing).

Log-likelihoodl(θ) = ln L(θ) = ∑i=1n ln f(xi;θ)

The maximum likelihood estimate θ̂ is the value of θ that maximizes l(θ). When l is smooth and concave in θ, this is found by setting the derivative (the score function) equal to zero.

Score equation∂l(θ)/∂θ = 0

With several parameters, this becomes a system: one partial derivative per parameter, all set to zero simultaneously. You should always confirm the solution is a maximum, not a minimum or saddle point — usually by checking that the second derivative is negative at θ̂, or by noting that the likelihood vanishes at the boundaries of the parameter space so the interior stationary point must be the peak.

1.2 A Distribution Family That Reduces to the Sample Mean

For the exponential distribution, f(x;θ) = θ-1e-x/θ, x>0. Its log-likelihood is

Exponential log-likelihoodl(θ) = -n lnθ - (1/θ)∑xi

Differentiating, l'(θ) = -n/θ + (1/θ2)∑xi. Setting this to zero and multiplying through by θ2 gives -nθ + ∑xi = 0, so

Exponential MLEθ̂ = (1/n)∑xi = x̄

The same phenomenon — the MLE collapsing to the sample mean — happens for the Poisson frequency distribution, p(x;λ) = e-λλx/x!. Here l(λ) = -nλ + (lnλ)∑xi - ∑ln(xi!), and l'(λ) = -n + (1/λ)∑xi = 0 gives λ̂ = x̄ directly. This is not a coincidence: both distributions belong to a class where the score equation is linear in the parameter, so no iteration is ever needed. Most other named distributions (Pareto, gamma with unknown shape, Weibull, lognormal with unknown σ, etc.) do not have this property — their score equations are nonlinear in at least one parameter, and while some (like the lognormal below) still reduce to closed form through a clever transformation, others genuinely require numerical root-finding.

Quick check: Whenever you see "exponential severity" or "Poisson frequency" paired with complete individual data, you can usually skip the calculus entirely and write down θ̂ or λ̂ as the sample mean. Save your time for the harder distributions.

1.3 A Distribution Requiring Real Algebra: the Pareto

The two-parameter Pareto has density f(x;α,θ) = αθα/(x+θ)α+1, x>0. If the scale parameter θ is known (e.g., fixed by prior industry studies) and only the shape α is estimated, the log-likelihood is

Pareto log-likelihood (θ known)l(α) = n lnα + nαlnθ - (α+1)∑ln(xi+θ)

Differentiating with respect to α: l'(α) = n/α + nlnθ - ∑ln(xi+θ) = 0, which rearranges to a closed form:

Pareto MLE (θ known)α̂ = n / ∑ln[(xi+θ)/θ]

Since l''(α) = -n/α2 < 0 everywhere, this stationary point is guaranteed to be the maximum. When both α and θ are unknown, a second score equation, nα/θ = (α+1)∑1/(xi+θ), must hold simultaneously. Substituting the first equation into the second leaves a single nonlinear equation in θ alone, which has no algebraic solution and must be solved by an iterative numerical method such as Newton–Raphson.

Example 3.1

Five liability claims (in $) are recorded in full: 120, 340, 275, 560, 405. Assuming an exponential severity model, find the MLE of θ.

Show Solution

Since the score equation for the exponential is linear, θ̂ = x̄. The sum is 120+340+275+560+405 = 1700, and n = 5, so θ̂ = 1700/5 = 340.

Example 3.2

Five ground-up losses are 200, 450, 700, 900, 1600. Prior experience fixes the Pareto scale parameter at θ = 500. Find the MLE of α.

Show Solution

Compute (xi+500)/500 for each observation: 1.4, 1.9, 2.4, 2.8, 4.2. Taking natural logs: 0.3365, 0.6419, 0.8755, 1.0296, 1.4351, which sum to 4.3185. Then α̂ = 5/4.3185 = 1.158 (to 3 d.p.).

Example 3.3

A portfolio of 10 auto policies produced these claim counts over one year: 2, 0, 3, 1, 4, 2, 1, 0, 3, 2. Fit a Poisson frequency model.

Show Solution

The Poisson score equation gives λ̂ = x̄. The counts sum to 18, and n = 10, so λ̂ = 18/10 = 1.8 expected claims per policy per year.

Example 3.4

Four losses are recorded as 400, 900, 250, and 800. Fit a lognormal model, i.e., find μ̂ and σ̂2 for ln X ~ Normal(μ,σ2).

Show Solution

The key trick: if X is lognormal, then Y = ln X is normal, and the lognormal likelihood in terms of the xi is algebraically identical to a normal likelihood in terms of yi = ln xi (the Jacobian term from the transformation does not involve μ or σ, so it drops out of the score equations). The normal MLEs are the sample mean and the sample variance divided by n (not n−1): μ̂ = ȳ and σ̂2 = (1/n)∑(yi−ȳ)2.

Here yi = ln(400), ln(900), ln(250), ln(800) = 5.9915, 6.8024, 5.5215, 6.6846, summing to 25.6931, so μ̂ = 25.6931/4 = 6.4233. The squared deviations from 6.4233 are 0.0435, 0.4446, 0.5177, 0.0683, summing to 1.0741, so σ̂2 = 1.0741/4 = 0.2685 (σ̂ ≈ 0.518).

Common trap: The MLE of the normal (and hence lognormal) variance divides by n, not n−1. This makes it a biased estimator of the true variance, even though it is still the maximum likelihood estimator. Don't reflexively reach for the "sample variance" formula from introductory statistics unless the question specifically asks for an unbiased estimator.

1.4 A Useful Shortcut: the Invariance Property

One more fact makes MLE especially convenient. If θ̂ is the MLE of θ, then for essentially any function g, the plugged-in value g(θ̂) is automatically the MLE of g(θ) — there is no need to set up and re-maximize a separate likelihood for the transformed quantity.

Invariance propertyIf θ̂ is the MLE of θ, then g(θ̂) is the MLE of g(θ)

For instance, once Example 3.1 gives θ̂=340 for an exponential model, the MLE of the median loss, m=θln2, is simply m̂=340ln2≈235.7 — no additional optimization required. The same idea lets you convert an MLE of a Pareto's parameters directly into an MLE of, say, the probability a loss exceeds some fixed amount, or the mean excess loss, by substituting θ̂ and α̂ into the relevant formula.

2. MLE: Grouped Data

Learning Outcomes Covered: Construct the likelihood function when observations are known only to fall within specified intervals rather than at exact values; derive the grouped-data log-likelihood as a sum of interval-probability terms; solve for the MLE in representative cases; compare the information content of grouped versus individual data.

2.1 Why Grouping Changes the Likelihood

Insurance summary data is often published as a table — "38 claims between $0 and $500, 22 claims between $500 and $1,500," and so on — without the exact dollar amount of each claim. If observation i is known only to lie in the interval (cj-1, cj], its contribution to the likelihood is no longer the density f(xi;θ) at a point, but the probability of landing in that interval:

Interval probabilityP(cj-1 < X ≤ cj) = F(cj;θ) − F(cj-1;θ)

If group j contains nj observations (all contributing the same factor, since we don't know which value within the interval each one took), the full likelihood is a product over groups, each factor raised to the corresponding count:

Grouped likelihoodL(θ) = ∏j [F(cj;θ) − F(cj-1;θ)]nj
Grouped log-likelihoodl(θ) = ∑j nj ln[F(cj;θ) − F(cj-1;θ)]

The mechanics from here are identical to the individual-data case — differentiate, set to zero, solve — but each term now involves the CDF at two boundary points instead of a density at one exact point. If the highest interval is open-ended, (ck, ∞), its probability is simply the survival function S(ck;θ).

2.2 A Two-Group Shortcut

When data is split into just two groups by a single cutoff c (below vs. above), and only one parameter is unknown, the log-likelihood l(θ) = n1lnF(c;θ) + n2ln[1−F(c;θ)] has exactly the shape of a binomial log-likelihood in the "success probability" p = F(c;θ). Its maximizer is always the observed proportion, p̂ = n1/n. So the MLE recipe reduces to: set the theoretical CDF at the cutoff equal to the empirical proportion below it, then solve algebraically for θ.

Two-group shortcutF(c;θ̂) = n1/n
Example 3.5

A study of 50 claims reports 10 below $50, 25 between $50 and $150, and 15 above $150. Fit an exponential severity distribution.

Show Solution

Let a = e-50/θ. Because 150 = 3×50, e-150/θ = a3, so the three group probabilities are 1−a, a−a3, and a3. Using a−a3=a(1-a)(1+a), the log-likelihood simplifies to l(a) = 35ln(1−a) + 70ln(a) + 25ln(1+a) (after collecting the 10, 25, and 15 counts). Differentiating and clearing denominators leads to the quadratic 13a2 + a − 7 = 0, whose positive root is a = (−1+√365)/26 ≈ 0.6963. Since ln(a) = −50/θ, we get θ̂ = 50/(−ln 0.6963) = 50/0.3619 ≈ 138.2.

Example 3.6

Of 50 claims, 30 are below $200 and 20 are above. Fit an exponential distribution using only this split.

Show Solution

By the two-group shortcut, 1−e-200/θ = 30/50 = 0.6, so e-200/θ = 0.4 and θ̂ = 200/(−ln0.4) = 200/0.9163 ≈ 218.3.

Example 3.7

Of 50 losses, 15 are below $100 and 35 are above. Fit a Weibull distribution with known shape parameter τ = 2 and unknown scale θ (recall F(x)=1−e−(x/θ)τ).

Show Solution

The two-group shortcut gives 1−e−(100/θ)2 = 15/50 = 0.3, so (100/θ)2 = −ln(0.7) = 0.3567, giving 100/θ = 0.5972 and θ̂ = 100/0.5972 ≈ 167.4.

Example 3.8

Losses are known to exceed a single-parameter Pareto threshold θ0=100 (so F(x)=1−(100/x)α, x≥100). Of 50 losses, 12 fall in (100,200], 18 in (200,400], 14 in (400,800], and 6 exceed 800. Estimate α.

Show Solution

The boundaries double each time, so let β=(100/200)α=2-α; then S(200)=β, S(400)=β2, S(800)=β3. The group probabilities become 1−β, β(1−β), β2(1−β), and β3. Collecting terms, l(β) = 44ln(1−β) + 64lnβ (44 = 12+18+14; 64 = 18+2(14)+3(6)). This is again a binomial-shape log-likelihood, maximized at β̂=64/108=16/27≈0.5926. Since β=2-α, α̂ = −ln(β̂)/ln2 = 0.5232/0.6931 ≈ 0.755.

Individual vs. grouped data — what changes and what doesn't
Individual dataGrouped data
Likelihood factor per observationf(xi;θ)[F(cj;θ)−F(cj-1;θ)] shared by whole group
Maximization principleSame — maximize log-likelihoodSame — maximize log-likelihood
Precision of θ̂HigherLower (coarser data → larger standard error)
Grouping never changes how you maximize a likelihood — only what each factor looks like. But because a whole interval of possible values is compressed into a single count, less information about θ survives the grouping, so the grouped-data MLE is generally less precise (larger variance) than the individual-data MLE from the same underlying observations.

The interval boundaries in Examples 3.5 and 3.8 were deliberately chosen in a common ratio (150 = 3×50; 200, 400, 800 doubling each time), which is what allowed the substitution trick to turn the score equation into a polynomial that factors cleanly. Real published intervals are rarely so obliging — a table might report counts for (0,75], (75,140], (140,300], and (300,∞), with no tidy ratio linking the boundaries. The log-likelihood is built exactly the same way in that case, but the resulting score equation generally has no algebraic solution and must be solved by evaluating l(θ) at a grid of trial values (or by Newton–Raphson) until the derivative is acceptably close to zero. The principle — write the sum of njln[F(cj;θ)−F(cj-1;θ)] and maximize it — never changes; only the tool used to find the maximum does.

Don't forget the open-ended top interval. If the last group is "claims above $ck," its likelihood contribution is S(ck;θ), not a density and not 1−F(ck;θ) written incorrectly as a density term — a very common setup error.

3. MLE: Truncated & Censored Data

Learning Outcomes Covered: Construct likelihood contributions for left-truncated observations (data unobservable below a threshold) and right-censored observations (data known only to exceed a threshold); combine truncation and censoring, possibly varying by observation, into a single log-likelihood; solve for the MLE in representative insurance settings such as deductibles and policy limits.

3.1 Left Truncation: Data You Never See

A deductible d creates a fundamentally different problem from grouping: losses below d are not merely unrecorded in detail — they never generate a claim report at all, so they are entirely absent from the insurer's data. The observed sample is really a sample from the conditional distribution of X given X>d. The likelihood contribution of an observed loss x>d must therefore be the conditional density, not the ordinary density:

Left-truncated density contributionf(x;θ) / S(d;θ), for observed x > d

Dividing by S(d;θ) re-normalizes the density so it integrates to 1 over the truncated range (d,∞). Omitting this division is the single most common error in this topic — it silently biases θ̂ because it treats a conditional sample as if it were a complete one.

3.2 Right Censoring: Data You See Only Partially

A policy limit u creates a different kind of incompleteness. A loss that reaches or exceeds u is still reported — the insurer knows it happened — but the exact size above u is unknown (only the capped payment is recorded, or the loss is simply flagged as "at least u"). Such an observation contributes not a density value but a survival probability:

Right-censored contributionS(u;θ), for an observation known only to satisfy X ≥ u

3.3 The General Recipe

Real data sets mix all three observation types: exact values, left-truncated at (possibly varying) thresholds di, and right-censored at (possibly varying) limits ui. The recipe is always to multiply together one factor per observation, choosing the right factor for what was actually observed about that unit, and to divide every factor by S(di;θ) whenever that observation was only reported because it exceeded its own truncation point:

General log-likelihoodl(θ) = ∑exact i [ln f(xi;θ) − ln S(di;θ)] + ∑censored i [ln S(ui;θ) − ln S(di;θ)]

Set di=0 for any observation with no deductible, and simply drop an observation from the censored sum if it was never actually censored. This single formula subsumes every special case in this chapter.

Example 3.9

An insurer sells policies with a $50 deductible and a $500 policy limit, so it only observes ground-up losses above $50, and any loss of $500 or more is recorded merely as "≥$500." Five observations: exact losses of 80, 150, 300, and two records of "≥500." Fit an exponential severity model.

Show Solution

With d=50 common to all five and u=500 for the two censored records, the score equation (after substituting f and S for the exponential and simplifying) reduces to θ̂ = [∑exactxi + ncu − nd] / nexact. Here ∑xi=80+150+300=530, ncu=2(500)=1000, nd=5(50)=250, and nexact=3, giving θ̂=(530+1000−250)/3=1280/3≈426.7.

Equivalently — and this is worth remembering — because the exponential is memoryless, this same answer comes from averaging (yi−d) over the number of exact observations, where yi is the exact loss or the censoring point: (30+100+250+450+450)/3 = 1280/3, matching exactly.

Memoryless shortcut: For the exponential distribution only, left truncation can be handled by simply subtracting the deductible from every observed value (exact or censoring point) before averaging — because the excess X−d given X>d is again Exponential(θ). This shortcut does not extend to the Pareto, gamma, Weibull, or any other severity distribution, where you must divide by S(d;θ) explicitly.
Example 3.10

A $300-scale Pareto model (θ=300 known) is fit to losses observed only above a $100 deductible. Five ground-up losses: 150, 220, 350, 500, 900. Estimate α.

Show Solution

The truncated density is f(x)/S(100) = α(100+300)α/(x+300)α+1, giving log-likelihood l(α)=nlnα+nαln(400)−(α+1)∑ln(xi+300), whose score equation solves to α̂=n/∑ln[(xi+300)/400]. The ratios (xi+300)/400 are 1.125, 1.30, 1.625, 2.00, 3.00, with logs summing to 2.6574. So α̂=5/2.6574≈1.882. Notice this is not the same as simply shifting the data by 100 and re-fitting an ordinary Pareto — the Pareto lacks the exponential's memoryless property, so the deductible enters through S(100;θ) in a genuinely nonlinear way.

Example 3.11

Four policies have different deductibles and share a $600 cap. Policy A (d=0): exact loss 300. Policy B (d=100): exact loss 350. Policy C (d=0): censored at 600. Policy D (d=200): censored at 600. Fit an exponential model.

Show Solution

Using θ̂=∑(yi−di)/nexact (the per-observation generalization justified by the memoryless property): A gives 300−0=300; B gives 350−100=250; C gives 600−0=600; D gives 600−200=400. These sum to 300+250+600+400=1550. With nexact=2 (A and B), θ̂=1550/2=775.

Example 3.12

A $400-scale Pareto (θ=400 known, no deductible) is fit to four losses, two exact (150, 500) and two censored at a $1000 limit. Estimate α.

Show Solution

With no truncation, l(α)=nexactlnα+nexactαln400−(α+1)∑exactln(xi+400)+ncαln[400/1400]. Solving the score equation gives the general pattern α̂=nexact/∑all iln[(yi+400)/400], where yi is the exact value or the censoring point 1000. The ratios are 550/400=1.375, 900/400=2.25, and 1400/400=3.5 (twice), with logs 0.3185, 0.8109, 1.2528, 1.2528, summing to 3.6350. So α̂=2/3.6350≈0.550 — note the numerator uses only the count of exact observations, while every observation (exact or censored) contributes to the denominator sum.

Watch the numerator versus the denominator. In truncated/censored likelihoods, the count of exact (uncensored) observations typically appears in the numerator of the final closed-form estimator, while sums involving every observation (including censored ones, via their censoring point) appear in the denominator. Mixing these up is a frequent algebra slip under time pressure.

3.4 Combining Truncation with Grouping

The most realistic (and most exam-favored) setup layers truncation and grouping together: a deductible removes the smallest losses from view entirely, and whatever remains is only summarized by interval, not reported exactly. The fix is simply to combine the two constructions already developed — take an ordinary grouped-interval probability and renormalize it by dividing by S(d;θ), exactly as Section 3.1 renormalized a single density:

Truncated, grouped-interval probability[F(cj;θ) − F(cj-1;θ)] / S(d;θ), for an interval entirely above d

For the exponential distribution specifically, this renormalization has a pleasant side effect: because S(x;θ)/S(d;θ) = e-(x-d)/θ, the truncated-grouped probabilities are numerically identical to what you would get by subtracting d from every interval boundary and solving an ordinary (untruncated) grouped-data problem — the memoryless shortcut from Section 3.1 extends naturally to grouped intervals. For any other severity distribution, no such shortcut exists, and the division by S(d;θ) must be carried through explicitly in the log-likelihood.

Part III Practice Problems

1. Six claims are recorded in full: 90, 150, 220, 310, 180, 260. Find the MLE of θ under an exponential severity assumption.
Show Answer
θ̂=x̄. Sum = 1210, n = 6, so θ̂ = 1210/6 ≈ 201.7.
2. Eight policies produce claim counts 1, 0, 2, 3, 1, 0, 2, 1 over a year. Fit a Poisson frequency model.
Show Answer
λ̂=x̄. Sum = 10, n = 8, so λ̂ = 10/8 = 1.25.
3. With Pareto scale θ=1000 known, four losses are 300, 600, 1200, 2500. Find α̂.
Show Answer
Ratios (xi+1000)/1000: 1.3, 1.6, 2.2, 3.5; logs sum to 0.2624+0.4700+0.7885+1.2528=2.7736. α̂=4/2.7736 ≈ 1.442.
4. Three losses are 400, 900, 250. Fit a lognormal model (find μ̂ and σ̂2).
Show Answer
yi=ln(xi)=5.9915, 6.8024, 5.5215, summing to 18.3153, so μ̂=6.1051. Squared deviations sum to 0.8398, so σ̂2=0.8398/3 ≈ 0.280 (σ̂≈0.529).
5. Of 50 claims, 25 fall below $150 and 25 fall above. Fit an exponential distribution using this single cutoff.
Show Answer
By the two-group shortcut, 1−e−150/θ=0.5, so e−150/θ=0.5 and θ̂=150/ln2 ≈ 216.4.
6. Of 40 claims grouped by an exponential model, 8 fall in (0,100], 14 in (100,200], and 18 exceed 200. Find θ̂.
Show Answer
Let a=e−100/θ, so e−200/θ=a2. The log-likelihood simplifies to l(a)=22ln(1−a)+50ln(a) (22=8+14, 50=14+2×18). Maximizing: 50(1−a)=22a ⇒ a=50/72≈0.6944. Then θ̂=100/(−ln0.6944)=100/0.3646 ≈ 274.2.
7. Four policies: A (d=0, exact loss 180), B (d=100, exact loss 350), C (d=0, censored at u=600), D (d=200, censored at u=600). Fit an exponential model.
Show Answer
Sum of (yi−di) over all four: (180−0)+(350−100)+(600−0)+(600−200)=180+250+600+400=1430. Divide by nexact=2 (A and B): θ̂=1430/2=715.
8. With Pareto scale θ=500 known and deductible d=200, three ground-up losses are observed: 250, 500, 900. Find α̂.
Show Answer
Ratios (xi+500)/(200+500)=(xi+500)/700: 750/700=1.0714, 1000/700=1.4286, 1400/700=2.0; logs sum to 0.0690+0.3567+0.6931=1.1188. α̂=3/1.1188 ≈ 2.681.
9. True or False: for an exponential severity model observed above a deductible d, the correct MLE of θ is simply the average of the raw observed values xi (with no adjustment for d). Briefly justify.
Show Answer
False. Because of the memoryless property, X−d given X>d is again Exponential(θ), so the conditional mean of the observed values is E[X|X>d]=θ+d, not θ. The correct MLE averages the excess amounts (xi−d), not the raw values — using raw xi without subtracting d systematically overstates θ by approximately d.
10. A block of business has a $100 deductible, so only ground-up losses above $100 are ever observed, and those are further summarized into two groups: 20 losses in (100,300] and 15 losses above 300. Assuming an exponential severity model, find θ̂.
Show Answer
By the memoryless shortcut for truncated, grouped exponential data, shift both boundaries down by d=100: this is equivalent to an ordinary two-group problem in W=X−100 with cutoff 200, n1=20 below, n2=15 above, n=35. The two-group shortcut gives 1−e−200/θ=20/35=4/7, so e−200/θ=3/7. Then θ̂=200/[−ln(3/7)]=200/[ln7−ln3]=200/(1.9459−1.0986)=200/0.8473 ≈ 236.0.

Part IV — Introduction to Credibility (2.5–5%)

Every rate-making decision runs into the same tension: a policyholder's own claim history is the most relevant information about that policyholder's true risk, yet it is almost always too thin to be trusted on its own. Credibility theory is the actuary's formal toolkit for blending a small, noisy individual data set with a larger, more stable class-wide rate. This part introduces the conceptual foundation of credibility weighting and then develops the classical (limited fluctuation) approach, which asks a single practical question: how much data is "enough" before we let a risk's own experience speak for itself? The material here is intentionally compact — it is a small-weight topic on the syllabus — but the ideas reappear throughout ratemaking practice, so a clean grasp of the mechanics pays off far beyond its point value.

1. The Concept of Credibility

Learning Outcomes Covered: Explain the pricing problem that motivates credibility weighting; express the credibility premium as a weighted blend of individual experience and a manual (class) rate; interpret the credibility factor Z, including its bounds and the qualitative factors that drive its size; distinguish full credibility from partial credibility at a conceptual level.

1.1 Why a Single Rate Isn't Enough

Suppose an insurer already has a well-established manual rate — sometimes called the class rate or prior rate — built from a large, stable book of similar risks. A new policyholder, or an existing one up for renewal, arrives with its own claim history. Two extreme pricing strategies are both flawed. Charging every risk the manual rate ignores everything unique about that particular policyholder: a fleet operator with an unusually strong safety program, or an unusually poor one, would be charged the same as an "average" fleet. Charging strictly on the policyholder's own observed average loss ignores that a small number of claims over a short history is a noisy, unreliable estimate of that policyholder's true underlying risk level — a single large claim or a lucky claim-free year can swing the observed average wildly.

Credibility theory resolves this by blending the two sources of information. The weight assigned to the individual's own data depends on how much we trust it, which in turn depends primarily on how much of it there is (and how variable it is). This is the core idea behind the entire topic: more and more-stable individual data should count for more; thin or highly variable individual data should count for less.

1.2 The Credibility Premium

The standard way to combine the two sources is a simple weighted average, called the credibility premium (or credibility-weighted rate):

Credibility PremiumP = Z · (observed experience) + (1 − Z) · (manual/prior rate)

Here "observed experience" is some statistic computed from the policyholder's own data — commonly an average loss per exposure, a loss ratio, or an average pure premium — and the "manual rate" is the rate that would otherwise apply based on the broader class. The quantity Z is the credibility factor, and it always satisfies 0 ≤ Z ≤ 1.

At the two boundaries the formula collapses to familiar cases. When Z = 0, the policyholder's own data is deemed to carry no credibility whatsoever, and the manual rate is charged in full — this is appropriate when the individual's data set is too small or too erratic to say anything reliable. When Z = 1, the policyholder is said to have earned full credibility: its own experience is judged stable enough to be used on its own, with no blending at all. Every value strictly between 0 and 1 is partial credibility, and it produces a premium that sits somewhere on the line segment between the manual rate and the observed rate — closer to whichever one is weighted more heavily.

Tip: Read Z as a trust weight, not a probability of anything. A convenient sanity check: if Z is close to 1, the credibility premium should land close to the observed rate; if Z is close to 0, it should land close to the manual rate. If your computed premium doesn't move in the direction you'd expect from the size of Z, re-check your arithmetic before moving on.

1.3 What Drives the Size of Z

At this conceptual stage — before any formulas for Z itself are introduced — it's worth internalizing the qualitative drivers of the credibility factor, since Chapter 2 will turn these into precise formulas:

  • Volume of data. More exposures, more claims, or a longer observation period all push Z upward, because a larger sample gives a more precise estimate of the policyholder's true mean loss level.
  • Variability of the underlying risk. Holding volume fixed, a line of business with highly variable claim sizes (think products liability) needs more data to reach the same Z than a line with very predictable claim sizes (think short-tail property), because variance in the data makes any fixed sample size a less precise estimate.
  • The standard being demanded. An insurer that requires very tight accuracy (say, wanting the estimate within 2.5% of the truth) will need more data to reach full credibility than one that tolerates a wider band (say, within 10%). This "accuracy standard" idea is formalized in Chapter 2.

Note carefully what does not belong on this list: whether the observed experience happens to be favorable or unfavorable relative to the manual rate has no bearing on Z. Credibility is about the reliability of the estimate, not its direction.

Common trap: Do not confuse "bad experience" with "credible experience." A policyholder with only 3 claims and a terrible loss ratio does not automatically deserve a high Z just because the result looks dramatic — with so little data, that result could easily be random noise. Z should be driven by the stability/volume of the data underlying the observation, never by how extreme the observation itself looks.

1.4 Full vs. Partial Credibility, Informally

Because thin data is unreliable, no responsible ratemaking scheme hands out Z = 1 lightly. Classical credibility theory (the subject of Chapter 2) sets an explicit numerical bar — a minimum expected claim count, or minimum expected exposure — below which a risk simply cannot be assigned full credibility, no matter how compelling its observed average looks. Below that bar, credibility is assigned on a smooth partial scale that grows as the data set grows, so that a risk with, say, a quarter of the required data gets meaningfully less weight on its own experience than a risk with three-quarters of the required data — but still more than a risk with almost none. The next chapter makes this precise.

Example 4.1

A regional trucking class has a manual pure premium of $1,200 per unit. A particular fleet's own experience over the rating period shows an average pure premium of $1,450 per unit. The insurer's credibility procedure assigns this fleet a credibility factor of Z = 0.35, based on its exposure volume. Find the credibility-weighted premium.

Show Solution

Apply the credibility premium formula directly:

P = 0.35(1450) + 0.65(1200) = 507.50 + 780.00 = 1287.50

The fleet is charged $1,287.50 per unit — noticeably above the manual rate of $1,200 (reflecting its worse-than-average observed experience) but well below its own raw observed average of $1,450, since only 35% weight is placed on that thin individual data set.

Example 4.2

Two employers, A and B, are both rated off the same $1,000 manual rate, and both happen to show an observed average 20% above manual (i.e., $1,200). Employer A is small and is assigned Z = 0.20; Employer B is a large national account assigned Z = 0.75. Compute each employer's credibility premium and comment on the difference.

Show Solution

Employer A: PA = 0.20(1200) + 0.80(1000) = 240 + 800 = 1040

Employer B: PB = 0.75(1200) + 0.25(1000) = 900 + 250 = 1150

Even though both employers show the identical 20% adverse deviation from manual, Employer B's premium moves much further away from the manual rate ($150 above manual vs. only $40 above manual for A). This is exactly the intended behavior: B's larger, more credible data set is treated as more informative about its true risk level, so its own experience is allowed to influence the final premium far more heavily than A's thin data set.

Example 4.3

A book of business has a manual rate of $800 and observed average losses of $950. The insurer's actuary ultimately charges a premium of $890. What credibility factor Z is implied by this premium?

Show Solution

Set up the credibility premium equation and solve for Z:

890 = Z(950) + (1−Z)(800) = 800 + 150Z

90 = 150Z ⟹ Z = 0.60

The insurer's pricing is consistent with placing 60% weight on this policyholder's own experience.

Example 4.4

A small employer had a credibility factor of Z = 0.30 last year, based on 50 expected claims worth of exposure, against a manual rate of $2,000 and an observed average of $2,300. This year, the employer's exposure has grown to 200 expected claims worth of volume (four times as much), and the insurer's procedure now assigns Z = 0.60. Assuming the observed average loss is unchanged at $2,300, compute the credibility premium in each year, and note what relationship between exposure growth and credibility-factor growth this illustrates.

Show Solution

Last year: P1 = 0.30(2300) + 0.70(2000) = 690 + 1400 = 2090

This year: P2 = 0.60(2300) + 0.40(2000) = 1380 + 800 = 2180

Notice that exposure quadrupled (multiplied by 4) but Z only doubled (multiplied by 2). Since 2 = √4, this is consistent with Z growing in proportion to the square root of the data volume rather than in direct proportion to it — a relationship that Chapter 2 derives explicitly as the partial credibility formula. It's a useful pattern to notice now: credibility grows more slowly than the data itself.

2. Limited Fluctuation (Classical) Credibility

Learning Outcomes Covered: Derive the full credibility standard for claim frequency using a normal approximation to a Poisson claim count; extend the standard to account for severity fluctuation and for combined (aggregate/pure premium) fluctuation; compute partial credibility factors using the square-root rule; compute a credibility-weighted premium from raw claim data.

2.1 Setting Up the Full Credibility Standard

The classical (also called "limited fluctuation") approach to credibility asks a very concrete question: how many claims must we expect to observe before we're willing to trust the observed aggregate loss amount as a stand-in for the true mean, without any blending at all? The answer is built from an accuracy criterion chosen by the actuary: fix a tolerance k (e.g., "within 5% of the true mean") and a confidence level p (e.g., "95% of the time"). Full credibility is granted once there is enough expected data that the observed aggregate losses S satisfy

Full Credibility CriterionP( |S − E[S]| ≤ k · E[S] ) ≥ p

Different assumptions about what is "random" in S — just the number of claims, just the severity of each claim, or both together — lead to three related but distinct standards. We derive the simplest one first.

Standard 1: Claim Frequency Alone

Suppose the number of claims N in the period follows a Poisson distribution with mean λ, and suppose (for this first standard) that we are only worried about fluctuation in the count of claims — treat the severity of each individual claim as fixed, or simply set that concern aside for now. The criterion becomes P(|N − λ| ≤ kλ) ≥ p.

A Poisson random variable has the convenient property Var(N) = E[N] = λ. For λ reasonably large, the Central Limit Theorem lets us approximate N by a normal distribution with mean λ and variance λ. Standardizing:

P( −kλ ≤ N − λ ≤ kλ ) = P( −k√λ ≤ (N−λ)/√λ ≤ k√λ ) ≈ P( −k√λ ≤ Z ≤ k√λ )

where Z here denotes a standard normal variable. For this probability to reach at least p, the boundary k√λ must reach at least the (1+p)/2 quantile of the standard normal distribution — call it z(1+p)/2 (this is the usual two-sided critical value; e.g. for p = 90%, we need the 95th percentile, since 5% of probability is left in each tail). Setting k√λ = z(1+p)/2 and solving for the smallest such λ gives the standard for full credibility:

Full Credibility Standard — Claim Frequencyλ0 = ( z(1+p)/2 / k )²

λ0 is interpreted as the minimum number of expected claims the book of business must generate before its observed claim count (and, under this simplified standard, its resulting aggregate losses) is trusted on its own.

Standard normal quantiles z(1+p)/2 for common confidence levels
p(1+p)/2z(1+p)/2
80%0.9001.282
90%0.9501.645
95%0.9751.960
99%0.9952.576
Full credibility standards λ0 (expected claim count) for claim-frequency fluctuation, by tolerance k and confidence p
kp = 90%p = 95%p = 99%
10%271385664
5%1,0831,5372,655
2.5%4,3306,14710,618
Tip: Always locate the quantile as z(1+p)/2, not zp. For p = 90%, the correct z-value is 1.645 (the 95th percentile), not the 90th percentile (1.282). The 1.282 value corresponds to p = 80%, and mixing these up is one of the most common arithmetic slips on this topic.

Standard 2: Severity Alone (Estimating Average Claim Size)

A related but different question: if we plan to observe a fixed number of claims n and use their sample average severity X̄ to estimate the true mean severity μ, how large must n be? Let σ denote the standard deviation of an individual claim's severity, and define the severity's coefficient of variation as CVsev = σ/μ. Since Var(X̄) = σ²/n, the same normal-approximation argument as above (now standardizing X̄ instead of N) gives

k·μ / (σ/√n) ≥ z(1+p)/2 ⟹ √n ≥ z(1+p)/2· (σ/μ) / k ⟹ n ≥ ( z(1+p)/2/k )² · CVsev²

Full Credibility Standard — Severityn0 = ( z(1+p)/2 / k )² · CVsev²

Notice this is simply the frequency standard λ0 multiplied by CVsev². When severity has no variability at all (CV = 0, i.e. every claim costs exactly the same), this standard collapses to zero claims — no surprise, since a single claim would already reveal the true severity exactly.

Standard 3: Aggregate Losses / Pure Premium (Both Frequency and Severity Random)

The most realistic case lets both the claim count and the individual claim sizes vary. Let S = X1 + X2 + ⋯ + XN be aggregate losses, with N Poisson(λ) and the Xi iid severities independent of N. Standard results for compound distributions give E[S] = λμ and Var(S) = λ·E[X²] = λ(σ² + μ²). Running the same normal-approximation argument on S:

k·λμ / √( λ(σ²+μ²) ) ≥ z(1+p)/2 ⟹ λ ≥ ( z(1+p)/2/k )² · (σ²+μ²)/μ² = ( z(1+p)/2/k )² · (1 + CVsev²)

Full Credibility Standard — Aggregate Losses / Pure Premiumλ0 = ( z(1+p)/2 / k )² · (1 + CVsev²)

A tidy way to remember all three at once: the aggregate standard is exactly the sum of the frequency-only standard and the severity-only standard, since (z/k)²(1+CV²) = (z/k)² + (z/k)²CV². Frequency fluctuation and severity fluctuation each add their own claim-count "cost," and full credibility for the combined pure premium requires paying both.

Comparison of the three limited-fluctuation full-credibility standards
StandardWhat is randomFormula
Claim frequencyNumber of claims N onlyλ0 = (z/k)²
SeverityIndividual claim size, n claims fixedn0 = (z/k)²·CVsev²
Aggregate losses / pure premiumBoth N and claim sizeλ0 = (z/k)²·(1+CVsev²)
Common trap: The coefficient of variation used in these formulas is a property of the assumed severity distribution (population CV), not something computed from the small sample currently being rated. Also, once you compute a full credibility standard measured in expected claims, keep track of whether the actual data you're comparing it to (see Section 2.2) is measured in the same units — claims vs. exposures vs. premium volume are not interchangeable without a conversion factor.

2.2 Partial Credibility: The Square-Root Rule

Very few risks generate enough data to clear the full credibility bar. Classical credibility handles everyone below the bar with a simple interpolation rule: credibility grows with the square root of the ratio of actual to required data.

Partial Credibility (Square-Root Rule)Z = √( n / n0 ), capped at Z = 1

Here n is the actual amount of data available (expected or actual claim count, matched to whichever standard n0 was built from), and n0 is the relevant full credibility standard from Section 2.1. If n ≥ n0, the risk has already cleared the bar and is simply assigned Z = 1 — the square root of a ratio exceeding 1 is never used, since credibility cannot exceed full credibility. This square-root relationship is exactly what Example 4.4 in Chapter 1 previewed: quadrupling the data volume doubles Z, because √4 = 2.

Example 4.5

Using a 95% probability / 5% tolerance criterion, find the full credibility standard for claim frequency alone.

Show Solution

Here p = 0.95, so z(1+p)/2 = z0.975 = 1.960, and k = 0.05.

λ0 = (1.960/0.05)² = (39.2)² = 1536.64

Rounding up to a whole claim (since partial claims can't occur), the standard is 1,537 expected claims.

Example 4.6

A liability line has claim severities with coefficient of variation CVsev = 1.2. Using the same 95%/5% criterion as Example 4.5 (so the frequency-only standard is 1,536.64 expected claims), find (a) the full credibility standard for aggregate losses, and (b) the full credibility standard if only the average severity is being estimated from a fixed claim count.

Show Solution

CVsev² = 1.44.

(a) Aggregate standard: λ0 = 1536.64 × (1 + 1.44) = 1536.64 × 2.44 = 3749.4, rounded up to 3,750 expected claims.

(b) Severity-only standard: n0 = 1536.64 × 1.44 = 2212.76, rounded up to 2,213 claims.

Check: 1536.64 + 2212.76 = 3749.4, confirming the aggregate standard equals the sum of the frequency-only and severity-only standards, as expected from the algebraic identity noted in Section 2.1.

Example 4.7 — Fully Worked Credibility Premium

An insurer rates a book of business using a 90%/5% criterion (so z0.95 = 1.645, and the frequency-only standard is λ0 = 1082.41). The book's severity distribution has CVsev = 1.0. The book actually generated 1,200 claims during the experience period. The manual pure premium is $350 per exposure, and the observed pure premium computed from the book's own 1,200 claims is $410 per exposure. Find the credibility-weighted pure premium.

Show Solution

Step 1 — Full credibility standard (aggregate). Since both frequency and severity are being blended into a pure premium, use the aggregate standard: λ0 = 1082.41 × (1 + 1.0²) = 1082.41 × 2 = 2164.82, i.e. n0 ≈ 2,165 expected claims.

Step 2 — Partial credibility factor. The book has 1,200 actual claims against a standard of 2,165:

Z = √(1200/2165) = √0.5543 ≈ 0.7445

Step 3 — Credibility premium.

P = 0.7445(410) + 0.2555(350) = 305.25 + 89.43 = 394.67

The book is charged a pure premium of approximately $394.67 per exposure — much closer to its own observed $410 than to the $350 manual rate, since Z ≈ 0.74 places substantial weight on the book's own (fairly extensive, though not fully credible) experience.

Example 4.8

An actuary wants the full credibility standard for claim frequency to equal exactly 1,500 expected claims, using a 95% confidence level. What tolerance k does this correspond to?

Show Solution

Start from λ0 = (z/k)² with z0.975 = 1.960 and λ0 = 1500:

1500 = (1.960/k)² ⟹ √1500 = 38.7298 = 1.960/k ⟹ k = 1.960/38.7298 ≈ 0.0506

So this standard corresponds to roughly k ≈ 5.06% — i.e., with 1,500 expected claims, observed aggregate losses will fall within about 5.06% of the true mean 95% of the time.

Part IV Practice Problems

1. A manual rate is $2,500 and a policyholder's observed average loss is $2,150. The policyholder is assigned Z = 0.40. Find the credibility premium.
Show Answer
P = 0.40(2150) + 0.60(2500) = 860 + 1500 = $2,360.
2. A manual rate is $600 and observed experience is $900. The insurer ultimately charges $780. What credibility factor Z is implied?
Show Answer
780 = 600 + 300Z ⟹ 180 = 300Z ⟹ Z = 0.60.
3. A national restaurant chain and a single independent restaurant both show observed loss ratios 15 percentage points above their shared manual loss ratio. Explain, in words, why the chain would typically be assigned a higher credibility factor Z than the independent restaurant, even though the size of the deviation from manual is identical for both.
Show Answer
Credibility is driven by the volume and stability of the underlying data, not by the magnitude or direction of the observed deviation. The chain generates vastly more exposures and claims, so its observed average loss ratio is a much more precise (lower-variance) estimate of its true underlying risk level. The independent restaurant's result is based on a small, noisy sample, so relying heavily on it risks reacting to random fluctuation rather than genuine risk difference. Hence the chain clears more of the full-credibility standard and receives a higher Z, regardless of the fact that both deviations look the same size.
4. Using a 99% confidence level and a 10% tolerance, find the full credibility standard for claim frequency.
Show Answer
z0.995 = 2.576. λ0 = (2.576/0.10)² = (25.76)² = 663.58, rounded up to 664 expected claims.
5. A line of business has severity coefficient of variation CVsev = 0.8. Using a 90% confidence level and 5% tolerance (frequency-only standard λ0 = 1082.41), find the full credibility standard for aggregate losses.
Show Answer
CVsev² = 0.64. λ0,agg = 1082.41 × (1 + 0.64) = 1082.41 × 1.64 = 1775.15, rounded up to 1,776 expected claims.
6. A full credibility standard for a given book is n0 = 1,600 claims. The book actually produced 900 claims. Its manual rate is $1,000 per exposure and its own observed pure premium is $1,120 per exposure. Find Z and the resulting credibility premium.
Show Answer
Z = √(900/1600) = √0.5625 = 0.75. P = 0.75(1120) + 0.25(1000) = 840 + 250 = $1,090 per exposure.
7. Using a 95% confidence level and 2.5% tolerance, a portfolio's severity distribution has CVsev = 1.3. The portfolio produced 4,000 actual claims. The manual pure premium is $275 per exposure, and observed pure premium is $301 per exposure. Find the credibility-weighted pure premium. (Work in stages: frequency standard, then aggregate standard, then Z, then the blended premium.)
Show Answer
z0.975 = 1.960. Frequency standard: λ0 = (1.960/0.025)² = (78.4)² = 6146.56. Aggregate standard: λ0,agg = 6146.56 × (1 + 1.3²) = 6146.56 × 2.69 = 16,534.25 (≈16,535 claims). Z = √(4000/16534.25) = √0.24191 ≈ 0.4918. Premium: P = 0.4918(301) + 0.5082(275) = 148.05 + 139.76 ≈ $287.80 per exposure.
8. True or False: The full credibility standard for aggregate (pure premium) fluctuation is always at least as large as the standard for claim-frequency fluctuation alone, for the same (p, k) criterion. Justify your answer.
Show Answer
True. Since λ0,agg = λ0,freq × (1 + CVsev²) and CVsev² ≥ 0 always, the multiplier (1 + CVsev²) is always at least 1. The two standards are equal only in the degenerate case CVsev = 0 (claim severity is a constant with no variability); whenever severity has any variability at all, the aggregate standard strictly exceeds the frequency-only standard.
9. For a given (p, k) criterion, the full credibility standard for aggregate losses is found to be 2.5 times the full credibility standard for claim frequency alone. What coefficient of variation of severity does this imply?
Show Answer
λ0,agg/λ0,freq = 1 + CVsev² = 2.5 ⟹ CVsev² = 1.5 ⟹ CVsev = √1.5 ≈ 1.225.

Part V — Pricing & Reserving for Short-Term Insurance Coverages (10–15%)

Short-term (property & casualty) coverages such as auto, homeowners, and general liability create two closely related actuarial problems. First, an insurer must hold an adequate reserve for claims that have already happened but are not yet fully paid — some may not even be reported yet. Second, the insurer must set rates for future policies that are high enough to cover projected losses, expenses, and a reasonable profit margin, but not so high that the company loses business to competitors. This part builds the toolkit for both problems. Chapters 1–3 develop three reserving methods — Expected Loss Ratio, Chain-Ladder, and Bornhuetter-Ferguson — that estimate how much more will ultimately be paid on claims from a given period. Chapters 4–7 turn the same underlying ideas (development, trend, and historical data adjustment) toward the ratemaking problem: converting messy historical experience into a clean, current-cost-level estimate of future losses, and then translating that estimate into an indicated rate or rate change. Throughout, a handful of running fictional companies — Prairie Mutual Insurance for reserving and Beacon Auto Insurance Company for ratemaking — are used to keep the arithmetic connected across chapters.

1. Reserving: Expected Loss Ratio Method

Learning Outcomes Covered: Estimate unpaid claim liabilities using the Expected Loss Ratio (ELR) technique; identify the situations in which relying on an a priori loss ratio, rather than on the claims actually reported so far, is the more defensible reserving choice.

Whenever an insurer closes its books at the end of a quarter or a year, someone has to answer an uncomfortable question: how much more will we eventually have to pay on claims from accident years that are not yet fully settled? The claims already reported and paid are only part of the ultimate cost — some claims are still open and will grow before they close, and others have not even been reported yet. The gap between what has been recorded so far and what will eventually be paid is called the unpaid claim reserve, which is commonly split conceptually into a case-development piece and an incurred-but-not-reported (IBNR) piece, although most of the estimation techniques on this exam simply solve for the total unpaid amount directly.

The simplest of the three reserving techniques covered here does not look at the claims data for the period being reserved at all. Instead, it starts from an outside estimate of what the loss ratio for that period should be — the a priori expected loss ratio — and multiplies it by the earned premium for the period to get an estimate of ultimate losses.

Expected Loss Ratio MethodUltimate Losses = (A Priori Expected Loss Ratio) × (Earned Premium)
Resulting ReserveReserve = Ultimate Losses − Losses Reported (or Paid) to Date

The a priori expected loss ratio does not come from thin air — it is typically the loss ratio the company's pricing actuaries built into the rates when the policies were priced, or an average loss ratio pulled from older, more mature accident years, or an industry benchmark for a brand-new class of business the company has never written before. Because the method never looks at how the current period's claims are actually developing, it produces the same ultimate loss estimate whether the period is one month old or five years old — which is precisely its strength and its weakness at once.

The strength shows up when the data for a period is extremely immature: a brand-new line of business, or an accident year that closed only a month ago, simply does not have enough reported claims for the actual experience to be a trustworthy guide. A handful of large early claims (or the accidental absence of any) can make a young accident year's reported loss ratio wildly unrepresentative of where it will eventually land. In that setting, substituting a stable, pre-set expectation avoids overreacting to statistical noise. The weakness is the flip side: as a period matures and real claims experience accumulates, continuing to ignore that experience means the reserve estimate never learns anything from what has actually happened. This tension — trust the a priori assumption, or trust the emerging data — is exactly what the Bornhuetter-Ferguson method in Chapter 3 is built to resolve, by blending the two rather than picking one exclusively.

A common exam trap is forgetting that the ELR method's ultimate loss estimate is completely insensitive to the reported-to-date figure — that figure is used only after the ultimate is computed, to back into the reserve. If a problem gives you reported losses and asks for the ELR ultimate, the reported figure is a distractor for that first step.
Example 5.1

Prairie Mutual Insurance launches a new small commercial cyber-liability endorsement in accident year 2024. Earned premium for the new endorsement in AY2024 is $2,600,000, and the pricing department's built-in target loss ratio for this coverage is 65%. At the first evaluation, 12 months after the start of the accident year, only $1,250,000 of losses have been reported. Estimate the ultimate losses and the resulting reserve using the Expected Loss Ratio method.

Show Solution

Ultimate Losses = 0.65 × $2,600,000 = $1,690,000.
Reserve = $1,690,000 − $1,250,000 = $440,000.
Because this endorsement is brand new and only 12 months of data exist, the actuary has little confidence that $1,250,000 reported so far is representative of where the year will ultimately land, so an a priori estimate anchored to the pricing target is preferred over projecting the thin actual data forward. (This same AY2024 block of business is revisited in Chapters 2 and 3 using the chain-ladder and Bornhuetter-Ferguson methods, so the three approaches can be compared side by side.)

Example 5.2

A regional insurer enters the equine mortality insurance market for the first time. Earned premium in the first accident year is $4,000,000. Since the company has no history in this niche, its actuaries adopt an industry-benchmark loss ratio of 58% as the a priori assumption. Only $500,000 of losses have been reported so far. Find the ELR ultimate loss estimate and the reserve.

Show Solution

Ultimate Losses = 0.58 × $4,000,000 = $2,320,000.
Reserve = $2,320,000 − $500,000 = $1,820,000.
Note how large the reserve is relative to what has been reported — $500,000 reported versus a $2,320,000 ultimate. This is typical of a first-year, first-evaluation ELR estimate for a genuinely new line: almost all of the eventual cost is still sitting in the reserve rather than in reported claims.

Example 5.3

An actuary reviewing a specialty inland-marine program sets the a priori expected loss ratio equal to the loss & loss-adjustment-expense ratio that was filed with the regulator when the current rates were approved: 62%. Earned premium for the accident year is $1,800,000, and $200,000 of losses have been reported to date. Compute the ELR ultimate and reserve, and briefly note one alternative source the actuary could have used for the a priori loss ratio instead of the rate filing.

Show Solution

Ultimate Losses = 0.62 × $1,800,000 = $1,116,000.
Reserve = $1,116,000 − $200,000 = $916,000.
Besides the rate filing's target loss ratio, the actuary could instead have used the average ultimate loss ratio observed in several prior, fully-matured accident years for the same program (adjusted for any known trend or rate changes since then) as the a priori assumption.

2. Reserving: Chain-Ladder Method

Learning Outcomes Covered: Construct a cumulative loss development triangle; compute volume-weighted age-to-age (link) development factors and chain them into age-to-ultimate factors; apply those factors to project each accident year's losses to an ultimate value and derive the corresponding reserve.

Where the Expected Loss Ratio method deliberately ignores the claims data on hand, the chain-ladder method goes to the opposite extreme: it estimates ultimate losses purely from the pattern by which the company's own claims have historically grown from one evaluation date to the next, applied to however much has been reported for the accident year in question. The raw material for this method is a loss development triangle: a table with accident years running down the rows and maturity ("development age," typically in months since the start of the accident year) running across the columns, with cumulative reported (or paid) losses filling each cell. It is called a triangle because older accident years have had time to be observed at more maturities than younger ones, so the bottom-left of the table is empty — that empty region is exactly what the method is trying to fill in.

Always confirm whether a triangle shows cumulative losses (the running total as of each evaluation date) or incremental losses (only the amount added since the prior evaluation). Age-to-age factors are computed from cumulative values. If you are handed an incremental triangle, first convert it to cumulative by summing across each row before computing any development factors — dividing incremental figures directly will give a meaningless ratio.

Age-to-Age (Link) Development Factors

An age-to-age factor measures, on average across all accident years for which both maturities are observed, how much cumulative losses grow from one evaluation age to the next. The standard exam approach is the volume-weighted average: sum the cumulative losses at the older age across every accident year that has reached that age, sum the cumulative losses at the corresponding younger age for those same years, and divide.

Volume-Weighted Age-to-Age Factorf(k, k+1) = Σ Ci, k+1 / Σ Ci, k (sum over all accident years i observed at both ages k and k+1)
The volume-weighted average automatically gives more influence to larger accident years, since it sums dollars before dividing rather than averaging each year's individual ratio with equal weight. A simple (unweighted) average of the individual yearly ratios is a different, less common alternative — always check which one a problem is asking for, since they generally give slightly different factors.

Once every age-to-age factor is computed, they are chained together — multiplied in sequence — to build an age-to-ultimate factor, often called the cumulative development factor (CDF), for each maturity. The CDF at a given age is the product of every age-to-age factor from that age through to the final observed maturity, further multiplied by a tail factor if the company's experience shows that losses keep developing even after the last age shown in the triangle.

Age-to-Ultimate (Cumulative Development) FactorCDFk = f(k,k+1) × f(k+1,k+2) × ⋯ × f(last age, last age+1) × (tail factor, if any)
Projected Ultimate LossesUltimatei = (Latest Cumulative Losses for accident year i) × CDF(latest age for year i)
Chain-Ladder ReserveReservei = Ultimatei − (Latest Cumulative Losses for accident year i)

Worked Triangle: Prairie Mutual Insurance, General Liability

Prairie Mutual's general liability book has four accident years of data as of the most recent evaluation, with cumulative reported losses (in $000s) evaluated every 12 months as follows.

Table 5.1 — Cumulative Reported Losses ($000s) by Accident Year and Development Age
Accident Year12 months24 months36 months48 months
20219401,4101,5501,585
20221,0201,5501,700—
20231,1501,730——
20241,250———

Step 1 — 12-to-24 factor (years 2021, 2022, 2023 all reach both ages): (1,410 + 1,550 + 1,730) / (940 + 1,020 + 1,150) = 4,690 / 3,110 = 1.508.

Step 2 — 24-to-36 factor (years 2021, 2022 reach both ages): (1,550 + 1,700) / (1,410 + 1,550) = 3,250 / 2,960 = 1.098.

Step 3 — 36-to-48 factor (year 2021 only): 1,585 / 1,550 = 1.023.

Step 4 — Tail factor: based on industry data for similar general liability books, Prairie Mutual's actuary assumes an additional 1.0% of development remains after 48 months, so the tail factor from 48 months to ultimate is 1.010.

Table 5.2 — Age-to-Age Factors and Cumulative Development Factors (CDFs)
Age IntervalAge-to-Age FactorAgeCDF to Ultimate
12 → 241.50812 months1.508 × 1.098 × 1.023 × 1.010 = 1.711
24 → 361.09824 months1.098 × 1.023 × 1.010 = 1.134
36 → 481.02336 months1.023 × 1.010 = 1.033
48 → Ultimate1.010 (tail)48 months1.010

Applying the appropriate CDF to each accident year's latest diagonal (its most recent cumulative value) produces the projected ultimate losses and reserve.

Table 5.3 — Chain-Ladder Projected Ultimate Losses and Reserve ($000s)
Accident YearLatest Cumulative LossesAgeCDFProjected UltimateReserve
20211,58548 mo1.0101,60116
20221,70036 mo1.0331,75656
