Total6,2657,4581,193

Notice the pattern: the youngest accident year (2024), which is only 12 months mature, is developed by the largest factor (1.711) and correspondingly carries by far the largest reserve relative to what has been reported so far — its ultimate is projected to be 71% higher than its current reported figure. The oldest year (2021), at 48 months, only needs the small tail factor of 1.010, so almost all of its ultimate cost is already reflected in reported losses. This is the chain-ladder method's central mechanism: young, thin accident years are leveraged up heavily by a large development factor, which is exactly the feature that Chapter 3's Bornhuetter-Ferguson method is designed to temper.

Example 5.4

Two accident years for a small commercial auto book have the following cumulative losses at 12 and 24 months (in $000s): Year A: 400 at 12 months, 610 at 24 months. Year B: 450 at 12 months, 690 at 24 months. Compute the volume-weighted 12-to-24 age-to-age factor.

Show Solution

f(12,24) = (610 + 690) / (400 + 450) = 1,300 / 850 = 1.529.

Example 5.5

A liability line has the following age-to-age factors: 24-to-36 is 1.15, 36-to-48 is 1.05, and the company assumes no further development past 48 months (tail factor = 1.00). An accident year is currently at 24 months of maturity with cumulative reported losses of $800,000. Project its ultimate losses.

Show Solution

CDF24 = 1.15 × 1.05 × 1.00 = 1.2075.
Ultimate = $800,000 × 1.2075 = $966,000.

Example 5.6

Using Table 5.1's Prairie Mutual triangle above, verify the 24-to-36 age-to-age factor and use it, together with the 36-to-48 factor and the 1.010 tail factor, to independently confirm accident year 2022's projected ultimate loss shown in Table 5.3.

Show Solution

24-to-36 factor (years 2021 and 2022 both observed at 24 and 36 months): (1,550 + 1,700) / (1,410 + 1,550) = 3,250 / 2,960 = 1.098.
AY2022 is at 36 months of maturity, so its CDF is the product of the 36-to-48 factor and the tail: CDF36 = 1.023 × 1.010 = 1.033.
Ultimate2022 = 1,700 × 1.033 = 1,756 ($000s), matching Table 5.3.

3. Reserving: Bornhuetter-Ferguson Method

Learning Outcomes Covered: Apply the Bornhuetter-Ferguson (BF) technique to estimate ultimate losses and IBNR by blending a priori expected losses with actual reported-to-date losses; explain why this blend reduces the chain-ladder method's over-sensitivity to immature, thinly-developed accident years.

The chain-ladder method has one uncomfortable property, visible clearly in Table 5.3 above: for the least mature accident year, the entire ultimate estimate hinges on multiplying a relatively small reported figure by a large, uncertain development factor (1.711 for AY2024). If that single 12-month reported figure happens to be unusually high or low — plausible with so little claims volume reported — the projected ultimate swings by a large dollar amount. The Expected Loss Ratio method from Chapter 1 has the opposite problem: it never updates at all, no matter how much real information accumulates. The Bornhuetter-Ferguson method, named for its two originators, blends the two ideas so that immature years lean more on the stable a priori assumption while mature years lean more on their own actual emerging experience.

The key insight is to split the ultimate loss estimate into two pieces: the amount already reported (which is taken as known and fully credible, since it already happened), plus an estimate of the IBNR that has not yet emerged. Rather than getting that IBNR piece by trending the reported losses upward (as chain-ladder does), BF estimates it directly from the a priori expected losses, scaled down by the percentage of losses still expected to be unreported at the current maturity.

Percentage of Ultimate Losses Still Unreported% Unreported = 1 − 1/CDF
Bornhuetter-Ferguson IBNRIBNRBF = (A Priori Expected Losses) × (1 − 1/CDF)
Bornhuetter-Ferguson Ultimate LossesUltimateBF = (Actual Losses to Date) + (A Priori Expected Losses) × (1 − 1/CDF)

Where "A Priori Expected Losses" is computed exactly as in Chapter 1 — an a priori loss ratio multiplied by earned premium — and CDF is the same chain-ladder cumulative development factor from Chapter 2 for the accident year's current maturity. The 1/CDF term represents the fraction of ultimate losses that the development pattern says should already be reported by this maturity, so (1 − 1/CDF) is the complementary fraction still unreported. A very young accident year has a large CDF, so 1/CDF is small and almost all of the a priori expected losses flow through as IBNR — the method behaves almost like pure ELR. A very mature accident year has a CDF near 1.0, so 1/CDF is close to 1, almost none of the a priori estimate is used, and the method converges to something very close to the chain-ladder answer, since virtually all of its ultimate is already reported.

Think of (1 − 1/CDF) as a "percentage unreported" weight applied only to the a priori assumption — it is never applied to actual reported losses, which always enter the BF ultimate at their full, unadjusted value. A frequent scoring mistake is applying the factor to the wrong piece of the formula.

Worked Triangle: Bornhuetter-Ferguson for Prairie Mutual

Reusing Prairie Mutual's general liability triangle from Chapter 2, suppose the a priori expected loss ratio is a constant 65% for every accident year (consistent with Example 5.1's pricing target), applied to each year's earned premium.

Table 5.4 — Bornhuetter-Ferguson Ultimate Loss Projection ($000s)
AYEarned PremiumA Priori ELRExpected LossesActual to DateCDF% UnreportedBF IBNRBF Ultimate

Sample calculation for AY2024: % unreported = 1 − 1/1.711 = 1 − 0.5845 = 0.4155 (41.55%). IBNR = $1,690 × 0.4155 = $702 (rounded, $000s). Ultimate = $1,250 + $702 = $1,952.

Comparing the two methods side by side is the whole point of this worked example: chain-ladder projected AY2024's ultimate at $2,139 (Table 5.3), while BF projects $1,952 — noticeably lower, because chain-ladder's 12-month reported figure of $1,250 gets leveraged by the full 1.711 factor, while BF instead trusts the a priori 65% loss ratio for the still-unreported 41.55% of the year and only lets the actual $1,250 reported flow through at full value. By contrast, AY2021 — nearly fully mature — gives almost identical answers under both methods ($1,601 chain-ladder versus $1,598 BF), exactly as the convergence argument above predicts. Across all four years, BF's total reserve ($945) is meaningfully lower than chain-ladder's ($1,193), driven almost entirely by the difference in the youngest, most leveraged year.

Example 5.7

A newly-reported accident year has actual losses to date of $200,000, a CDF to ultimate of 3.00 (very immature — only the first evaluation), and a priori expected losses of $900,000. Compute the BF IBNR and BF ultimate.

Show Solution

% unreported = 1 − 1/3.00 = 1 − 0.3333 = 0.6667.
IBNR = $900,000 × 0.6667 = $600,000.
Ultimate = $200,000 + $600,000 = $800,000. Note that this is far below the chain-ladder answer of $200,000 × 3.00 = $600,000 (wait — compare carefully: chain-ladder gives $600,000, BF gives $800,000, so here BF is actually the higher of the two estimates. BF does not always sit below chain-ladder; it sits between chain-ladder and the pure a-priori estimate, and lands above chain-ladder whenever the a priori expected losses imply a richer ultimate than the thin actual data alone would suggest.)

Example 5.8

A well-seasoned accident year has actual losses to date of $1,400,000 and a CDF of only 1.05 (nearly fully mature). A priori expected losses are $1,800,000. Find the BF ultimate and compare it to the chain-ladder ultimate.

Show Solution

% unreported = 1 − 1/1.05 = 1 − 0.9524 = 0.0476.
IBNR = $1,800,000 × 0.0476 = $85,714 ≈ $85,700.
BF Ultimate = $1,400,000 + $85,700 = $1,485,700.
Chain-ladder ultimate = $1,400,000 × 1.05 = $1,470,000.
The two answers are close (within about 1%), illustrating that for mature accident years the choice between chain-ladder and BF matters far less than it does for immature years like Example 5.7.

Example 5.9

Using Table 5.4, compute the total BF reserve for all of Prairie Mutual's general liability accident years combined, and state which single accident year drives most of the difference between the BF total reserve and the chain-ladder total reserve from Chapter 2.

Show Solution

Total BF reserve = Total BF Ultimate − Total Actual to Date = $7,210 − $6,265 = $945 ($000s), matching the sum of the individual IBNR values (13+46+184+702=945).
The chain-ladder total reserve was $1,193 ($000s, Table 5.3). The $248 difference ($1,193 − $945) is driven almost entirely by AY2024, where chain-ladder's reserve of $889 is far above BF's IBNR of $702 — the other three years' reserves are close to identical under both methods.

4. Ratemaking Fundamentals & Data

Learning Outcomes Covered: State the overall goal of ratemaking and identify the components a rate must cover; select an appropriate exposure base for a given coverage; distinguish accident-year, policy-year, and calendar-year data organization and identify which is appropriate for a given analytical purpose.

Ratemaking is the process of setting the price the company will charge for a future policy period. Unlike many other pricing problems, the insurer does not know its "cost of goods" at the time it sells the product — the losses a policy will generate are only discovered, sometimes years, after the premium was collected. The overarching goal of ratemaking is straightforward to state even though it is hard to execute well: the rates charged for a future period should be sufficient, in the aggregate, to cover the losses that period will generate, the expenses of acquiring and servicing the business, and a reasonable allowance for profit and the risk the company is taking on — no more, and no less, since either persistent overcharging or persistent undercharging eventually damages the company's competitive position or its solvency.

Exposure Bases

To turn a book of business into rates, the actuary needs a consistent unit of risk exposure to divide by — something that scales roughly proportionally with expected loss potential, is objectively measurable, and is not itself distorted by rate changes. Typical exposure bases include:

  • Private passenger auto — car-years (or car-months) of coverage in force
  • Homeowners — house-years, sometimes scaled by amount of insurance
  • Workers compensation — payroll, usually expressed per $100 of payroll
  • General liability — sales revenue or payroll, depending on the classification, again typically per $100
  • Commercial property — amount of insurance in force

A subtlety worth internalizing: premium itself is almost never used as an exposure base, even though it is readily available and obviously related to the size of the risk. The reason is that premium changes whenever rates change, so using it as the exposure denominator would silently mix a true change in the amount of risk insured together with a pure pricing decision, contaminating the very rate indication the actuary is trying to compute.

Example 5.10

Beacon Auto Insurance Company is designing a rate review for its commercial delivery-van fleet program. An analyst proposes using each fleet's total annual premium as the exposure base, reasoning that bigger fleets pay more premium and so premium is a fine proxy for exposure to loss. Explain why an experienced ratemaking actuary would reject this proposal.

Show Solution

Premium reflects both the amount of risk insured and whatever rate level was in effect when it was charged. If Beacon raised rates 10% last year, premium for an unchanged fleet would rise 10% with no change in actual exposure to loss, which would distort any loss-ratio-style analysis built on that base. Vehicle-years (or vehicle-months) is a better exposure base because it is a physical count that is unaffected by the level of rates the company happens to be charging.

Example 5.11

Compute the number of car-years of exposure generated by two policies: Policy X, in force for a full 12 months, and Policy Y, in force for exactly 6 months before cancellation.

Show Solution

Policy X contributes 12/12 = 1.0 car-year. Policy Y contributes 6/12 = 0.5 car-year. Combined, the two policies generate 1.5 car-years of exposure — exposures are additive and can be fractional, which is why "car-years" rather than "number of policies" is the standard unit.

Accident Year, Policy Year, and Calendar Year Data

The same underlying premiums and losses can be sliced into summary statistics in three fundamentally different ways, and picking the wrong one for a given analytical question is a classic source of error.

  • Calendar year (CY) data captures whatever was earned or paid/reported during a fixed twelve-month calendar window, regardless of when the underlying policy was written or when the loss occurred. Calendar-year figures are final the moment the period ends — they never change retroactively — which makes them convenient for financial reporting, but they mix together losses from many different accident dates and are not well suited to answering "how much will accident year 2024 ultimately cost?"
  • Accident year (AY) data groups every loss by the calendar date on which the covered event actually occurred, and matches it against the premium earned by all policies in force at that time, aggregated over an entire year of accident dates. Accident-year figures are exactly what feeds the loss development triangles of Chapters 2 and 3, because losses assigned to an accident year continue to be revised (case reserves changed, new claims reported) for years after the accident year itself has ended.
  • Policy year (PY) data groups every premium dollar and every loss dollar by the year in which the generating policy was written, no matter when within the policy's term the premium was earned or the loss occurred. A policy year is not considered complete (fully "closed off" for the purpose of first assembling its data) until the last policy written in that year, which could have been written on the final day of the year, has finished its entire term — so policy-year data becomes available to analyze later than accident-year data for the same underlying business.
A single loss event can appear in three different "years" simultaneously under the three systems. A loss occurring on February 1, 2025 under a 12-month policy that incepted on April 1, 2024 belongs to accident year 2025 (date of loss), calendar year 2025 (date recorded), and policy year 2024 (date the policy was written) all at once. Exam problems frequently test whether you can correctly place a single loss into each of the three systems.

Because policy-year data ties each loss cleanly back to the exact rates, forms, and underwriting rules in force when the generating policy was sold, it is the natural choice when the goal is to evaluate the performance of a specific rate program as a self-contained unit. Accident-year data, by contrast, is generally preferred for reserving work and is also common in ratemaking because it becomes available to analyze sooner than policy-year data and still avoids calendar year's problem of blending many different accident dates together. Calendar-year (or the closely related calendar-accident-year hybrid) figures are mainly used where a fixed evaluation cutoff is required, such as regulatory financial statements.

Example 5.12

A policy is written on April 1, 2023 for a 12-month term with level (unchanging) annual premium of $1,200. No mid-term endorsements occur. Determine how this policy's premium is split between calendar year 2023 and calendar year 2024 under calendar-year earned premium accounting, and state which single policy year and which single accident year(s) the policy could contribute losses to.

Show Solution

The policy is in force April 1, 2023 – March 31, 2024. Under calendar-year accounting, premium is earned pro-rata over the term: 9 of the 12 months (April–December 2023) fall in CY2023, earning 9/12 × $1,200 = $900 in CY2023, and the remaining 3 months (January–March 2024) earn 3/12 × $1,200 = $300 in CY2024.
The entire policy, however, belongs to a single policy year: PY2023, since that is when it was written — its full $1,200 premium and all resulting losses are grouped there together regardless of the calendar dates involved.
Losses from this policy could fall in accident year 2023 (if the loss date is April–December 2023) or accident year 2024 (if the loss date is January–March 2024), so a single policy can straddle two different accident years even though it belongs to only one policy year.

5. Ratemaking Adjustments: Development, Trend & On-Leveling

Learning Outcomes Covered: Apply loss development factors to bring immature historical losses to an ultimate basis for ratemaking; trend historical losses and premiums forward to the future policy period using a compound trend factor; restate historical premium at current rate level ("on-leveling") using a weighted rate-level-index approach; explain why all three adjustments are needed before historical experience can be used to set future rates.

Historical premium and loss data are never directly usable for setting future rates — they need three separate corrections first, each addressing a different way in which the raw historical numbers fail to represent the future period the new rates will apply to. This chapter builds Beacon Auto Insurance Company's AY2023 private passenger auto liability experience through all three adjustments; Chapters 6 and 7 then carry the same numbers forward into a complete rate indication.

Development: Bringing Immature Losses to an Ultimate Basis

The historical accident year being used for ratemaking is frequently still immature at the time of the rate review — exactly the situation Chapter 2's chain-ladder machinery was built for. The same loss development factors computed from a triangle of past accident years are applied here, for the identical reason: reported losses understate what the accident year will ultimately cost, so a development factor scales them up to an estimated ultimate basis before any further ratemaking adjustment is applied.

Developed (Ultimate) Losses for RatemakingDeveloped Losses = (Reported Losses at Evaluation) × (Loss Development Factor to Ultimate)
Example 5.13

Beacon Auto's AY2023 private passenger auto liability experience shows $6,000,000 of reported losses at the evaluation date used for this rate review. A companion development triangle (built the same way as Chapter 2's Prairie Mutual example) indicates a loss development factor to ultimate of 1.15 for losses at this maturity. Compute the developed ultimate losses.

Show Solution

Developed Losses = $6,000,000 × 1.15 = $6,900,000.

Trend: Projecting to the Future Policy Period

Even a fully-developed historical ultimate loss figure reflects the cost of claims as they occurred years ago, under the economic conditions, medical costs, repair costs, and claims-handling environment of that earlier period. Since the new rates will apply to losses that have not happened yet, the developed historical losses must be trended forward — typically using an exponential (compound) growth factor — from the historical period's average date of loss to the future period's average date of loss.

Trend FactorTrend Factor = (1 + annual trend rate)n, where n = number of years between the average historical loss date and the average future loss date

Beacon Auto's AY2023 losses have an average date of loss of roughly July 1, 2023 (the midpoint of the accident year). The new rates, once approved, are expected to be in effect for policies incepting January 1, 2026 through December 31, 2026 and written for a 12-month term, giving those policies an average date of loss of roughly January 1, 2027 (a standard approximation: the midpoint of the period during which losses on policies sold across the rate-effective year will occur). The trend period is therefore 3.5 years, from July 1, 2023 to January 1, 2027.

Example 5.14

Using an annual trend rate of 5% and a trend period of 3.5 years, trend Beacon Auto's Example 5.13 developed ultimate losses of $6,900,000 forward to the future policy period.

Show Solution

Trend Factor = 1.053.5. Since 1.053 = 1.157625 and 1.050.5 ≈ 1.024695, the trend factor = 1.157625 × 1.024695 ≈ 1.18621.
Trended (Developed) Losses = $6,900,000 × 1.18621 ≈ $8,184,900.
This $8,184,900 figure — developed and trended — is what Chapter 7 will use as the projected future loss level for Beacon Auto's rate indication.

A frequent error is trending from the wrong starting date, or trending an already-current figure a second time. Trend is always measured between two average dates of loss (or, for expenses that scale with claim cost rather than claim count, sometimes two average payment dates) — not between two evaluation dates, and not between two calendar year-ends. Always identify the average date of loss for the historical period and the average date of loss for the future period explicitly before computing n.

On-Leveling: Restating Historical Premium at Current Rate Level

Premium has an analogous, but distinct, problem. If Beacon Auto changed its rates partway through — or even before — the historical experience period, the earned premium recorded for that period reflects a blend of old and new rate levels, not the rate level that will actually be charged going forward. Simply trending the loss side and leaving premium alone would compare future-cost-level losses against a premium base that is not stated at any single consistent rate level, which would bias the resulting loss ratio. On-leveling restates historical premium as if it had all been collected at today's current rate level.

The standard technique (often illustrated with a parallelogram diagram, though the diagram itself is not required to perform the calculation) works by determining what fraction of the historical premium was actually earned at each historical rate level, based on when rate changes took effect relative to the policies' terms, and then computing a weighted-average "earned rate level index" for the period. Dividing the current rate level index by that weighted-average earned index gives the on-level factor.

On-Level FactorOn-Level Factor = (Current Rate Level Index) / (Weighted-Average Rate Level Index Actually Earned During the Historical Period)
On-Level PremiumOn-Level Premium = (Historical Earned Premium) × (On-Level Factor)
Example 5.15

Beacon Auto's AY2023 earned premium is $10,000,000. A single rate change of +6% took effect during the year; based on the timing of that change relative to policy terms, the actuary determines that 78% of AY2023's earned premium was earned at the old rate level (index 1.000) and the remaining 22% was earned at the new, current rate level (index 1.060). Compute the on-level factor and the on-level premium.

Show Solution

Weighted-average earned index = 0.78 × 1.000 + 0.22 × 1.060 = 0.78 + 0.2332 = 1.0132.
On-Level Factor = 1.060 / 1.0132 ≈ 1.0462.
On-Level Premium = $10,000,000 × 1.0462 ≈ $10,461,900.
Because the current rate level (1.060) is above the blended historical level actually earned (1.0132), the on-level factor exceeds 1.0 and pushes historical premium upward to what it would have been had every policy in AY2023 been charged at today's rates.

All three adjustments are necessary and address different problems: development corrects for claims that have not yet been reported, trend corrects for the fact that future losses will cost more (or less) than past losses due to inflation and other cost drivers, and on-leveling corrects for the fact that historical premium was not collected at a single, consistent, current rate level. Skipping any one of the three leaves the resulting loss ratio biased.

6. Expenses & Profit Loading

Learning Outcomes Covered: Distinguish fixed and variable expenses in a rate; compute the permissible loss ratio from the variable expense provision and the target profit and contingencies provision; incorporate fixed expenses directly into the indicated rate.

A rate has to cover more than just claims. Every policy sold carries acquisition and servicing costs — commissions, premium taxes, underwriting expense, claims-handling overhead — and the company also needs to earn a reasonable return for the capital it holds to back the risk it is taking on. Ratemaking splits expenses into two behaviorally different categories.

  • Variable expenses scale (roughly) proportionally with premium — commissions and premium taxes are the classic examples, since both are typically calculated as a flat percentage of the premium charged.
  • Fixed expenses do not scale with premium — they are closer to a flat dollar cost per policy issued or serviced, such as the cost of processing an application, running a motor vehicle report, or issuing policy documents, regardless of how large or small that policy's premium happens to be.

Because variable expenses are, by definition, a percentage of premium, they can be built into the rate as a simple percentage load. The permissible loss ratio is the share of every premium dollar left over for losses (and loss adjustment expense) after the variable-expense percentage and the target profit-and-contingencies percentage have both been carved out.

Permissible Loss RatioPermissible Loss Ratio = 1 − (Variable Expense Ratio) − (Target Profit & Contingencies Provision)
Example 5.16

Beacon Auto's private passenger auto liability rate includes a 10% commission provision, a 3% premium tax, and 2% of additional variable general expense, for a total variable expense ratio of 15%. The company's target profit & contingencies provision is 5%. Compute the permissible loss ratio.

Show Solution

Permissible Loss Ratio = 1 − 0.15 − 0.05 = 0.80, i.e., 80%. This means 80 cents of every premium dollar is available to pay losses and loss adjustment expense while still leaving room for the targeted variable expenses and profit margin — Chapter 7 uses this exact 80% figure to complete Beacon Auto's rate indication.

Fixed expenses cannot be folded into a simple percentage-of-premium provision the same way, because a flat dollar cost represents a much larger percentage load on a small policy than on a large one. Instead, fixed expenses are typically estimated on a per-exposure basis and added directly to the projected pure premium (projected losses per exposure) before dividing by the permissible-loss-ratio-style denominator, so that they are recovered dollar-for-dollar per exposure rather than as a percentage that would otherwise overcharge large policies and undercharge small ones.

Indicated Rate, Including a Fixed Expense ProvisionIndicated Rate = (Projected Pure Premium + Fixed Expense per Exposure) / (1 − Variable Expense Ratio − Profit & Contingencies Provision)
Example 5.17

A commercial umbrella program has a projected pure premium of $120 per exposure and a fixed expense provision of $15 per exposure (policy issuance and underwriting review costs). The variable expense ratio is 17% and the target profit provision is 4%. Compute the indicated rate per exposure.

Show Solution

Denominator = 1 − 0.17 − 0.04 = 0.79.
Indicated Rate = ($120 + $15) / 0.79 = $135 / 0.79 ≈ $170.89 per exposure.

Example 5.18

Fixed expenses, like losses, are stated in future dollars, not in the dollars of the historical period on which they were estimated — so they, too, need a trend adjustment, generally using a unit-cost trend appropriate to the expense category rather than the loss trend. A company's current fixed expense estimate is $15.00 per exposure. If fixed expenses are trending upward at 3% per year and the projected rate-effective period is 2.0 years after the current estimate, find the trended fixed expense provision.

Show Solution

Trended Fixed Expense = $15.00 × 1.032 = $15.00 × 1.0609 ≈ $15.91 per exposure.

Do not assume every expense category is either purely fixed or purely variable in every problem — some study problems will describe a "partially variable, partially fixed" expense and ask you to split it before computing the permissible loss ratio and the per-exposure fixed load separately. Read the expense description carefully rather than defaulting to a standard percentage split.

7. Rate Level Indications: Loss Cost & Loss Ratio Methods

Learning Outcomes Covered: Calculate an indicated overall rate change using the loss ratio method; calculate an indicated rate using the pure premium (loss cost) method; combine development, trend, on-leveling, and the permissible loss ratio into a single end-to-end rate indication.

Everything built in Chapters 4 through 6 comes together here. There are two standard, closely related ways of turning developed-and-trended losses and on-level premium into an actual rate recommendation: the loss ratio method, which produces an indicated percentage change to current rates, and the pure premium (loss cost) method, which builds an indicated rate from the ground up on a per-exposure basis. When set up consistently — in particular, when every expense is treated as variable in both methods, or fixed expenses are handled identically in both — the two methods algebraically reconcile to the same indicated percentage change, so they are best thought of as two lenses on the same underlying calculation rather than as competing answers.

The Loss Ratio Method

Experience Loss RatioExperience Loss Ratio = (Developed & Trended Ultimate Losses) / (On-Level Earned Premium)
Indicated Rate Change — Loss Ratio MethodIndicated % Rate Change = (Experience Loss Ratio) / (Permissible Loss Ratio) − 1

The intuition is direct: if the actual, fully-adjusted loss ratio the company is experiencing is higher than the loss ratio the current rates were designed to permit, rates are inadequate and need to rise by the percentage gap between the two; if the experience loss ratio is below the permissible loss ratio, current rates are more than adequate and a decrease is indicated.

Example 5.19

A homeowners program has an experience loss ratio (developed, trended losses over on-level premium) of 68%, and the permissible loss ratio built into current rates is 72%. Compute the indicated rate change.

Show Solution

Indicated % Change = 0.68 / 0.72 − 1 = 0.9444 − 1 = −0.0556, i.e., a rate decrease of about 5.6%.

The Pure Premium (Loss Cost) Method

Rather than expressing everything as a ratio to premium, the pure premium method works directly in per-exposure dollar terms — often preferred when a company writes at multiple, changing rate levels across different distribution channels or territories, since it does not require restating premium onto a single common level for every calculation, only for the final comparison step.

Projected Pure PremiumProjected Pure Premium = (Developed & Trended Ultimate Losses) / (Number of Exposures)
Indicated Rate — Pure Premium MethodIndicated Rate = (Projected Pure Premium) / (Permissible Loss Ratio) [+ Fixed Expense per Exposure, handled as in Chapter 6, if applicable]
Indicated Rate Change — Pure Premium MethodIndicated % Rate Change = (Indicated Rate) / (Current Average On-Level Premium per Exposure) − 1
Example 5.20

A specialty marine program has a projected pure premium of $95 per exposure, no separately-loaded fixed expense provision (all expenses are treated as variable for this simplified rate review), and a permissible loss ratio of 70%. The current average on-level premium is $150 per exposure. Compute the indicated rate and the indicated percentage rate change.

Show Solution

Indicated Rate = $95 / 0.70 ≈ $135.71 per exposure.
Indicated % Change = $135.71 / $150 − 1 = 0.9048 − 1 = −0.0952, a decrease of about 9.5%.

Full Worked Rate Indication: Beacon Auto Insurance Company

Pulling together every adjustment from Chapters 5 and 6 for Beacon Auto's AY2023 private passenger auto liability review: developed-and-trended ultimate losses were $8,184,900 (Example 5.14), on-level earned premium was $10,461,900 (Example 5.15), the permissible loss ratio is 80% (Example 5.16), and the number of exposures is 40,000 car-years.

Table 5.5 — Beacon Auto AY2023 Rate Indication, Both Methods
ItemLoss Ratio MethodPure Premium Method
Developed & trended losses$8,184,900
Exposures—40,000
Pure premium—$8,184,900 / 40,000 = $204.62
On-level premium$10,461,900—
Current avg. on-level premium per exposure—$10,461,900 / 40,000 = $261.55
Experience loss ratio$8,184,900 / $10,461,900 = 78.24%—
Indicated rate—$204.62 / 0.80 = $255.78
Permissible loss ratio80%80% (used above)
Indicated rate change78.24% / 80% − 1 = −2.2%$255.78 / $261.55 − 1 = −2.2%

Both methods land on the same indicated change: an overall rate decrease of approximately 2.2%. This is not a coincidence — with fixed expenses excluded from this simplified example, the loss ratio method's formula and the pure premium method's formula are algebraically identical once the exposures cancel out, which is exactly why the two methods are described as two views of one calculation rather than independent cross-checks. In practice, actuaries often compute both anyway: the loss ratio method is usually more convenient when reliable current-level premium is easy to obtain, while the pure premium method is often preferred for classification-level ratemaking, brand-new coverages with little historical premium to on-level, or lines where a fixed per-exposure expense load needs to be handled explicitly and separately from the variable-expense percentage.

When a rate indication problem gives you both an experience loss ratio and enough information to compute pure premium and current average premium, the two methods should reconcile (subject to rounding) whenever fixed expenses are handled the same way in both — a fast way to sanity-check your own arithmetic on a multi-step problem is to compute the answer both ways and confirm they roughly agree.

Part V Practice Problems

1. Meridian Specialty Re launches a parametric weather-index product in AY2024. Earned premium is $5,000,000 and the a priori expected loss ratio, based on the pricing model, is 55%. Only $800,000 of losses have been reported to date. Estimate the ultimate losses and the reserve using the Expected Loss Ratio method.
Show Answer
Ultimate = 0.55 × $5,000,000 = $2,750,000. Reserve = $2,750,000 − $800,000 = $1,950,000.
2. A newly-licensed insurer's first-year travel insurance line has earned premium of $3,000,000 and an a priori expected loss ratio of 60%, drawn from an industry benchmark since the company has no history of its own. Reported losses to date are $150,000. Find the ELR ultimate and reserve, and explain briefly why the ELR method — rather than chain-ladder — is the appropriate choice here.
Show Answer
Ultimate = 0.60 × $3,000,000 = $1,800,000. Reserve = $1,800,000 − $150,000 = $1,650,000. Because this is a first-year line with essentially no claims history of its own, there is no credible internal development pattern to use for a chain-ladder projection, and the small reported figure to date is too thin to be trusted on its own — an outside a priori assumption is the more defensible starting point.
3. Two accident years of a small package policy line show cumulative losses at 12 and 24 months (in $000s): Year A — 500 at 12 months, 760 at 24 months; Year B — 600 at 12 months, 930 at 24 months. Compute the volume-weighted 12-to-24 age-to-age factor.
Show Answer
f = (760 + 930) / (500 + 600) = 1,690 / 1,100 = 1.536.
4. A three-year triangle (cumulative losses, $000s) shows: AY1 — 800 at 12mo, 1,200 at 24mo, 1,320 at 36mo. AY2 — 850 at 12mo, 1,300 at 24mo. AY3 — 900 at 12mo. Assume no development beyond 36 months (i.e., AY1 is fully mature). Compute AY3's projected ultimate loss and reserve.
Show Answer
12-to-24 factor = (1,200 + 1,300) / (800 + 850) = 2,500 / 1,650 = 1.5152. 24-to-36 factor = 1,320 / 1,200 = 1.1000. CDF at 12 months = 1.5152 × 1.1000 = 1.6667. AY3 Ultimate = 900 × 1.6667 = 1,500 ($000s). Reserve = 1,500 − 900 = 600 ($000s).
5. Using the same triangle as the previous problem, compute the total IBNR reserve for all three accident years combined.
Show Answer
AY1 is fully mature (36 months = ultimate under this triangle's assumption), so its reserve is 0. AY2 Ultimate = 1,300 × 1.1000 = 1,430; Reserve = 1,430 − 1,300 = 130. AY3 Reserve = 600 (from the previous problem). Total reserve = 0 + 130 + 600 = 730 ($000s).
6. An accident year at its very first evaluation has a CDF to ultimate of 2.00, actual reported losses to date of $300,000, and a priori expected losses of $1,000,000. Compute the Bornhuetter-Ferguson ultimate and IBNR, and compare to the chain-ladder ultimate.
Show Answer
% unreported = 1 − 1/2.00 = 0.50. BF IBNR = 0.50 × $1,000,000 = $500,000. BF Ultimate = $300,000 + $500,000 = $800,000. Chain-ladder ultimate = $300,000 × 2.00 = $600,000. Here BF's ultimate ($800,000) is higher than chain-ladder's ($600,000), because the a priori expected losses imply a richer ultimate than the thin 12-month actual data alone suggests — BF does not always produce a lower answer than chain-ladder.
7. An accident year at 24 months of maturity has a CDF to ultimate of 1.25, a priori expected losses of $1,400,000 (from 65% ELR times $2,000,000 earned premium — wait, use the figures directly: expected losses are given as $1,400,000), and actual reported losses to date of $1,000,000. Compute the BF reserve and compare it to the chain-ladder reserve.
Show Answer
% unreported = 1 − 1/1.25 = 1 − 0.80 = 0.20. BF IBNR (= BF reserve, since actual-to-date is already reported) = 0.20 × $1,400,000 = $280,000. BF Ultimate = $1,000,000 + $280,000 = $1,280,000. Chain-ladder Ultimate = $1,000,000 × 1.25 = $1,250,000, so the chain-ladder reserve is $250,000. BF's reserve ($280,000) is somewhat higher here, since the a priori expected losses imply a slightly richer ultimate than chain-ladder's actual-to-date trajectory alone.
8. Two accident years have: AY X — actual losses to date $500,000, CDF 1.10, a priori expected losses $650,000. AY Y — actual losses to date $300,000, CDF 1.80, a priori expected losses $650,000. Compute the total BF reserve across both years.
Show Answer
AY X: % unreported = 1 − 1/1.10 = 0.0909. IBNR = 0.0909 × $650,000 ≈ $59,100. AY Y: % unreported = 1 − 1/1.80 = 0.4444. IBNR = 0.4444 × $650,000 ≈ $288,900. Total BF reserve ≈ $59,100 + $288,900 = $348,000.
9. A workers compensation actuary is choosing an exposure base for a rate review. Explain why "payroll per $100" is a more appropriate exposure base than "number of policies," and why it is more appropriate than "premium volume."
Show Answer
"Number of policies" ignores the enormous variation in risk size between a small employer and a large employer — two employers with wildly different payrolls and staff counts would count identically as "1 policy" each, even though their expected loss potential is completely different. Payroll scales much more directly with the amount of workplace injury exposure a business generates. Premium volume is unsuitable as an exposure base because it directly reflects whatever rate level is currently in effect, so a pure rate change (with no actual change in the underlying risk) would distort a premium-based exposure count, contaminating the very indication the actuary is computing.
10. A policy is written on October 1, 2023 for a 12-month term. A loss occurs under this policy on December 15, 2023. State the accident year, policy year, and calendar year(s) this policy's premium is split across, assuming level (unchanging) premium of $2,400 over the term.
Show Answer
The loss (December 15, 2023) falls in accident year 2023. The policy, written October 1, 2023, belongs entirely to policy year 2023 regardless of when within its term losses occur. For calendar-year earned premium: the term runs October 1, 2023 – September 30, 2024, so 3/12 × $2,400 = $600 is earned in calendar year 2023 (Oct–Dec) and 9/12 × $2,400 = $1,800 is earned in calendar year 2024 (Jan–Sep).
11. Developed ultimate losses of $500,000 need to be trended forward 2.25 years at an annual trend rate of 4%. Compute the trended losses.
Show Answer
Trend factor = 1.042.25. 1.042 = 1.0816; 1.040.25 ≈ 1.00985. Trend factor ≈ 1.0816 × 1.00985 ≈ 1.0923. Trended losses ≈ $500,000 × 1.0923 ≈ $546,100.
12. Reported losses for AY2024 are $2,000,000. The applicable loss development factor to ultimate is 1.20. Trend is 3% annually and the trend period is 3.0 years. Compute the developed-and-trended ultimate losses.
Show Answer
Developed ultimate = $2,000,000 × 1.20 = $2,400,000. Trend factor = 1.033 = 1.092727. Trended ultimate = $2,400,000 × 1.092727 ≈ $2,622,500.
13. Calendar year 2023 earned premium for a book of business is $8,000,000. A rate change of +8% took effect on September 1, 2023. Based on the timing of the change relative to policy terms, 67% of CY2023's earned premium was earned at the old rate level (index 1.00) and 33% at the new, current level (index 1.08). Compute the on-level factor and the on-level premium.
Show Answer
Weighted-average earned index = 0.67 × 1.00 + 0.33 × 1.08 = 0.67 + 0.3564 = 1.0264. On-level factor = 1.08 / 1.0264 ≈ 1.0522. On-level premium = $8,000,000 × 1.0522 ≈ $8,417,800.
14. A rate includes an 12% commission, a 2% premium tax, 3% of other variable general expense, and a 4% target profit & contingencies provision. Compute the permissible loss ratio.
Show Answer
Total variable expense ratio = 12% + 2% + 3% = 17%. Permissible Loss Ratio = 1 − 0.17 − 0.04 = 0.79, i.e., 79%.
15. A personal umbrella program has a projected pure premium of $150 per exposure and a fixed expense provision of $25 per exposure. The variable expense ratio is 18% and the target profit provision is 6%. Compute the indicated rate per exposure.
Show Answer
Denominator = 1 − 0.18 − 0.06 = 0.76. Indicated Rate = ($150 + $25) / 0.76 = $175 / 0.76 ≈ $230.26 per exposure.
16. An experience loss ratio of 72% is observed against a permissible loss ratio of 80%. Compute the indicated overall rate change using the loss ratio method.
Show Answer
Indicated % change = 0.72 / 0.80 − 1 = 0.90 − 1 = −0.10, a rate decrease of 10%.
17. A book of business has a projected pure premium of $310 per exposure and a permissible loss ratio of 75% (all expenses treated as variable). Current average premium at current rate level is $500 per exposure. Compute the indicated rate and the indicated percentage rate change using the pure premium method.
Show Answer
Indicated Rate = $310 / 0.75 ≈ $413.33 per exposure. Indicated % Change = $413.33 / $500 − 1 = 0.8267 − 1 = −0.1733, a decrease of about 17.3%.
18. Redwood Casualty's AY2023 general liability review shows: reported losses at evaluation of $4,500,000 with an LDF to ultimate of 1.10; annual trend of 5% applied over a 2.5-year trend period; on-level earned premium (already computed) of $9,800,000; and a permissible loss ratio of 78%. Compute the developed-and-trended losses, the experience loss ratio, and the indicated overall rate change.
Show Answer
Developed losses = $4,500,000 × 1.10 = $4,950,000. Trend factor = 1.052.5. Since 1.052 = 1.1025 and 1.050.5 ≈ 1.024695, trend factor ≈ 1.1025 × 1.024695 ≈ 1.12974. Trended ultimate losses ≈ $4,950,000 × 1.12974 ≈ $5,592,200. Experience Loss Ratio = $5,592,200 / $9,800,000 ≈ 0.5706, i.e. 57.1%. Indicated % Change = 0.5706 / 0.78 − 1 ≈ 0.7315 − 1 ≈ −0.2685, an indicated rate decrease of roughly 26.8%.
19. Explain, in a sentence or two, why the loss development factor used in Chapter 2's reserving triangle plays essentially the same analytical role as the loss development adjustment used in ratemaking (Chapter 5). Then: an accident year's actual reported losses at 12 months are $940,000, and the chain-ladder CDF from 12 months to ultimate is 2.10. Compute the estimated unpaid claim reserve for this accident year.
Show Answer
In both settings, the development factor converts a partial, still-growing reported figure into a projected ultimate figure by applying a pattern of historical growth from one maturity to the next — in reserving it produces the unpaid claim reserve directly, while in ratemaking that same ultimate figure becomes the starting point that is then trended and compared to on-level premium. Numerically: Ultimate = $940,000 × 2.10 = $1,974,000. Reserve = $1,974,000 − $940,000 = $1,034,000.

Part VI — Option Pricing Fundamentals (2.5–7.5%)

An option gives its owner a choice rather than an obligation, and that one-sided feature drives everything about how it must be priced. Chapter 1 defines what a call and a put pay their owner at expiration and sets the vocabulary — moneyness, premium, long and short. Chapter 2 shows how a simple two-outcome ("binomial") model of stock movement prices an option by no-arbitrage, without ever needing the true probability that the stock rises. Chapter 3 extends that idea to continuous time with the Black-Scholes formula and pairs it with delta, the hedge-ratio measure dealers use to manage an option book. Chapter 4 closes the loop with put-call parity, a model-free relationship for cross-checking option prices and spotting arbitrage. Together these four chapters are the analytic backbone for the hedging and risk-management material later in the syllabus.

1. Puts, Calls & Payoff Diagrams

Learning Outcomes Covered: Identify the defining cash flows and contractual features of put and call options; describe long and short positions in each; compute an option's payoff and profit at expiration; classify a position as in-the-money, at-the-money, or out-of-the-money.

1.1 What an Option Actually Promises

A call option grants its owner the right — never the obligation — to buy one unit of an underlying asset at a fixed strike price K, at a fixed future date T (European style, the focus of this part). A put grants the mirror-image right to sell the underlying at K at time T. The owner is the long position; the party who wrote the contract and must perform if exercised is the short position, or writer.

Because the long side holds a right, not a duty, the worst outcome is that the option expires worthless and is never exercised — payouts only ever flow from short to long. That asymmetry is why a premium changes hands at initiation: the short side is compensated up front for accepting one-sided risk. The premium is sunk at time 0 regardless of what happens afterward.

Long Call Payoff at Expirationmax(ST − K, 0)
Long Put Payoff at Expirationmax(K − ST, 0)

Here ST is the price of the underlying at expiration. A call pays off only when the stock has risen above the strike, since only then is "buy at K" better than buying at market. A put pays off only when the stock has fallen below the strike. Neither payoff can ever be negative for the long holder — the "max with zero" converts a forward's two-sided payoff into a one-sided option payoff.

Profit nets out the cost of establishing the position. For a long position, profit at expiration is the payoff minus the premium paid (or minus the accumulated value of that premium, if the problem asks you to account for time value). For a short position, profit is premium received minus the payoff owed.

Common trap: "payoff" and "profit" are graded differently on exam problems. Payoff is always non-negative for a long option and never references the premium. Profit subtracts (or, for the writer, adds) the premium and can be negative. If a question asks for "the gain on the position," read carefully to see whether it wants payoff or profit — they are not the same number.

1.2 Shape of the Payoff Diagrams

Here is a verbal description of each diagram, plotted with ST on the horizontal axis and payoff on the vertical axis.

  • Long call: flat at 0 for ST ≤ K, then kinks upward at ST = K and rises with slope 1 — a hockey stick opening to the upper right. Maximum loss (ignoring premium) is 0; upside is unlimited.
  • Long put: a slope-−1 segment from (0, K) down to (K, 0), then flat at 0 for ST > K — a hockey stick opening to the upper left. Maximum gain (ignoring premium) is capped at K.
  • Short call: the vertical reflection of the long call — flat at 0, then sloping downward without bound past K. Theoretically unlimited loss for the writer.
  • Short put: the reflection of the long put — flat at 0 for ST ≥ K, then sloping down toward a floor of −K as the stock falls to zero.

Adding the premium shifts each diagram down for the long side (cash left the buyer's pocket) or up for the short side (cash was received), converting a payoff diagram into a profit diagram.

1.3 Moneyness

At any point in time before expiration, comparing the current asset price S to the strike K tells you the option's moneyness:

  • In-the-money (ITM): exercising right now would produce a positive payoff — S > K for a call, S < K for a put.
  • At-the-money (ATM): S = K, or close enough that the ITM/OTM distinction is immaterial.
  • Out-of-the-money (OTM): exercising now would give a zero payoff — S < K for a call, S > K for a put.

A call and a put struck at the same K are never simultaneously in the money (barring S = K) — their moneyness always points in opposite directions.

Memory aid: a call gives you the right to "call" the stock away from someone at a fixed price, which is valuable when the market price has risen above that fixed price. A put gives you the right to "put" (hand off, sell) the stock to someone at a fixed price, which is valuable when the market price has fallen below it.
Example 6.1

An investor buys one European call option on a stock with strike price 50 for a premium of 4.00. At expiration the stock is trading at 58. Find the payoff and the profit to the investor.

Show Solution

Payoff = max(ST − K, 0) = max(58 − 50, 0) = 8.00. Profit = payoff − premium = 8.00 − 4.00 = 4.00.

Example 6.2

An investor buys one European put option with strike price 40 for a premium of 3.00. At expiration the stock is trading at 32. Find the payoff and the profit.

Show Solution

Payoff = max(K − ST, 0) = max(40 − 32, 0) = 8.00. Profit = 8.00 − 3.00 = 5.00.

Example 6.3

A trader writes (sells) one European call option with strike 100, collecting a premium of 6.00. At expiration the stock closes at 115. Find the writer's profit.

Show Solution

The long side's payoff is max(115 − 100, 0) = 15.00, so the writer must pay out 15.00. Writer's profit = premium received − payoff owed = 6.00 − 15.00 = −9.00, a loss, illustrating a short call's unlimited downside.

Example 6.4

A stock currently trades at 75. Classify the moneyness of (a) a call struck at 70, (b) a call struck at 80, and (c) a put struck at 80.

Show Solution

(a) Call, K = 70: S = 75 > K, so in the money. (b) Call, K = 80: S = 75 < K, so out of the money. (c) Put, K = 80: S = 75 < K, so selling at 80 is favorable — in the money. The K = 80 call and put have opposite moneyness, as expected.

2. The Binomial Option Pricing Model

Learning Outcomes Covered: Apply the one-period and multi-period binomial model to price a simple European-style derivative on a single non-dividend-paying asset, using a no-arbitrage replicating-portfolio argument and the resulting risk-neutral pricing formula.

2.1 The One-Period Tree

The binomial model reduces reality to one question: over a short period of length h, does the stock go up or down? Starting from price S0, it moves to exactly one of two values at time h: S0u (up, u > 1) or S0d (down, 0 < d < 1). A risk-free bond is also available, growing continuously at rate r, so one dollar today is worth erh at time h.

For the model to admit no riskless arbitrage, the up and down factors must straddle the risk-free growth factor:

No-Arbitrage Conditiond < erh < u

If this failed — say u ≤ erh — the stock could never outperform the bond even in the best case, so no one would hold it, and a riskless arbitrage (short the stock, buy the bond) would exist.

2.2 Replicating Portfolio and the Pricing Formula

The central trick of the binomial model is that we never need the true (physical) probability of the stock going up. Instead, build a portfolio of Δ shares of stock and B dollars in the risk-free bond today, chosen to reproduce the option's payoff in both states one period ahead. Writing Cu, Cd for the option's payoffs:

Replication EquationsΔS0u + Berh = Cu   and   ΔS0d + Berh = Cd

Subtracting eliminates B and isolates Δ:

Replicating Share PositionΔ = (Cu − Cd) / (S0u − S0d)

Either replication equation then gives B. Since the stock-and-bond portfolio reproduces the option's payoff in every future state, the law of one price says the option costs exactly what the portfolio costs today:

Option Price via ReplicationC0 = ΔS0 + B

Substituting the solved Δ and B back in and simplifying produces an equivalent, more convenient formula: the option price as a discounted expected payoff under special "risk-neutral" probabilities that make the discounted stock price behave like a fair game:

Risk-Neutral Probabilityp* = (erh − d) / (u − d)
Risk-Neutral Pricing FormulaC0 = e−rh [ p*Cu + (1 − p*)Cd ]

Both routes give identical prices — the second is just an algebraic repackaging of the first. p* is not anyone's actual belief about the stock; it is the unique probability under which the stock's discounted expected value grows at the risk-free rate, which is exactly what makes the no-arbitrage price come out as a clean expectation.

Discrete vs. continuous compounding trap: the exponent in erh must use the same period length h that separates the up/down nodes. If u, d are given for a six-month step but r is an annual rate, use er(0.5), not er — a common scoring error on binomial problems.

2.3 Extending to Two Periods

A two-period tree repeats the one-period logic twice, moving backward from expiration to today. Assuming the same u, d, r apply in both periods (a "recombining" tree), the stock reaches three possible values after two steps: S0u², S0ud, S0d². Compute the option's payoff at each terminal node, then apply the one-period risk-neutral formula working backward one column at a time: from the two terminal nodes into each of the two nodes at time h, then from those into the single price at time 0. Since p* depends only on u, d, r, the same risk-neutral probability is reused at every step.

Example 6.5

A non-dividend-paying stock currently trades at 50. Over the next year it will move to either 60 (u = 1.2) or 45 (d = 0.9). The continuously compounded risk-free rate is 6%. Price a one-year European call with strike 50 using the replicating-portfolio method, and state the number of shares and bond position required.

Show Solution

Terminal payoffs: Cu = max(60 − 50, 0) = 10, Cd = max(45 − 50, 0) = 0.

Δ = (Cu − Cd)/(S0u − S0d) = (10 − 0)/(60 − 45) = 10/15 = 0.6667 shares.

Solve for B using the up-state equation: ΔS0u + Berh = Cu ⟹ 0.6667(60) + B·e0.06 = 10 ⟹ 40 + 1.061837B = 10 ⟹ B = −30/1.061837 = −28.253.

Price = ΔS0 + B = 0.6667(50) − 28.253 = 33.333 − 28.253 = 5.08. The negative B means the strategy borrows about 28.25 at the risk-free rate to help buy 0.6667 shares — a call is a leveraged, partly-borrowed stock position.

Example 6.6

Using the same tree as Example 6.5 (S0 = 50, u = 1.2, d = 0.9, r = 6%), price a one-year European put with strike 50 using the risk-neutral formula.

Show Solution

First find p*: p* = (erh − d)/(u − d) = (e0.06 − 0.9)/(1.2 − 0.9) = (1.061837 − 0.9)/0.3 = 0.161837/0.3 = 0.53946. So 1 − p* = 0.46054.

Put payoffs: Pu = max(50 − 60, 0) = 0, Pd = max(50 − 45, 0) = 5.

P0 = e−0.06[0.53946(0) + 0.46054(5)] = e−0.06(2.3027) = 0.941765 × 2.3027 = 2.169. The same p* = 0.53946 used for the call in Example 6.5 applies here too, since p* depends only on u, d, r, never on the option itself.

Two-period recombining binomial tree — stock prices and call payoffs (S₀ = 50, u = 1.2, d = 0.9, K = 50)
Node (path)Stock PriceCall Payoff (if terminal)
Start50.00—
Up (u)60.00—
Down (d)45.00—
Up-Up (uu)72.0022.00
Up-Down / Down-Up (ud)54.004.00
Down-Down (dd)40.500.00
Example 6.7

Using the tree above (S0 = 50, u = 1.2, d = 0.9, r = 6% per period, two one-year periods), price a two-year European call with strike 50.

Show Solution

p* = 0.53946 and 1 − p* = 0.46054, exactly as in Example 6.6, since u, d, r are unchanged each period.

Step back from expiration to the up node (S = 60): value = e−0.06[p*(22) + (1−p*)(4)] = e−0.06[0.53946(22) + 0.46054(4)] = e−0.06(11.868 + 1.842) = 0.941765 × 13.710 = 12.915.

Step back to the down node (S = 45): value = e−0.06[p*(4) + (1−p*)(0)] = e−0.06(0.53946 × 4) = 0.941765 × 2.1578 = 2.0326.

Step back to today: C0 = e−0.06[p*(12.915) + (1−p*)(2.0326)] = e−0.06(6.967 + 0.936) = 0.941765 × 7.903 = 7.44. Cross-check: applying the terminal binomial weights p*² = 0.2910, 2p*(1−p*) = 0.4968, (1−p*)² = 0.2121 directly and discounting two periods by e−0.12 = 0.88692 gives 0.88692 × [0.2910(22) + 0.4968(4)] = 0.88692 × 8.390 ≈ 7.44, matching.

Example 6.8

A stock trades at 100. Over one year it will move to 110 (u = 1.1) or 95 (d = 0.95). The continuously compounded risk-free rate is 4%. Price a one-year European call with strike 105.

Show Solution

Terminal payoffs: Cu = max(110 − 105, 0) = 5, Cd = max(95 − 105, 0) = 0.

p* = (e0.04 − 0.95)/(1.1 − 0.95) = (1.040811 − 0.95)/0.15 = 0.090811/0.15 = 0.60541.

C0 = e−0.04[0.60541(5) + 0.39459(0)] = e−0.04(3.0270) = 0.960789 × 3.0270 = 2.91.

3. The Black-Scholes Formula & Delta Hedging

Learning Outcomes Covered: Apply the Black-Scholes formula to price a European call or put on a single non-dividend-paying asset; compute delta and use it as the hedge ratio for delta-hedging an option position.

3.1 From Binomial to Continuous Time

Chop the life of an option into more and more binomial steps — hourly, then by the minute, then continuously — and the tree converges to a model where the stock follows a continuous random path with constant annualized volatility σ and, under the risk-neutral measure, average continuously compounded growth rate r. The Black-Scholes formula is the closed-form limit of that convergence for a European option on a non-dividend-paying stock.

Black-Scholes Call PriceC = S0N(d1) − Ke−rTN(d2)
d₁ and d₂d1 = [ ln(S0/K) + (r + σ²/2)T ] / (σ√T)     d2 = d1 − σ√T

Here N(·) is the standard normal CDF — on the exam you would read this off the provided normal distribution calculator or table. Intuitively, S0N(d1) is the present value of receiving the stock conditional on the option finishing in the money, and Ke−rTN(d2) is the present value of paying the strike, weighted by N(d2), the risk-neutral probability the call is exercised at all.

The put price uses the same d1, d2:

Black-Scholes Put PriceP = Ke−rTN(−d2) − S0N(−d1)

Because N(−x) = 1 − N(x), this put formula is always consistent with put-call parity (Chapter 4) — computing the put directly, or backing it out of the call price via parity, gives the same answer.

3.2 Delta

Delta measures how much the option's price moves for a small move in the stock price — the partial derivative of the option price with respect to S0. For the Black-Scholes call and put, delta has a clean closed form:

Call DeltaΔcall = N(d1)
Put DeltaΔput = N(d1) − 1

Call delta always lies between 0 and 1: a deep out-of-the-money call barely moves with the stock (delta near 0), while a deep in-the-money call moves almost dollar-for-dollar (delta near 1). Put delta always lies between −1 and 0, since a put's value moves opposite to the stock.

Sign trap: put delta is negative. Forgetting the "−1" and reporting Δput = N(d1) instead of N(d1) − 1 is a common slip — check that the sign matches intuition: a put falls in value when the stock rises, so its delta must be negative.

Delta is also the hedge ratio: the number of shares a dealer must hold per option sold (or short per option bought) to make a portfolio momentarily insensitive to a small stock move. A dealer who sells a call and buys Δcall shares per option is "delta-neutral" — gains or losses on the short call are instantaneously offset by the stock position. Because delta changes as the stock price and time to expiration change, the hedge is not "set and forget": the dealer must periodically rebalance the share count, a practice called dynamic delta hedging.

Exam tip: you won't derive Black-Scholes, but you must plug in numbers correctly. Compute d1 first (note the "+σ²/2," not "−"), then get d2 by subtracting σ√T. Keep at least four decimals through every intermediate step — small rounding errors get magnified by the normal CDF lookup.
Example 6.9

A non-dividend-paying stock trades at 50. A European call has strike 50, one year to expiration, continuously compounded risk-free rate 6%, and volatility 30%. Find the Black-Scholes price.

Show Solution

ln(S0/K) = ln(50/50) = 0. r + σ²/2 = 0.06 + 0.045 = 0.105.

d1 = [0 + 0.105(1)] / (0.30 × 1) = 0.105/0.30 = 0.35. d2 = 0.35 − 0.30(1) = 0.05.

From the standard normal table: N(0.35) = 0.6368, N(0.05) = 0.5199.

Ke−rT = 50e−0.06 = 50(0.941765) = 47.0883.

C = S0N(d1) − Ke−rTN(d2) = 50(0.6368) − 47.0883(0.5199) = 31.840 − 24.481 = 7.36.

Example 6.10

Using the same inputs as Example 6.9 (S0 = 50, K = 50, r = 6%, σ = 30%, T = 1), find the Black-Scholes put price directly from the put formula, and confirm it matches put-call parity.

Show Solution

N(−d1) = 1 − N(0.35) = 1 − 0.6368 = 0.3632. N(−d2) = 1 − N(0.05) = 1 − 0.5199 = 0.4801.

P = Ke−rTN(−d2) − S0N(−d1) = 47.0883(0.4801) − 50(0.3632) = 22.607 − 18.160 = 4.45.

Check via parity (Chapter 4): P = C − S0 + Ke−rT = 7.36 − 50 + 47.0883 = 4.45. The two methods agree, as they always must.

Example 6.11

A dealer sells 1,000 of the calls priced in Example 6.9 (delta = N(d1) = 0.6368). How many shares of stock should the dealer buy to be delta-neutral immediately after the sale?

Show Solution

Each short call has delta 0.6368, so the dealer buys 0.6368 shares per option sold: 1,000 × 0.6368 = 636.8 shares (≈637 shares). As the stock moves, N(d1) changes and the dealer must rebalance to stay hedged — the essence of dynamic delta hedging.

Example 6.12

A non-dividend-paying stock trades at 80. A European call has strike 75, six months to expiration, continuously compounded risk-free rate 5%, and volatility 25%. Find the Black-Scholes call price and its delta.

Show Solution

ln(80/75) = 0.064539. r + σ²/2 = 0.05 + 0.03125 = 0.08125; over T = 0.5 this contributes 0.040625. Numerator of d1 = 0.064539 + 0.040625 = 0.105164. σ√T = 0.25√0.5 = 0.176777.

d1 = 0.105164/0.176777 = 0.5949. d2 = 0.5949 − 0.176777 = 0.4181.

Interpolating the standard normal table: N(0.59)=0.7224, N(0.60)=0.7257 ⟹ N(0.5949) ≈ 0.7240. N(0.41)=0.6591, N(0.42)=0.6628 ⟹ N(0.4181) ≈ 0.6621.

Ke−rT = 75e−0.025 = 73.1483. C = 80(0.7240) − 73.1483(0.6621) = 57.920 − 48.431 = 9.49. Delta = N(d1) = 0.7240.

4. Put-Call Parity

Learning Outcomes Covered: State and derive put-call parity for European options on a non-dividend-paying stock; use parity to infer one option price from another; use parity to detect and quantify arbitrage in a set of quoted option prices.

4.1 The Relationship

Put-call parity is a strikingly simple, model-free relationship connecting the prices of a European call and put with the same strike and expiration on a non-dividend-paying underlying:

Put-Call ParityC − P = S0 − Ke−rT

This requires no assumption about volatility, no probability distribution, and no pricing model at all — it follows purely from the absence of arbitrage. Whether prices come from Black-Scholes, a binomial tree, or any other model, they must satisfy this equation, or a riskless profit exists.

4.2 A No-Arbitrage Derivation

Consider two portfolios built today, both maturing at time T:

  • Portfolio A: one long European call (strike K) plus Ke−rT invested in the risk-free bond (grows to exactly K at T).
  • Portfolio B: one long European put (strike K) plus one share of the underlying stock.

Compare their expiration values in each case:

  • If ST ≥ K: A is worth (ST − K) + K = ST (call pays off, bond matures to K). B is worth 0 + ST (put worthless, stock worth ST). They match.
  • If ST < K: A is worth 0 + K = K (call worthless). B is worth (K − ST) + ST = K (put pays off, plus stock). They match again.

In every state both portfolios are worth max(ST, K). Portfolios with identical payoffs in every state must, by the law of one price, cost the same today — otherwise buying the cheaper one and selling the pricier one locks in a riskless profit with no future obligation. Setting the two costs equal:

Equal Cost TodayC + Ke−rT = P + S0

Rearranging gives put-call parity as stated above.

4.3 Using Parity Without a Pricing Model

Because parity links C, P, S0, and Ke−rT in one linear equation, knowing any three pins down the fourth immediately — no Black-Scholes calculation, volatility estimate, or binomial tree required. This is the fastest tool whenever a problem gives a call price and asks for the corresponding put, or vice versa.

4.4 Detecting Arbitrage

If quoted C and P don't satisfy parity, arbitrage exists. Compare C − P (cost of the synthetic long-stock position: buy the call, sell the put) against S0 − Ke−rT (cost of the actual position: buy the stock, borrow the PV of the strike). Buy whichever side is cheaper, sell the other. Since both sides produce the same payoff at T (namely ST − K), this nets to zero payoff at expiration but a positive cash inflow today.

Sign-convention trap: always write out both sides of the equation explicitly — C − P on one line and S0 − Ke−rT on the next — before deciding which side is "cheap." Trying to compare the quantities from memory, without writing them down, is the single most common source of arbitrage-direction errors on this topic.
Scope note: the parity formula in this simple form applies to European options on a stock that pays no dividends before expiration. If the underlying pays dividends, or the options are American-style, the relationship must be adjusted (subtracting the present value of dividends, or replaced by an inequality for American options) — that refinement belongs to later material, not this introductory form.
Example 6.13

For a non-dividend-paying stock with S0 = 50, K = 50, r = 6% continuously compounded, T = 1 year, a European call is priced at 7.36 (as in Example 6.9). Use put-call parity to find the European put price without redoing a Black-Scholes calculation.

Show Solution

Ke−rT = 50e−0.06 = 50(0.941765) = 47.0883.

P = C − S0 + Ke−rT = 7.36 − 50 + 47.0883 = 4.45, matching the direct Black-Scholes put calculation in Example 6.10.

Example 6.14

A non-dividend-paying stock trades at 90. A European put with strike 95, nine months to expiration, is priced at 6.50. The continuously compounded risk-free rate is 4%. Find the price of the corresponding European call.

Show Solution

Ke−rT = 95e−0.04(0.75) = 95e−0.03 = 95(0.970446) = 92.192.

C = P + S0 − Ke−rT = 6.50 + 90 − 92.192 = 4.31.

Example 6.15

A non-dividend-paying stock trades at 60. The continuously compounded risk-free rate is 5%. A European call with strike 55 and six months to expiration is quoted at 8.00; the corresponding put is quoted at 2.00. Determine whether these prices admit an arbitrage opportunity, and if so, describe the trade and quantify the riskless profit per unit.

Show Solution

Ke−rT = 55e−0.05(0.5) = 55e−0.025 = 55(0.975310) = 53.642.

S0 − Ke−rT = 60 − 53.642 = 6.358. C − P = 8.00 − 2.00 = 6.00.

Since C − P = 6.00 is less than S0 − Ke−rT = 6.358, the synthetic side (call minus put) is underpriced relative to the actual side (stock minus borrowed strike). Arbitrage: buy the call, sell the put, short the stock, and lend (invest) Ke−rT = 53.642 at the risk-free rate.

Cash flow today: −8.00 (buy call) + 2.00 (sell put) + 60.00 (short sale proceeds) − 53.642 (buy the bond) = +0.358, an immediate cash inflow.

Cash flow at T: the option position pays exactly ST − 55 (long call minus short put always equals ST − K); the bond pays +55; closing the short stock costs −ST. Net: (ST − 55) + 55 − ST = 0 in every state — zero risk at expiration and a guaranteed profit of about 0.36 per unit today.

Example 6.16

A non-dividend-paying stock trades at 40. The continuously compounded risk-free rate is 5%. A one-year European call with strike 40 is quoted at 3.50, and the corresponding put is quoted at 1.55. Check whether these prices are consistent with put-call parity.

Show Solution

Ke−rT = 40e−0.05 = 40(0.951229) = 38.049.

S0 − Ke−rT = 40 − 38.049 = 1.951. C − P = 3.50 − 1.55 = 1.95.

The two sides, 1.951 and 1.95, agree to within rounding. There is no material arbitrage here — the quotes are consistent with put-call parity, and the tiny residual difference (about 0.001) reflects rounding in the quoted prices rather than a genuine mispricing.

Part VI Practice Problems

1. An investor buys a European put option with strike 45 for a premium of 2.50. At expiration the stock is trading at 38. Find the payoff and the profit.
Show Answer
Payoff = max(45 − 38, 0) = 7.00. Profit = 7.00 − 2.50 = 4.50.
2. A stock currently trades at 62. Classify the moneyness of (a) a call struck at 65 and (b) a put struck at 65.
Show Answer
(a) Call, K = 65: S = 62 < K, so the call is out of the money. (b) Put, K = 65: S = 62 < K, so exercising to sell at 65 (above market) is favorable — the put is in the money.
3. A trader writes a European put option with strike 30, collecting a premium of 1.80. At expiration the stock closes at 25. Find the writer's profit or loss.
Show Answer
The long put's payoff is max(30 − 25, 0) = 5.00, so the writer owes 5.00. Writer's profit = premium received − payoff owed = 1.80 − 5.00 = −3.20 (a loss of 3.20).
4. A stock trades at 40. Over one period it moves to 46 (u = 1.15) or 34 (d = 0.85). The continuously compounded risk-free rate is 5%. Find the risk-neutral probability p*.
Show Answer
p* = (erh − d)/(u − d) = (e0.05 − 0.85)/(1.15 − 0.85) = (1.051271 − 0.85)/0.30 = 0.201271/0.30 = 0.6709.
5. Using the tree in Problem 4, price a one-period European call with strike 40.
Show Answer
Cu = max(46 − 40, 0) = 6, Cd = max(34 − 40, 0) = 0. C0 = e−0.05[0.6709(6) + 0.3291(0)] = e−0.05(4.0254) = 0.951229 × 4.0254 = 3.83.
6. A stock trades at 60. Over each of two six-month periods (T = 1 year total) it moves up by a factor of 1.1 or down by a factor of 0.9. The continuously compounded annual risk-free rate is 5%. Price a one-year European call with strike 60.
Show Answer
Per-period growth factor: er(0.5) = e0.025 = 1.025315. p* = (1.025315 − 0.9)/(1.1 − 0.9) = 0.125315/0.2 = 0.626576.
Terminal stock prices: Suu = 60(1.21) = 72.6, Sud = 60(0.99) = 59.4, Sdd = 60(0.81) = 48.6.
Terminal payoffs: Cuu = 12.6, Cud = 0, Cdd = 0.
Since only the uu node pays off, expected payoff = p*²(12.6) = (0.626576)²(12.6) = 0.392597(12.6) = 4.9467.
Discount over the full year: C0 = e−0.05(4.9467) = 0.951229(4.9467) = 4.71.
7. A non-dividend-paying stock trades at 100, strike 100, r = 3% continuously compounded, σ = 20%, T = 1 year. Compute d1 and d2.
Show Answer
ln(100/100) = 0. r + σ²/2 = 0.03 + 0.02 = 0.05. d1 = [0 + 0.05(1)]/(0.20 × 1) = 0.25. d2 = 0.25 − 0.20 = 0.05.
8. Using the d1, d2 from Problem 7 and N(0.25) = 0.5987, N(0.05) = 0.5199, find the Black-Scholes call price.
Show Answer
Ke−rT = 100e−0.03 = 100(0.970446) = 97.0446. C = 100(0.5987) − 97.0446(0.5199) = 59.87 − 50.454 = 9.42.
9. Using the result of Problem 8, find the corresponding European put price via put-call parity, and state the put's delta.
Show Answer
P = C − S0 + Ke−rT = 9.42 − 100 + 97.0446 = 6.46. Delta of the put = N(d1) − 1 = 0.5987 − 1 = −0.4013.
10. A trader is long 500 of the calls from Problem 8 (delta = N(d1) = 0.5987). How many shares must the trader short to be delta-neutral?
Show Answer
Shares to short = 500 × 0.5987 = 299.35, i.e., approximately 299 shares.
11. A non-dividend-paying stock trades at 45. The continuously compounded risk-free rate is 4%. A three-month European call with strike 50 is quoted at 1.20, and the corresponding put is quoted at 6.30. Determine whether an arbitrage opportunity exists, and if so, find the riskless profit per unit available today.
Show Answer
Ke−rT = 50e−0.01 = 49.5025. S0 − Ke−rT = 45 − 49.5025 = −4.5025. C − P = 1.20 − 6.30 = −5.10.
Since −5.10 < −4.5025, the synthetic side (buy call, sell put) is cheaper: buy the call, sell the put, short the stock, buy a bond costing 49.5025.
Cash flow today: −1.20 + 6.30 + 45.00 − 49.5025 = +0.5975, a riskless profit of about 0.60 per unit.
12. A non-dividend-paying stock trades at 55. A six-month European call struck at 55 is quoted at 4.20, and the corresponding put is quoted at 3.10. The continuously compounded risk-free rate is 5%. Check whether these quotes satisfy put-call parity, and if not, identify which side (synthetic or actual) is overpriced.
Show Answer
Ke−rT = 55e−0.025 = 53.642. S0 − Ke−rT = 55 − 53.642 = 1.358. C − P = 4.20 − 3.10 = 1.10.
Since 1.10 < 1.358, the actual side (long stock, short bond) is overpriced relative to the synthetic side — profit by buying the call, selling the put, shorting the stock, and lending Ke−rT, per the recipe in Section 4.4. Mispricing ≈ 0.258 per unit.

Part VII — Long-Term Insurance Coverages & Retirement Financial Security Programs (2.5–5%)

This part steps back from pricing and reserving mechanics and asks a more basic question: what are these products for, and who is entitled to buy them? We start with insurable interest, the legal doctrine that keeps life insurance from becoming a bet on other people's deaths. We then survey long-term life, health, and annuity products at a conceptual level, focusing on what problem each one solves. Finally, we compare defined benefit and defined contribution retirement plans and examine who bears investment and longevity risk under each. This topic is narrow and largely qualitative, so expect exam questions to test precise definitions, correct classification, and sound judgment on scenarios rather than heavy computation.

1. Insurable Interest

Learning Outcomes Covered: Define insurable interest and explain the rationale behind the requirement; identify who possesses insurable interest in a given scenario; describe when insurable interest must exist for a life insurance contract to be valid.

1.1 What Insurable Interest Means

A life insurance policy is a contract under which a policyowner pays premiums in exchange for the insurer's promise to pay a benefit on the death of a designated insured. Insurable interest requires that, at purchase, the policyowner stand to suffer a genuine loss — financial, or in some cases a sufficiently close personal relationship — if the insured were to die. The owner must have a real stake in the insured staying alive, not just a stake in the insured dying.

1.2 Why the Requirement Exists

  • Preventing insurance from becoming gambling. A death-benefit contract purchased by a stranger is economically a bet on that person's death. Courts and regulators refuse to enforce such contracts.
  • Reducing moral hazard. A policyowner without insurable interest who profits from the insured's death has a perverse incentive. Requiring a real stake keeps the owner better off with the insured alive.
Ask: "Does this policyowner lose something real if the insured dies?" If not, the policy should not be issued.

Insurable interest is distinct from insurability. Insurability concerns whether the person to be insured is an acceptable risk from a medical and lifestyle underwriting standpoint — age, health history, occupation, hobbies. Insurable interest concerns whether the policyowner is an acceptable party to hold the contract in the first place, independent of anything about the insured's own health. An insurer can decline an application for either reason, but they answer different questions: one asks "is this a good risk?", the other asks "is this the right person to be betting on that risk staying favorable?"

1.3 Who Typically Has Insurable Interest

Table 7.1 — Common Sources of Insurable Interest
PolicyownerInsuredBasis
An individualThemselvesAlways presumed — unlimited interest in one's own life
A spouse/partnerThe other spouse/partnerClose personal and shared economic relationship
A parentA dependent childFinancial responsibility for the child's support
A business partnerA co-ownerLoss of skills, capital, or clients would harm the firm
A creditorA debtorRisk of losing the loan balance; capped near the debt
An employerA key employeeLoss of expertise driving business value ("key person")

Close family bonds (spouse, child) are presumed sufficient without proving a dollar amount. Economic relationships (a debt, a partnership stake, a key employee's contribution) generally cap the justifiable coverage near the quantifiable loss. A stranger or casual acquaintance has no insurable interest.

1.4 When Must Insurable Interest Exist?

For life insurance, insurable interest generally must exist at the time the policy is applied for and issued — not necessarily at the time of the insured's death. Relationships change: a debt gets repaid, a partnership dissolves, a marriage ends. Requiring proof of insurable interest again at claim time would make legitimately purchased, long-held policies unworkable and vulnerable to denial over changes unrelated to fraud.

Contrast this with property insurance, where insurable interest is generally required both at inception and at the time of loss, because a property policy indemnifies an actual loss suffered by the current owner. Life insurance pays a fixed, agreed sum rather than measuring loss at death, which is why the timing rule differs.
Example 7.1

David lends Maria $50,000 and, as protection, buys a policy on Maria's life with a $50,000 benefit, naming himself beneficiary. Three years later Maria repays the loan, but David keeps paying premiums. Two years after that, Maria dies. Should David's claim be paid?

Show Solution

Yes. Insurable interest is assessed at issue, and David had it then — he stood to lose $50,000 if Maria died before repaying. That the debt was later repaid does not retroactively void the policy, since insurable interest need not persist to the date of death.

Example 7.2

A stranger with no connection to a local philanthropist tries to buy a $2,000,000 policy on the philanthropist's life without her knowledge, naming himself owner and beneficiary. Should an insurer issue this?

Show Solution

No. The applicant has no insurable interest — no financial loss and no personal relationship. Issuing the policy would create a pure wager on a third party's death and a troubling incentive. A properly underwritten insurer declines it; if issued, it would typically be voidable.

Example 7.3

A manufacturer buys a $1,000,000 policy on its founder, whose patents drive the flagship product line, naming the company as owner and beneficiary. Is this an appropriate use of insurable interest?

Show Solution

Yes — this is key person insurance. The company has a genuine, quantifiable interest in the founder's continued life, since her death would threaten revenue tied to her patents. Coverage is typically sized to a reasonable estimate of the resulting economic loss.

1.5 Consent of the Insured

Most jurisdictions also require the written consent of the insured (or a guardian, for a minor) before a third party can insure their life. Even a party with genuine insurable interest generally cannot secretly insure another adult without that person's knowledge. The main exceptions are insuring oneself, and, in many places, a parent insuring a minor child in their care.

2. Long-Term Coverages: Life, Health & Annuities
