Learning Outcomes Covered: Identify and distinguish the major categories of long-term life insurance, health insurance, and annuity products; describe the financial protection need each product category addresses.

2.1 Two Opposite Risks

Long-term personal insurance addresses two mirror-image risks. Dying too soon: a person dies before accumulating enough to support dependents or obligations left behind. Life insurance pays a benefit on death to address this. Living too long: a person outlives their savings — longevity risk. Annuities convert assets into income that continues no matter how long the annuitant lives. Long-term health coverages sit alongside these, protecting against the cost of extended illness, disability, or care needs, which can derail financial security either way.

Notice that none of these products eliminate the underlying uncertainty about how long any one individual will live or stay healthy — nobody can know that in advance. What they do is pool that uncertainty across many people, so the insurer's overall experience becomes far more predictable than any single person's outcome, and each individual trades a variable, potentially catastrophic personal outcome for a fixed, budgetable premium.

2.2 Life Insurance: Whole Life vs. Term

The quantitative pricing of these products is developed later in this manual; here we focus on features and purpose.

Table 7.2 — Term vs. Whole Life Insurance
Term LifeWhole Life
DurationA fixed period; coverage ends if insured survives itThe insured's entire lifetime, if premiums are paid
Cash valueNone — pure protectionYes — accumulates and belongs to the owner
PremiumLower initially; may rise sharply at renewalHigher, but typically level for life
Typical useA temporary need — e.g., years left on a mortgageA permanent need — e.g., final expenses, estate transfer
Term life is like renting — pure protection for a window of time, nothing back if you outlive it. Whole life is like buying a home — part of every premium builds equity you own.

2.3 Long-Term Health Coverages

  • Disability income insurance replaces earned income if the insured cannot work due to illness or injury, protecting the accumulation phase of financial life.
  • Long-term care (LTC) insurance helps pay for extended personal care — in a facility or at home — due to chronic illness or frailty, costs that ordinary health coverage typically does not fully address and that can quickly consume retirement savings.

Both are long-duration products: premiums are paid for years anticipating a benefit that may not be needed for decades, so pricing depends heavily on long-range assumptions about incidence, claim duration, and persistency.

2.4 Annuities: Converting Savings into Income

An annuity is conceptually the opposite of life insurance: it pays income while the annuitant survives, transferring longevity risk to the insurer. Annuities are classified along two independent dimensions.

When payments begin

  • Immediate annuity: income starts almost right away (typically within a year).
  • Deferred annuity: there is an accumulation period, often years, before income begins; the annuitant later "annuitizes" the balance.

How premiums are paid

  • Single premium: funded with one lump sum.
  • Flexible premium: funded with contributions over time.
"Immediate vs. deferred" describes when income starts; "single vs. flexible premium" describes how money goes in. These are independent — a single-premium deferred annuity and a flexible-premium deferred annuity are both common. An immediate annuity is essentially always single premium, since income must start almost right away.
Example 7.4

At retirement, Wen has $400,000 and worries about outliving it, but has no death-benefit need. Which product category fits, and why?

Show Solution

An annuity. Wen's concern is longevity risk, not leaving a benefit at death. An immediate annuity could convert her $400,000 into guaranteed lifetime income, directly matching her need.

Example 7.5

A 35-year-old with a new mortgage wants affordable coverage only until it's paid off in 25 years, with no interest in cash value. Which product fits, and why not the alternative?

Show Solution

A 25-year term policy. His need is temporary and tied to the mortgage horizon. Whole life would mean paying for lifelong coverage, at a much higher premium, largely to fund a cash-value element he does not want.

Example 7.6

A 45-year-old contributes from each paycheck into a tax-deferred annuity, planning to convert it to income at 65. Classify this annuity along both dimensions.

Show Solution

Flexible premium, since she contributes periodically rather than paying a lump sum; currently deferred, since income will not begin until retirement, 20 years later.

3. Defined Benefit & Defined Contribution Plans

Learning Outcomes Covered: Distinguish defined benefit and defined contribution pension plan designs; identify who bears investment and longevity risk under each; describe hybrid plan designs conceptually and the industry trend toward defined contribution plans.

3.1 Two Philosophies

Employer-sponsored retirement plans help workers accumulate income for after they stop working. Defined benefit and defined contribution designs differ in what is promised, and consequently in who bears the risk that the promise can be kept. It can help to think of the DB/DC distinction as a question of timing: a DB plan defines the output — the eventual pension — and leaves the funding path to be whatever it needs to be, while a DC plan defines the input — the contribution — and leaves the eventual outcome to be whatever the markets deliver.

3.2 Defined Benefit (DB) Plans

In a defined benefit plan, the employer promises a specific benefit, usually by formula based on salary and years of service — e.g., 1.5% of final average salary times years of service, paid for life. The employer must fund and invest a pension trust so promised benefits can be paid regardless of market performance or how long retirees live. A shortfall from weak returns or longer-than-expected lifespans requires additional employer contributions; the employee's formula-based benefit does not change. The employer bears both investment risk and longevity risk.

3.3 Defined Contribution (DC) Plans

In a defined contribution plan (e.g., a 401(k)), the employer and/or employee contribute a specified amount, often a percentage of salary, into an individual account. There is no promised benefit formula — the eventual retirement income depends entirely on contributions and investment performance. The employee typically selects investments and bears the investment risk directly. At retirement the employee also generally bears longevity risk, since the account must simply last as long as the retiree lives, unless it is voluntarily annuitized.

Accumulated DC balance at retirement, with contribution Ct at the start of year t and annual return i:

AV = Σt=1n Ct (1+i)n−t+1

Unlike a DB formula, AV is not fixed in advance — it moves with the realized i each year, which is why the employee bears the investment risk in a DC plan.

3.4 Side-by-Side Comparison

Table 7.3 — Defined Benefit vs. Defined Contribution Plans
Defined Benefit (DB)Defined Contribution (DC)
What is promisedA benefit formula (salary & service)A contribution rate; no promised final benefit
Investment riskEmployerEmployee
Longevity riskEmployer (pooled)Employee, unless annuitized
Benefit depends onFormula onlyContributions plus actual returns
PortabilityOften limitedGenerally high
Funding vehicleSingle pooled trustIndividual account
Employer cost predictabilityLow — varies with experienceHigh — capped at agreed contribution

3.5 Hybrid Designs: Cash Balance Plans

A cash balance plan blends both designs. Legally it is a defined benefit plan — the employer bears investment risk and must fund promised amounts — but it is communicated in DC-like terms: each employee has a hypothetical account that grows by a contribution credit plus an interest credit (often tied to a benchmark rate, not actual plan returns). At retirement the balance can often be taken as a lump sum or annuitized. Cash balance plans show that the DB/DC distinction is about risk allocation and the form of the promise, not a rigid either/or.

3.6 Why the Industry Has Shifted from DB to DC

  • Cost predictability. DB plans expose employers to open-ended funding obligations; DC plans cap the employer's obligation at the agreed contribution.
  • Workforce mobility. DC balances are typically portable; DB benefits often favor long-tenured employees.
  • Regulatory/accounting burden. DB plans carry heavier funding, reporting, and insurance obligations.
  • Employee preference. Some employees find a visible account balance more tangible than an abstract future promise.
The DB-to-DC shift is often framed as "more choice," but it is equally accurate to describe it as a transfer of investment and longevity risk from employers to individual employees, who are typically less able to pool or diversify those risks on their own.
Example 7.7

Alan, 65, retires after 30 years at a final salary of $80,000. Company A's DB plan pays 1.5% × years × final salary for life. Company B's DC plan has grown to $650,000 due to strong markets. In which plan is Alan's income more directly exposed to how markets performed?

Show Solution

Company B's DC plan. Alan's DB pension = 1.5% × 30 × $80,000 = $36,000/year, fixed regardless of investment performance. His $650,000 DC balance is the direct result of contributions compounded by actual returns; weaker markets would have left him with less.

Example 7.8

A company adopts a cash balance plan. An employee asks if her benefit fluctuates with investment returns like a 401(k). What should she be told?

Show Solution

No — her hypothetical balance grows by the plan's stated credit formula, not actual investment performance. Despite the account-balance format, it is legally a defined benefit plan, and the employer bears the investment risk.

Example 7.9

A mature DB plan's retirees are living noticeably longer than assumed. Whose financial position is most directly affected, and how?

Show Solution

The plan sponsor. Since DB benefits are fixed and paid for life, longer lifespans mean more total payments than funded for, requiring additional employer contributions — a direct illustration of the employer bearing longevity risk. A DC sponsor would face no analogous shortfall, since its obligation ends once contributions are made.

Part VII Practice Problems

1. Explain why regulators require a policyowner to have insurable interest in the insured at policy issue.
Show Answer
It prevents life insurance from functioning as a wager on a stranger's death and reduces moral hazard — without it, any party could profit from a death they have no real stake in avoiding, creating a dangerous incentive.
2. A woman insures her own life. Must she separately demonstrate insurable interest?
Show Answer
No. A person is presumed to have unlimited insurable interest in their own life; no separate showing is required.
3. A landlord buys a $500,000 policy on a tenant's life, citing potential lost rent. Is this sufficient insurable interest?
Show Answer
No, or at most very little. A landlord-tenant relationship does not create the substantial financial dependency required; the potential loss (a few months' rent, easily replaced) is far too small to justify $500,000, so this would look like a wager rather than genuine insurable interest.
4. Contrast when insurable interest must exist for life insurance versus property insurance.
Show Answer
For life insurance, insurable interest generally only needs to exist at issue, not at death. For property insurance, it is generally required both at issue and at the time of loss, since the policy indemnifies the actual owner for an actual loss at that time.
5. Contrast the core risk life insurance transfers to the insurer versus the risk an annuity transfers.
Show Answer
Life insurance transfers the risk of dying too soon, paying a benefit on premature death. An annuity transfers longevity risk, guaranteeing income for as long as the annuitant lives.
6. A 30-year-old buys a deferred annuity, contributing from each paycheck for 35 years before drawing income at 65. Classify it by (a) timing of income and (b) premium structure.
Show Answer
(a) Deferred — income starts decades after purchase. (b) Flexible premium — funded by recurring contributions, not a lump sum.
7. Which coverage — disability income or long-term care insurance — fits each: (i) replacing a 40-year-old's paycheck after a disabling injury; (ii) helping an 82-year-old pay for assisted living due to dementia?
Show Answer
(i) Disability income insurance. (ii) Long-term care insurance.
8. A company moves new hires from a DB pension to a 401(k), citing predictable costs. What has changed for employees, in terms of risk?
Show Answer
Employees now bear both investment risk (their account depends on actual returns) and longevity risk (their own savings must last as long as they live, absent annuitization), whereas the employer previously bore both under the DB formula. The employer's cost becomes predictable because its obligation is capped at the agreed contribution.
9. Priya and Sam both retire after 25 years at a $90,000 final salary. Priya's DB plan pays 1.6% × years × final salary for life. Sam's DC account has grown to $480,000. Compute Priya's pension, and explain why Sam's retirement income cannot be stated as a single guaranteed figure.
Show Answer
Priya's pension = 0.016 × 25 × 90,000 = $36,000/year for life, fixed regardless of investment performance. Sam's $480,000 must still be converted into income; the sustainable amount depends on his withdrawal or annuitization choice, future returns, and how long he lives — none of which are fixed the way Priya's formula is.
10. Why is a cash balance plan called a "hybrid," and who legally bears the investment risk?
Show Answer
It communicates benefits using a DC-style hypothetical account, but is legally a defined benefit plan: the employer must fund the credited amounts regardless of actual investment performance, so the employer bears the investment risk, just as in a traditional DB plan.

Part VIII — Mortality Models (10–15%)

Every present-value calculation in this manual — the expected value of a death benefit, the price of an annuity, the reserve held against a policy — starts from a single question: how likely is this person to be alive, or to have died, at a given future time? This part builds the machinery for answering that question. We start from first principles with the future-lifetime random variable and its survival function, layer on the force of mortality and the four parametric mortality laws the exam expects you to know cold (De Moivre, constant force, Gompertz, Makeham), then switch to the discrete world of life tables and curtate lifetimes, fractional-age assumptions for interpolating between table ages, and finally select-and-ultimate tables, where mortality depends on time since selection as well as attained age. Master the notation here — tpx, eox, l[x]+k, and the rest — because Parts IX and X use it without re-deriving it every time. A reasonable way to read the five chapters that follow is as two related but distinct toolkits: Chapters 1–3 build the continuous and discrete probability theory for a single life in the abstract, while Chapters 4–5 show how that theory is actually implemented in practice against real, tabulated mortality data. Exam problems draw freely from both — sometimes handing you a clean formula for the survival function, sometimes a table excerpt with a handful of rows, and expecting the same underlying identities to apply either way.

1. Future Lifetime & the Survival Function

Learning Outcomes Covered: Define and interpret the future-lifetime random variable and the survival function; derive conditional survival and mortality probabilities from a survival model, including deferred mortality probabilities; apply the standard actuarial notation for these quantities.

1.1 The future lifetime of a newborn

Think of a single newborn life. Its age at death is a random variable — call it T0 (some texts write X). It is continuous, non-negative, and its distribution is what actuaries mean by a "mortality model" or "survival model." Rather than working with the density or the CDF directly, actuarial notation is built around the survival function:

Survival FunctionS(x) = S0(x) = P(T0 > x), for x ≥ 0

S(x) is the probability that a newborn lives past age x. It must satisfy S(0) = 1 (everyone is alive at birth) and it must be non-increasing in x (you can't become more likely to be alive as time passes). Most models used on this exam also send S(x) → 0 as x grows, either in the limit or at a finite terminal age ω ("omega"), beyond which survival is impossible. If F0(x) = 1 − S(x) is the CDF of T0 and it is differentiable, the density is f0(x) = F0′(x) = −S′(x).

1.2 The future lifetime of a life aged x

Almost everything in this manual concerns a life who has already survived to some age x — a policyholder at issue, say. Conditional on having reached age x, the remaining lifetime is a new random variable, written Tx or T(x), and read "the future lifetime of (x)." Formally, Tx = T0 − x given T0 > x. All of its probabilities are ordinary conditional probabilities built from S.

It helps to keep two mental pictures side by side. The first is the "newborn" picture: imagine a single survival curve S(x) traced out from birth to the terminal age, and every quantity for a life aged x is read off that one curve, just conditioned to start counting from x. The second is the "cohort" picture, used throughout Chapter 4: imagine a large group of newborns and watch how many of them are still alive at each age. Both pictures describe exactly the same mathematics — a parametric model such as Gompertz's law gives you the curve directly, while a life table gives you the cohort counts at integer ages, and Chapter 4 makes the bridge between the two explicit. Keeping both pictures available is useful because exam problems switch between them freely, sometimes handing you a formula for S(x) and sometimes handing you a table of lx values, and expecting you to recognize that the same identities apply either way.

1.3 Core notation: tpx and tqx

Actuarial notation places a left subscript for the time period in question and a right subscript for the age of the life. Reading tpx left to right: "the probability that (a life age) x (survives) t (more years)." When the left subscript is 1, it is simply omitted: px means 1px.

t-year survival probabilitytpx = P(Tx > t) = S(x+t) / S(x)
t-year mortality probabilitytqx = P(Tx ≤ t) = 1 − tpx

The division by S(x) in the definition of tpx is exactly the conditioning: we are asking for survival to age x+t given survival to age x already occurred. A useful consequence is the chain rule for survival probabilities: since S(x+s+t)/S(x) = [S(x+s+t)/S(x+s)]·[S(x+s)/S(x)], we get

Chain rules+tpx = spx · tpx+s

i.e., surviving s+t years total equals surviving the first s years, then (given that) surviving the next t years starting from the new, older age.

1.4 Deferred mortality probabilities

Often we care about a death that occurs neither immediately nor "eventually," but specifically within a window that starts after some deferral period. The notation stacks a deferral period before the vertical bar:

Deferred mortality probabilityu|tqx = P(u < Tx ≤ u+t) = upx − u+tpx = upx · tqx+u

Read u|tqx as: "(x) survives u years, then dies within the following t years." When t = 1 the trailing subscript is dropped and it is written u|qx (some texts write u|qx as u|qx — both mean the same one-year deferred probability). The middle expression above, upx − u+tpx, is just "probability of surviving past the start of the window minus probability of surviving past the end of it" — the probability mass that falls strictly inside the window. The right-hand expression, upx·tqx+u, restates the same thing as "survive to the start of the window, then die within it" and is usually the more convenient form for computation.

Deferred probabilities are not just notational bookkeeping — they are exactly what a term insurance policy pays on. A policy that only covers deaths occurring, say, between the fifth and tenth policy years is paying a benefit contingent on the event 5 < Tx ≤ 10, which is precisely a deferred mortality probability, 5|5qx. Part IX builds insurance present-value formulas directly on top of quantities like this one, so it is worth building genuine comfort with the "survive-then-die-within-a-window" reading now, rather than treating it as an isolated formula to memorize.

Reading the subscripts. Left subscript = duration/time span being measured; right subscript = the age you are measuring from. 10p45 is a 10-year survival probability for someone currently 45 — it says nothing about anyone aged 10 or 55 in isolation. When no left subscript appears (as in plain px or qx), the time span is always exactly one year.
Example 8.1

A newborn's survival function is given by S0(x) = (100 − x)/100 for 0 ≤ x ≤ 100. Find 20p30, 10q40, and 5|10q20.

Show Solution

S0(30) = 70/100, S0(50) = 50/100, S0(40) = 60/100, S0(25) = 75/100, S0(35) = 65/100, S0(20) = 80/100.

20p30 = S0(50)/S0(30) = 50/70 = 0.714286

10q40 = 1 − S0(50)/S0(40) = 1 − 50/60 = 0.166667

5|10q20 = [S0(25) − S0(35)]/S0(20) = (0.75 − 0.65)/0.80 = 0.125

The next examples work directly from a life table — a tabulation of lx, the expected number of survivors to age x out of some starting cohort (this idea is developed fully in Chapter 4; for now, just use tpx = lx+t/lx, the discrete analog of S(x+t)/S(x)).

Table 8.1 — Life table excerpt, ages 60–65
x606162636465
lx100,00099,00097,80096,40094,80093,000
Example 8.2

Using Table 8.1, find 3p60, 2q62, and 1|2q61.

Show Solution

3p60 = l63/l60 = 96,400/100,000 = 0.964

2q62 = 1 − l64/l62 = 1 − 94,800/97,800 = 0.030675

1|2q61 = (l62 − l64)/l61 = (97,800 − 94,800)/99,000 = 3,000/99,000 = 0.030303

Example 8.3

Verify the identity 1|2q61 = 1p61 · 2q62 using Table 8.1.

Show Solution

1p61 = l62/l61 = 97,800/99,000 = 0.987879. From Example 8.2, 2q62 = 0.030675.

Product: 0.987879 × 0.030675 = 0.030303, matching the deferred probability computed directly in Example 8.2. The two computation routes agree, as the identity requires.

Example 8.4

For a certain life, qx = 0.02, qx+1 = 0.025, and qx+2 = 0.03. Find 3px, 2qx, and 2|qx.

Show Solution

px = 0.98, px+1 = 0.975, px+2 = 0.97.

3px = 0.98 × 0.975 × 0.97 = 0.926985

2qx = 1 − 2px = 1 − (0.98 × 0.975) = 1 − 0.9555 = 0.0445

2|qx = 2px · qx+2 = 0.9555 × 0.03 = 0.028665

Common trap. tqx is not the same as t · qx in general — that shortcut only becomes valid under the uniform-distribution-of-deaths fractional-age assumption covered in Chapter 4, and even then only for 0 ≤ t ≤ 1. Don't apply it to multi-year spans or without stating the assumption.

2. Force of Mortality & Parametric Survival Models

Learning Outcomes Covered: Define the force of mortality and relate it to the survival function and to survival probabilities; work with the standard parametric mortality laws (De Moivre, constant force, Gompertz, Makeham); compute moments of future lifetime, including the complete expectation of life and its variance.

2.1 Definition

The force of mortality at exact age x, written μ(x), is the instantaneous rate of mortality at that exact instant — the continuous-time analog of a "hazard rate." Formally,

Force of mortalityμ(x) = limh→0 P(T0 ≤ x+h | T0 > x)/h = −S′(x)/S(x) = f0(x)/S(x)

Unlike qx, which is a probability bounded between 0 and 1, μ(x) is a rate and can exceed 1 at very old ages. It plays the same role here that a hazard function plays in survival analysis generally.

A useful way to build intuition for μ(x) is to read the definition as saying that, for a small interval of length h, P(x < T0 ≤ x+h | T0 > x) ≈ μ(x)·h. In other words, μ(x) is the "instantaneous annualized death rate" a life currently faces at that exact instant — multiply it by a short span of time and you get an approximate probability of dying in that span. Human mortality, plotted as a function of age, has a well-known shape: relatively high right after birth, falling to a minimum somewhere in the pre-teen years, then climbing roughly exponentially through adulthood into old age. The four laws below are stylized attempts to capture pieces of that shape with a small number of parameters — none of them (with the partial exception of Makeham) attempts to model the early-life decline, since exam problems and pricing applications mostly concern adult and senior ages where mortality is rising.

2.2 Recovering S(x) and tpx from μ

Since μ(x) = −d/dx[ln S(x)], integrating both sides from 0 to x (using ln S(0) = ln 1 = 0) gives the survival function purely in terms of the force of mortality:

Survival function from μS(x) = exp[ −∫0x μ(s) ds ]

The same argument applied to ln(tpx) = ln S(x+t) − ln S(x), differentiated with respect to t, gives d/dt[ln tpx] = −μ(x+t), and integrating from 0 to t:

t-year survival from μtpx = exp[ −∫0t μ(x+s) ds ]

It is also worth recording the density of Tx in terms of these pieces, since it is needed for the moment calculations below: differentiating tpx with respect to t gives fx(t) = tpx·μ(x+t) — survive to duration t, then die in the next instant.

2.3 The four standard parametric laws

The exam expects fluency with four named force-of-mortality shapes. Each is defined by its μ(x); the survival function follows by direct integration. It's worth noticing a pattern across all four derivations before working through them individually: in every case the recipe is (1) write down μ(x), (2) integrate it from 0 to x to get S(x) via the boxed formula from §2.2, and (3) form S(x+t)/S(x) and simplify. The four models differ only in how complicated step (2) is — De Moivre and constant force integrate to simple closed forms in a line or two, while Gompertz and Makeham require the exponential integral ∫csds = cs/ln c.

Table 8.2 — Standard parametric mortality laws
Modelμ(x)tpx
De Moivre (uniform)1 / (ω − x),  0 ≤ x < ω(ω − x − t) / (ω − x)
Constant forceμ  (constant)e−μt
GompertzBcx,  B>0, c>1exp[ −Bcx(ct−1)/ln c ]
MakehamA + Bcx,  c>1exp[ −At − Bcx(ct−1)/ln c ]

De Moivre. With μ(x) = 1/(ω−x), ∫0x 1/(ω−s) ds = ln[ω/(ω−x)], so S(x) = (ω−x)/ω — future lifetime of a newborn is literally uniform on (0, ω). This is the simplest model on the exam and often used to build intuition before tackling harder ones.

Constant force. With μ(x) = μ for all x, S(x) = e−μx, and tpx = e−μt — notice this does not depend on x at all. Future lifetime under constant force is exponentially distributed and memoryless: a 90-year-old and a 20-year-old face the identical probability of surviving the next 5 years. That's an unrealistic feature for a lifetime lifetime model, which is precisely why it's used mainly as a short-run approximation (e.g., across a single year of a life table, Chapter 4) rather than a model of a whole life.

Gompertz. Force of mortality grows exponentially with age, μ(x) = Bcx, which fits observed adult mortality far better than a constant. Integrating: ∫0t Bcx+s ds = Bcx∫0t cs ds = Bcx(ct−1)/ln c, giving the tpx formula in Table 8.2.

Makeham. Adds a constant "background" hazard A (accident, non-senescent causes) on top of a Gompertz term: μ(x) = A + Bcx. Integrating the constant piece contributes At to the exponent alongside the Gompertz piece.

It's worth pausing on what the parameters are doing. In Gompertz's law, c controls how fast mortality accelerates with age (values a little above 1, such as 1.08–1.12, are typical for adult human mortality over a decade of age) while B is mostly a scaling constant that sets the overall level. Makeham's extra term A is meant to capture causes of death that don't get meaningfully more likely as you age — accidents being the classic example — so that the model separates "background risk" from "aging risk." Historically Makeham's law was found to fit observed adult mortality data noticeably better than Gompertz's alone, which is why it, rather than pure Gompertz, became the more commonly cited law in traditional actuarial practice, even though both remain on the syllabus and both are tested.

For Gompertz and Makeham, the algebra is always the same three steps: (1) compute cx and ct (watch for large exponents — use logs and re-exponentiate rather than a calculator's power key blindly), (2) form cx(ct−1)/ln c, (3) multiply by B (and add At for Makeham), then exponentiate the negative of the whole thing. Keep at least 5–6 significant figures in cx — small rounding errors there get amplified.
Example 8.5

Mortality follows De Moivre's law with ω = 105. For a life age 25, find 30p25 and the complete expectation of life eo25 (moments are covered in §2.4 below; the De Moivre result eox = (ω−x)/2 is derived there).

Show Solution

30p25 = (105 − 25 − 30)/(105 − 25) = 50/80 = 0.625

eo25 = (105 − 25)/2 = 40 years.

Example 8.6

Mortality follows a constant force μ = 0.03 for all ages. Find 5p40 and the age t such that a life age 40 has a 50% chance of surviving t more years (the median future lifetime).

Show Solution

5p40 = e−0.03(5) = e−0.15 = 0.860708

Solve e−0.03t = 0.5 ⟹ t = ln(2)/0.03 = 23.105 years. (Note this doesn't depend on starting at age 40 — memorylessness again.)

Example 8.7

Mortality follows Gompertz's law with B = 0.00002, c = 1.09. Find 5p60.

Show Solution

ln c = ln(1.09) = 0.086178. Compute c60 = 1.0960 = 176.09 and c5 = 1.095 = 1.538624.

Exponent: −Bc60(c5−1)/ln c = −(0.00002)(176.09)(0.538624)/0.086178 = −0.022014

5p60 = e−0.022014 = 0.97823

Example 8.8

Mortality follows Makeham's law with A = 0.0007, B = 0.00005, c = 1.1. Find 5p40.

Show Solution

ln c = ln(1.1) = 0.095310. Compute c40 = 1.140 = 45.259, c5 = 1.15 = 1.61051.

Gompertz-type piece: Bc40(c5−1)/ln c = (0.00005)(45.259)(0.61051)/0.095310 = 0.014497

Add the Makeham constant piece At = 0.0007 × 5 = 0.0035. Total exponent: −(0.0035 + 0.014497) = −0.017997

5p40 = e−0.017997 = 0.98216

2.4 Complete expectation of life and its moments

The complete expectation of life, eox (an "e" with a small circle, read "e-circle-x," to distinguish it from the curtate expectation introduced in Chapter 3), is simply E[Tx] — the expected number of years (x) has left, counting fractional years. It is computed with a clean integral of survival probabilities rather than the density:

Complete expectation of lifeeox = E[Tx] = ∫0∞ tpx dt

This follows from integration by parts. Starting from E[Tx] = ∫0∞ t·fx(t) dt and using fx(t) = −d/dt[tpx]: let u = t, dv = −d(tpx). Then E[Tx] = [−t·tpx]0∞ + ∫0∞ tpx dt. The boundary term vanishes at both ends (it's 0 at t=0, and for any well-behaved survival model t·tpx → 0 as t → ∞), leaving exactly the integral above. The same integration-by-parts trick, applied one power higher, gives the second moment:

Second moment of TxE[Tx²] = 2∫0∞ t·tpx dt,   Var(Tx) = E[Tx²] − (eox)²

For a model with finite terminal age ω, replace the upper limit ∞ with ω − x throughout (survival is impossible beyond that).

eox is the single number most often quoted to summarize "how long a life is expected to live," and it will reappear constantly in Parts IX and X as the building block for whole-life annuity and insurance values under a continuous (fully continuous) payment structure. It is worth noting explicitly that eox is a mean, not a median — for a right-skewed lifetime distribution (which most realistic mortality models are, at least at younger ages) the median remaining lifetime can differ noticeably from eox, as Example 8.6 illustrates for the constant-force case. Don't assume a life has "even odds" of surviving to age x + eox — that is true only for special distributions, not in general.

Example 8.9

Mortality follows De Moivre's law with ω = 100. For a life age 20, find eo20 and Var(T20).

Show Solution

Let n = ω − x = 80. Then tp20 = (80−t)/80 for 0≤t≤80 — this is exactly the survival function of a Uniform(0, 80) random variable, so T20 ~ Uniform(0, 80) directly.

eo20 = ∫080 (80−t)/80 dt = n/2 = 40 years — in general, for De Moivre, eox = (ω−x)/2.

Using the Uniform(0,80) variance formula n²/12: Var(T20) = 80²/12 = 6400/12 = 533.33.

Two shortcuts worth memorizing outright, since they recur constantly in Parts IX–X: for De Moivre with parameter ω, eox = (ω−x)/2 and Var(Tx) = (ω−x)²/12. For constant force μ, eox = 1/μ and Var(Tx) = 1/μ², because Tx is exactly Exponential(μ). Gompertz and Makeham have no such closed form — their moments must be found by numerical integration.

3. Curtate Future Lifetime & Actuarial Notation

Learning Outcomes Covered: Define curtate future lifetime and derive its probability mass function; compute the curtate expectation of life and its variance; apply the full standard set of actuarial survival-model notation.

3.1 From continuous to discrete: Kx

So far every quantity has come from the continuous random variable Tx. But a great deal of real insurance business runs on an annual clock: premiums are typically due once a year, annuity payments to a retiree are commonly paid once a year, and even when a death benefit is paid immediately on death, many valuation formulas approximate it using an annual grid for tractability. That annual structure calls for a discrete counterpart to Tx — one that records whole years survived rather than the exact fractional instant of death. Many actuarial calculations — especially where benefits or premiums are paid on policy anniversaries rather than continuously — only need to know the number of complete years a life survives. That quantity is the curtate future lifetime:

Curtate future lifetimeKx = ⌊Tx⌋  (the greatest integer ≤ Tx)

Kx takes values 0, 1, 2, … and represents the number of full years (x) completes before dying — e.g., Kx=7 means (x) died sometime during the 8th year after age x, having completed exactly 7 full years. Its distribution follows directly from the survival probabilities of Chapter 1:

pmf of KxP(Kx = k) = kpx − k+1px = kpx · qx+k,   k = 0, 1, 2, …

This is a deferred one-year mortality probability in disguise: P(Kx=k) = k|qx — survive k years, then die in the next one.

3.2 Curtate expectation of life

Curtate expectation of lifeex = E[Kx] = Σk=1∞ kpx

This sum-of-survival-probabilities form mirrors the integral form of eox in Chapter 2, and follows from the same general fact used there: for a non-negative integer-valued random variable K, E[K] = Σk=1∞ P(K ≥ k). Since Kx ≥ k exactly when Tx ≥ k (for integer k), P(Kx≥k) = kpx, giving the formula above.

The same identity extended one step further gives the second moment. Using Σk=1∞ k·P(K≥k) = ½(E[K²]+E[K]) (group terms by which outcome j contributes to which k ≤ j), we get:

Variance of KxE[Kx²] = 2Σk=1∞ k·kpx − ex,   Var(Kx) = E[Kx²] − ex²

Var(Kx) is not just an abstract moment — it measures how spread out the timing of death is around its mean, which is exactly what drives the uncertainty (and hence the risk) in a single policy's payout timing. A life with a small Var(Kx) has a fairly predictable death year; a life with a large one could plausibly die soon or live decades longer, and that extra uncertainty is precisely what pools of policies — and the reserving techniques built on the normal approximation in later parts of this manual — are designed to absorb.

3.3 Relating eox and ex

Tx = Kx + Rx, where Rx is the fraction of the year of death already lived, 0 ≤ Rx < 1. Under the uniform-distribution-of-deaths assumption developed formally in Chapter 4, deaths are spread evenly across each year of age, so on average a life who dies partway through a year does so at the year's midpoint: E[Rx] ≈ 0.5. This gives the widely used approximation:

UDD approximationeox ≈ ex + 0.5

It is only an approximation (exact under UDD, close otherwise) — never treat it as an identity when a problem gives you an exact parametric model instead.

3.4 Notation reference table

By this point a fair amount of notation has piled up, and future parts of this manual will use all of it without re-explaining it. The table below collects every symbol introduced so far (plus the life-table and select-table symbols from Chapters 4–5) in one place for quick reference — it is worth returning to this table whenever a formula in a later part uses a symbol you don't immediately recognize.

Table 8.3 — Actuarial survival-model notation glossary
SymbolMeaning
S(x), S0(x)Probability a newborn survives past age x
T0Future lifetime (age at death) of a newborn
Tx, T(x)Future lifetime of a life currently age x (continuous)
tpxProbability (x) survives at least t more years
tqxProbability (x) dies within t years
px, qxOne-year survival / death probability (t = 1 implied)
u|tqx, u|qxDeferred mortality probability: survive u years, then die within the next t years (or next 1 year)
μ(x)Force of mortality — instantaneous death rate at exact age x
KxCurtate future lifetime — complete years (x) survives (integer)
exCurtate expectation of life, E[Kx]
eoxComplete expectation of life, E[Tx]
ωLimiting/terminal age of the model (survival is 0 beyond it), if finite
lxExpected number of survivors to age x in a life-table cohort
dxExpected deaths between ages x and x+1, = lx − lx+1
l[x]Number of lives selected (e.g. newly underwritten) at exact age x
l[x]+kSurvivors k years after selection at age x (attained age x+k, duration k)
Example 8.10

A small illustrative table has l60=100{,}000, and (for this problem only) no one survives past age 66, so l61,…,l65 = 99{,}000,\ 97{,}800,\ 96{,}400,\ 94{,}800,\ 93{,}000 and l66=0. Find e60 and P(K60=2).

Show Solution

Since no one survives past 66, K60 can only take values 0–5, and the sum for e60 runs k=1 to 5:

1p60=0.99, 2p60=0.978, 3p60=0.964, 4p60=0.948, 5p60=0.93

e60 = 0.99+0.978+0.964+0.948+0.93 = 4.81 years

P(K60=2) = 2p60 − 3p60 = 0.978 − 0.964 = 0.014

Example 8.11

Using the same truncated table as Example 8.10, find Var(K60).

Show Solution

Compute Σ k·kp60 for k = 1 to 5: 1(0.99)+2(0.978)+3(0.964)+4(0.948)+5(0.93) = 0.99+1.956+2.892+3.792+4.65 = 14.28

E[K60²] = 2(14.28) − 4.81 = 28.56 − 4.81 = 23.75

Var(K60) = 23.75 − 4.81² = 23.75 − 23.1361 = 0.6139

Example 8.12

Confirm that the pmf values for K60 in Examples 8.10–8.11's table sum to 1.

Show Solution

P(K=0)=1−0.99=0.010, P(K=1)=0.99−0.978=0.012, P(K=2)=0.014 (above), P(K=3)=0.964−0.948=0.016, P(K=4)=0.948−0.93=0.018, P(K=5)=0.93−0=0.930.

Sum: 0.010+0.012+0.014+0.016+0.018+0.930 = 1.000 ✓ — note how the mass piles up heavily at K=5 because the table forces everyone left alive at 65 to die before 66.

Example 8.13

A mortality study estimates eo50 = 32.7 years. Estimate e50 using the standard approximation.

Show Solution

e50 ≈ eo50 − 0.5 = 32.7 − 0.5 = 32.2 years.

Complete vs. curtate — don't mix them up. eox counts fractional years and is always the larger of the two (by roughly half a year); ex only counts whole completed years. A premium or reserve formula that calls for ex and is fed eox by mistake will be off by about 0.5 — small-looking, but exam answer choices are frequently spaced closer than that specifically to catch this error.

4. Life Tables & Fractional Age Assumptions

Learning Outcomes Covered: Use a life table's lx and dx columns to compute survival and mortality probabilities directly; apply the uniform-distribution-of-deaths and constant-force fractional-age assumptions to obtain probabilities and forces of mortality at non-integer ages.

4.1 The life table as a tabular survival model

A life table starts from an arbitrary starting cohort size l0 (the "radix," often 100,000) and tabulates lx, the expected number of members of that cohort still alive at each integer age x. Formally lx = l0·S0(x) — the life table is nothing more than a survival function S0, rescaled to a convenient cohort size and evaluated only at integer ages. Everything from Chapters 1–3 carries over unchanged; the table just replaces the formula for S with a lookup.

The choice of radix is arbitrary and carries no information by itself — a table built with l0=100{,}000 and one built with l0=1{,}000{,}000 from the same underlying mortality experience describe the identical survival model, since every probability calculation is a ratio of two l values and the radix cancels out. What does carry information is how the table was built: a table can represent a single real generation of people followed from birth to extinction (a "cohort" or "generation" table), or it can represent a hypothetical population built by applying one calendar year's age-specific mortality rates across all ages at once (a "period" table). Exam problems almost always intend the latter, simpler interpretation — a static table of rates, applied consistently — and that is the interpretation used throughout this manual.

Deaths and survival from lxdx = lx − lx+1,   npx = lx+n/lx,   nqx = (lx−lx+n)/lx = (dx+dx+1+⋯+dx+n−1)/lx

dx is the expected number of deaths between exact ages x and x+1. All the machinery of Chapters 1 and 3 — chain rule, deferred probabilities, Kx's pmf, ex — applies with S(x+t)/S(x) replaced by lx+t/lx whenever t is an integer.

lx and dx are expected values, not head counts. Because lx = l0·S0(x) and S0 is generally not a "nice" fraction, real published life tables show non-integer values like l62 = 97{,}812.4 — there is nothing wrong with a fractional number of "survivors" here, since lx is best understood as the expected size of the surviving population, not a literal count of specific people. The clean whole numbers used in this manual's examples are chosen purely to keep the arithmetic easy to follow.

4.2 Why fractional ages need an assumption

A standard life table only gives lx at integer ages. But real problems routinely ask for things like 0.4q61 or μ(61.5) — quantities strictly between tabulated ages. This is not merely a theoretical nuisance: insurance policies are issued on arbitrary calendar dates, valuations happen at arbitrary points in a policy year, and a life who is exactly 61 years and 5 months old at a valuation date needs a fractional-age probability to be handled correctly. Because the table alone doesn't tell us the shape of the survival curve within a year, we must adopt an explicit interpolation rule. The exam uses two standard ones.

4.3 Uniform distribution of deaths (UDD)

UDD assumes that, within each single year of age, deaths are spread uniformly — equivalently, that lx+s is a straight line connecting lx to lx+1 for 0 ≤ s ≤ 1:

UDD: linear interpolation of lxlx+s = lx − s·dx = (1−s)lx + s·lx+1,   0 ≤ s ≤ 1

Dividing through by lx gives the single most-used fractional-age formula on the exam:

UDD: fractional mortality probabilitysqx = 1 − lx+s/lx = s·dx/lx = s·qx,   0 ≤ s ≤ 1

Under UDD, the force of mortality is not constant within the year — it actually increases as s increases, even though deaths are uniformly spread. Differentiating ln(spx) = ln(1 − sqx) with respect to s gives:

UDD: force of mortality within the yearμ(x+s) = qx / (1 − s·qx),   0 ≤ s < 1

This is small at s=0 (equal to qx itself) and grows toward qx/(1−qx) as s→1 — makes sense, since under UDD the pool of survivors shrinks steadily through the year while the (constant) number of deaths per unit time stays fixed, so the death rate relative to the shrinking pool must rise.

4.4 Constant force fractional-age assumption

The alternative assumption treats the force of mortality as constant, not the deaths as uniform, within each year — equal to whatever value makes the full-year survival probability come out to px:

Constant force within the yearμ(x+s) = μx = −ln(px)  (constant for 0≤s<1);   spx = (px)s = e−sμx

Under this assumption, sqx = 1 − (px)s, which is generally close to — but not exactly equal to — the UDD value s·qx, especially when qx is small. The two assumptions coincide only in the trivial case qx=0.

Neither assumption is "more correct" in any absolute sense — both are approximations of an unknown true within-year mortality pattern, chosen for algebraic convenience rather than biological realism. UDD tends to be the default choice for computations built around a life table (it makes sqx and related quantities linear in s, which keeps downstream formulas simple), while the constant-force assumption is sometimes preferred when a problem is more naturally phrased in terms of the force of mortality itself, since it avoids the awkward feature that UDD's μ(x+s) is not constant. On this exam, always take your cue from what the problem states explicitly — if it doesn't say, UDD is the more commonly intended default.

Example 8.14

Using Table 8.1 (§1.3), find 0.4q61 and 0.7p62 under UDD.

Show Solution

q61 = d61/l61 = 1{,}200/99{,}000 = 0.0121212; 0.4q61 = 0.4(0.0121212) = 0.0048485

q62 = d62/l62 = 1{,}400/97{,}800 = 0.0143150; 0.7p62 = 1 − 0.7(0.0143150) = 1 − 0.0100205 = 0.9899795

Example 8.15

Repeat Example 8.14's two calculations under the constant-force assumption, and compare.

Show Solution

p61 = 1 − 0.0121212 = 0.9878788. ln(p61) = −0.0121947. 0.4p61 = (p61)0.4 = e0.4(−0.0121947) = e−0.0048779 = 0.995135, so 0.4q61 = 0.004865 — compare to the UDD value 0.0048485; nearly the same, but not identical.

p62 = 1 − 0.0143150 = 0.9856850. ln(p62) = −0.0144175. 0.7p62 = e0.7(−0.0144175) = e−0.0100923 = 0.989960 — compare to the UDD value 0.9899795; again close but not equal.

Both comparisons show the two assumptions nearly agreeing because qx is small here (roughly 1%–1.5%). The gap widens noticeably at older ages where qx is larger — see Example 8.17's companion problem in the practice set for a case with more separation.

Example 8.16

Using q61 = 0.0121212 from Table 8.1, find μ(61.5) under UDD and compare it to the constant-force value μ61 = −ln(p61) used across the whole year.

Show Solution

UDD: μ(61.5) = q61/(1−0.5q61) = 0.0121212/(1−0.0060606) = 0.0121212/0.9939394 = 0.0121953

Constant force (flat across the year): μ61 = −ln(0.9878788) = 0.0121947

The two nearly coincide at the midpoint of the year (s=0.5) — this is not a coincidence: UDD's μ(x+s) rises from qx=0.0121212 at s=0 to qx/(1−qx)=0.012272 at s=1, and its midpoint value is close to the average of those two endpoints, which is close to the constant-force value. Away from the midpoint the two assumptions diverge more.

Example 8.17

For a life age x, qx = 0.05. Under UDD, find s such that sqx = 0.02.

Show Solution

Under UDD, sqx = s·qx, so s = 0.02/0.05 = 0.4.

UDD ≠ constant force within the year — a frequent point of confusion. "Uniform distribution of deaths" describes the deaths as spread evenly across the year (linear lx+s), which actually makes μ(x+s) increase over the year. The constant-force fractional-age assumption instead holds μ(x+s) flat and lets deaths cluster more heavily early in the year (since the survivor pool is largest then). These are two different, competing approximations for the same missing information — always use the one the problem specifies, and don't assume "uniform" means "constant."
Rule of thumb for checking your own work: whichever assumption you use, spx must equal exactly px when s=1 and exactly 1 when s=0. If your fractional-age formula fails either boundary check, you've mis-applied it.

5. Select and Ultimate Life Tables

Learning Outcomes Covered: Understand why mortality depends on time since selection as well as attained age; use select-and-ultimate life table notation to compute probabilities for lives within the select period, beyond it, and spanning the boundary between the two.

5.1 Why selection matters

A life who just passed medical underwriting for a new insurance policy is, on average, healthier than a randomly chosen person of the same age drawn from the general population — someone who might have been insured (and medically screened) years or decades earlier, or never at all. This "just screened" effect is called selection, and it fades with time: five years after underwriting, a 55-year-old who was selected at 50 looks mortality-wise much like any other 55-year-old, but a 55-year-old selected last month looks noticeably healthier. A select-and-ultimate life table captures this by tracking mortality separately by duration since selection, for a limited window, before merging into a single attained-age-only ("ultimate") table.

Ignoring the select effect would systematically understate the mortality of a block of newly issued policies relative to an average-age-matched population, and pricing without it would leave an insurer charging too little for the healthiest, most-recently-underwritten lives. Select periods used in practice vary by product and jurisdiction — some tables use as little as a 1-year select period, others extend to 10, 15, or even 25 years for certain products where medical underwriting effects are believed to persist a long time — but the exam typically works with short, clearly stated select periods (often 1 to 3 years) so that the arithmetic stays manageable. Whatever the stated period, the modeling idea is identical: track mortality by duration until the stated cutoff, then fall back to attained-age-only rates.

5.2 Select notation

A life who is selected (e.g., underwritten) at exact age x is denoted [x]. The bracket travels with the life as it ages: k years after selection, this life is at attained age x+k but is still labeled by where it started, written [x]+k — read "select at age x, duration k."

Select table quantitiesl[x] = lives just selected at age x;   l[x]+k = survivors k years later (attained age x+k);   kp[x] = l[x]+k / l[x]

The table has a fixed select period, n years, after which the select effect is assumed to have fully worn off. From duration n onward, mortality depends only on attained age, and the select notation simply reverts to ordinary (bracket-free) notation:

Merging into the ultimate tablel[x]+k = lx+k  (ordinary, "ultimate" table value)   for all k ≥ n

Because more-recently-selected lives are typically healthier than less-recently-selected lives of the same attained age, published select-and-ultimate tables generally show l[y−k]+k (attained age y, duration k) decreasing as k increases, for fixed y — the least-recently-selected column (duration n, the ultimate column) has the heaviest mortality of all durations at that attained age. This is a typical pattern the exam expects you to recognize, not a mathematical law that must hold in every table.

5.3 Computing across the select/ultimate boundary

Nothing new is required mathematically to compute select-table probabilities — you simply look up the right column. The only subtlety is bookkeeping: track whether the duration involved in your calculation is still within the select period (use the bracketed column) or has passed n years since selection (use the ultimate column, i.e., an ordinary lx+k). A single survival or mortality probability can straddle the two: part of the time period may occur while still "select," part after merging into "ultimate."

A practical habit that avoids errors on these problems: before computing anything, write down the selection age x, the select period n, and every duration k that appears in the quantity you need (both endpoints of any deferred probability). For each such k, decide up front whether it is select (k < n) or ultimate (k ≥ n) before touching the table — this turns what looks like a special "boundary" case into an ordinary lookup problem, since the survival-probability machinery from Chapter 1 (ratios of l-values, chain rule, deferred-probability decomposition) doesn't care which column each value came from.

Table 8.4 — Select-and-ultimate excerpt, 2-year select period
xl[x]l[x]+1lx+2x+2
60100,00099,60098,80062
6199,30098,70097,60063
6298,20097,50096,20064
6397,00096,30094,60065

The rightmost column is already the ultimate table, continuing (beyond what's needed to build the select rows above) with l66=92{,}800 and l67=90{,}800 for later reference. Since the select period is 2 years, l[x]+2 = lx+2 by construction — that's why the third column is already labeled without brackets.

Example 8.18

Using Table 8.4, compare the one-year mortality rate for a life selected at 61 (duration 0, i.e., q[61], covering attained ages 61–62) against the one-year rate for a life selected at 60 who is now at duration 1 (q[60]+1, also covering attained ages 61–62).

Show Solution

q[61] = (l[61] − l[61]+1)/l[61] = (99{,}300−98{,}700)/99{,}300 = 600/99{,}300 = 0.0060423

q[60]+1 = (l[60]+1 − l62)/l[60]+1 = (99{,}600−98{,}800)/99{,}600 = 800/99{,}600 = 0.0080321

Both cover the same attained-age interval (61 to 62), but q[61] < q[60]+1: the life selected one year ago (duration 1) has higher mortality than the life selected this instant (duration 0), exactly the fading-selection pattern described in §5.2.

Example 8.19

Using Table 8.4, find 3p[61] — the probability a life selected at age 61 survives 3 more years.

Show Solution

Duration 3 exceeds the 2-year select period, so l[61]+3 = l64 = 96{,}200 (read off the ultimate table at attained age 64).

3p[61] = l64/l[61] = 96{,}200/99{,}300 = 0.968782

Example 8.20

A life is selected at age 60. Find 1|2q[60] — the probability this life survives 1 year (to duration 1, still inside the select period), then dies within the following 2 years (i.e., sometime between duration 1 and duration 3, a window that crosses the duration-2 boundary where the table merges into ultimate).

Show Solution

We need l[60]+1 (duration 1 — still select, from the table directly: 99,600) and l[60]+3 (duration 3 — beyond the select period, so this equals the ultimate value l63 = 97{,}600).

1|2q[60] = (l[60]+1 − l[60]+3)/l[60] = (99{,}600 − 97{,}600)/100{,}000 = 2{,}000/100{,}000 = 0.02

This calculation genuinely spans the boundary: the starting point of the window (duration 1) is read from the select column, and the ending point (duration 3) is read from the ultimate column, but the formula itself needs no special adjustment — just correct lookups at each endpoint.

Example 8.21

Using Table 8.4 and the UDD fractional-age assumption from Chapter 4, find 0.5q[60].

Show Solution

Fractional-age assumptions apply to select tables exactly as they do to ultimate tables — just apply UDD within the relevant select year. First, q[60] = (100{,}000−99{,}600)/100{,}000 = 0.004.

Under UDD, 0.5q[60] = 0.5 × q[60] = 0.5 × 0.004 = 0.002.

Reading select-period length from a table. The select period is however many bracketed columns the table publishes before it switches to plain lx notation. In Table 8.4 there are two bracketed columns (l[x] and l[x]+1) before the ultimate column — hence a 2-year select period. SOA tables commonly use select periods of 1, 2, 3, 5, or 10 years depending on context; always read the period off the table structure rather than assuming a standard length.
Common trap. Once duration reaches the select period n, every life at that attained age — regardless of when it was selected — shares the same ultimate mortality. It's tempting but wrong to keep writing l[x]+n as if it depends on x; by definition l[x]+n = lx+n, identical to a life selected at any other age who has also reached attained age x+n.

Part VIII Practice Problems

1. A newborn's survival function is S0(x) = (110−x)/110 for 0≤x≤110. Find 15p45 and 10q70.
Show Answer

S0(60)=50/110, S0(45)=65/110, so 15p45 = 50/65 = 0.769231.

S0(80)=30/110, S0(70)=40/110, so 10p70 = 30/40 = 0.75, hence 10q70 = 0.25.

2. For a life age x, qx=0.01, qx+1=0.015, qx+2=0.02. Find 2|1qx — the probability of surviving 2 years and then dying in the third.
Show Answer

px=0.99, px+1=0.985. 2px=0.99×0.985=0.97515.

2|1qx = 2px·qx+2 = 0.97515×0.02 = 0.019503

3. A life table shows l50=95{,}000, l51=94{,}200, l52=93{,}300, l53=92{,}300. Find 3p50 and 1|1q51.
Show Answer

3p50 = l53/l50 = 92{,}300/95{,}000 = 0.971579

1|1q51 = (l52−l53)/l51 = (93{,}300−92{,}300)/94{,}200 = 1{,}000/94{,}200 = 0.010616

4. Mortality follows a constant force μ=0.02 for all ages. Find 25px (for any x) and eox.
Show Answer

25px = e−0.02(25) = e−0.5 = 0.606531 (same for every starting age x, by memorylessness).

eox = 1/μ = 1/0.02 = 50 years.

5. Mortality follows De Moivre's law with ω=100. For a life age 50, find 20p50, eo50, and Var(T50).
Show Answer

20p50 = (100−50−20)/(100−50) = 30/50 = 0.6

eo50 = (100−50)/2 = 25 years

Var(T50) = 50²/12 = 2500/12 = 208.33

6. Mortality follows Gompertz's law with B=0.00002, c=1.09. Find 5p60.
Show Answer

ln c = 0.086178, c60=176.09, c5=1.538624.

Exponent: −(0.00002)(176.09)(0.538624)/0.086178 = −0.022014

5p60 = e−0.022014 = 0.97823

7. For a certain survival model, tpx = (1 − t/40)² for 0≤t≤40. Find eox.
Show Answer

eox = ∫040 (1−t/40)² dt. Using the antiderivative F(t) = −(40/3)(1−t/40)³: F(40)−F(0) = 0 − [−40/3] = 40/3 = 13.333 years.

8. A truncated life table has l70=80{,}000, l71=78{,}000, l72=75{,}500, l73=72{,}500, l74=69{,}000, and l75=0 (no survivors past 75). Find e70.
Show Answer

1p70=0.975, 2p70=0.94375, 3p70=0.90625, 4p70=0.8625, 5p70=0.

e70 = 0.975+0.94375+0.90625+0.8625 = 3.6875 years

9. Using the table from Problem 8, find Var(K70).
Show Answer

Σk·kp70 = 1(0.975)+2(0.94375)+3(0.90625)+4(0.8625) = 0.975+1.8875+2.71875+3.45 = 9.03125

E[K70²] = 2(9.03125) − 3.6875 = 14.375

Var(K70) = 14.375 − 3.6875² = 14.375 − 13.597656 = 0.777344

10. A study estimates eox = 32.7. Approximate ex.
Show Answer

ex ≈ eox − 0.5 = 32.2

11. A life table has l40=98{,}000 and d40=250. Under UDD, find 0.3q40.
Show Answer

q40 = 250/98{,}000 = 0.00255102. 0.3q40 = 0.3 × 0.00255102 = 0.00076531

12. Using the data of Problem 11, find μ(40.3) under UDD.
Show Answer

μ(40.3) = q40/(1−0.3q40) = 0.00255102/(1−0.00076531) = 0.00255102/0.99923469 = 0.00255297

13. Using the data of Problem 11 (q40=0.00255102), find 0.3p40 under the constant-force fractional-age assumption and compare to the UDD value.
Show Answer

p40=0.99744898, ln(p40) = −0.00255428.

0.3p40 = e0.3(−0.00255428) = e−0.00076628 = 0.999234, giving 0.3q40 = 0.000766 under constant force.

UDD gave 0.3q40 = 0.000765 (Problem 11). The two are nearly indistinguishable here because q40 is very small.

14. For a life age 65, q65=0.03. Find 0.5q65 under both UDD and constant force, and compare.
Show Answer

UDD: 0.5q65 = 0.5(0.03) = 0.015

Constant force: p65=0.97, ln(0.97)=−0.030459, 0.5p65 = e0.5(−0.030459) = e−0.015230 = 0.984886, so 0.5q65 = 0.015114.

The gap (0.015000 vs. 0.015114) is larger than in Problem 13, confirming that the two fractional-age assumptions diverge more as qx grows.

15. Using Table 8.4 (§5.3), find p[60] and p[60]+1.
Show Answer

p[60] = l[60]+1/l[60] = 99{,}600/100{,}000 = 0.996

p[60]+1 = l62/l[60]+1 = 98{,}800/99{,}600 = 0.991968

16. Using Table 8.4, find 2p[61].
Show Answer

Since the select period is 2 years, l[61]+2 = l63 = 97{,}600 (already the ultimate value shown in Table 8.4).

2p[61] = l63/l[61] = 97{,}600/99{,}300 = 0.982880

17. Using Table 8.4, find 1|2q[62] (survive 1 year, then die within the following 2 — a window that spans the select/ultimate boundary).
Show Answer

Duration 1 is still select: l[62]+1 = 97{,}500. Duration 3 exceeds the 2-year select period, so use the ultimate value at attained age 65: l[62]+3 = l65 = 94{,}600.

1|2q[62] = (l[62]+1 − l65)/l[62] = (97{,}500−94{,}600)/98{,}200 = 2{,}900/98{,}200 = 0.029531

18. Using Table 8.4, find 0.5p[62] under the constant-force fractional-age assumption.
Show Answer

First find the annual rate: q[62] = (l[62]−l[62]+1)/l[62] = (98{,}200−97{,}500)/98{,}200 = 700/98{,}200 = 0.0071284, so p[62] = 0.9928716.

ln(p[62]) = −0.0071538. Under constant force, 0.5p[62] = (p[62])0.5 = e0.5(−0.0071538) = e−0.0035769 = 0.996430.

This shows fractional-age assumptions apply identically to select-table durations — the bracket notation doesn't change any of the fractional-age algebra from Chapter 4.

Part IX — Present Value Random Variables for Long-Term Insurance Coverages (12.5–20%)

Every long-term life contingency calculation on this exam ultimately reduces to the same skeleton: a benefit payment tied to an uncertain future lifetime, discounted back to today at a fixed rate of interest. In Part VIII you built the survival-model machinery — tpx, tqx, Tx, Kx, μ(x), and lx — that describes when (x) dies or survives. This part supplies the other half: turning that timing uncertainty into a monetary present value random variable, and then extracting its distribution, mean, and variance. This is where mortality and interest theory finally merge into the object an actuary actually prices and reserves against.

Chapter 1 builds the discrete (annual) versions of the life insurance present value random variable — whole life, term, pure endowment, and endowment insurance — and their expected present values (EPVs). Chapter 2 refines the timing to 1/m-thly and continuous payment, introducing the claims acceleration approximation that lets you move between annual and finer-grained results without re-deriving everything. Chapter 3 develops the parallel machinery for life annuities, which fund premiums and pension-style income streams. Chapter 4 ties insurance and annuities together through one of the most heavily tested identities on the whole exam, and closes with a master reference table. Chapter 5 computes variances — essential for normal-approximation and risk-margin problems — using a "double force of interest" trick that trips up most first-time students. Chapter 6 finishes with sensitivity analysis: how EPVs move when your interest or mortality assumptions change, and why the direction of that movement is never arbitrary. Given this part's syllabus weight, expect it to dominate your exam-day point total more than any other single part except the annuity/insurance-funded reserving material that follows it.

Almost every problem in this part follows the same three-step skeleton, and it's worth internalizing before you touch the first formula. Step one: identify the correct present value random variable — what benefit is being paid, on what trigger (death, survival, or either), and at what point in time. Step two: translate that random variable into a summation (annual payment) or an integral (continuous payment), using whatever mortality assumption the problem hands you — often a full life table, but on exam problems more frequently a parametric shortcut like constant force of mortality or De Moivre's law, precisely because those admit closed-form sums and integrals you can compute by hand under time pressure. Step three: exploit relationships — the insurance/annuity identity of Chapter 4, the term-plus-pure-endowment decomposition of Chapter 1, the deferred-insurance shortcut — to avoid redoing a summation you've already effectively done in an earlier part of the same problem. Exam questions in this topic area are almost always multi-part precisely so that they can test whether you notice these shortcuts; students who re-derive every sub-answer from scratch routinely run out of time before students who recognize that part (c) is just part (a) minus part (b).

1. PV Random Variables: Discrete Life Insurance

Learning Outcomes Covered: Define and manipulate the present value random variable for annually-paid life insurance benefits (whole life, term, pure endowment, endowment, deferred); compute the corresponding expected present values; recognize the relationships among these EPVs.

Before diving in, a quick notational reminder carried over from Part VIII: a left subscript placed before the main symbol always attaches to the letter that follows it and denotes a time duration, while a right subscript denotes the age or life being described. So in tpx, the "t" (duration) sits immediately to the left of "p," and the "x" (age) sits to the right. You'll see this same convention extended to insurance and annuity symbols throughout this part — e.g., a left superscript "1" flags a benefit that is conditional on death, while its absence (or a different placement) flags a benefit conditional on survival.

1.1 Whole Life Insurance

Consider a policy that pays a benefit of 1 at the end of the year in which (x) dies, with no expiration date. Because the curtate future lifetime Kx counts the number of complete years (x) survives, death occurs during policy year Kx+1, and the benefit — discounted back to time 0 — is paid at time Kx+1. This gives the present value random variable:

Whole Life Insurance PV Random VariableZ = vKx+1

Z is a discrete random variable taking values v, v², v³, … according to the distribution of Kx. Its expectation is the actuarial present value, universally denoted Ax. Summing the payoff vk+1 against the probability that death occurs in year k+1 — which requires surviving k full years (probability kpx) and then dying in the following year (probability qx+k) — gives:

Whole Life Insurance EPVAx = E[Z] = Σk=0∞ vk+1 · kpx · qx+k
The single most common exam slip: writing the exponent on Z as Tx instead of Kx+1. Tx is the exact (continuous) future lifetime; Kx+1 is the integer number of the policy year in which death falls. For an annually-paid benefit, the payment date is always an integer, so the exponent must be an integer-valued random variable — that's Kx+1, never Tx itself. Save the vTx exponent for continuous insurance in Chapter 2.
Example 9.1

Suppose (x) is subject to a constant force of mortality μ = 0.02, and the annual effective interest rate is i = 5% (so v = 1/1.05 and δ = ln(1.05) = 0.048790). Because the force of mortality is constant, every policy year carries the same one-year death probability q = 1 − e−0.02 = 0.019801, and Kx is (memorylessly) geometric with that success probability. Find Ax.

Show Solution

With kpx = e−0.02k and qx+k = q = 0.019801 for every k, the EPV sum is geometric:

Ax = v·q · Σk=0∞ (v·e−0.02)k = v·q / (1 − v·e−0.02)

Numerically, v = 0.952381 and e−0.02 = 0.980199, so v·e−0.02 = 0.933523, and v·q = 0.952381 × 0.019801 = 0.018858. Then:

Ax = 0.018858 / (1 − 0.933523) = 0.018858 / 0.066477 ≈ 0.28372

We will reuse this exact "world" (μ = 0.02, i = 5%) repeatedly in later chapters, so keep Ax ≈ 0.28372 handy.

1.2 n-Year Term Insurance

Term insurance pays the benefit only if death occurs within a fixed n-year window; if (x) survives to time n, nothing is ever paid. The PV random variable is Z truncated to zero after time n:

n-Year Term Insurance PV Random VariableZ = vKx+1 if Kx ≤ n − 1, Z = 0 if Kx ≥ n

The left superscript "1" over the A marks a death-contingent (as opposed to survival-contingent) benefit, and the subscript "x:n⌐" marks the n-year temporary duration:

n-Year Term Insurance EPVA1x:n⌐ = Σk=0n−1 vk+1 · kpx · qx+k
Example 9.2

Now switch to a De Moivre (uniform remaining-lifetime) world: (x) is age 40, the limiting age is ω = 100 (so the maximum remaining lifetime is n₀ = 60 years, and Kx is discrete-uniform on {0, 1, …, 59}), and i = 5%. Find A140:20⌐, the EPV of 20-year term insurance.

Show Solution

Under De Moivre, each value of Kx from 0 to 59 has probability 1/60. Restricting to Kx = 0, …, 19 (death within the first 20 years):

A140:20⌐ = (1/60) Σk=019 vk+1 = a20⌐ / 60

where a20⌐ is the ordinary 20-year annuity-certain factor, (1 − v²⁰)/i. With v²⁰ = 1/(1.05)²⁰ = 0.376889, a20⌐ = (1 − 0.376889)/0.05 = 12.46222. Therefore:

A140:20⌐ = 12.46222 / 60 ≈ 0.20770

This "divide the certain-annuity factor by the total number of equally likely lifetimes" shortcut is a hallmark of De Moivre problems and is worth memorizing for speed.

1.3 n-Year Pure Endowment

A pure endowment pays a benefit only if the insured survives to time n — the mirror image of term insurance. Since the payment date is fixed at n (no timing uncertainty conditional on survival), its PV random variable is simple:

n-Year Pure Endowment PV Random Variable and EPVZ = vn if Tx ≥ n, else 0; EPV = nEx = vn · npx

We denote this EPV nEx to keep it visually distinct from term insurance (some texts instead write it as Ax:n⌐ with a small "1" positioned above the n rather than above the A; nEx avoids that typographic ambiguity and is standard in most modern SOA materials, so we use it throughout).

Example 9.3

Continuing the De Moivre world of Example 9.2 (x = 40, ω = 100, i = 5%), find 20E40.

Show Solution

Under De Moivre with 60 years of remaining lifetime available, 20p40 = (60 − 20)/60 = 40/60 = 0.666667. Then:

20E40 = v²⁰ · 20p40 = 0.376889 × 0.666667 ≈ 0.25126

1.4 n-Year Endowment Insurance

Endowment insurance combines the two: it pays 1 at the end of the year of death if death occurs within n years, or 1 at time n if the insured survives that long — whichever comes first. Every policyholder collects exactly one benefit payment eventually (assuming the contract doesn't lapse), which is the defining feature of endowment insurance and a favorite source of exam trick questions.

n-Year Endowment Insurance PV Random Variable and EPVZ = vmin(Kx+1,n); Ax:n⌐ = A1x:n⌐ + nEx
It is tempting, when a problem says "endowment insurance," to compute only the term-insurance piece and forget the survival benefit entirely — especially under time pressure. Endowment insurance is strictly more expensive than the corresponding term insurance because it guarantees a payout. If your computed Ax:n⌐ comes out smaller than A1x:n⌐, you've made an arithmetic error — endowment EPV can never be less than the term EPV alone.
Example 9.4

Using Examples 9.2 and 9.3, find the EPV of 20-year endowment insurance on (40) in the same De Moivre world.

Show Solution

A40:20⌐ = A140:20⌐ + 20E40 = 0.20770 + 0.25126 = 0.45896

Sanity check: 0.45896 > 0.20770, consistent with the endowment always exceeding the pure term piece.

1.5 Deferred Whole Life Insurance

A benefit that pays 1 at the end of the year of death, but only if death occurs after an n-year deferral period, is written n|Ax (read "n-deferred A sub x"). Two equivalent ways to compute it: subtract the term insurance piece from the whole life EPV, or condition on surviving the deferral period and then buying whole life insurance at the attained age:

n-Year Deferred Whole Life Insurance EPVn|Ax = Ax − A1x:n⌐ = nEx · Ax+n

The second form deserves a moment's thought, because it is the template you'll reuse constantly for every "deferred" benefit in this manual: to get the EPV of any benefit that only "switches on" after surviving n years, multiply the pure endowment factor nEx (the cost of a dollar delivered at time n conditional on being alive to receive it) by the EPV of that same benefit evaluated at the new, older starting age x+n. This works because of the tower property of expectation: first average over what happens in the deferral window (either die, contributing nothing to this benefit, or survive to age x+n with probability npx and discount factor vn), and then, only in the surviving branch, average again over everything that can happen afterward as if the policy had been freshly written on a life aged x+n. You will see this same "endowment factor times restart-at-later-age" pattern again for deferred annuities in Chapter 3.

Example 9.5

In the De Moivre world (x = 40, ω = 100, i = 5%), compute 20|A40 two independent ways and confirm they agree. You will need A40 (whole life on 40) and A60 (whole life on 60, i.e. after the deferral, with 40 years of remaining lifetime under De Moivre).

Show Solution

Method 1 — subtract the term piece. First find A40 using the same shortcut as Example 9.2 but over the full 60-year span: A40 = a60⌐/60. With v⁶⁰ = 0.053535, a60⌐ = (1 − 0.053535)/0.05 = 18.9293, so A40 = 18.9293/60 = 0.31549. Then:

20|A40 = A40 − A140:20⌐ = 0.31549 − 0.20770 = 0.10779

Method 2 — deferral × attained-age insurance. A life aged 60 under this table has ω − 60 = 40 remaining years, so A60 = a40⌐/40. With v⁴⁰ = 0.142046, a40⌐ = (1 − 0.142046)/0.05 = 17.1591, so A60 = 17.1591/40 = 0.42898. Then:

20|A40 = 20E40 · A60 = 0.25126 × 0.42898 ≈ 0.10778

Both methods agree at 0.1078 (rounding). This cross-check technique — computing an EPV two structurally different ways — is one of the best ways to catch your own arithmetic errors on the actual exam.

2. PV Random Variables: 1/m-thly & Continuous Insurance

Learning Outcomes Covered: Define the present value random variable and EPV for life insurance paid at the end of the 1/m-thly period of death and at the exact moment of death; apply the claims acceleration approximation to relate mthly and continuous EPVs to the annual EPV.

2.1 mthly Insurance and Claims Acceleration

Real insurers don't wait up to a full year to pay a death claim — they typically pay within days or weeks of learning of the death. A useful intermediate model divides each policy year into m equal periods and pays the benefit at the end of the 1/m-thly period in which death occurs, rather than the end of the full year. This EPV is denoted Ax(m). As m grows, the payment date creeps earlier within the year of death, so Ax(m) is always at least as large as Ax — the insurer is discounting the same eventual claim over a shorter horizon.

Computing Ax(m) exactly requires a sub-year mortality assumption. The standard choice — the uniform distribution of deaths (UDD) within each year of age — leads to a strikingly clean relationship: the ratio between annual and mthly interest functions carries over directly to the insurance EPV. This is often called the claims acceleration relationship, since it captures how much value is added by accelerating claim payment from year-end to period-end.

Claims Acceleration Approximation (mthly Insurance)Ax(m) ≈ (i / i(m)) · Ax, where i(m) = m[(1+i)1/m − 1]

Because i(m) < i for any m > 1 (compounding more frequently at the same effective annual rate requires a smaller nominal rate), the ratio i/i(m) always exceeds 1 — confirming Ax(m) > Ax. As m → ∞, i(m) → δ = ln(1+i), and this approximation converges to the continuous relationship developed in section 2.2.

Example 9.6

Using the constant-force world of Example 9.1 (μ = 0.02, i = 5%, Ax ≈ 0.28372), approximate Ax(2), the EPV of insurance paid at the end of the half-year of death.

Show Solution

First, i(2) = 2[(1.05)0.5 − 1] = 2(1.024695 − 1) = 0.049390. Then i/i(2) = 0.05/0.049390 = 1.012351. Applying claims acceleration:

Ax(2) ≈ 1.012351 × 0.28372 ≈ 0.28722

Note the modest but real increase over Ax = 0.28372 — paying half a year sooner, on average, is worth about 1.2% more here.

2.2 Continuous Whole Life Insurance

Pushing m → ∞ gives a benefit paid at the exact instant of death — the cleanest theoretical model, and the one used whenever a problem specifies "continuous" payment or gives you a force of mortality μ(x) and asks for an integral. The future lifetime Tx is now the exact (continuous) exponent:

Continuous Whole Life Insurance PV Random Variable and EPVZ = vTx; Āx = E[Z] = ∫0∞ vt · tpx · μ(x+t) dt

The integrand has a clean interpretation: tpx·μ(x+t)dt is the probability that death occurs in the infinitesimal interval (t, t+dt), and vt discounts a payment made at that instant back to time 0.

Continuous term, pure endowment, and endowment insurance follow immediately by restricting or combining the integral exactly as in the discrete case — nothing conceptually new is required, only a change to the limits of integration:

Continuous Term, Pure Endowment, and Endowment InsuranceĀ1x:n⌐ = ∫0n vt tpx μ(x+t) dt ; nEx = vn · npx (unchanged from the discrete case) ; Āx:n⌐ = Ā1x:n⌐ + nEx

Notice the pure endowment piece is identical whether the death benefit inside the same contract is paid annually or continuously — a pure endowment has no "year of death" timing question to resolve, since it is purely a survival benefit paid at the single fixed date n. Only the death-contingent piece of an endowment contract's EPV changes as you move from annual to mthly to continuous payment; the survival-contingent piece never does.

Example 9.7

Under constant force of mortality μ, the integral collapses to a closed form because tpx·μ(x+t) = μ·e−μt and vt = e−δt, giving Āx = μ∫0∞ e−(μ+δ)tdt = μ/(μ+δ). Evaluate this for μ = 0.02, i = 5% (δ = 0.048790), and compare to Ax and Ax(2) from Examples 9.1 and 9.6.

Show Solution

Āx = 0.02 / (0.02 + 0.048790) = 0.02/0.068790 ≈ 0.29074

Lining the three up: Ax ≈ 0.28372 < Ax(2) ≈ 0.28722 < Āx ≈ 0.29074. This ordering — annual < mthly < continuous — always holds for the same underlying mortality and interest assumptions, because more frequent payment always means less average discounting.

Example 9.8

Under De Moivre's law with remaining lifetime uniform on (0, n₀), tpxμ(x+t)dt reduces to the flat density 1/n₀ dt on (0, n₀), so the continuous whole life EPV becomes an average of discount factors: Āx = (1/n₀)∫0n₀ vt dt = ān₀⌐/n₀, where ān₀⌐ = (1 − vn₀)/δ is the continuous n₀-year annuity-certain factor. Evaluate this for the De Moivre world of Example 9.2 (x = 40, ω = 100, n₀ = 60, i = 5%, δ = ln 1.05 = 0.048790).

Show Solution

ā60⌐ = (1 − v⁶⁰)/δ = (1 − 0.053535)/0.048790 = 0.946465/0.048790 = 19.4029. Then:

Ā40 = 19.4029/60 ≈ 0.32338

Compare to the discrete A40 ≈ 0.31549 found in Example 9.5 — again continuous exceeds annual, as expected.

Example 9.9

A fresh constant-force world: μ = 0.01, i = 4% (δ = ln 1.04 = 0.039221). Find Āx and, using claims acceleration with m = 4, approximate Ax(4). (You'll need the discrete Ax first — it follows the same geometric-series pattern as Example 9.1.)

Show Solution

Discrete Ax: v = 1/1.04 = 0.961538, e−0.01 = 0.990050, so v·e−μ = 0.951971, and v·q = v(1 − e−μ) = 0.961538 × 0.009950 = 0.009567. Then Ax = 0.009567/(1 − 0.951971) = 0.009567/0.048029 ≈ 0.19923.

Continuous Āx: Āx = μ/(μ+δ) = 0.01/0.049221 ≈ 0.20317.

mthly Ax(4): i(4) = 4[(1.04)0.25 − 1] = 4(1.009853 − 1) = 0.039412. i/i(4) = 0.04/0.039412 = 1.014919. Ax(4) ≈ 1.014919 × 0.19923 ≈ 0.20220.

Ordering check: 0.19923 < 0.20220 < 0.20317. Consistent, as always.

Claims acceleration is an approximation that relies on the UDD assumption within each year of age (or an exactly analogous exact result if the problem explicitly states UDD). If a problem gives you a fully specified survival model (constant force, De Moivre, or a full life table) and asks for the exact mthly or continuous EPV, integrate or sum directly as in Examples 9.7–9.9 rather than reaching for the shortcut — the shortcut is for when you're only given the annual EPV and asked to extend it.

2.3 Why the Claims Acceleration Ratio Works, and Extending It to Term and Endowment Insurance

It's worth understanding, at least in outline, why a single ratio i/i(m) is able to convert every term of the Ax summation at once, rather than requiring a fresh calculation for each policy year. Under UDD, the exact time of death within any given year of age is uniformly distributed on that year, independent of which year it turns out to be. Conditional on death falling in policy year k+1 (probability kpxqx+k, exactly as in the annual formula), the mthly contract pays not at time k+1 but at the end of whichever 1/m-thly subinterval contains the death — on average, m/(2m) = 1/2 of a period earlier than the full year-end, converted into a slightly larger present value than vk+1 by a fixed multiplicative factor that turns out to be exactly i/i(m), independent of k. Because that adjustment factor is the same constant for every single term in the sum, it factors straight out of the summation — which is exactly why Ax(m) = (i/i(m))Ax holds as an exact identity under UDD, not merely an approximation.
