It only becomes an approximation when you apply the UDD-derived ratio to a population that doesn't actually follow UDD within each year — which is the typical exam situation, since UDD is rarely stated as exactly true for the underlying table, only assumed for computational convenience.

The same ratio extends without modification to term, endowment, and deferred insurance, because those benefits are all built from the identical building block — a death-contingent payment in a given policy year — restricted to a different range of years. The pure endowment piece of an endowment contract, by contrast, is untouched by claims acceleration, since a survival benefit paid at a fixed future date n has no "year of death" whose timing could be accelerated.

mthly Term and Endowment InsuranceA1(m)x:n⌐ ≈ (i/i(m)) A1x:n⌐ ; A(m)x:n⌐ ≈ (i/i(m)) A1x:n⌐ + nEx

Notice the pure endowment term nEx in the second formula carries no i/i(m) adjustment at all — only the death-contingent piece accelerates. Forgetting to strip the adjustment factor back off the survival-benefit component before combining it with the accelerated term-insurance piece is a common and entirely avoidable error.

3. PV Random Variables: Life Annuities

Learning Outcomes Covered: Define the present value random variable and EPV for whole life, temporary, and deferred life annuities under annual, 1/m-thly, and continuous payment; distinguish annuity-due from annuity-immediate timing.

3.1 Whole Life Annuity-Due

A life annuity-due pays 1 at the start of each year, for as long as (x) is alive to receive it — at time 0, time 1, time 2, …, up to and including time Kx (the last full year survived). Its PV random variable is an annuity-certain of Kx+1 payments:

Whole Life Annuity-Due PV Random VariableY = äKx+1⌐ = (1 − vKx+1) / d

The EPV, denoted äx, has a second, equally important derivation via indicator random variables that avoids working with Kx's distribution directly. Write the total payment as a sum of 1-dollar indicators, one per possible payment date: the annuity pays a dollar at time k if and only if (x) is still alive at time k. Taking expectations term by term (linearity of expectation applies regardless of dependence):

Whole Life Annuity-Due EPVäx = E[Y] = Σk=0∞ vk · kpx

This "sum of discounted survival probabilities" form is usually the faster way to compute äx directly, and it generalizes immediately to temporary and deferred versions by simply changing the range of the sum.

It's worth contrasting the two derivation styles side by side, because both reappear constantly throughout this manual. The first style — sum over each possible value of Kx, weighting the corresponding annuity-certain value äk+1⌐ by P(Kx=k) — treats the random variable Y as a function of Kx and applies the definition of expectation directly; it is conceptually the most natural starting point but tends to produce messier algebra (as Chapter 4's identity derivation shows, this route is exactly how the fundamental Ax–äx relationship falls out). The second style — decompose the total payment into a sum of one-dollar indicator variables, one per possible payment date, and push the expectation inside the sum — sidesteps Kx's distribution almost entirely and instead needs only the survival probabilities kpx, which are usually the quantities a life table hands you most directly. Both derivations are exact and always agree; which one is faster depends on whether the problem more naturally supplies you with the distribution of Kx or with a table of survival probabilities.

Example 9.10

Return to the constant-force world (μ = 0.02, i = 5%). Since kpx = e−0.02k, find äx as a geometric series.

Show Solution

äx = Σk=0∞ (v·e−0.02)k = 1 / (1 − v·e−0.02) = 1/0.066477 ≈ 15.0427

(recall v·e−0.02 = 0.933523 from Example 9.1). We will reuse äx ≈ 15.0427 throughout Chapters 4–6.

3.2 Temporary and Deferred Life Annuities

A temporary life annuity-due pays for at most n years — 1 at times 0, 1, …, min(Kx, n−1):

n-Year Temporary Life Annuity-Due EPVäx:n⌐ = Σk=0n−1 vk · kpx

A deferred annuity, n|äx, withholds payment until year n and then pays for life; it satisfies äx = äx:n⌐ + n|äx, exactly parallel to how whole life insurance splits into term plus deferred insurance.

Example 9.11

In the De Moivre world (x = 40, ω = 100, n₀ = 60, i = 5%), find ä40:20⌐, the 20-year temporary life annuity-due.

Show Solution

Here kp40 = (60 − k)/60 for k = 0, …, 59, so:

ä40:20⌐ = (1/60) Σk=019 (60 − k) vk = (1/60)[60·ä20⌐ − Σk=019 k·vk]

where ä20⌐ = (1 − v²⁰)/d is the 20-year annuity-due-certain factor. With d = 0.05/1.05 = 0.047619, ä20⌐ = (1 − 0.376889)/0.047619 = 13.0854. The increasing-annuity identity (Iä)20⌐ = (ä20⌐ − 20v²⁰)/d gives Σk=019(k+1)vk = (13.0854 − 20×0.376889)/0.047619 = (13.0854 − 7.53778)/0.047619 = 116.50, so Σk=019 k·vk = 116.50 − 13.0854 = 103.415.

Putting it together: 60×13.0854 − 103.415 = 785.12 − 103.42 = 681.71, and

ä40:20⌐ = 681.71/60 ≈ 11.3618

We will re-derive this same number in seconds using a much faster shortcut in Chapter 4 — keep it in mind as a benchmark.

3.3 Annuity-Immediate

An annuity-immediate pays at the end of each year survived rather than the start — at times 1, 2, …, Kx. It is simply the annuity-due shifted back one payment: drop the guaranteed payment at time 0, and every remaining payment date is identical. Algebraically,

Whole Life Annuity-Immediate EPVax = äx − 1
Example 9.12

Using äx ≈ 15.0427 from Example 9.10 (constant-force world), find ax.

Show Solution

ax = 15.0427 − 1 = 14.0427

3.4 Continuous Life Annuities

The continuous whole life annuity āx pays income at rate 1 per year, continuously, for as long as (x) lives:

Continuous Whole Life Annuity EPVāx = ∫0∞ vt · tpx dt

Under constant force this again collapses cleanly: āx = ∫₀^∞ e−(μ+δ)tdt = 1/(μ+δ). Under De Moivre, āx = ān₀⌐/n₀ paired with the pure-averaging logic from Example 9.8.

Example 9.13

Compute āx in both the constant-force world (μ = 0.02, i = 5%) and the De Moivre world (x = 40, ω = 100, i = 5%), and compare each to its discrete counterpart.

Show Solution

Constant force: āx = 1/(μ+δ) = 1/(0.02+0.048790) = 1/0.068790 ≈ 14.5370, versus äx ≈ 15.0427. The continuous annuity is smaller because payment shifts from the start of each year to a continuous drip within the year — some value is lost relative to getting the whole year's payment up front.

De Moivre: ā40 = ā60⌐/60 = 19.4029/60 ≈ 0.32338 [insurance figure carried over from Example 9.8 — for the annuity itself] — actually for the annuity we need ā60⌐ divided differently. Since āx = (1 − Āx)/δ (proved in Chapter 4), ā40 = (1 − 0.32338)/0.048790 = 0.67662/0.048790 ≈ 13.8724, versus ä40 ≈ 14.3747 (from A40 = 0.31549 via the same identity). Same directional pattern: continuous < annual-due, in both mortality models.

3.5 1/m-thly Annuities

An mthly annuity-due pays 1/m at the start of each 1/m-thly period the annuitant survives to see. As with insurance, an exact UDD-based approximation lets you adjust an annual annuity-due EPV to mthly frequency without re-summing:

mthly Annuity-Due Approximation (Claims-Acceleration Style)äx(m) ≈ äx − (m−1)/(2m)

The intuition: splitting each year's payment of 1 into m level installments of 1/m paid throughout the year, instead of the lump sum paid at the start, costs the payer roughly half a period's worth of interest per year on average — and that average shortfall is (m−1)/(2m) of a year's payment.

Example 9.14

In the μ = 0.01, i = 4% world of Example 9.9, first find äx from Ax ≈ 0.19923 (using the identity from Chapter 4 in advance: äx = (1 − Ax)/d, d = 0.04/1.04 = 0.038462), then approximate äx(4).

Show Solution

äx = (1 − 0.19923)/0.038462 = 0.80077/0.038462 ≈ 20.8214.

äx(4) ≈ 20.8214 − 3/8 = 20.8214 − 0.375 = 20.4464

Insurance EPVs and annuity EPVs move in opposite directions as you increase payment frequency: Ax(m) > Ax (paying the death benefit sooner is worth more), but äx(m) < äx (spreading the year's payment out into smaller, later installments is worth less to the recipient). Keep this contrast straight — exam distractors love to flip the direction of one or the other.

3.6 Deferred Annuities and Why Any of This Matters for Premiums

A deferred whole life annuity-due, n|äx, follows the identical "endowment factor times restart-at-later-age" pattern introduced for deferred insurance in section 1.5:

n-Year Deferred Whole Life Annuity-Due EPVn|äx = äx − äx:n⌐ = nEx · äx+n

This structure is not just an algebra exercise — it's literally how a deferred pension annuity is priced: a worker pays premiums during the deferral window, nothing is paid out until retirement at age x+n, and once retirement begins the annuity behaves exactly like a freshly issued whole life annuity-due on a life then aged x+n, scaled down by the probability and discounting of surviving to get there (nEx).

It's worth stepping back at this point to note why this entire chapter exists alongside Chapter 1 rather than being folded into it. Life insurance EPVs answer "how much does the insurer expect to eventually pay out." Life annuity EPVs answer the complementary question of "how much can be collected as level premiums, or paid out as level pension income, in exchange for that same expected liability." Every level-premium life insurance policy you'll price in later parts of this manual is, structurally, a whole life (or temporary) insurance benefit funded by a whole life (or temporary) annuity-due of premium payments — which is precisely why the insurance/annuity identity of Chapter 4 turns out to be the single most load-bearing formula in premium and reserve calculations later in the syllabus, not just a standalone curiosity about this part.

4. Insurance–Annuity Relationships

Learning Outcomes Covered: Derive and apply the fundamental identity linking whole life insurance and whole life annuity-due EPVs (and its continuous analog); extend the identity to endowment insurance and temporary annuities; organize all EPV symbols introduced so far into one coherent reference.

4.1 The Fundamental Identity: Ax = 1 − d·äx

This single relationship is arguably the most exam-relevant formula in the entire syllabus — it converts an annuity computation into an insurance computation (or vice versa) with a single subtraction and multiplication, no re-summation required. The derivation is worth doing carefully once, because it rests on an algebraic identity that holds for every single outcome, not just on average.

Recall the standard annuity-due-certain formula for j level payments of 1 at times 0, 1, …, j−1: äj⌐ = (1 − vj)/d. This is pure time-value-of-money algebra — no randomness involved yet. Now set j = Kx+1: the whole life annuity-due random variable Y (payments at times 0 through Kx, i.e., Kx+1 payments total) satisfies, for every possible realization of Kx,

Pathwise Insurance–Annuity Link (holds for every outcome, not just in expectation)Y = äKx+1⌐ = (1 − vKx+1)/d = (1 − Z)/d

Because this equation holds outcome-by-outcome, it survives taking expectations of both sides intact:

Fundamental Insurance–Annuity Identityäx = (1 − Ax)/d ⇔ Ax = 1 − d·äx

A quick sanity intuition for why this must be true, independent of the algebra: think of äx as the price of an annuity that returns your entire premium of 1/d back to you in installments while you're alive, and 1 − Ax as "the fraction of a dollar you keep after buying insurance." The identity says these describe the same cash flow viewed from two angles — every dollar you would have received as an annuity payment is a dollar you didn't have to spend insuring your own death that instant.

A quick numerical sanity check is worth building into your habits for every problem that uses this identity: since d is always strictly between 0 and 1, and Ax is always strictly between 0 and 1 for a life with any positive probability of eventually dying, äx = (1 − Ax)/d must always come out positive, and — because 1/d itself equals ä∞⌐, the value of an annuity-certain paid forever with no mortality risk at all — äx must always be strictly less than 1/d. If your computed äx ever exceeds 1/d, or your computed Ax ever falls outside (0, 1), you have made an arithmetic error somewhere upstream, and it is worth the five seconds it takes to check this before moving on to the next part of a multi-part problem.

The continuous version follows by an entirely analogous argument (replace the annuity-certain-due formula with its continuous counterpart, āt⌐ = (1 − vt)/δ, and set t = Tx):

Continuous Fundamental Identityāx = (1 − Āx)/δ ⇔ Āx = 1 − δ·āx
Example 9.15

Verify the identity holds for the constant-force world using the values already computed: Ax ≈ 0.28372 (Example 9.1) and äx ≈ 15.0427 (Example 9.10), with d = 0.047619.

Show Solution

1 − d·äx = 1 − 0.047619 × 15.0427 = 1 − 0.71632 = 0.28368

This matches Ax ≈ 0.28372 up to rounding accumulated across the two independent derivations — exactly the confirmation we want.

Example 9.16

A pension actuary tells you a client's whole life insurance EPV is Ax = 0.25 at i = 6%, but needs the whole life annuity-due value äx for a premium calculation and has no time to re-derive it from a life table. Find äx using the identity.

Show Solution

d = 0.06/1.06 = 0.056604.

äx = (1 − Ax)/d = (1 − 0.25)/0.056604 = 0.75/0.056604 ≈ 13.2500

No mortality table needed at all — the identity does all the work once you already have one of the two EPVs.

4.2 Extending the Identity to Endowment Insurance

The same pathwise argument (äj⌐ = (1−vj)/d for any duration j, random or not) applies equally well when the random duration is min(Kx+1, n) rather than Kx+1 — i.e., to endowment insurance and the corresponding temporary annuity-due:

Temporary/Endowment Identityäx:n⌐ = (1 − Ax:n⌐)/d
This identity uses the full endowment insurance EPV, Ax:n⌐ — never the term-insurance-only piece A1x:n⌐. The pathwise argument requires the insurance benefit to be paid at exactly the same random time the annuity stops (min(Kx+1, n) in both cases). Term insurance alone stops paying after n years with nothing, while the temporary annuity keeps running until n regardless of survival — they don't share a common random duration, so plugging A1 into this formula silently produces a wrong answer with no error message. This mismatch is one of the most common point-losing mistakes on this topic.
Example 9.17

Use the shortcut identity to recompute ä40:20⌐ from the De Moivre world, and compare to the direct-summation answer of 11.3618 found the hard way in Example 9.11. You'll need A40:20⌐ = 0.45896 from Example 9.4.

Show Solution

d = 0.05/1.05 = 0.047619.

ä40:20⌐ = (1 − 0.45896)/0.047619 = 0.54104/0.047619 ≈ 11.3618

Identical to the direct-summation result in Example 9.11 — but this time it took one subtraction and one division instead of an increasing-annuity derivation. This is exactly the kind of time savings the identity is built for.

Example 9.18

A problem gives you äx = 18 exactly at i = 4%, and separately tells you Ax(4) should be estimated using the mthly claims-acceleration shortcut together with the continuous-analog approximation Āx ≈ (i/δ)Ax. Find Ax, then Āx.

Show Solution

d = 0.04/1.04 = 0.038462. By the fundamental identity, Ax = 1 − d·äx = 1 − 0.038462×18 = 1 − 0.692308 = 0.307692.

δ = ln(1.04) = 0.039221, so i/δ = 0.04/0.039221 = 1.019862.

Āx ≈ 1.019862 × 0.307692 ≈ 0.31381

This chains together three separate tools from this part — the fundamental identity (Chapter 4) and the claims-acceleration-style continuous approximation (Chapter 2) — which is exactly the kind of multi-step problem the real exam favors.

4.3 Master Reference Table

By this point you have met a dozen closely related symbols, and it is easy to lose track of which one applies to which combination of trigger (death vs. survival vs. either) and timing (annual, mthly, continuous). The table below collects every insurance and annuity EPV symbol introduced across Chapters 1–3 in one place, with its defining sum or integral and its place in the web of identities. When you sit down with a new problem, the fastest way to avoid notational errors is to first identify which row of this table you're being asked about — trigger and timing together pin down the symbol uniquely — and only then start substituting numbers.

Master Reference Table — Single-Life Insurance & Annuity EPV Symbols
SymbolPlain-English DescriptionPayment TriggerTimingDefining Formula
AxWhole life insuranceDeath, any timeEnd of year of deathΣ vk+1 kpx qx+k
A1x:n⌐n-year term insuranceDeath within n yearsEnd of year of deathΣk=0n−1 vk+1 kpx qx+k
