āxContinuous whole life annuityAlive at time tContinuously, rate 1/year∫₀^∞ vt tpx dt
äx(m)mthly whole life annuity-dueAlive at each 1/m-thly dateStart of each 1/m-thly period≈ äx − (m−1)/(2m)

Every row involving A connects to its matching ä-row through the fundamental identity of this chapter — memorize the identity once, and you effectively get half this table for free from the other half. Notice also the family resemblance running down the "Defining Formula" column: every insurance formula is a sum or integral of (discount factor) × (probability of dying in that instant), and every annuity formula is a sum or integral of (discount factor) × (probability of being alive at that instant) — the entire table is really only two ideas, endlessly recombined across different triggers and timings. When a problem introduces a symbol you don't immediately recognize (a two-life version, an increasing benefit, a benefit with a guarantee period), the fastest way to make sense of it is to ask which of these two underlying ideas it's built from, and over what range of times.

5. Variance of Insurance & Annuity Random Variables

Learning Outcomes Covered: Derive and apply the "double force of interest" formula for the variance of the whole life insurance PV random variable; convert an insurance variance into the corresponding annuity variance; compute variances for term insurance and compare relative dispersion across benefit types.

5.1 Variance of Z: The Double-Force Trick

For any random variable, Var(Z) = E[Z²] − (E[Z])². Here Z = vKx+1, so Z² = v2(Kx+1) = (v²)Kx+1. That is exactly the same functional form as Z itself, except the discount factor v has been replaced by v² everywhere. Since v² is the discount factor you'd get from a force of interest 2δ (i.e., double the original force of interest, equivalently an effective annual rate of (1+i)² − 1), E[Z²] is nothing more than the whole life insurance EPV recomputed with the discount factor v² substituted for v throughout the exact same sum. Actuarial notation flags this substitution with a small left superscript 2:

Variance of Whole Life Insurance PV Random VariableVar(Z) = 2Ax − (Ax)², where 2Ax = Σ v2(k+1) kpx qx+k
"Double force of interest" means literally recomputing the EPV formula with v replaced by v² (equivalently, at the doubled force 2δ, or at the effective annual rate (1+i)² − 1) — it does not mean doubling Ax itself, and it does not mean doubling i. Students who take "double the interest" too literally sometimes compute Ax at rate 2i, which is a completely different (and wrong) number. The correct move is always: take the exact same summation or integral formula used for Ax, and substitute v² for v (or 2δ for δ in a continuous formula) everywhere it appears.
Example 9.19

In the constant-force world (μ = 0.02, i = 5%), find Var(Z) and the standard deviation of Z. Recall Ax ≈ 0.28372.

Show Solution

Doubling the force of interest means using v² = 1/(1.05)² = 0.907029 in place of v = 0.952381 in the Example 9.1 formula. With e−μ = 0.980199 unchanged (mortality doesn't change):

v²·e−μ = 0.907029 × 0.980199 = 0.889069, and v²·q = 0.907029 × 0.019801 = 0.017960.

2Ax = 0.017960 / (1 − 0.889069) = 0.017960/0.110931 ≈ 0.16190

Then:

Var(Z) = 0.16190 − (0.28372)² = 0.16190 − 0.08050 = 0.08140

SD(Z) = √0.08140 ≈ 0.2853

Notice the standard deviation (0.2853) is actually larger than the mean (0.2837) — unsurprising for a variable that's usually near zero (if death is far off, Z is small) but occasionally near 1 (if death is imminent), a classically right-skewed, high-dispersion payoff.

5.2 Covariance Between the Term Insurance and Pure Endowment Pieces

Because n-year endowment insurance is defined as the sum of a term insurance PV random variable Z₁ and a pure endowment PV random variable Z₂, its variance is not simply Var(Z₁) + Var(Z₂) unless the two pieces are uncorrelated — and they are decidedly not independent, since they are triggered by mutually exclusive events. If (x) dies within the n years, Z₁ = vKx+1 and Z₂ = 0; if (x) survives to time n, Z₁ = 0 and Z₂ = vn. The product Z₁Z₂ is therefore always exactly zero, no matter what happens — a fact that makes the covariance easy to compute directly from its definition rather than requiring any new machinery:

Covariance of Term Insurance and Pure Endowment PV Random VariablesCov(Z1,Z2) = E[Z1Z2] − E[Z1]E[Z2] = 0 − A1x:n⌐ · nEx = −A1x:n⌐ · nEx

The covariance is always negative, which makes sense: whenever the term insurance piece pays something, the pure endowment piece is guaranteed to pay nothing, and vice versa — a textbook case of negatively associated random variables. This lets you assemble the full endowment insurance variance from pieces you already know how to compute, using the standard variance-of-a-sum rule Var(Z1+Z2) = Var(Z1) + Var(Z2) + 2Cov(Z1,Z2).

Example 9.20

In the De Moivre world (x = 40, ω = 100, n = 20, i = 5%), you already have A140:20⌐ ≈ 0.20770 and 20E40 ≈ 0.25126 (Examples 9.2–9.3), and Var(Z1) ≈ 0.09636 for the term insurance piece (Example 9.22). Find Cov(Z1,Z2), then Var(Z2) for the pure endowment piece directly, and finally assemble Var(Z) for the full 20-year endowment insurance. Confirm your answer against a direct computation of 2A40:20⌐ − (A40:20⌐)².

Show Solution

Covariance: Cov(Z1,Z2) = −0.20770 × 0.25126 ≈ −0.05219.

Var(Z2): Z2 = v²⁰ · I, where I is a Bernoulli indicator of surviving 20 years with 20p40 = 0.666667. So Var(Z2) = v⁴⁰ · 20p40(1 − 20p40) = 0.142046 × 0.666667 × 0.333333 ≈ 0.031566.

Assembled Var(Z): Var(Z) = 0.09636 + 0.031566 + 2(−0.05219) = 0.127931 − 0.104372 ≈ 0.023559.

Direct check: 2A40:20⌐ = 2A140:20⌐ + 2(20E40), where the doubled-force pure endowment term is v⁴⁰ · 20p40 = 0.142046 × 0.666667 ≈ 0.094697 (note only the discount factor doubles — the survival probability is untouched). With 2A140:20⌐ ≈ 0.139505 from Example 9.23, 2A40:20⌐ ≈ 0.139505 + 0.094697 = 0.234202. Then Var(Z) = 0.234202 − (0.45896)² = 0.234202 − 0.210645 ≈ 0.023557 — matching the covariance-based figure of 0.023559 up to rounding, exactly as it must.

5.3 Converting Insurance Variance to Annuity Variance

The pathwise identity from Chapter 4, Y = (1 − Z)/d, is a linear transformation of Z, so the standard variance-of-a-linear-transformation rule applies directly — no new derivation needed:

Variance of Whole Life Annuity-Due PV Random VariableVar(Y) = Var((1−Z)/d) = Var(Z)/d²

The subtraction from a constant (the "1") and the sign flip inside the parentheses don't affect variance at all — only the division by d survives into the variance formula, and it enters squared, exactly as it would for Var(aX+b) = a²Var(X).

Example 9.21

Using Var(Z) ≈ 0.08140 from Example 9.19 and d = 0.047619, find Var(Y) and SD(Y) for the constant-force world.

Show Solution

d² = 0.047619² = 0.0022676.

Var(Y) = 0.08140 / 0.0022676 ≈ 35.90

SD(Y) = √35.90 ≈ 5.991

Compared to a mean annuity value of äx ≈ 15.04, a standard deviation of about 6.0 reflects genuinely large uncertainty in how long the life annuity will actually run — entirely driven by the same mortality uncertainty embedded in Z, just rescaled.

Example 9.22

Repeat the whole life variance calculation in the De Moivre world (x = 40, ω = 100, i = 5%), where A40 ≈ 0.31549. You'll need 2A40 = a60⌐ evaluated at the doubled force, i.e. at effective annual rate i* = (1.05)² − 1 = 0.1025, divided by 60.

Show Solution

At i* = 10.25%, v* = 1/1.1025 = 0.907029, and (v*)²⁰ = v⁴⁰ = 0.142046 (since squaring the discount factor for 20 years at the doubled rate reaches the same place as 40 years at the original rate). So:

a60⌐ at i* = (1 − (v*)⁶⁰)/i*. Since (v*)⁶⁰ = v¹²⁰ = (v⁶⁰)² = 0.053535² = 0.0028660:

a60⌐(i*) = (1 − 0.0028660)/0.1025 = 0.99713/0.1025 ≈ 9.7285

2A40 = 9.7285/60 ≈ 0.16214

Var(Z) = 0.16214 − (0.31549)² = 0.16214 − 0.09954 = 0.06260

Converting to the annuity: Var(Y) = 0.06260/0.0022676 ≈ 27.61, so SD(Y) ≈ 5.254 against a mean ä40 ≈ 14.37.

Example 9.23

Compute the variance of the 20-year term insurance PV random variable in the De Moivre world (A140:20⌐ ≈ 0.20770 from Example 9.2), and compare its coefficient of variation (SD/mean) to that of the whole life insurance found in Example 9.21.

Show Solution

The double-force trick applies to term insurance identically: 2A140:20⌐ is the term insurance EPV recomputed at v² = (v*)²⁰... more precisely, using (v*)²⁰ = v⁴⁰ = 0.142046 and i* = 10.25%: a20⌐(i*) = (1 − 0.142046)/0.1025 = 0.857954/0.1025 ≈ 8.3703, so 2A140:20⌐ = 8.3703/60 ≈ 0.13950.

Var(Zterm) = 0.13950 − (0.20770)² = 0.13950 − 0.04314 = 0.09636

SD(Zterm) = √0.09636 ≈ 0.3104. Its coefficient of variation is 0.3104/0.20770 ≈ 1.495.

For the whole life insurance in Example 9.21: SD(Z) = √0.06260 ≈ 0.2502, mean 0.31549, coefficient of variation ≈ 0.793.

Term insurance is nearly twice as volatile, relative to its mean, as whole life insurance on the same life. This makes intuitive sense: term insurance is closer to an all-or-nothing bet (pays a fixed amount if death strikes in a narrow window, otherwise pays exactly zero), while whole life insurance is a near-certainty of eventually paying something, with only the timing — not the fact — of payment in question.

5.4 The General Pattern, and Why Variance Matters Beyond This Chapter

Every variance formula in this chapter follows one recipe, and it's worth stating it in full generality so you can apply it to any benefit structure this exam throws at you, not just the four cases worked above. If Z is any life-contingent PV random variable built from a discount factor raised to a random policy-year exponent, then Z² is always the same type of random variable with the discount factor replaced by its square — so E[Z²] is always "the same EPV formula, evaluated at double the force of interest." This holds for term insurance (Example 9.23), endowment insurance, deferred insurance, and even mthly or continuous benefits, with the doubled force applied to whichever discount factor appears in that benefit's own formula (v² in the discrete case, e−2δt in the continuous case). Once you have E[Z²] and E[Z], Var(Z) = E[Z²] − (E[Z])² finishes the job exactly as in Examples 9.19 and 9.21–9.23.

Why go to this trouble at all? On the syllabus, single-life variances rarely appear as an end in themselves — they are the building block for questions about a portfolio of similar, independent policies, where the Central Limit Theorem lets you approximate the total claims or total annuity payout with a normal distribution whose mean and variance are just the sum of the individual means and variances (independence makes the covariance terms vanish). A question that asks for "the amount of assets needed so that a portfolio of 500 independent whole life policies has only a 5% chance of exceeding it" is, underneath the probability language, nothing more than an application of the single-life Var(Z) formula from this chapter multiplied by 500, plus a standard normal quantile. Getting comfortable with the double-force trick here pays off well beyond this chapter's own practice problems.

6. Sensitivity to Assumptions

Learning Outcomes Covered: Determine and explain the direction and approximate magnitude of change in insurance and annuity EPVs when interest rate or mortality assumptions change.

6.1 Sensitivity to the Interest Rate

Both Ax and äx are weighted averages of discount factors vt (weighted by the probability of a payment at time t). Raising i lowers v = 1/(1+i) at every single future time point, and lowers it by more the further out the payment date — a claim payable in year 40 is discounted far more severely by a one-point interest rate hike than a claim payable in year 2. Since every term in both EPV sums shrinks (or, at worst, stays the same for a time-0 payment), raising i strictly decreases both Ax and äx. There is no scenario in which raising the assumed interest rate increases either EPV.

Example 9.24

In the constant-force world (μ = 0.02), recompute Ax and äx at i = 6% instead of 5%, and find the percentage change from the Example 9.1/9.10 baseline (Ax ≈ 0.28372, äx ≈ 15.0427).

Show Solution

At i = 6%: v = 1/1.06 = 0.943396. With e−μ = 0.980199 unchanged, v·e−μ = 0.924716, so 1 − v·e−μ = 0.075284. And v·q = v(1−e−μ) = 0.943396 × 0.019801 = 0.018680.

Ax(6%) = 0.018680/0.075284 ≈ 0.24811

äx(6%) = 1/0.075284 ≈ 13.2831

Percentage changes from the i = 5% baseline: Ax falls from 0.28372 to 0.24811, a decrease of about 12.6%. äx falls from 15.0427 to 13.2831, a decrease of about 11.7%. Both EPVs move down, as the general argument predicts, and by roughly comparable (double-digit) percentages for this one-point rate change.

6.2 Sensitivity to Mortality

Mortality's effect is more subtle because it pulls Ax and äx in opposite directions. Making mortality heavier (higher force of mortality, or a table with systematically higher q's) shifts probability mass toward earlier deaths. For insurance, an earlier death means the fixed benefit of 1 is discounted less — paid sooner — so Ax rises. For the annuity, an earlier death means fewer expected payments collected before the income stream stops, so äx falls. Both movements point the same underlying way (mortality risk being realized sooner), but insurance and annuities sit on opposite sides of that risk, so their EPVs respond with opposite signs.

Example 9.25

Still holding i = 5%, raise the force of mortality from μ = 0.02 to μ = 0.03 (heavier mortality) and find the resulting Ax and äx, comparing percentage changes to the Example 9.1/9.10 baseline.

Show Solution

At μ = 0.03: e−μ = 0.970446, q = 1 − 0.970446 = 0.029554. With v = 0.952381 unchanged: v·e−μ = 0.952381 × 0.970446 = 0.924234, so 1 − v·e−μ = 0.075766. And v·q = 0.952381 × 0.029554 = 0.028147.

Ax(μ=0.03) = 0.028147/0.075766 ≈ 0.37148

äx(μ=0.03) = 1/0.075766 ≈ 13.1985

Ax rises from 0.28372 to 0.37148, an increase of about 30.9%. äx falls from 15.0427 to 13.1985, a decrease of about 12.3%. As predicted: insurance up, annuity down, from the same directional change in mortality. Notice Ax's percentage swing (+30.9%) dwarfs äx's (−12.3%) — insurance EPVs, being closer to zero on average, tend to show much larger relative sensitivity to mortality shifts than annuity EPVs do, even when the underlying assumption change is the same.

Example 9.26

Confirm the same qualitative mortality pattern in a completely different mortality model. In the De Moivre world, shortening the limiting age from ω = 100 to ω = 90 (with x still 40, so remaining lifetime drops from uniform(0,60) to uniform(0,50)) represents heavier mortality — deaths are now compressed into a shorter maximum span. Find A40 and ä40 at ω = 90 and compare to the ω = 100 baseline (A40 ≈ 0.31549, ä40 ≈ 14.3747).

Show Solution

With n₀ = 50: v⁵⁰ = v⁴⁰·v¹⁰ = 0.142046 × 0.613913 = 0.087202. a50⌐ = (1 − 0.087202)/0.05 = 0.912798/0.05 = 18.2560.

A40(ω=90) = 18.2560/50 ≈ 0.36512

ä40(ω=90) = (1 − 0.36512)/0.047619 ≈ 13.3325

A40 rises from 0.31549 to 0.36512 (+15.7%), and ä40 falls from 14.3747 to 13.3325 (−7.3%). Same qualitative pattern as Example 9.25 — insurance up, annuity down under heavier mortality — even though the underlying survival law (De Moivre vs. constant force) is completely different. The direction of these sensitivities is a property of the insurance/annuity structure itself, not of any particular mortality model.

Example 9.27

Interest and mortality assumptions rarely move one at a time in practice. Combine both changes from Examples 9.24–9.25 simultaneously — i = 6% and μ = 0.03 together — and find Ax and äx. Compare the combined percentage changes to what you'd naively expect from simply adding the two separate effects.

Show Solution

v = 1/1.06 = 0.943396, e−μ = e−0.03 = 0.970446, q = 0.029554.

v·e−μ = 0.943396 × 0.970446 = 0.915515, so 1 − v·e−μ = 0.084485. v·q = 0.943396 × 0.029554 = 0.027881.

Ax(i=6%,μ=0.03) = 0.027881/0.084485 ≈ 0.33000

äx(i=6%,μ=0.03) = 1/0.084485 ≈ 11.8367

Versus baseline Ax ≈ 0.28372: an increase to 0.33000, or about +16.3%. This is much smaller than the +30.9% seen from the mortality change alone (Example 9.25) — the interest rate increase (which alone would have decreased Ax by 12.6%, per Example 9.24) partially cancels the mortality-driven increase. The two assumption changes push Ax in opposite directions and partly offset.

Versus baseline äx ≈ 15.0427: a decrease to 11.8367, or about −21.3%. Here the two changes reinforce rather than cancel — both a higher interest rate and heavier mortality independently push äx down (Examples 9.24 and 9.25 each showed roughly −12% alone), and combined they compound to roughly double the single-assumption impact. This asymmetry — offsetting on the insurance side, reinforcing on the annuity side — is exactly what the opposite-sign relationship from section 6.2 predicts, and is a favorite theme for "what happens if both assumptions change" exam questions.

6.3 Summary of Directions, and a Duration Intuition

It's useful to have the four directional results of this chapter in one place before moving on:

Direction of EPV Change Given an Increase in Each Assumption
Assumption IncreasedEffect on Ax (insurance)Effect on äx (annuity)
Interest rate iDecreasesDecreases
Force of mortality μ (heavier mortality)IncreasesDecreases

Only one cell in that table ever has an ambiguous combined effect when both assumptions move in the "adverse" direction at once (higher i together with heavier mortality): Ax, which has one assumption pushing it down and the other pushing it up, as Example 9.27 demonstrated. The annuity value äx never faces that ambiguity — every entry in its column points the same way (down) whenever either assumption rises, so an increase in either i or μ (or both) is guaranteed to reduce a life annuity's value, with no offsetting possible.

A useful intuition for why interest sensitivity in particular can feel larger or smaller depending on the underlying benefit is to think in terms of an average payment date. A benefit whose expected payment date is far in the future (a long-duration deferred annuity, or a term insurance policy on a very old life where death is likely soon relative to a whole-life contract on a young life) is more sensitive, in percentage terms, to a change in i than a benefit whose expected payment date is close to today — precisely the same "duration" concept used to measure the interest-rate sensitivity of a bond. This is why longer-dated benefits (whole life insurance issued at a young age, or deferred annuities with a long deferral period) generally show larger percentage swings under an interest rate assumption change than short-dated term insurance or immediate annuities, even though the direction of the change is always the same.

Part IX Practice Problems

1. A life (x) is subject to a constant force of mortality μ = 0.015, with i = 5%. Find Ax.
Show Answer

v = 0.952381, e−0.015 = 0.985112, so v·e−μ = 0.938201 and v·q = v(1 − 0.985112) = 0.952381 × 0.014888 = 0.014179.

Ax = 0.014179/(1 − 0.938201) = 0.014179/0.061799 ≈ 0.22943.

2. Under De Moivre's law with x = 50 and ω = 90 (so remaining lifetime is uniform on 0 to 40 years), find A50 at i = 5%.
Show Answer

n₀ = 40, v⁴⁰ = 0.142046, a40⌐ = (1 − 0.142046)/0.05 = 17.1591.

A50 = 17.1591/40 ≈ 0.42898.

3. In the same De Moivre world as Examples 9.2/9.3 (x = 40, ω = 100, i = 5%), find A140:15⌐, the EPV of 15-year term insurance.
Show Answer

v¹⁵ = 0.481017 (from 1.05¹⁵ = 2.078928). a15⌐ = (1 − 0.481017)/0.05 = 10.37966.

A140:15⌐ = 10.37966/60 ≈ 0.17299.

4. Using the De Moivre world of Example 9.2, find the EPV of a 10-year deferred whole life insurance on (40), 10|A40. (You will need A140:10⌐ and the whole life EPV A40 ≈ 0.31549.)
Show Answer

v¹⁰ = 0.613913, a10⌐ = (1 − 0.613913)/0.05 = 7.72174, so A140:10⌐ = 7.72174/60 ≈ 0.12870.

10|A40 = A40 − A140:10⌐ = 0.31549 − 0.12870 ≈ 0.18679.

5. Constant force of mortality μ = 0.025, i = 5%. Find the continuous whole life insurance EPV Āx.
Show Answer

δ = ln(1.05) = 0.048790. Āx = μ/(μ+δ) = 0.025/0.073790 ≈ 0.33880.

6. A block of business has Ax = 0.35 at i = 6% under the annual (end-of-year) benefit model. Using claims acceleration, approximate Ax(4).
Show Answer

i(4) = 4[(1.06)0.25 − 1] = 4(1.014676 − 1) = 0.058704. i/i(4) = 0.06/0.058704 ≈ 1.02207.

Ax(4) ≈ 1.02207 × 0.35 ≈ 0.35772.

7. In the De Moivre world of Example 9.2 (x = 40, ω = 100, i = 5%), find the percentage difference between the continuous EPV Ā40 ≈ 0.32338 and the discrete EPV A40 ≈ 0.31549.
Show Answer

(0.32338 − 0.31549)/0.31549 ≈ 0.00789/0.31549 ≈ 2.50% higher under continuous payment.

8. Given äx = 18 at m = 12 installments per year, approximate äx(12) using the standard mthly annuity approximation.
Show Answer

äx(12) ≈ 18 − 11/24 = 18 − 0.4583 ≈ 17.5417.

9. In the De Moivre world of problem 2 (x = 50, ω = 90, i = 5%, A50 ≈ 0.42898), find ä50 using the fundamental identity.
Show Answer

d = 0.047619. ä50 = (1 − 0.42898)/0.047619 = 0.57102/0.047619 ≈ 11.9915.

10. In the De Moivre world of Example 9.2, find the 15-year temporary annuity-due ä40:15⌐, given A40:15⌐ = A140:15⌐ + 15E40, where A140:15⌐ ≈ 0.17299 (problem 3) and 15E40 = v¹⁵ · 15p40.
Show Answer

15p40 = (60−15)/60 = 0.75, so 15E40 = 0.481017 × 0.75 ≈ 0.36076.

A40:15⌐ = 0.17299 + 0.36076 = 0.53376. ä40:15⌐ = (1 − 0.53376)/0.047619 ≈ 9.7912.

11. Constant-force world (μ = 0.02, i = 5%): if äx ≈ 15.0427, find the annuity-immediate value ax.
Show Answer

ax = äx − 1 = 15.0427 − 1 ≈ 14.0427.

12. Constant force μ = 0.025, i = 5% (as in problem 5, Āx ≈ 0.33880). Find the continuous annuity āx using the continuous fundamental identity.
Show Answer

δ = 0.048790. āx = (1 − 0.33880)/0.048790 = 0.66120/0.048790 ≈ 13.5522.

13. If äx = 16.5 at i = 5%, find Ax using the fundamental identity.
Show Answer

d = 0.047619. Ax = 1 − 0.047619 × 16.5 = 1 − 0.785714 ≈ 0.21429.

14. If Āx = 0.40 at δ = 0.06, find āx using the continuous fundamental identity.
Show Answer

āx = (1 − 0.40)/0.06 = 0.60/0.06 = 10.0000.

15. Verify, for the De Moivre world of problem 10, that ä40:15⌐ computed via the identity in problem 10 is consistent — i.e., recompute A40:15⌐ from ä40:15⌐ ≈ 9.7912 and confirm you recover ≈ 0.53376.
Show Answer

A40:15⌐ = 1 − d·ä40:15⌐ = 1 − 0.047619 × 9.7912 = 1 − 0.46625 ≈ 0.53375, matching problem 10 up to rounding.

16. Explain briefly why the identity äx:n⌐ = (1 − Ax:n⌐)/d cannot be applied with A1x:n⌐ (term insurance) in place of Ax:n⌐ (endowment insurance).
Show Answer

The pathwise identity Y = (1−Z)/d requires the annuity's payment duration and the insurance benefit's payment time to be driven by the same random stopping time, min(Kx+1, n). Endowment insurance is paid at exactly that stopping time (death or survival to n, whichever comes first) — matching the temporary annuity's own stopping rule. Term insurance instead pays nothing at all if the insured survives to n, so its payment time isn't min(Kx+1, n) in the "always pays something" sense the identity needs; using A1 in the formula produces a value with no consistent random-variable interpretation and will not match a direct summation of äx:n⌐.

17. In the μ = 0.015, i = 5% world of problem 1 (Ax ≈ 0.22943), find Var(Z) for whole life insurance. (Double the force of interest means using v² = 1/(1.05)² = 0.907029 with mortality unchanged.)
Show Answer

v²·e−μ = 0.907029 × 0.985112 = 0.893527, so 1 − v²e−μ = 0.106473. v²·q = 0.907029 × 0.014888 = 0.013503.

2Ax = 0.013503/0.106473 ≈ 0.126824.

Var(Z) = 0.126824 − (0.22943)² = 0.126824 − 0.052638 ≈ 0.074186.

18. Using Var(Z) ≈ 0.074186 from problem 17 and d = 0.047619, find Var(Y) for the whole life annuity-due, and its standard deviation.
Show Answer

d² = 0.0022676. Var(Y) = 0.074186/0.0022676 ≈ 32.716. SD(Y) = √32.716 ≈ 5.720.

19. In the De Moivre world of problem 2 (x = 50, ω = 90, n₀ = 40, i = 5%, A50 ≈ 0.42898), find Var(Z) for whole life insurance. You'll need 2A50 = a40⌐ at the doubled rate i* = (1.05)² − 1 = 10.25%, divided by 40, where (v*)⁴⁰ = v⁸⁰ = (v⁴⁰)² = 0.142046² = 0.020177.
Show Answer

a40⌐(i*=10.25%) = (1 − 0.020177)/0.1025 = 0.979823/0.1025 ≈ 9.55925.

2A50 = 9.55925/40 ≈ 0.238981.

Var(Z) = 0.238981 − (0.42898)² = 0.238981 − 0.183983 ≈ 0.054998. SD(Z) ≈ 0.2345.

20. Without doing any new arithmetic, explain whether you'd expect the coefficient of variation of a 10-year term insurance PV random variable to be larger or smaller than that of the corresponding whole life insurance PV random variable on the same life, and why.
Show Answer

Larger. Term insurance is a closer approximation to an all-or-nothing bet — it pays a fixed amount with a relatively small probability (dying within the term) and pays exactly zero otherwise (surviving the term), which produces high relative dispersion around a small mean. Whole life insurance, by contrast, pays out with probability 1 eventually, so its randomness is confined to the discount factor at the (uncertain but inevitable) time of payment — a narrower range of outcomes relative to its mean than term insurance exhibits. (This mirrors the numeric comparison worked out in Example 9.23, where the term insurance's coefficient of variation was roughly double the whole life insurance's.)

21. Constant-force world (μ = 0.02, baseline i = 5%, Ax ≈ 0.28372, äx ≈ 15.0427). Recompute Ax and äx at a lower interest rate, i = 4%, and state the direction and approximate percentage change for each.
Show Answer

v = 1/1.04 = 0.961538, e−μ = 0.980199, v·e−μ = 0.942497, 1 − v·e−μ = 0.057503. v·q = 0.961538 × 0.019801 = 0.019040.

Ax(4%) = 0.019040/0.057503 ≈ 0.33110 — an increase of about +16.7% from the 5% baseline (lower interest means less discounting, so insurance is worth more).

äx(4%) = 1/0.057503 ≈ 17.3908 — an increase of about +15.6% (lower interest also makes future annuity payments worth more today).

22. Same constant-force baseline as problem 21, but now hold i = 5% fixed and reduce mortality to μ = 0.01 (lighter mortality). Find Ax and äx, and state the direction and approximate percentage change for each versus the μ = 0.02 baseline.
Show Answer

v = 0.952381 (unchanged), e−0.01 = 0.990050, v·e−μ = 0.942905, 1 − v·e−μ = 0.057095. v·q = 0.952381 × 0.009950 = 0.009476.

Ax(μ=0.01) = 0.009476/0.057095 ≈ 0.16595 — a decrease of about −41.5% from the μ=0.02 baseline (lighter mortality means the death benefit is, on average, paid much further in the future, so it's discounted more).

äx(μ=0.01) = 1/0.057095 ≈ 17.5147 — an increase of about +16.4% (lighter mortality means more expected annuity payments collected).

23. Combine problems 21 and 22: lower the interest rate to i = 4% and lighten mortality to μ = 0.01 simultaneously (still relative to the μ = 0.02, i = 5% baseline). Find Ax and äx, and comment on whether the two individual effects reinforce or offset each other for each quantity.
Show Answer

v = 1/1.04 = 0.961538, e−0.01 = 0.990050, v·e−μ = 0.961538 × 0.990050 ≈ 0.951971, 1 − v·e−μ = 0.048029. v·q = 0.961538 × 0.009950 ≈ 0.009567.

Ax = 0.009567/0.048029 ≈ 0.19923 — a decrease of about −29.8% from baseline 0.28372. Here the two individual effects offset: lower interest alone would have pushed Ax up (+16.7%, problem 21), but the lighter-mortality effect alone (−41.5%, problem 22) is stronger and dominates, so the combined result is a net decrease, smaller in magnitude than the mortality effect alone would suggest.

äx = (1 − 0.19923)/0.038462 ≈ 20.8214 — an increase of about +38.4% from baseline 15.0427. Here the two individual effects reinforce: both lower interest (+15.6%, problem 21) and lighter mortality (+16.4%, problem 22) push äx up on their own, and combined they compound to roughly double either single effect.

24. Express a "10-year deferred, 20-year term" insurance on (x) — which pays 1 at the end of the year of death only if death occurs between policy years 11 and 30 inclusive — in standard actuarial notation, and give a formula for its EPV as a difference of two term insurance EPVs you've already seen in this part.
Show Answer

In standard notation this is written 10|A1x:20⌐ (a 20-year term insurance, deferred 10 years). Two equivalent EPV formulas: 10|A1x:20⌐ = 10Ex · A1x+10:20⌐ (a 10-year pure endowment factor times a fresh 20-year term insurance purchased at the attained age x+10), or equivalently as a difference of two term insurances measured from age x: 10|A1x:20⌐ = A1x:30⌐ − A1x:10⌐ (a 30-year term insurance minus the first 10 years of coverage that the deferred policy excludes). Both formulas mirror the deferred whole life insurance relationship from section 1.5, just capped at a finite term instead of running for life.

Part 10 — Premiums & Policy Values for Long-Term Insurance Coverages (15–22.5%)

This part is the largest single block of the FAM syllabus, and for good reason: it is where survival-model theory finally turns into the two numbers an insurer actually has to produce — the premium it charges and the reserve it must hold on its balance sheet. We start by writing down the loss a contract generates from the insurer's point of view as a random variable, then use that random variable three different ways to set a premium (equivalence, a portfolio percentile target, a target-profit constraint). From there we turn to policy values — snapshots, at any duration, of how much the company should be holding against a policy still in force — first under the idealized "net premium" assumptions, then with realistic expenses folded in, then with modified first-year reserving used for statutory relief. We finish with the recursive machinery that lets a reserve be rolled forward one year at a time, and with the standard toolkit for pricing substandard (extra-risk) lives. Every numeric example below is built from one of two small, fully transparent mortality assumptions — either a De Moivre (uniform remaining-lifetime) law with limiting age ω = 100, or a flat constant-mortality-rate model — chosen specifically so you can re-derive every number by hand and confirm it yourself.

1. The Future Loss Random Variable

Learning Outcomes Covered: Define and construct the future loss random variable for whole life, term, and endowment insurance, and for life annuities, on a single life; recognize how the random variable is driven entirely by the curtate (or continuous) future lifetime; distinguish insurance-type contracts, where premiums fund a benefit paid on death or survival, from annuity-type contracts, where the annuity income stream is itself the benefit and premiums are typically confined to an initial funding period.

Every long-term life insurance contract commits the insurer to a stream of possible cash inflows (premiums) and a stream of possible cash outflows (benefits), and the timing of both streams depends on how long the insured lives. From the insurer's chair, the natural object to study is not "the premium" or "the benefit" in isolation, but the net financial result: benefits paid out minus premiums taken in, all discounted back to the policy issue date. Because that result depends on an unknown future lifetime, it is a random variable — traditionally called the future loss random variable and denoted L0, with the subscript reminding us that we are valuing everything as of time 0, the moment the policy is issued. A positive value of L0 represents a shortfall for the insurer (benefits outweigh premiums in present-value terms); a negative value represents a gain.

General DefinitionL0 = PV(future benefits) − PV(future premiums)

For a whole life insurance of 1 issued to (x), with level annual premium P payable at the start of each year the insured survives, the benefit is vKx+1 (paid at the end of the year of death) and the premium stream is an annuity-due of P for Kx+1 years (the insured pays at the start of every year they are alive to pay it, including the year they eventually die in, since the last premium is due before the death that ends the contract). This gives:

Whole Life Insurance — L0L0 = vKx+1 − P·ä(Kx+1⌐)

Both pieces of this expression are functions of the single random variable Kx, the curtate future lifetime of (x). That is the key structural fact about L0: for a single-life contract with no other random elements (no random lapses, no stochastic interest), the entire future loss collapses to a function of one discrete random variable. Once you know Kx = k for some integer k ≥ 0, L0 is a known number — there is no remaining randomness. This is what makes the expected-value and percentile techniques of the next two chapters tractable: we are really just computing E[g(Kx)] and Var[g(Kx)] for a specific function g.

Using the annuity-due-to-insurance conversion ä(k+1⌐) = (1 − vk+1)/d, the whole life loss variable can be rewritten in a form that is often more convenient for algebra because it isolates all the randomness into a single vKx+1 term:

Whole Life Insurance — Alternative FormL0 = vKx+1(1 + P/d) − P/d

This second form is worth memorizing, because both the expectation and the variance of L0 follow immediately from it: since (1 + P/d) and P/d are constants, E[L0] and Var[L0] reduce directly to the mean and variance of vKx+1, which are exactly Ax and (²Ax − Ax²), where ²Ax denotes the whole life insurance EPV computed with v² in place of v (equivalently, at the interest rate that makes (1+i)² the discount base). We will use exactly this shortcut in Chapter 3.

Everything above was built on the curtate future lifetime Kx, which assumes death benefits are paid only at the end of the policy year of death — the standard discrete-time convention used throughout this manual. A fully continuous version of the same contract, in which the death benefit is paid at the instant of death and premiums are collected continuously rather than annually, replaces Kx with the continuous future lifetime Tx and vKx+1 with vTx = e−δTx, where δ is the force of interest. The loss random variable becomes L0 = vTx − P̄·ā(Tx⌐), with ā denoting a continuous annuity and P̄ a continuously-paid premium rate; the structural logic — a single random future-lifetime variable driving both the benefit and premium terms, and the equivalence principle setting the expectation of the whole expression to zero — carries over unchanged. FAM problems overwhelmingly use the discrete (curtate) formulation, since real premiums are collected annually (or monthly) in practice, so this manual follows that convention throughout; the continuous version is worth recognizing on sight but rarely needs to be evaluated numerically on this exam.

Term and Endowment Insurance

The same construction applies to any death-benefit contract; only the benefit function of Kx changes. For an n-year term insurance of 1 on (x), the death benefit is paid only if death occurs within the first n years, so the loss variable is a piecewise function of Kx:

n-Year Term Insurance — L0L0 = vKx+1 − P·ä(min(Kx+1,n)⌐), if Kx < n; L0 = −P·ä(n⌐), if Kx ≥ n

Notice that once the insured survives past duration n, both the benefit and the future premium obligation vanish (premiums stop at n, and no death benefit will ever be paid), so L0 becomes the fixed, already-known quantity −P·ä(n⌐) — a certain gain to the insurer equal to the premiums already banked, viewed from time 0.

For an n-year endowment insurance, the benefit of 1 is paid at the end of the year of death if death occurs within n years, or at time n if the insured survives to time n — so a benefit is paid under every possible outcome, which is what makes endowment insurance the most expensive of the three basic single-life death-benefit designs per dollar of coverage:

n-Year Endowment Insurance — L0L0 = vmin(Kx+1,n) − P·ä(min(Kx+1,n)⌐)
Sign-convention trap. Always define L0 as benefits minus premiums, never the reverse. Under this convention a positive expectation is bad news for the insurer (premiums are, on average, insufficient) and the equivalence principle in Chapter 2 is simply the statement E[L0] = 0. Some worked solutions define the loss the other way around (premiums minus benefits); if you ever mix conventions mid-problem, every subsequent inequality and percentile flips direction. Pick one convention at the start of a problem and stay with it.

Annuity-Type Contracts

Life annuities flip the insurance structure on its head: the annuity payments are the benefit, and they are typically funded by premiums collected only over a limited initial period (often the same period during which the annuitant is still working, before payments begin, or — for an immediate annuity purchased with a single premium — no ongoing premium period at all). For an annuity-due of 1 per year for life on (x), funded by a level premium P payable for at most the first n years while (x) survives, the future loss random variable is:

Life Annuity Funded over n Years — L0L0 = ä(Kx+1⌐) − P·ä(min(Kx+1,n)⌐)

Here the benefit term ä(Kx+1⌐) runs for the insured's entire remaining lifetime while the premium term is capped at n years, so for Kx ≥ n the two annuity terms no longer track each other and L0 keeps growing (in the insurer's favor or against it, depending on the sign of the funding gap) for every additional year the annuitant survives beyond the premium-paying period. This is the defining risk profile of a life annuity: the insurer's downside is unbounded in duration (a very long-lived annuitant), which is the mirror image of a term insurance, whose downside is bounded by the fixed term.

Example 10.1

A whole life insurance of 1 is issued to (90), with premiums payable annually for life. Mortality follows a De Moivre law with limiting age ω = 100 (so the curtate future lifetime K90 is equally likely to be 0, 1, 2, …, 9), and i = 5%. Write L0 as a function of K90, and compute its value if the insured dies in the 4th policy year (i.e., K90 = 3).

Show Solution

By definition, L0 = vK90+1 − P·ä(K90+1⌐), where K90 ∈ {0,1,…,9}, each with probability 0.1. If K90 = 3, the death benefit is paid at the end of year 4, so the benefit term is v4 = 1.05−4 = 0.822702, and 4 premiums were paid (at times 0, 1, 2, 3), so ä(4⌐) = 1 + v + v² + v³ = 1 + 0.952381 + 0.907029 + 0.863838 = 3.723248. Using the whole-life net premium for this same De Moivre model, computed in Example 10.3 below, P = 0.1614, so L0 = 0.822702 − 0.1614(3.723248) = 0.822702 − 0.601034 = 0.221668. The insurer loses (in present-value terms) about 0.2217 per unit of face amount if the insured dies in year 4 — unsurprising, since early death always produces a loss under a level-premium whole life contract, because too few premiums have been collected to offset the benefit.

Example 10.2

For the same policy as Example 10.1, what is L0 if the insured survives all the way to age 100 (the limiting age, so death occurs at exactly the end of year 10, K90=9)? Compare this to the loss if death occurs in year 1.

Show Solution

If K90 = 9: benefit = v10 = 0.613913; all 10 premiums are paid, so ä(10⌐) = (1−v10)/d = (1−0.613913)/(1/21) = 0.386087 × 21 = 8.107827. L0 = 0.613913 − 0.1614(8.107827) = 0.613913 − 1.308803 = −0.694890, a substantial gain to the insurer, because ten years of premiums were collected before the (still-certain) benefit was paid. If instead K90 = 0 (death in year 1): benefit = v = 0.952381; only 1 premium was paid, ä(1⌐) = 1, so L0 = 0.952381 − 0.1614(1) = 0.790981, the worst outcome the insurer can experience on this contract, since the maximum benefit is paid against the minimum possible premium income. This illustrates a general pattern for level-premium death-benefit insurance: L0 is a strictly decreasing function of Kx — the longer the insured survives, the better the outcome for the insurer.

Example 10.3

Under the same De Moivre(ω=100) mortality assumption and i = 5%, verify that the whole life net premium for (90) used above, P = 0.1614, is consistent with A90 = 0.7722 and ä90 = 4.7844 (values that will be derived from first principles in Chapter 2).

Show Solution

The equivalence-principle premium for whole life insurance is P = Ax/äx. Here P = 0.7722/4.7844 = 0.16140, which rounds to the stated 0.1614. As a cross-check, using the identity Ax = 1 − d·äx with d = 1/21: 1 − (1/21)(4.7844) = 1 − 0.22783 = 0.77217, matching A90 to rounding. Both relationships hold, confirming internal consistency of the figures used throughout this Part.

Example 10.4

A deferred whole life annuity-due on (x) pays 1 per year for life starting at age x+n, funded by level premiums of P payable annually while (x) survives, but only for the first n years. Write the future loss random variable L0, and simplify it for the case Kx = n−1 (the annuitant dies one year before payments would have begun).

Show Solution

The benefit is an n-year deferred whole life annuity-due, whose EPV given survival is captured by n|ä(Kx+1⌐) — formally, the benefit random variable pays 1 at each of times n, n+1, …, Kx, so its present value is vnä(Kx−n+1⌐) if Kx ≥ n, and 0 if Kx < n. The premium random variable is P·ä(min(Kx+1,n)⌐) as before. So: L0 = vnä(Kx−n+1⌐) · 1{Kx≥n} − P·ä(min(Kx+1,n)⌐). If Kx = n−1 (death in the last premium-paying year, one year short of collecting any annuity income), the benefit indicator is 0 — no annuity payment is ever made — and all n premiums were paid, so L0 = −P·ä(n⌐), a pure gain to the insurer equal in present value to the entire premium stream actually collected. This is the annuity analogue of Example 10.2's early-death case for insurance: the insurer's best outcome on a deferred annuity is for the annuitant to die during the accumulation period, before any income has been paid out.

2. Premiums: The Equivalence Principle

Learning Outcomes Covered: Calculate net premiums for whole life, term, and endowment insurance using the equivalence principle; derive and apply the simplified premium identities that follow from Ax = 1 − d·äx; calculate premiums for contracts with a limited premium-paying period shorter than the coverage period.

The equivalence principle is the oldest and simplest premium-setting rule in actuarial practice: choose the premium P so that, on average, the policy breaks even — that is, so that the expected future loss at issue is exactly zero.

Equivalence PrincipleE[L0] = 0 ⟺ EPV(future premiums) = EPV(future benefits)

A premium derived this way is called a net premium (sometimes "benefit premium") because it reflects only mortality and interest, with no allowance for the insurer's expenses or profit margin — those are layered on separately in Chapter 5. Because E[L0] is linear in P for every contract type introduced in Chapter 1 (P always enters through a single term, an EPV-of-premium-annuity multiplied by −P), solving E[L0]=0 for P is always just isolating that one term.

Whole Life Insurance

Setting E[L0] = 0 for the whole life loss variable of Chapter 1 gives E[vKx+1] − P·E[ä(Kx+1⌐)] = 0, i.e., Ax − P·äx = 0. Solving:

Whole Life Net PremiumPx = Ax / äx

Substituting the identity Ax = 1 − d·äx into this quotient produces a second, algebraically equivalent form that avoids computing Ax separately whenever äx is already known:

Whole Life Net Premium — Alternative FormPx = 1/äx − d

Both forms must give an identical numeric answer; using both is a fast, free error check on any exam problem where you have already computed äx.

Term and Endowment Insurance

The same logic gives the net premium for an n-year term insurance (superscript 1 flags "death benefit only, within n years") and an n-year endowment insurance:

n-Year Term & Endowment Net PremiumsP1x:n⌐ = A1x:n⌐ / äx:n⌐ and Px:n⌐ = Ax:n⌐ / äx:n⌐ = 1/äx:n⌐ − d

The endowment identity mirrors the whole life one exactly (Ax:n⌐ = 1 − d·äx:n⌐ holds for endowment insurance just as Ax = 1 − d·äx holds for whole life), which is why the "1/ä − d" shortcut works for the endowment premium but not, in general, for the pure term premium — term insurance has no matching identity because a term policy's benefit is not certain to be paid.

Example 10.5

Using the De Moivre(ω=100) model for (90), i = 5%, derive the net premium for a 5-year term insurance of 1, given A190:5⌐ = 0.4329 and ä90:5⌐ = 3.6811 (both computable directly by summing over the 5 equally likely death years and the temporary annuity-due payments, exactly as in the whole life case but truncated at 5 years).

Show Solution

P190:5⌐ = A190:5⌐ / ä90:5⌐ = 0.4329 / 3.6811 = 0.11761. Note this is much smaller than the whole life premium P90 = 0.1614 found earlier — term insurance is cheaper per unit of coverage because the insurer is on the hook only if death occurs in the (comparatively short) 5-year window, whereas whole life insurance pays a benefit with certainty (under this bounded De Moivre model, everyone dies by age 100).

Example 10.6

Same model as Example 10.5. A 5-year endowment insurance of 1 is issued to (90). Given A90:5⌐ = 0.8247 and ä90:5⌐ = 3.6811 (the same annuity value as the term case, since the premium-paying pattern is identical), find the net premium two ways and confirm they agree.

Show Solution

Direct form: P90:5⌐ = A90:5⌐/ä90:5⌐ = 0.8247/3.6811 = 0.22404. Shortcut form: 1/ä90:5⌐ − d = 1/3.6811 − 1/21 = 0.27166 − 0.04762 = 0.22404. Both forms agree to the precision shown. This premium (0.2240) is nearly double the term premium (0.1176) from Example 10.5, because the endowment contract adds a pure-endowment benefit (a certain payout of 1 at year 5 if the insured survives) on top of the same death benefit — survival to year 5 under this model has probability 0.5, so roughly half of the extra premium is funding that additional certain-ish payout.

Example 10.7 — Limited Premium-Paying Period

A whole life insurance of 1 is issued to (90) under the same De Moivre(ω=100), i=5% model, but premiums are payable for at most 5 years (a "5-pay whole life" contract) rather than for the insured's entire life. Coverage continues for life regardless. Find the net premium, given A90 = 0.7722 and ä90:5⌐ = 3.6811.

Show Solution

The benefit side is unchanged from ordinary whole life (coverage is still lifelong), but the premium annuity is now the 5-year temporary annuity-due, not the lifetime annuity-due, since premiums stop after 5 years even though the insured may survive well beyond that. Equivalence principle: 5P90 · ä90:5⌐ = A90, so 5P90 = 0.7722/3.6811 = 0.20976. This is higher than the ordinary whole life premium of 0.1614 (as it must be — the same total benefit is being funded by a shorter premium stream, so each payment must be larger) but lower than paying the entire 0.7722 face value up front as a single premium, since the insurer still earns interest and receives some mortality release credit over the 5-year funding window.

Example 10.8

A 3-year endowment insurance of 1 is issued to (90) under the same model. Using A190:3⌐ = 0.2723, 3E90 = 0.6047 (the EPV of the pure endowment piece, 0.7×v³), and ä90:3⌐ = 2.5828, find the net premium.

Show Solution

A90:3⌐ = A190:3⌐ + 3E90 = 0.2723 + 0.6047 = 0.8770. Then P90:3⌐ = 0.8770/2.5828 = 0.33955. Consistency check via the shortcut form: 1/2.5828 − 1/21 = 0.38718 − 0.04762 = 0.33956 — matches to rounding. Shortening the endowment period from 5 years (Example 10.6, premium 0.2240) to 3 years (premium 0.3396) raises the premium substantially, since the same near-certain payout must now be funded over a shorter horizon with less time for interest to help.

A very common error is applying the "1/äx − d" shortcut to term insurance. It does not apply there, because the identity Ax = 1 − d·äx is special to contracts that pay a benefit with certainty (whole life and endowment insurance). For pure term insurance you must compute A1x:n⌐ directly (or via Ax:n⌐ − nEx) and divide by äx:n⌐.
When a problem gives a limited premium-paying period of m years on a contract with coverage lasting n > m years (m-pay endowment, m-pay whole life, m-pay n-year term with m < n), the benefit EPV always uses the full coverage period while the premium annuity always uses the shorter of the payment period and survival — i.e., äx:m⌐, not äx:n⌐. Mismatching these two periods is the single most common setup error in this topic.

3. Premiums: Portfolio Percentile & Target Profit

Learning Outcomes Covered: Calculate a premium using the portfolio percentile principle, applying a normal approximation to the aggregate loss of a large block of similar independent policies; calculate a premium that produces a specified (non-zero) expected present value of profit at issue.

The equivalence principle sets E[L0] = 0 for a single policy. Real insurers rarely price this way in practice — a premium that merely breaks even in expectation leaves no margin for error and no profit. This chapter covers two closely related refinements: pricing so that a whole block of similar policies is profitable with a specified probability (the portfolio percentile principle), and pricing a single policy so its expected loss equals a specified negative target rather than zero (the target-profit approach).

The Portfolio Percentile Principle

Suppose an insurer issues n structurally identical, mutually independent whole life policies (same age, same mortality, same interest, same premium P), and let Li denote the future loss random variable of the i-th policy. The company's total future loss is L = L1 + L2 + ⋯ + Ln. Because the policies are independent and identically distributed, the Central Limit Theorem tells us that for large n, L is approximately normal, with mean n·E[L0] and variance n·Var[L0] (the same E[L0] and Var[L0] you would compute for a single policy at the chosen premium P). The portfolio percentile premium principle chooses P so that the probability the aggregate loss is negative or zero — i.e., the block is profitable or break-even — equals a target confidence level π:

Portfolio Percentile ConditionPr(L ≤ 0) = π ⟺ n·E[L0] + zπ·√n·SD[L0] = 0

where zπ is the π-th percentile of the standard normal distribution (e.g., z0.95 = 1.645, z0.90 = 1.282). The equation above comes directly from standardizing L: Pr(L ≤ 0) = Pr[(L − E[L])/SD[L] ≤ −E[L]/SD[L]] = π forces −E[L]/SD[L] = zπ, and E[L] = n·E[L0], SD[L] = √n·SD[L0] by independence. Because both E[L0] and Var[L0] are functions of the unknown premium P (recall from Chapter 1 that L0 = vKx+1(1+P/d) − P/d for whole life), this equation must be solved for P — it is not, in general, simply "the equivalence premium plus a margin," although the two premiums move together and the portfolio percentile premium approaches the equivalence premium as n → ∞ (the CLT variance term shrinks like 1/√n relative to the mean term, so for a very large block, almost no margin above the net premium is needed to reach a given confidence level).

Whole Life Loss Moments (needed for the CLT step)E[L0] = Ax − P·äx Var[L0] = (1 + P/d)²·(²Ax − Ax²)

Here ²Ax denotes the whole life insurance EPV computed with v² wherever the ordinary formula uses v (i.e., evaluated at the doubled force of discount). The quantity ²Ax − Ax² is Var[vKx+1], a fixed property of the mortality and interest assumptions with no dependence on P; only the (1+P/d)² scaling factor carries the premium.

It is worth pausing on why the Central Limit Theorem is the right tool here at all, rather than, say, working out the exact distribution of L directly. Each individual policy's loss Li is a badly-behaved, highly skewed random variable — with high probability it takes a large negative value (the policyholder survives the year and the insurer simply pockets the premium net of a small expected mortality cost), and with small probability it takes a large positive value (an early death produces a big shortfall). Summing one such variable tells you almost nothing about typical portfolio behavior. But summing hundreds or thousands of independent, identically distributed copies of it is exactly the setting the CLT was built for: however skewed a single Li is, the standardized sum (L − E[L])/SD[L] converges in distribution to a standard normal as n grows, so for a large enough block the awkward skewness of the individual policy washes out and a normal approximation to the aggregate becomes reliable. This is precisely the same CLT argument used elsewhere in actuarial statistics to justify normal approximations to aggregate claims — the portfolio percentile premium principle is simply that argument applied to a specific, premium-shaped random variable.

Target Profit

A simpler variant of the same idea sets the expected loss to a specified negative number rather than zero: if the insurer wants an expected present-value profit of exactly π0 per policy at issue (a positive number), it solves E[L0] = −π0:

Target-Profit Premium (Whole Life)Ax − P·äx = −π0 ⟹ P = (Ax + π0) / äx

This is algebraically the easiest of the three premium principles in this Part — it is just the equivalence-principle calculation with a nonzero right-hand side — but it is conceptually distinct: it targets an expected result for one policy in isolation, with no reference to portfolio size or probability of loss, whereas the portfolio percentile principle targets a probability of aggregate loss across many policies and requires the CLT step above.

Example 10.9

Using the whole life De Moivre(ω=100) model for (90) with i = 5% (A90=0.7722, ä90=4.7844), an insurer wants an expected present-value profit of 0.01 per policy (per unit face amount) at issue. Find the required premium and compare it to the equivalence-principle premium of 0.1614.

Show Solution

P = (A90 + 0.01)/ä90 = (0.7722 + 0.01)/4.7844 = 0.7822/4.7844 = 0.16350. The target-profit premium (0.1635) exceeds the equivalence premium (0.1614) by 0.0021; multiplying that difference by ä90 = 4.7844 recovers the target profit: 0.0021 × 4.7844 ≈ 0.0100, confirming the calculation. Note how small a premium adjustment (about 1.3%) was needed to generate a 0.01 expected profit on a unit-benefit policy — a reminder that even thin premium margins compound meaningfully across a large in-force book.

Example 10.10 — Portfolio Percentile

An insurer issues n = 100 independent whole life policies, each on a life like (90) in the De Moivre(ω=100), i=5% model above, with A90 = 0.7722, ä90 = 4.7844, d = 1/21, and Var[vK90+1] = 0.011660 (so SD = 0.10798, computed from ²A90 = 0.60791 less A90² = 0.59625). Find the premium P such that the probability the aggregate loss on the 100 policies is negative or zero is 95% (z0.95 = 1.645).

Show Solution

The condition is n(A90 − Pä90) + z√n(1+P/d)(SD) = 0. Substituting n=100, √n=10, z=1.645, d=1/21 so P/d = 21P: 100(0.7722 − 4.7844P) + 1.645(10)(1+21P)(0.10798) = 0. The constant 1.645 × 10 × 0.10798 = 1.7763; distributing: 77.22 − 478.44P + 1.7763(1+21P) = 0 ⟹ 77.22 − 478.44P + 1.7763 + 37.30P = 0 ⟹ 78.996 − 441.14P = 0 ⟹ P = 78.996/441.14 = 0.17909. The portfolio percentile premium (0.1791) is meaningfully above the equivalence premium (0.1614) — about a 10.9% loading — reflecting the margin needed so that, with 95% confidence, the random mortality experience of only 100 lives does not push the block into an aggregate loss. Had the block size been much larger (say n = 10,000), the same calculation would produce a loading far closer to zero, since SD[L]/|E[L]| shrinks like 1/√n while n·E[L0] grows proportionally to n.

Example 10.11

Explain, without recomputing, why the portfolio percentile premium in Example 10.10 must be smaller if the confidence level is lowered from 95% to 90%, and confirm the direction using z0.90 = 1.282 in place of z0.95 = 1.645.

Show Solution

Lowering the confidence level from 95% to 90% means the insurer is willing to accept a higher chance of an aggregate loss, so less margin above the equivalence premium is required — the required P should decrease toward (but not below) 0.1614. Substituting z=1.282: the constant 1.282×10×0.10798 = 1.3843. 77.22 − 478.44P + 1.3843 + 29.06P = 0 (using 1.3843×21=29.07, close enough) ⟹ 78.604 − 449.38P = 0 ⟹ P ≈ 0.17492. Indeed 0.1749 < 0.1791, confirming the expected direction: a lower confidence requirement produces a lower — though still positive — margin over the net premium.

Example 10.12

For a single policy (not a portfolio), an insurer targets an expected loss of exactly −0.02 (i.e., an expected profit of 0.02) on a 5-year endowment insurance of 1 issued to (90), where A90:5⌐ = 0.8247 and ä90:5⌐ = 3.6811. Find the required premium.

Show Solution

P = (A90:5⌐ + 0.02)/ä90:5⌐ = (0.8247+0.02)/3.6811 = 0.8447/3.6811 = 0.22948. Compare to the pure equivalence-principle premium of 0.2240 from Example 10.6 — a modest increase of about 0.0055 per unit face amount funds the targeted 0.02 expected profit, since that increase is amplified by the 3.6811-year premium annuity: 0.0055 × 3.6811 ≈ 0.0202, matching the target to rounding.

Watch the units of the target profit and the loading. Both the portfolio percentile margin and the target-profit margin are expressed per policy, per unit of face amount, exactly like the premium itself. If a problem gives a face amount other than 1, or a portfolio-level (not per-policy) target profit, convert first — divide an aggregate target profit by n before treating it like π0 in the single-policy formula, and multiply per-unit premiums by the face amount at the very end.
The Central Limit Theorem step in the portfolio percentile principle requires the individual policy losses to be independent and identically distributed. If the block mixes different ages, benefit amounts, or underwriting classes, you cannot simply scale a single-policy Var[L0] by n — each policy's own mean and variance must be summed instead (sums of independent, but not identical, normal approximations still work via the CLT, but the "n × single-policy variance" shortcut does not).

4. Policy Values: Net Premium Reserves

Learning Outcomes Covered: Define the policy value (reserve) at a given duration as the conditional expected future loss given the policy remains in force; calculate prospective net premium reserves for whole life, term, and endowment insurance; derive the retrospective reserve formula from first principles and show it agrees with the prospective formula; interpret why the reserve starts at zero and how it evolves over the life of a whole life or endowment contract.

A policy value, also called a reserve, answers a question the future loss random variable of Chapter 1 does not directly answer on its own: given that a specific policy is still in force at some future duration t, how much should the insurer be holding right now, at that duration, to cover its remaining obligations under the contract? This is exactly the liability the insurer must carry on its books for that policy, and it is what makes reserving distinct from pricing: pricing happens once, at issue; reserving happens every valuation date for the life of every in-force contract.

The Prospective Formula

Formally, the net premium policy value at duration t, written tV (or tVx for a whole life policy on (x)), is the expected value, conditional on survival to duration t, of the future loss measured from time t onward, using the same net premium P that was set at issue:

Prospective Reserve — General DefinitiontV = E[future loss at time t | policy in force at time t] = EPV(future benefits from t) − EPV(future net premiums from t)

For whole life insurance on (x), conditioning on survival to duration t is the same as evaluating the whole life formulas at the attained age x+t, since the future lifetime of a survivor to age x+t behaves exactly like the future lifetime of a newly observed life aged x+t (this is precisely the Markov-like "memorylessness of the age-x+t survival model" idea used throughout this manual):

Prospective Reserve — Whole LifetVx = Ax+t − Px·äx+t

Note carefully that the premium Px in this formula is always the original premium fixed at issue (based on age x), even though the benefit and annuity factors Ax+t and äx+t are evaluated at the current attained age x+t. This is one of the most common sources of arithmetic slips in this topic.

Why the Reserve Starts at Zero and Grows

At issue (t=0), the prospective formula gives 0Vx = Ax − Px·äx, which is exactly zero by the equivalence principle that defined Px in the first place. As duration increases, for a level-premium whole life or endowment contract, the reserve rises steadily: benefits become more imminent (Ax+t rises as the insured ages) while the remaining premium-funding annuity äx+t shrinks, so the net EPV of future benefits net of future premiums grows from 0 up toward the full face amount as the policy approaches its terminal duration. This buildup is the mathematical expression of "prefunding" — level premiums deliberately overcharge in the early years (relative to the pure cost of that year's mortality risk) so the excess accumulates as a reserve that offsets underfunding in the later years, when the same level premium is no longer enough to cover the now much higher mortality cost on its own.

The Retrospective Formula

An equivalent way to compute the same reserve looks backward instead of forward: accumulate, to duration t, the net premiums actually collected, and subtract the accumulated cost of the death benefits actually funded so far, all divided through by the probability of having survived to be in this calculation at all (since the reserve is a per-survivor quantity, and only survivors are being valued).

Retrospective Reserve — Whole LifetVx = [Px·äx:t⌐ − A1x:t⌐] / tEx

Here Px·äx:t⌐ is the EPV, at issue, of the premiums collected during the first t years; A1x:t⌐ is the EPV, at issue, of the death benefits paid during the first t years (a t-year term insurance); and dividing by tEx = vt·tpx (the pure endowment factor) converts an issue-date EPV, which already reflects the probability of surviving to t, into a duration-t conditional value given survival to t. The retrospective formula must equal the prospective formula exactly — both describe the same reserve, just built from opposite ends of the timeline — because the whole-portfolio equivalence-principle identity Ax = A1x:t⌐ + tEx·Ax+t and äx = äx:t⌐ + tEx·äx+t (splitting each lifetime EPV into "what happens in the first t years" plus "what happens after, given survival to t") can be substituted into Px = Ax/äx and rearranged algebraically into either formula.

Example 10.13

Whole life insurance of 1 on (90), De Moivre(ω=100), i=5%, net premium P90=0.1614 (as established earlier). At duration t=2 the insured is alive (attained age 92, with remaining lifetime uniform on {0,…,7}, i.e., m=8 years to the certain limiting age). Given A92 = 0.8079 and ä92 = 4.0341, find 2V90.

Show Solution

2V90 = A92 − P90·ä92 = 0.8079 − 0.1614(4.0341) = 0.8079 − 0.6511 = 0.1568. So after 2 years, the insurer should be holding a reserve of about 0.1568 per unit of face amount for this policy, growing from the 0 it held at issue.

Example 10.14

Continue Example 10.13 to duration t=6 (attained age 96, m=4 years remaining, uniform on {0,1,2,3}). Given A96 = 0.8865 and ä96 = 2.3838, find 6V90, and comment on the trend versus 2V90 from Example 10.13.

Show Solution

6V90 = 0.8865 − 0.1614(2.3838) = 0.8865 − 0.3848 = 0.5017. The reserve has more than tripled from duration 2 (0.1568) to duration 6 (0.5017), consistent with the expected pattern: as the insured ages under this bounded De Moivre model, death becomes both more imminent and more certain, so the net obligation the insurer carries per survivor grows steadily toward the full benefit amount of 1.

Example 10.15 — Prospective vs. Retrospective

For the same policy, at duration t=2, verify the retrospective formula agrees with the prospective answer of 0.1568 found in Example 10.13. Use A190:2⌐ = 0.1795 (EPV of death benefit in the first 2 years: (1/10)(v+v²) = 0.1(0.952381+0.907029) = 0.185941 — recompute carefully below), ä90:2⌐ = 1.9524 (=1+0.9v = 1+0.9×0.952381=1.857143 — recompute below), and 2E90 = v²×2p90 = 0.907029×0.8 = 0.725623.

Show Solution

First, correct the two intermediate figures precisely: A190:2⌐ = (1/10)(v+v²) = 0.1(0.952381+0.907029) = 0.1(1.859410) = 0.185941. ä90:2⌐ = 1 + v·1p90 = 1 + (0.952381)(0.9) = 1 + 0.857143 = 1.857143. 2E90 = v²·2p90 = 0.907029 × 0.8 = 0.725623. Retrospective formula: 2V90 = [P90·ä90:2⌐ − A190:2⌐] / 2E90 = [0.1614(1.857143) − 0.185941] / 0.725623 = [0.299763 − 0.185941] / 0.725623 = 0.113822/0.725623 = 0.15685. This matches the prospective answer of 0.1568 from Example 10.13 to rounding, confirming the two formulas describe the same underlying reserve.

Example 10.16

A 5-year endowment insurance of 1 on (90), same model, net premium P90:5⌐ = 0.2240 (Example 10.6). At duration t=3 (attained age 93, coverage has 2 years left, m=2 remaining to the endowment date), the insured is alive. Given A93:2⌐ = 0.9297 and ä93:2⌐ = 1.4762 (both are the m=2 De Moivre figures used earlier for age 98's whole-life-to-100 calculation, reused here because "2 years left until a certain payout" has the same structure whether the certain payout is death by the limiting age or the endowment date), find 3V90:5⌐.

Show Solution

3V90:5⌐ = A93:2⌐ − P90:5⌐·ä93:2⌐ = 0.9297 − 0.2240(1.4762) = 0.9297 − 0.3307 = 0.5990. With only 2 years left on a 5-year endowment, the reserve (0.5990) is already well over half the face amount, reflecting how close the contract is to its (near-certain, since survival probability to year 5 is 0.6 from duration 3 here) terminal payout.

Net premium reserve schedule, whole life insurance of 1, (90), De Moivre(ω=100), i=5%, P90=0.1614
