This table will be reused and extended in Chapter 6 to demonstrate the recursive relationship between consecutive rows.

The reserve is a conditional expectation — conditional on the policy being in force. Do not confuse tV (the amount held per surviving policyholder at duration t) with tEx·tV or any other quantity discounted or weighted by survival probability back to issue; the reserve itself already "lives" entirely at duration t, from the point of view of someone who knows the policy is still active.
A fast sanity check for any reserve you compute on a whole life or endowment policy: it should lie strictly between 0 and 1 (per unit face amount) for all durations strictly between issue and the terminal duration, start at exactly 0, and — for level net premiums with non-decreasing mortality — increase monotonically toward the face amount as the terminal duration is approached. If your computed reserve is negative, exceeds the face amount, or moves the wrong direction from one duration to the next, re-check your formula before trusting the number.

5. Gross Premium & Modified Net Premium Reserves

Learning Outcomes Covered: Calculate gross premiums and gross premium policy values incorporating realistic expense assumptions; explain the purpose of modified net premium reserving methods such as Full Preliminary Term; calculate modified net premium reserves and compare net, modified net, and gross premium reserves for the same contract.

Net premium reserves, as developed in Chapter 4, are a clean theoretical construct built entirely from mortality and interest. Real insurers, however, incur real expenses — commissions, underwriting costs, policy issue costs, ongoing administration — and these expenses are heavily front-loaded: acquiring a new policyholder (medical underwriting, agent commission, paperwork) typically costs far more in the first policy year than maintaining that same policyholder costs in any later year. This chapter develops two refinements that deal with that front-loading from two different directions: gross premium valuation builds expenses explicitly into both the premium and the reserve, while modified net premium reserving keeps the simplicity of a net-premium calculation but reshapes it to avoid an artifact that front-loaded expenses create.

Gross Premiums and Gross Premium Reserves

A gross premium G is found by the same equivalence-principle logic as a net premium, except that the EPV of expenses is added to the EPV of benefits on the right-hand side of the equivalence equation:

Gross Premium Equivalence EquationG·äx = EPV(benefits) + EPV(expenses)

A typical expense structure charges a percentage of premium (representing commission and premium-tax-like costs, often much higher in year 1 to reflect first-year commission) plus a flat per-policy amount (representing underwriting and administrative costs, again often higher in year 1). Once G is found, a gross premium reserve at duration t is defined exactly as in Chapter 4's prospective formula, but with gross premiums replacing net premiums and with future expenses added to future benefits on the "outflow" side:

Gross Premium Reserve — General FormtVg = EPV(future benefits) + EPV(future expenses) − EPV(future gross premiums), all evaluated at duration t given the policy is in force

Expense assumptions in a gross premium problem are almost always given as some combination of four pieces: a percentage of premium in the first year, a percentage of premium in renewal years, a flat per-policy amount in the first year, and a flat per-policy amount in renewal years — and it is extremely common for all four to differ from one another, with the first-year percentage and the first-year flat amount both set noticeably higher than their renewal counterparts. When translating a word problem into the equivalence equation, it helps to build the EPV of expenses as two separate pieces exactly as in Example 10.17 below: a "certain, since paid at issue" first-year piece, and a renewal piece valued using an annuity-due for years 2, 3, … (which is äx − 1 for a whole life contract, or äx:n⌐ − 1 for an n-year contract, since the first payment of the full annuity-due äx falls at time 0 and must be stripped out to isolate the renewal-year payments alone).

Modified Net Premium Reserves

Even without switching to a full gross premium valuation, front-loaded first-year expenses create a practical problem for a regulator or an insurer's own capital planning: a level net premium reserve, as built in Chapter 4, assumes the insurer collects the same premium every year and needs no allowance at all for the fact that year-1 costs of acquiring the policy are unusually high. If a policy lapses early — which happens disproportionately often in year 1, before the policyholder is fully committed — the insurer has already spent the high first-year acquisition cost but collected only one (undiscounted, unmodified) net premium, creating a period of "surplus strain": the true net worth tied up in the policy in year 1 is lower than the standard net premium reserve formula would suggest, precisely when lapse risk is at its highest.

Modified net premium reserving methods, of which Full Preliminary Term (FPT) is the standard example, solve this without abandoning the net-premium framework. FPT treats the first policy year as if it were pure one-year term insurance — funded by a first-year premium equal to exactly the one-year term cost — and treats the whole life (or endowment) coverage as if it were newly issued, at the attained age x+1, at the start of year 2, funded from that point on by the ordinary net premium for a policy issued at age x+1. This construction guarantees the reserve at the end of year 1 under FPT is exactly zero — matching the (typically small or zero) true economic reserve after acquisition costs, rather than the artificially high level implied by a naive level-net-premium calculation that has not yet accounted for the year-1 expense spike.

Full Preliminary Term — Modified Premiums (Whole Life)Year 1: PFPT1 = v·qx (one-year term cost) Years 2+: PFPT2+ = Px+1 (ordinary whole life net premium at age x+1)
Full Preliminary Term — Reserves0VFPT = 0, 1VFPT = 0, tVFPT for t≥2 equals the ordinary net premium reserve, at duration t−1, of a whole life policy issued at age x+1 with premium Px+1

Because FPT reserves are, by construction, lower than ordinary net premium reserves in the early durations (and identical to them from some later duration onward, once the "one-year-later, one-age-older" whole life reserve schedule catches back up), FPT is best understood as a statutory and financial-reporting relief mechanism — it lets an insurer avoid holding a reserve larger than the surplus strain the policy has actually created, without needing a full gross-premium valuation.

Example 10.17 — Gross Premium

Whole life insurance of 1 on (90), De Moivre(ω=100), i=5%, A90=0.7722, ä90=4.7844. Expenses: 15% of gross premium plus a flat 0.02 in year 1; 5% of gross premium plus a flat 0.005 in each renewal year (years 2, 3, … while the policy is in force). Find the gross premium G.

Show Solution

EPV of expenses = [first-year piece, certain since paid at issue] + [renewal piece, an annuity-due for years 2,3,… i.e. ä90 − 1 = 3.7844]: EPV(expenses) = (0.15G + 0.02) + (0.05G + 0.005)(3.7844) = 0.15G + 0.02 + 0.18922G + 0.01892 = 0.33922G + 0.03892. Equivalence: G·ä90 = A90 + EPV(expenses) ⟹ 4.7844G = 0.7722 + 0.33922G + 0.03892 ⟹ 4.7844G − 0.33922G = 0.81112 ⟹ 4.44518G = 0.81112 ⟹ G = 0.18248. Compare to the net premium P90 = 0.1614: the gross premium is about 13% higher, reflecting the loaded expense structure.

Example 10.18 — Full Preliminary Term

Same policy as Example 10.17. Find the FPT modified first-year and renewal net premiums. Recall q90 = 1/10 = 0.1 (De Moivre, first-year mortality rate at age 90 with 10 years to the limiting age), v = 0.952381, and the whole life net premium at age 91 (one year older, m=9 years remaining) is P91 = A91/ä91 = 0.7898/4.4151 = 0.17888.

Show Solution

First-year FPT premium: PFPT1 = v·q90 = 0.952381 × 0.1 = 0.09524 — substantially below both the net premium (0.1614) and gross premium (0.1825), because it is funding only one year of pure term coverage. Renewal FPT premium: PFPT2+ = P91 = 0.17888, higher than the ordinary net premium of 0.1614, since the renewal premium must now make up, from age 91 onward, for the fact that no reserve was built up during year 1.

Example 10.19 — Comparing Reserve Bases

Continuing Examples 10.17–10.18, compare the three reserve bases at duration t=1 for this policy: the ordinary net premium reserve (Chapter 4 methodology, level premium P90=0.1614), the FPT modified net premium reserve, and (given, without full re-derivation here) a gross premium reserve of approximately 0.0465 at t=1.

Show Solution

Ordinary net premium reserve at t=1 (from the Chapter 4 table): 1V = 0.0772. FPT reserve at t=1: 1VFPT = 0 exactly, by construction — the defining feature of Full Preliminary Term. Gross premium reserve at t=1: 1Vg ≈ 0.0465 (this nets the higher gross premium collected against both future benefits and the ongoing renewal expense loading; it happens to fall between the FPT reserve and the ordinary net premium reserve in this example, though that ordering is not a general law — it depends on the specific expense assumptions). The ranking 1VFPT = 0 < 1Vg ≈ 0.0465 < 1V = 0.0772 illustrates exactly the reserve relief FPT is designed to provide relative to an unmodified net premium reserve, which — holding no allowance at all for front-loaded acquisition expense — overstates the true year-1 liability most severely of the three bases.

Example 10.20

Using the figures from Example 10.18, find the FPT reserve at duration t=2, given that this equals the ordinary net-premium whole-life reserve at duration 1 for a policy issued at age 91 with premium P91=0.17888, and that (evaluated at age 92, m=8 years remaining) A92=0.8079 and ä92=4.0341.

Show Solution

2VFPT = A92 − P91·ä92 = 0.8079 − 0.17888(4.0341) = 0.8079 − 0.72161 = 0.08629. Compare to the ordinary net premium reserve at the same duration, 2V = 0.1568 (from the Chapter 4 table): the FPT reserve (0.0863) remains noticeably lower even at duration 2, though the gap between the two bases narrows with each additional duration as the "one-year lag" built into FPT becomes proportionally less significant.

Reserve comparison at selected durations, whole life insurance of 1, (90), same assumptions as Examples 10.17–10.20
Duration tNet Premium ReserveFPT Reserve
00.00000.0000
10.07720.0000
20.15680.0863
Full Preliminary Term modifies the premium and reserve calculation for the first policy year only. It is a common mistake to assume the "term insurance" treatment continues into year 2 or beyond — from duration 1 onward, FPT is nothing more than an ordinary net premium calculation, just re-based to attained age x+1 with a one-year lag. Do not apply v·qx+1 in year 2; year 2 already uses the full whole-life-at-(x+1) renewal premium Px+1.
A gross premium reserve can, in principle, come out negative in the first policy year if first-year expenses are large enough relative to the gross premium collected. A net premium reserve, by contrast, can never be negative for whole life or endowment insurance under the equivalence principle, since it always equals a genuine EPV of positive future net obligations. If you compute a negative net premium reserve, look for a setup error before concluding the reserve is really negative.

6. Recursive Relationships for Policy Values

Learning Outcomes Covered: Derive the year-by-year recursive reserve formula from the prospective definition; use the recursion to project a reserve forward one policy year at a time; build and interpret a multi-year recursive reserve schedule.

The reserve table built in Chapter 4 was computed by evaluating the prospective formula independently at each duration. In practice — and in many exam problems — it is far more useful to relate consecutive reserves to each other directly, without recomputing an EPV from scratch at every step. This is the role of the recursive reserve formula.

Deriving the Recursion

Start from the prospective definition of the reserve at duration t for a whole life policy on (x), and split the EPV of future benefits and future premiums into "what happens in the coming year" plus "what happens after, given survival through the coming year" — precisely the same splitting idea used to derive the retrospective formula in Chapter 4, but applied one year at a time instead of over the whole elapsed history:

Splitting StepAx+t = v·qx+t + v·px+t·Ax+t+1 äx+t = 1 + v·px+t·äx+t+1

Substituting both splits into tV = Ax+t − P·äx+t:

DerivationtV = v·qx+t + v·px+t·Ax+t+1 − P[1 + v·px+t·äx+t+1] = v·qx+t − P + v·px+t[Ax+t+1 − P·äx+t+1]

The bracketed term Ax+t+1 − P·äx+t+1 is exactly t+1V by definition. Rearranging (tV + P − v·qx+t = v·px+t·t+1V, then multiplying through by (1+i) = 1/v) gives the standard recursive reserve formula:

Recursive Reserve Formula (Whole Life, Benefit = 1)(tV + P)(1+i) = qx+t·1 + px+t·t+1V

This formula has a clean cash-flow interpretation that is worth internalizing rather than memorizing mechanically: tV + P is the fund the insurer holds per policy in force at the start of year t+1, right after collecting that year's premium. Accumulated with one year of interest, (tV+P)(1+i), that fund must be exactly enough, on average across the cohort of policies in force at the start of the year, to pay the death benefit of 1 to the fraction qx+t who die during the year and to leave behind a reserve of t+1V for each of the fraction px+t who survive to the next duration. Because those who die "release" their reserve entirely (it becomes part of the general fund used to pay claims) while only survivors carry a reserve forward, the recursion is sometimes read as: the fund built each year is shared between "paying off" the year's death claims and "financing" next year's reserve for survivors.

The same style of recursion applies, with the obvious modifications, to term insurance (drop the final t+1V term once t+1 = n, the term's expiry) and to endowment insurance (replace nV by the endowment benefit of 1 at the maturity duration, rather than letting it emerge from continued survival).

Example 10.21

Using the whole life reserve schedule for (90) built in Chapter 4 (P90=0.1614, i=5%, De Moivre ω=100), verify the recursion between duration 0 and duration 1, given 0V=0, 1V=0.0772, q90=0.1, p90=0.9.

Show Solution

Left side: (0V + P)(1+i) = (0 + 0.1614)(1.05) = 0.16947. Right side: q90(1) + p90(1V) = 0.1(1) + 0.9(0.0772) = 0.1 + 0.06948 = 0.16948. The two sides agree to rounding, confirming the recursion holds between these two consecutive durations.

Example 10.22

Verify the recursion between duration 8 and duration 9 for the same policy, given 8V=0.6915, 9V=0.7910, and — since attained age 98 has only m=2 years left to the certain limiting age 100 — q98=1/2=0.5, p98=0.5.

Show Solution

Left side: (0.6915+0.1614)(1.05) = (0.8529)(1.05) = 0.89555. Right side: 0.5(1) + 0.5(0.7910) = 0.5+0.3955 = 0.8955. The two sides agree (0.89555 vs. 0.8955, matching to rounding), and note how much higher both q98 and the reserve contribution are compared to the year-1 recursion in Example 10.21 — consistent with mortality accelerating sharply as attained age approaches the De Moivre limiting age.

Example 10.23 — Forward Projection

Suppose you are told only 5V90 = 0.4114 for this policy, along with q95=1/5=0.2, p95=0.8 (attained age 95 has m=5 years left), P90=0.1614, and i=5%. Use the recursion to project 6V90 forward, and compare to the value 0.5018 found independently in the Chapter 4 table.

Show Solution

Rearrange the recursion to solve for t+1V: 6V = [(5V+P)(1+i) − q95]/p95 = [(0.4114+0.1614)(1.05) − 0.2]/0.8 = [(0.5728)(1.05) − 0.2]/0.8 = [0.60144 − 0.2]/0.8 = 0.40144/0.8 = 0.50180. This matches the independently-computed prospective value of 0.5018 from Chapter 4, exactly as it should — the recursion and the prospective formula must always agree, since the recursion was derived directly from the prospective definition.

Example 10.24 — Multi-Year Recursive Schedule

Starting only from 0V90=0 and P90=0.1614, and the sequence of one-year mortality rates for De Moivre(ω=100) starting at age 90 (q90+t = 1/(10−t) for t=0,1,2,3), build the reserve at durations 1 through 4 purely by recursion (no independent prospective recomputation), and confirm the values match the Chapter 4 table.

Show Solution

t=0→1: q90=1/10=0.1, p90=0.9. 1V = [(0+0.1614)(1.05) − 0.1]/0.9 = [0.16947−0.1]/0.9 = 0.06947/0.9 = 0.07719 ≈ 0.0772. t=1→2: q91=1/9=0.1111, p91=0.8889. 2V = [(0.0772+0.1614)(1.05) − 0.1111]/0.8889 = [(0.2386)(1.05)−0.1111]/0.8889 = [0.25053−0.1111]/0.8889 = 0.13943/0.8889 = 0.15685 ≈ 0.1568. t=2→3: q92=1/8=0.125, p92=0.875. 3V = [(0.1568+0.1614)(1.05)−0.125]/0.875 = [(0.3182)(1.05)−0.125]/0.875 = [0.33411−0.125]/0.875 = 0.20911/0.875 = 0.23898 ≈ 0.2390. t=3→4: q93=1/7=0.142857, p93=0.857143. 4V = [(0.2390+0.1614)(1.05)−0.142857]/0.857143 = [(0.4004)(1.05)−0.142857]/0.857143 = [0.4204−0.142857]/0.857143 = 0.277543/0.857143 = 0.32380 ≈ 0.3238. Every recursively projected value (0.0772, 0.1568, 0.2390, 0.3238) matches the Chapter 4 table exactly, demonstrating that the entire reserve schedule for a policy can be generated from nothing more than the issue-date premium and a table of one-year mortality rates, without ever computing an Ax+t or äx+t factor directly.

Recursive reserve build, whole life insurance of 1, (90), De Moivre(ω=100), i=5%, P90=0.1614
tq90+tp90+t(tV+P)(1+i)t+1V
00.10000.90000.169470.0772
10.11110.88890.250530.1568
20.12500.87500.334110.2390
30.14290.85710.420350.3238
The recursion is a powerful checking tool on the exam: if a problem gives you a reserve at one duration and enough mortality/interest information to reach a neighboring duration, you very often do not need to compute Ax+t or äx+t at all — just apply (tV+P)(1+i) = q·(benefit) + p·t+1V directly and solve for whichever single unknown remains.
Do not forget the interest accumulation factor (1+i) on the left side of the recursion. A very common slip is writing tV + P = qx+t + px+t·t+1V, omitting the (1+i), which silently drops a full year of interest earnings from the fund and produces a reserve projection that is systematically too high.

7. Modeling Extra Risk

Learning Outcomes Covered: Apply age rating, a constant addition to the force of mortality, and a constant multiple of the mortality rate as methods for pricing substandard (extra) mortality risk; compare the resulting insurance and annuity EPVs and premiums produced by each method starting from the same base mortality assumption.

Underwriting sometimes identifies an applicant whose mortality is expected to be worse than standard — due to a health condition, occupation, or lifestyle factor — but who is not so far outside the standard risk pool that the insurer needs a fully separate substandard mortality table. Three simplified adjustment techniques are in common use for pricing such "rated" lives, each modifying the standard mortality assumption in a different way. All three start from the same base survival model and produce a new, worse mortality basis on which ordinary insurance and annuity formulas are then applied unchanged.

Age Rating

Age rating is the simplest technique: rather than build any new mortality assumption, the insurer simply prices (and reserves) the substandard life as though they were several years older than their true age. A life aged x rated up by k years is priced using the standard mortality and annuity/insurance functions evaluated at age x+k rather than age x.

Age RatingSubstandard life aged x, rated up k years ⟹ use Ax+k, äx+k (standard table, evaluated at the rated age) in place of Ax, äx

Age rating is attractive for its simplicity — no new formulas, just a shifted lookup — but it implicitly assumes the entire future shape of the substandard life's mortality matches the standard table's shape at a later age, not just the current year's mortality level. That assumption can be a poor fit for some medical conditions.

Constant Addition to the Force of Mortality

Under a constant addition to the force of mortality, the substandard life's force of mortality at every future age is the standard force plus a fixed constant c > 0:

Constant Addition to Force of Mortalityμ*(x+t) = μ(x+t) + c ⟹ tp*x = tpx · e−ct

The survival-probability relationship follows because tpx = exp(−∫0tμ(x+s)ds); adding a constant c to the force under the integral simply multiplies the survival probability by e−ct, a clean, closed-form adjustment. This technique is mathematically the most tractable of the three (it corresponds to multiplying every tpx by a fixed geometric decay factor) but is used less often in practice than the mortality-rate multiple below, because a flat addition to the force does not scale proportionally with the level of standard mortality — the same c represents a much larger relative loading on a young, healthy life than on an elderly one.

Constant Multiple of the Mortality Rate

The most commonly used technique in practice for rating substandard business is a constant multiple of the mortality rate: every annual mortality rate qx+t from the standard table is scaled up by a fixed factor k > 1 (a "mortality rating," often quoted as a percentage such as "150% of standard").

Constant Multiple of Mortality Rateq*x+t = k · qx+t for every future duration t, with p*x+t = 1 − k·qx+t

This method scales proportionally with the underlying mortality level at every age, which is usually a better fit to how medical underwriters actually think about extra risk (as a proportional loading on the life's baseline hazard), and it is straightforward to apply year by year to a full mortality table, recomputing tpx for the substandard life duration by duration as the product of the individually-scaled one-year survival probabilities. Unlike the constant-force addition, the multiple does not have as clean a closed form for multi-year survival probabilities in general — tp*x is the product of t individually adjusted one-year factors, not a simple transformation of tpx itself — except in special cases such as the flat-mortality-rate model used in the examples below.

It is worth noting that all three techniques adjust only the mortality assumption used to price and reserve a policy — they say nothing about the premium itself beyond what flows through the standard equivalence-principle (or portfolio-percentile, or target-profit) machinery once the substandard survival model is plugged in. In practice, underwriters typically select among the three methods based on which best matches the clinical or actuarial evidence for a given impairment: a chronic condition whose relative mortality impact is believed to be roughly constant in percentage terms across ages (many metabolic conditions behave this way) is usually rated with a constant multiple of qx; a condition believed to add a roughly constant extra hazard regardless of age (some occupational or lifestyle risks) may instead be modeled with a constant addition to the force of mortality; and cases where reliable substandard experience data simply is not available, but a rough approximate severity is known, are often handled with a quick age rating instead, precisely because it requires no new formulas at all — just a shift in which row of the standard table to read from.

All three techniques modify only the mortality assumption. Once the adjusted survival model is in hand — whether via a shifted age, a rescaled force, or a rescaled mortality rate — every premium, reserve, and EPV formula developed earlier in this Part applies completely unchanged; extra-risk pricing is a preprocessing step on the mortality basis, not a new family of formulas.
Example 10.25 — Age Rating

A life aged 90 is substandard and is rated up by 4 years for pricing purposes. Using the De Moivre(ω=100), i=5% model, the standard whole life net premium at age 90 is P90=0.1614. At the rated age 94 (m=6 years to the limiting age), A94=0.8459 and ä94=3.2351. Find the age-rated premium and compare it to standard.

Show Solution

Age rating simply substitutes age 94's factors for age 90's: P*90 (rated) = A94/ä94 = 0.8459/3.2351 = 0.26149. Compared to the standard premium of 0.1614, this is a substantial increase (about 62% higher) — reflecting that, under this particular De Moivre model, a 4-year age rating pushes the insured 4 years closer to the certain limiting age of 100, a large relative change in remaining lifetime for someone already at age 90.

Example 10.26 — Constant Multiple of q

A different, flatter base mortality model is used for this example: a life age x is subject to a constant annual mortality rate q = 0.02 at every future duration (i.e., qx+t=0.02 for all t ≥ 0), with i=5%. Under this base assumption, the whole life net premium simplifies to the clean closed form P = Ax/äx = q·v (a special feature of this flat-mortality model, verified by substituting Ax=qv/(1−vp) and äx=1/(1−vp) into the ratio and cancelling the common denominator), giving P = 0.02 × 0.952381 = 0.019048. A substandard applicant is rated at k=1.5 times standard mortality (q*=0.03 at every duration). Find the substandard premium.

Show Solution

Because the base model has a flat mortality rate at every duration, the rated life also has a flat (higher) mortality rate at every duration, q*=1.5(0.02)=0.03, and the same shortcut applies: P* = q*·v = 0.03 × 0.952381 = 0.028571. Notice the ratio of the two premiums, 0.028571/0.019048 = 1.500, exactly matches the mortality multiple k=1.5 — a direct consequence of the P=qv shortcut available in this flat-mortality special case; in general (non-flat) mortality tables, a k-fold increase in every qx+t does not translate into an exactly k-fold increase in the premium, because the mix of near-term versus far-term mortality, and the annuity denominator, both respond to the rating in more complex, age-dependent ways.

Example 10.27 — Constant Addition to the Force of Mortality

Using the same flat base model as Example 10.26 (q=0.02 at every duration, i=5%), first convert to the equivalent constant force: μ = −ln(1−q) = −ln(0.98) = 0.020203. A constant addition of c=0.01 is applied to the force at every future duration. Find the resulting one-year mortality rate q* and the substandard premium, and compare to the constant-multiple result of Example 10.26.

Show Solution

New force: μ* = 0.020203+0.01 = 0.030203. New one-year mortality rate: q* = 1 − e−μ* = 1 − e−0.030203 = 1 − 0.970249 = 0.029751. Since the resulting mortality rate is again flat across durations (a constant force produces a constant q at every duration, since 1−e−μ* does not depend on t), the same shortcut premium formula applies: P* = q*·v = 0.029751 × 0.952381 = 0.028335. This is very close to, but not identical to, the constant-multiple premium of 0.028571 found in Example 10.26 (a difference of about 0.8%) — even though both adjustments were calibrated to produce roughly a "50% worse" mortality assumption at this particular q level, the two techniques are structurally different, and would diverge further at mortality levels far from q=0.02.

Example 10.28

Summarize Examples 10.26–10.27 in a comparison table, and explain in one sentence each why the constant multiple and constant addition methods do not produce identical premiums even when calibrated to a similar overall severity.

Show Solution

Base premium (q=0.02): 0.019048. Constant multiple, k=1.5 (q*=0.03): 0.028571. Constant addition, c=0.01 to force (q*=0.029751): 0.028335. The constant multiple scales the mortality rate directly and proportionally, so its effect is exactly linear in k by design; the constant addition instead scales the force of mortality, and the relationship between a linear shift in force and the resulting shift in the one-year mortality rate q=1−e−μ is nonlinear (concave), so a force addition calibrated to look similar to a 50% rate multiple at one mortality level will not track the rate multiple exactly, and the two methods will generally diverge further apart at very low or very high base mortality levels.

Age rating changes the entire shape of future mortality (it borrows the standard table's rate of increase at older ages), while the constant-addition and constant-multiple methods preserve the original future shape of mortality and only rescale its current level. These are genuinely different modeling assumptions, not three equivalent ways of writing the same adjustment — do not assume they will produce similar answers except by coincidence (as in the flat-mortality special case of Examples 10.26–10.28) or by deliberate calibration.
When a problem states an extra-risk assumption as "rate as if 5 years older," that is unambiguously age rating. When it gives "150% of standard mortality" or "mortality rate multiplied by 1.5," that is the constant-multiple method. When it says "force of mortality increased by 0.01" or similar force-based language, that is the constant-addition method. Reading the problem's exact phrasing is the reliable way to identify which of the three techniques is intended — the numerical answers under each are generally different.

Part 10 Practice Problems

1. Define, in your own words, the future loss random variable L0 for a general life insurance contract, and state what determines its sign convention.
Show Answer
L0 is the present value, as of the policy's issue date, of all future benefit outflows minus all future premium inflows, viewed from the insurer's perspective. Under the standard convention used throughout this Part, a positive L0 represents a loss to the insurer (benefits exceed premiums in present-value terms) and a negative L0 represents a gain. The sign convention is a choice — "benefits minus premiums" — and must be applied consistently throughout a problem.
2. For a whole life insurance of 1 on (x) with level annual net premium P, write L0 as a function of Kx in the form c1·vKx+1 + c2, and identify c1 and c2 in terms of P and d.
Show Answer
L0 = (1 + P/d)vKx+1 − P/d, so c1 = 1 + P/d and c2 = −P/d. This form is obtained by substituting ä(Kx+1⌐) = (1−vKx+1)/d into the original definition L0 = vKx+1 − P·ä(Kx+1⌐) and collecting terms in vKx+1.
3. For a 10-year deferred whole life annuity-due on (x), funded by level premiums for the first 10 years while (x) survives and paying 1 per year for life starting at age x+10, write the future loss random variable for the case Kx = 12 (death in the 13th policy year, i.e., after 3 annuity payments have been made at ages x+10, x+11, x+12).
Show Answer
All 10 premiums were paid (Kx=12 ≥ 10), so the premium term is P·ä(10⌐). The annuity benefit paid is v10·ä(3⌐) — three payments of 1, at times 10, 11, and 12, discounted back to time 0 via v10. So L0 = v10ä(3⌐) − P·ä(10⌐).
4. Under a De Moivre(ω=100) model for (85) (so m=15 years to the limiting age) with i=5%, and given ä85 = 6.5000 and A85 = 0.6905, find the whole life net premium P85 two ways and confirm they agree.
Show Answer
Direct: P85 = A85/ä85 = 0.6905/6.5000 = 0.10623. Shortcut: 1/ä85 − d = 1/6.5000 − 1/21 = 0.15385 − 0.04762 = 0.10623. Both agree.
5. A 10-year term insurance of 1 is issued to (85) under the model of Problem 4, with A185:10⌐ = 0.5514 and ä85:10⌐ = 4.3295 (this ä value equals S5 from the standard De Moivre table used throughout this Part, since the annuity-due sum for 10 years from a life with 15 years to certain death has the same structure as the earlier m=10 whole-life-to-100 annuity; use the given figure directly). Find the net premium.
Show Answer
P185:10⌐ = 0.5514/4.3295 = 0.12735.
6. A 10-year endowment insurance of 1 is issued to (85) under the same model, with 10E85 = 0.3739 (=10p85·v10 = (5/15)(0.613913)). Using A185:10⌐=0.5514 from Problem 5, find A85:10⌐ and the endowment net premium.
Show Answer
A85:10⌐ = A185:10⌐ + 10E85 = 0.5514+0.3739 = 0.9253. P85:10⌐ = 0.9253/4.3295 = 0.21372. (Cross-check via shortcut: 1/4.3295 − 1/21 = 0.23098−0.04762=0.18336 — this does not match, which is expected, since 0.9253 was rounded from summed components; using the more precise identity form 1 − d·ä = 1−0.047619(4.3295)=1−0.20617=0.79383 reveals the given A85:10⌐ figure is illustrative rather than exactly internally consistent to 4 decimals — for exam purposes always trust whichever computation path the problem's given data supports directly, here the direct A1+10E route.)
7. A whole life policy on (90) (De Moivre ω=100, i=5%) has premiums payable for at most 3 years. Given A90=0.7722 and ä90:3⌐=2.5828, find the limited-pay net premium.
Show Answer
3P90 = A90/ä90:3⌐ = 0.7722/2.5828 = 0.29894.
8. Explain why the shortcut formula P = 1/äx − d cannot be used for a pure n-year term insurance premium, but can be used for both whole life and n-year endowment insurance.
Show Answer
The shortcut relies on the identity A = 1 − d·ä, which holds only when the insurance benefit is certain to be paid eventually (whole life: certain to die; endowment: certain to either die within n years or survive to n). Term insurance pays no benefit at all if the insured survives past duration n, so A1x:n⌐ ≠ 1 − d·äx:n⌐ in general, and the shortcut fails.
9. For the whole life policy on (90) with A90=0.7722, ä90=4.7844, Var[vK90+1]=0.011660, find Var[L0] at the equivalence-principle premium P90=0.1614 (d=1/21).
Show Answer
Var[L0] = (1+P/d)²·Var[vK+1] = (1+0.1614×21)² × 0.011660 = (1+3.3894)² × 0.011660 = (4.3894)² × 0.011660 = 19.267 × 0.011660 = 0.22467. So SD[L0] ≈ 0.4740.
10. Using the results of Problem 9, an insurer issues n=400 independent identical copies of this policy and wants the probability of an aggregate loss to be at most 5% (z0.95=1.645). Set up (but do not necessarily fully simplify) the equation for the portfolio percentile premium P*, and state qualitatively how the required loading over the net premium compares to the n=100 case from Example 10.10.
Show Answer
400(0.7722 − 4.7844P) + 1.645√400(1+21P)(0.10798) = 0, i.e., 400(0.7722−4.7844P) + 1.645(20)(1+21P)(0.10798)=0. Because n has quadrupled (100→400), √n has only doubled (10→20), so the interest-and-mortality-margin (variance) term grows more slowly than the mean term; the resulting loading over the net premium P=0.1614 will be smaller than in the n=100 case (roughly half the excess margin, since the margin scales approximately like 1/√n for large n), so P* will be closer to 0.1614 than the n=100 answer of 0.1791.
11. An insurer wants an expected present-value profit of exactly 0.03 per policy on the 5-year endowment insurance of Example 10.6 (A90:5⌐=0.8247, ä90:5⌐=3.6811). Find the required premium.
Show Answer
P = (0.8247+0.03)/3.6811 = 0.8547/3.6811 = 0.23217.
12. True or False, with a one-sentence justification: increasing the target expected profit π0 in the target-profit premium formula always increases the required premium by exactly π0/äx.
Show Answer
True. From P = (Ax+π0)/äx, the derivative of P with respect to π0 is 1/äx, a constant, so the premium increase for a given increase in target profit is always exactly (Δπ0)/äx, regardless of the starting premium level.
13. Whole life insurance on (90), same model as throughout, A92=0.8079, ä92=4.0341, P90=0.1614. Find 2V90 using the prospective formula.
Show Answer
2V90 = 0.8079 − 0.1614(4.0341) = 0.8079−0.6511 = 0.1568.
14. For the same policy, given P90ä90:2⌐=0.29976, A190:2⌐=0.18594, and 2E90=0.72562, find 2V90 using the retrospective formula and confirm it matches Problem 13.
Show Answer
2V90 = (0.29976−0.18594)/0.72562 = 0.11382/0.72562 = 0.15685 ≈ 0.1568, matching Problem 13.
15. Explain in one or two sentences why 0V is always exactly 0 for a policy priced by the equivalence principle, and why this is not generally true for a policy priced by the portfolio percentile or target-profit principle.
Show Answer
0V = Ax − Päx = E[L0], and the equivalence principle is defined precisely by setting E[L0]=0, so 0V=0 automatically. Under the portfolio percentile or target-profit principles, the premium is deliberately set so E[L0] is negative (a built-in expected profit margin), so 0V = E[L0] < 0 under those pricing bases if the reserve were (unusually) still defined using the same net-premium-reserve formula with the loaded premium.
16. A 5-year endowment insurance of 1 on (90), P=0.2240, at duration t=4 (m=1 year remaining to maturity) has A94:1⌐=v=0.952381 and ä94:1⌐=1. Find 4V90:5⌐.
Show Answer
4V = 0.952381 − 0.2240(1) = 0.72838.
17. Gross premium calculation: whole life on (90), A90=0.7722, ä90=4.7844. Expenses are 20% of gross premium in year 1 only (no flat expense, no renewal expense). Find the gross premium G.
Show Answer
G·ä90 = A90 + 0.20G ⟹ 4.7844G − 0.20G = 0.7722 ⟹ 4.5844G=0.7722 ⟹ G=0.16843.
18. Explain the core motivation for Full Preliminary Term reserving in one or two sentences, referencing the concept of surplus strain.
Show Answer
Because acquisition expenses (commission, underwriting) are heavily concentrated in policy year 1, a level net premium reserve calculation — which ignores expenses entirely — overstates the reserve an insurer needs to hold at the end of year 1, at exactly the duration where lapse risk is highest; this creates unnecessary "surplus strain." FPT relieves this by funding year 1 as pure term insurance (reserve = 0 at duration 1) and shifting the full whole-life funding burden to a higher, level renewal premium starting in year 2.
19. Using q90=0.1, v=0.952381, and P91=0.17888 (whole life net premium at age 91), find the FPT modified premiums for years 1 and 2+ on a whole life policy issued to (90).
Show Answer
Year 1: PFPT1 = vq90 = 0.952381×0.1 = 0.09524. Years 2+: PFPT2+ = P91 = 0.17888.
20. State whether each of the following is closer in spirit to a net premium reserve, a gross premium reserve, or a modified net premium reserve, and justify briefly: (a) a reserve computed with no reference to expenses at all; (b) a reserve computed by treating year 1 as term insurance and years 2+ as whole life issued one year later.
Show Answer
(a) Net premium reserve — by definition it uses only mortality and interest, no expense assumption. (b) Modified net premium reserve (specifically Full Preliminary Term) — it is still built entirely from net-premium-style mortality/interest formulas, but restructures the split between year 1 and later years to avoid overstating the year-1 reserve.
21. Derive the recursive reserve formula's cash-flow interpretation in your own words: what does the left-hand side, (tV+P)(1+i), represent, and what do the two terms on the right-hand side represent?
Show Answer
The left-hand side is the fund per policy in force at the start of the year (reserve carried in, plus the premium just collected), accumulated with one year of interest. The right-hand side splits that accumulated fund across the cohort's outcomes for the year: qx+t·(benefit) is the portion needed, on average, to pay the death benefit to those who die during the year, and px+t·t+1V is the portion needed, on average, to leave the required reserve in place for those who survive to the next duration.
22. Given 3V90=0.2390, P90=0.1614, i=5%, q93=1/7=0.142857, p93=0.857143, use the recursion to project 4V90, and compare to the value 0.3238 found independently in Chapter 4.
Show Answer
4V = [(0.2390+0.1614)(1.05) − 0.142857]/0.857143 = [(0.4004)(1.05)−0.142857]/0.857143 = [0.4204−0.142857]/0.857143 = 0.277543/0.857143 = 0.32380 ≈ 0.3238, matching the Chapter 4 value.
23. A life aged 70 is rated up by 3 years for underwriting purposes. Which of the three extra-risk methods in Chapter 7 is being applied, and what standard-table quantities would you use to price this life's whole life insurance?
Show Answer
This is age rating. Price using the standard whole life insurance and annuity factors evaluated at age 73 (A73 and ä73) rather than age 70.
24. Under a flat base mortality assumption of q=0.03 at every duration, i=6% (v=1/1.06=0.943396), find the standard whole life net premium P=qv, then find the premium under a constant multiple k=1.25 (q*=0.0375).
Show Answer
Standard: P = qv = 0.03×0.943396 = 0.028302. Rated (k=1.25): P* = q*v = 0.0375×0.943396 = 0.035387. Ratio P*/P = 1.250, matching k exactly, as expected in this flat-mortality special case.
25. Under the same base as Problem 24 (q=0.03, i=6%), convert to an equivalent constant force μ=−ln(0.97), then apply a constant addition c=0.015 to the force, and find the resulting q* and premium P*=q*v. Compare to Problem 24's constant-multiple answer.
Show Answer
μ = −ln(0.97) = 0.030459. μ* = 0.030459+0.015 = 0.045459. q* = 1−e−0.045459 = 1−0.955560 = 0.044440. P* = 0.044440×0.943396 = 0.041924. This is noticeably higher than the constant-multiple premium of 0.035387 from Problem 24 — the two extra-risk methods, calibrated independently here (not to match each other), diverge more at this higher base mortality level than they did at the lower q=0.02 level used in the Chapter 7 examples, illustrating that the two techniques are genuinely different models, not interchangeable relabelings of the same adjustment.
26. Summarize, in one or two sentences each, the practical purpose of (a) the portfolio percentile premium principle and (b) Full Preliminary Term reserving, and explain why both techniques become less consequential as portfolio size or policy duration, respectively, grows large.
Show Answer
(a) The portfolio percentile principle sets a premium margin so that a block of similar policies is profitable with a specified confidence level, using a normal approximation to the aggregate loss; as the block size n grows, the relative variability of the aggregate loss shrinks (SD scales like √n while the mean scales like n), so the required margin over the pure net premium shrinks toward zero. (b) Full Preliminary Term restructures reserving to avoid overstating the reserve in the high-lapse-risk first policy year by treating that year as term insurance; because the FPT and ordinary net premium reserve schedules are built from the same underlying mortality/interest assumptions and differ only by an initial one-year lag, the gap between the two narrows and eventually becomes negligible as duration increases.

Appendix A — Formula Reference

A single cram sheet spanning all ten parts of this manual. Every formula here is derived and explained in full earlier in this manual — use this appendix for quick lookup and last-pass memorization.

Binomial(m,q)−q/(1−q)(m+1)q/(1−q)
Negative Binomial(r,β)β/(1+β)(r−1)β/(1+β)
Collective Risk ModelS = X1+⋯+XN,   E[S]=E[N]E[X],   Var(S)=E[N]Var(X)+Var(N)E[X]²
Individual Risk ModelS = Y1+⋯+Yn,   Yi independent (not identical)
Normal ApproximationP(S≤s) ≈ Φ[(s−E[S])/√Var(S)]
Expected Stop-Loss PaymentE[(S−d)+] = E[S] − E[S∧d]
Value at RiskVaRp(S) = inf{s : FS(s) ≥ p}
Tail Value at RiskTVaRp(S) = VaRp(S) + E[(S−VaRp(S))+] / (1−p)

A.3 Parametric Estimation (Part III)

Likelihood — Complete DataL(θ) = Π f(xi; θ)
Likelihood — Grouped DataL(θ) = Π [F(cj+1;θ) − F(cj;θ)]nj
Likelihood — Left Truncation at dContribution = f(x;θ) / S(d;θ),   x > d
Likelihood — Right Censoring at uContribution = S(u;θ)

A.4 Credibility (Part IV)

Credibility PremiumP = Z·(observed) + (1−Z)·(prior/manual)
Standard for Full Credibility (Claim Counts)λ0 = (z(1+p)/2 / k)²
Partial CredibilityZ = √(n/n0),   capped at 1

A.5 Pricing & Reserving (Part V)

Expected Loss Ratio ReserveUltimate Losses = ELR × Earned Premium
Chain-Ladder Age-to-Age Factorfk = Σ (losses at age k+1) / Σ (losses at age k),   volume-weighted
Bornhuetter-FergusonUltimate = Actual-to-date + (1 − 1/CDF)×(Expected Losses)
Permissible Loss RatioPLR = 1 − Variable Expense Ratio − Target Profit %
Loss Ratio MethodIndicated Rate Change = (Experience Loss Ratio / PLR) − 1
Pure Premium MethodIndicated Rate = Projected Pure Premium / PLR

A.6 Option Pricing (Part VI)

Call / Put PayoffCall: max(ST−K,0)    Put: max(K−ST,0)
Binomial Risk-Neutral Probabilityp* = (erh − d) / (u − d)
Binomial Option PriceV0 = e−rh[p*·Vu + (1−p*)·Vd]
Black-Scholes Call PriceC = S0N(d1) − Ke−rTN(d2)
d1, d2d1 = [ln(S0/K) + (r+σ²/2)T] / (σ√T),   d2 = d1 − σ√T
Delta (Call)Δcall = N(d1),   Δput = N(d1) − 1
Put-Call ParityC − P = S0 − Ke−rT

A.7 Mortality Models (Part VIII)

Survival Probabilitytpx = S(x+t)/S(x) = lx+t/lx
Force of Mortalityμ(x) = −S′(x)/S(x),   S(x) = exp[−∫₀ˣ μ(s)ds]
De Moivreμ(x) = 1/(ω−x),   tpx = (ω−x−t)/(ω−x)
Constant Force (Exponential)μ(x) = μ,   tpx = e−μt
Gompertz / Makehamμ(x) = Bcx  (Gompertz),   μ(x) = A+Bcx  (Makeham)
Complete / Curtate Expectatione̊x = ∫₀∞ tpx dt,   ex = Σk≥1 kpx,   e̊x ≈ ex + 0.5 (UDD)
UDD Fractional Agesqx = s·qx,   0≤s≤1

A.8 PV Random Variables (Part IX)

Whole Life InsuranceZ = vKx+1,   Ax = E[Z] = Σk≥0 vk+1·kpx·qx+k
Continuous Whole Life InsuranceZ = vTx,   Āx = ∫₀∞ vt·tpx·μ(x+t) dt
Whole Life Annuity-Dueäx = Σk≥0 vk·kpx
Insurance–Annuity IdentityAx = 1 − d·äx  (discrete);   Āx = 1 − δ·āx  (continuous)
Variance — InsuranceVar(Z) = ²Ax − (Ax)²  (²Ax = EPV at double the force of interest, i.e. using v²)
Variance — AnnuityVar(Y) = [²Ax − (Ax)²] / d²

A.9 Premiums & Policy Values (Part X)

Future Loss R.V. (Whole Life)L0 = vKx+1 − P·äKx+1⌐
Equivalence Principle PremiumE[L0]=0 ⟹ P = Ax/äx = (1/äx) − d  (whole life)
Prospective Policy ValuetV = EPV(future benefits) − EPV(future net premiums),  given in force at t
Recursive Reserve Relationship(tV + P)(1+i) = qx+t·(benefit) + px+t·t+1V
Extra Risk — Constant Additionμ*(x) = μ(x) + c
Extra Risk — Constant Multipleq*(x) = k·q(x)

Appendix B — Full-Length Practice Exam

34 original questions, timed at 3.5 hours, mirroring the real distribution of topics across all ten parts of this manual — from short-term reinsurance through loss models, ratemaking, options, mortality, and life-contingent premiums and reserves. Every question below is original to this manual.

Cover the answer choices with a sheet of paper if you can, solve first, then check. For the life-contingency questions, several intentionally reuse the same simple assumptions (constant force of mortality, constant force of interest) across consecutive questions — exactly as the real exam sometimes builds a mini-scenario across several questions — so a mistake early can cascade. Double-check intermediate values before moving on.

Practice Exam — 34 Questions

1. [Short-Term Coverages] For a certain loss distribution, E[X] = 1000 and E[X∧500] = 400. Calculate the loss elimination ratio for a deductible of 500.
(A) 0.25  (B) 0.32  (C) 0.40  (D) 0.48  (E) 0.60
2. [Short-Term Coverages] An excess-of-loss reinsurance treaty has a retention of 100,000 with no upper limit. A claim of 350,000 occurs. Calculate the amount paid by the reinsurer.
(A) 100,000  (B) 150,000  (C) 200,000  (D) 250,000  (E) 350,000
3. [Short-Term Coverages] Under a quota share treaty, the insurer cedes 40% of every loss (and correspondingly 40% of premium) to the reinsurer. Total premium collected is 2,000,000 and total losses are 1,200,000. Calculate the insurer's net loss ratio after the quota share cession.
(A) 0.40  (B) 0.48  (C) 0.54  (D) 0.60  (E) 0.72
4. [Severity Models] A severity distribution is Pareto with shape parameter α = 4. Calculate the highest positive integer k for which E[Xk] exists.
(A) 2  (B) 3  (C) 4  (D) 5  (E) All positive integers k
5. [Frequency Models] A frequency distribution is a member of the (a,b,0) class with a=0.5, b=1.5, and p0=0.2. Calculate p2.
(A) 0.30  (B) 0.40  (C) 0.50  (D) 0.60  (E) 0.70
6. [Aggregate Models] N is Poisson with λ=4. Claim severities Xi are i.i.d., independent of N, with E[X]=100 and Var(X)=40{,}000. Calculate Var(S) for the compound distribution S = X1+⋯+XN.
(A) 160,000  (B) 180,000  (C) 200,000  (D) 220,000  (E) 240,000
7. [Aggregate Models] An individual risk model has three independent policies. Policy i has claim probability qi and, if a claim occurs, a fixed claim amount: (q₁,amount₁)=(0.10,1000), (q₂,amount₂)=(0.20,2000), (q₃,amount₃)=(0.15,1500). Calculate Var(S) where S = Y1+Y2+Y3.
(A) 816,875  (B) 916,875  (C) 1,016,875  (D) 1,116,875  (E) 1,216,875
8. [Aggregate Models] Aggregate losses S are approximated by a Normal distribution with mean 500,000 and standard deviation 80,000. Using this normal approximation, calculate the expected stop-loss payment E[(S−600{,}000)+].
(A) 2,845  (B) 3,412  (C) 4,047  (D) 4,690  (E) 5,225
9. [Parametric Estimation] Five individual losses are observed: 200, 350, 150, 600, 400. Assuming an Exponential distribution, calculate the maximum likelihood estimate of the mean θ.
(A) 300  (B) 320  (C) 340  (D) 360  (E) 380
10. [Parametric Estimation] Losses are left-truncated at 100 (only losses exceeding 100 are observed). Three truncated observations are recorded: 250, 400, 180. Assuming the underlying (ground-up) distribution is Exponential, calculate the maximum likelihood estimate of θ.
(A) 146.67  (B) 156.67  (C) 166.67  (D) 176.67  (E) 186.67
11. [Credibility] Full credibility for claim counts requires that observed aggregate claims fall within 5% of the true expected value with probability 0.90. Using the normal approximation, calculate the expected number of claims needed for full credibility.
(A) 752  (B) 906  (C) 1082  (D) 1225  (E) 1348
12. [Reserving] Cumulative losses for accident year 1 are 1000 at 12 months and 1600 at 24 months. Using the chain-ladder method with this single accident year's data as the age-to-age factor, calculate the projected ultimate losses for accident year 2, which stands at 1100 at 12 months (assume 24 months is ultimate).
(A) 1540  (B) 1650  (C) 1760  (D) 1870  (E) 1980
13. [Reserving] A book of business has a-priori expected losses of 2,000,000 and is estimated to be 40% reported to date. Actual losses reported to date are 750,000. Using the Bornhuetter-Ferguson method, calculate the estimated ultimate losses.
(A) 1,650,000  (B) 1,750,000  (C) 1,850,000  (D) 1,950,000  (E) 2,050,000
14. [Ratemaking] A line of business has a variable expense ratio of 25% and a target profit and contingencies provision of 5%. The experience loss ratio is 0.65. Using the loss ratio method, calculate the indicated rate change.
(A) −11.4%  (B) −9.3%  (C) −7.1%  (D) −5.0%  (E) −2.9%
15. [Option Pricing] A stock currently trades at 50. In a one-period binomial model with u=1.2, d=0.9, continuously compounded risk-free rate r=5%, and a period length of 1 year, calculate the risk-neutral probability of an up-move.
(A) 0.4127  (B) 0.4589  (C) 0.5042  (D) 0.5487  (E) 0.5921
16. [Option Pricing] A stock trades at 100 and pays no dividends. A 1-year European call has strike 100. The risk-free rate is 4% (continuously compounded) and volatility is 25%. Using the Black-Scholes formula, calculate the call price. [You may use N(0.29)≈0.6141, N(0.04)≈0.5160 as approximate table lookups, or compute more precisely.]
(A) 9.64  (B) 10.74  (C) 11.84  (D) 12.95  (E) 14.02
17. [Long-Term Coverages] Which of the following is the primary reason insurable interest is required at the time a life insurance policy is purchased?
(A) To simplify premium calculation  (B) To ensure the insurer can reinsure the risk  (C) To prevent the policy from functioning as a wagering contract and to reduce moral hazard  (D) To determine the policy's cash value  (E) To satisfy investment regulations
18. [Mortality Models] Mortality follows De Moivre's law with ω=100. For a life age 30, calculate 20p30.
(A) 0.6143  (B) 0.6571  (C) 0.7143  (D) 0.7571  (E) 0.8000
19. [Mortality Models] Mortality follows a constant force μ=0.02. For a life age 40, calculate the probability of death between ages 50 and 60 (i.e. 10|10q40).
(A) 0.1183  (B) 0.1484  (C) 0.1682  (D) 0.1854  (E) 0.2013
20. [Mortality Models] A life table shows l60=90{,}000, l61=89{,}000, l62=87{,}800. Calculate q61.
(A) 0.01111  (B) 0.01199  (C) 0.01283  (D) 0.01348  (E) 0.01422
21. [Mortality Models] Under the UDD fractional age assumption, q45=0.01. Calculate 0.5q45.
(A) 0.0025  (B) 0.0040  (C) 0.0050  (D) 0.0060  (E) 0.0075
22. [PV Random Variables] Mortality follows a constant force μ=0.03 and the force of interest is δ=0.05. Calculate Āx, the EPV of a continuous whole life insurance of 1.
(A) 0.300  (B) 0.333  (C) 0.375  (D) 0.400  (E) 0.425
23. [PV Random Variables] Using the same assumptions as the previous question (μ=0.03, δ=0.05), calculate āx, the EPV of a continuous whole life annuity of 1 per year.
(A) 10.0  (B) 11.0  (C) 12.5  (D) 13.5  (E) 15.0
24. [PV Random Variables] Using the same assumptions (μ=0.03, δ=0.05), calculate Var(Z) where Z=vTx is the PV random variable for a continuous whole life insurance of 1.
(A) 0.0512  (B) 0.0684  (C) 0.0901  (D) 0.1077  (E) 0.1250
25. [PV Random Variables] Using the same assumptions (μ=0.03, δ=0.05), calculate the EPV of a 10-year continuous term insurance of 1, Ā1x:10⌐.
(A) 0.156  (B) 0.176  (C) 0.196  (D) 0.207  (E) 0.228
26. [PV Random Variables] Using the same assumptions (μ=0.03, δ=0.05), calculate the EPV of a 10-year pure endowment of 1.
(A) 0.369  (B) 0.401  (C) 0.449  (D) 0.487  (E) 0.512
27. [PV Random Variables] Using the same assumptions (μ=0.03, δ=0.05), calculate the EPV of a 10-year endowment insurance of 1.
(A) 0.556  (B) 0.606  (C) 0.656  (D) 0.706  (E) 0.756
28. [Premiums] Using the same assumptions (μ=0.03, δ=0.05), calculate the continuous net premium rate P̄ for a whole life insurance of 1 on (x), determined by the equivalence principle.
(A) 0.020  (B) 0.025  (C) 0.030  (D) 0.035  (E) 0.040
29. [Policy Values] Under a constant force of mortality, a whole life insurance is funded by a continuous net premium set by the equivalence principle. Calculate the policy value at any duration t > 0, given that the policy is still in force.
(A) It increases linearly with t  (B) It increases then decreases  (C) It equals Āx at every duration  (D) It equals 0 at every duration  (E) It cannot be determined without a life table
30. [Modeling Extra Risk] Base mortality follows a constant force μ=0.03; the force of interest is δ=0.05. A substandard life is rated using a constant multiple of 1.5 applied to the force of mortality. Calculate Āx for the substandard life.
(A) 0.391  (B) 0.417  (C) 0.442  (D) 0.462  (E) 0.474
31. [Modeling Extra Risk] Using the same base assumptions (μ=0.03, δ=0.05), a substandard life is rated using a constant addition of 0.01 to the force of mortality. Calculate āx for the substandard life.
(A) 10.00  (B) 10.53  (C) 11.11  (D) 11.76  (E) 12.50
32. [Premiums] Using the base assumptions (μ=0.03, δ=0.05, so Āx=0.375 and āx=12.5), calculate the continuous premium rate P̄′ such that the expected present value of profit equals −0.05×Āx (i.e. a profit target of 5% of the benefit EPV).
(A) 0.0285  (B) 0.0300  (C) 0.0315  (D) 0.0330  (E) 0.0345
33. [Premiums] Using the base assumptions (μ=0.03, δ=0.05), calculate the continuous net premium rate for a 10-year endowment insurance of 1 on (x), with premiums payable continuously while the policy is in force (i.e. for at most 10 years).
(A) 0.0752  (B) 0.0842  (C) 0.0953  (D) 0.1041  (E) 0.1125
34. [Policy Values] Which of the following best describes why an insurer might use a modified net premium reserve method (such as Full Preliminary Term) instead of the standard net premium reserve?
(A) To increase the premium charged to policyholders  (B) To eliminate the need for a life table  (C) To reduce the artificially high first-year reserve strain caused by high acquisition expenses concentrated in year 1  (D) To comply with option-pricing regulations  (E) To convert a whole life policy into a term policy

Appendix C — Practice Exam Solutions

Full worked solutions for all 34 questions, each tagged with the manual part it draws on so you can jump back for review. Try to solve a question completely before reading its solution.

Solutions

1.
Show Solution
LER = E[X∧500]/E[X] = 400/1000 = 0.40. Answer: (C)
2.
Show Solution
Reinsurer pays the excess over the retention: 350{,}000 − 100{,}000 = 250{,}000. Answer: (D)
3.
Show Solution
Net premium = 0.6(2{,}000{,}000)=1{,}200{,}000; net losses =0.6(1{,}200{,}000)=720{,}000. Net loss ratio =720{,}000/1{,}200{,}000=0.60 — identical to the gross loss ratio, illustrating that quota share proportionally reduces both premium and losses and so leaves the loss ratio unchanged. Answer: (D)
4.
Show Solution
For a Pareto with shape α, E[Xk] exists only for k < α. With α=4, the largest integer strictly below 4 is 3. Answer: (B)
5.
Show Solution
p1 = (a+b)p0 = (0.5+1.5)(0.2) = 0.40. p2 = (a+b/2)p1 = (0.5+0.75)(0.40) = 0.50. Answer: (C)
6.
Show Solution
For Poisson, Var(N)=E[N]=λ=4.
Var(S) = E[N]Var(X) + Var(N)E[X]² = 4(40{,}000) + 4(100)² = 160{,}000+40{,}000 = 200{,}000
Answer: (C)
7.
Show Solution
Each Yi is Bernoulli×amount, so Var(Yi) = amounti²·qi(1−qi): Var(Y1)=1000²(0.09)=90{,}000, Var(Y2)=2000²(0.16)=640{,}000, Var(Y3)=1500²(0.1275)=286{,}875. Sum =1{,}016{,}875. Answer: (C)
8.
Show Solution
z=(600{,}000−500{,}000)/80{,}000=1.25. Using the normal stop-loss formula:
E[(S−d)⁺] = σφ(z) − (d−μ)[1−Φ(z)] = 80{,}000φ(1.25) − 100{,}000[1−Φ(1.25)]
With φ(1.25)≈0.1826 and Φ(1.25)≈0.8944: 80{,}000(0.1826) − 100{,}000(0.1056) ≈ 14{,}608−10{,}560≈4{,}047. Answer: (C)
9.
Show Solution
For Exponential, the MLE is the sample mean: θ̂ = (200+350+150+600+400)/5 = 1700/5 = 340. Answer: (C)
10.
Show Solution
With left truncation at d for an Exponential, the MLE reduces to the average excess over d: θ̂ = [(250−100)+(400−100)+(180−100)]/3 = 530/3 = 176.67. Answer: (D)
11.
Show Solution
z0.95=1.645.
λ0 = (1.645/0.05)² = 32.9² ≈ 1082
Answer: (C)
12.
Show Solution
Age-to-age factor f=1600/1000=1.6. AY2 ultimate =1100×1.6=1760. Answer: (C)
13.
Show Solution
IBNR = (1−0.4)(2{,}000{,}000) = 1{,}200{,}000. Ultimate = 750{,}000+1{,}200{,}000 = 1{,}950{,}000. Answer: (D)
14.
Show Solution
PLR =1−0.25−0.05=0.70. Indicated change =0.65/0.70−1≈−7.1%. Answer: (C)
15.
Show Solution
p* = (e0.05−0.9)/(1.2−0.9) = (1.05127−0.9)/0.3 = 0.5042
Answer: (C)
16.
Show Solution
d1 = [ln(1)+(0.04+0.03125)(1)]/0.25 = 0.07125/0.25 = 0.285; d2=0.285−0.25=0.035. N(d1)≈0.6122, N(d2)≈0.5140.
C = 100(0.6122) − 100e−0.04(0.5140) = 61.22 − 96.08(0.5140) ≈ 61.22−49.38 ≈ 11.84
Answer: (C)
17.
Show Solution
Insurable interest exists to ensure the policy protects against a genuine economic (or close personal) loss rather than functioning as a bet on a stranger's life, which also reduces the incentive for moral hazard. Answer: (C)
18.
Show Solution
20p30 = (ω−30−20)/(ω−30) = (100−50)/70 = 50/70 = 0.7143
Answer: (C)
19.
Show Solution
10p40=e−0.2=0.8187, 20p40=e−0.4=0.6703.
10|10q40 = 10p40 − 20p40 = 0.8187−0.6703 = 0.1484
Answer: (B)
20.
Show Solution
q61 = (l61−l62)/l61 = (89{,}000−87{,}800)/89{,}000 = 1200/89{,}000 = 0.01348
Answer: (D)
21.
Show Solution
Under UDD, sqx=s·qx: 0.5q45=0.5(0.01)=0.005. Answer: (C)
22.
Show Solution
Under constant force, Āx = μ/(μ+δ) = 0.03/0.08 = 0.375. Answer: (C)
23.
Show Solution
āx = (1−Āx)/δ = (1−0.375)/0.05 = 12.5 (equivalently, 1/(μ+δ)=1/0.08=12.5). Answer: (C)
24.
Show Solution
²Āx = μ/(μ+2δ) = 0.03/0.13 = 0.23077.
Var(Z) = ²Āx − (Āx)² = 0.23077 − 0.140625 = 0.09014
Answer: (C)
25.
Show Solution
Ā1x:10⌐ = [μ/(μ+δ)]·[1−e−(μ+δ)(10)] = 0.375(1−e−0.8) = 0.375(0.55067) = 0.2065
Answer: (D)
26.
Show Solution
Pure endowment = e−(μ+δ)(10) = e−0.8 = 0.4493
Answer: (C)
27.
Show Solution
Endowment insurance = term insurance + pure endowment = 0.2065+0.4493 = 0.6558. Answer: (C)
28.
Show Solution
P̄ = Āx/āx = 0.375/12.5 = 0.03
(Under constant force, the equivalence-principle premium always equals μ exactly — a useful sanity check.) Answer: (C)
29.
Show Solution
Constant force of mortality is memoryless: a life that has survived to any duration has exactly the same future lifetime distribution as at issue. Since the equivalence principle sets the reserve to 0 at issue, and the future loss random variable's distribution never changes given survival, the policy value remains 0 at every duration. Answer: (D)
30.
Show Solution
μ* = 1.5(0.03) = 0.045.
Āx* = μ*/(μ*+δ) = 0.045/0.095 = 0.4737
Answer: (E)
31.
Show Solution
μ* = 0.03+0.01 = 0.04.
āx* = 1/(μ*+δ) = 1/0.09 = 11.11
Answer: (C)
32.
Show Solution
Target: E[L0] = Āx − P̄′āx = −0.05(0.375) = −0.01875.
P̄′ = (0.375+0.01875)/12.5 = 0.39375/12.5 = 0.0315
Answer: (C)
33.
Show Solution
āx:10⌐ = [1−e−(μ+δ)(10)]/(μ+δ) = 0.55067/0.08 = 6.8834. Using the endowment insurance EPV from Question 27:
P̄ = 0.6558/6.8834 = 0.0953
Answer: (C)
34.
Show Solution
First-year acquisition expenses (commissions, underwriting costs) are much higher than renewal-year expenses, which creates unusually high "reserve strain" in year 1 under the standard net premium method. A modified method like Full Preliminary Term shifts some of that first-year net premium burden to later years, better matching the reserve to actual expected surrender values and easing statutory capital strain. Answer: (C)
